$\\begin{matrix} {\\gamma\_{I,y} = \\int\_{- L}^{L}{\\tau\_{yy}dx},} & {\\gamma\_{I,z} = \\int\_{- L}^{L}{\\tau\_{zz}dx}} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.37)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si136.png)(2.37) 

From our previous 1D analytic solution of the diffuse interface model, we know the interfacial tension is

$\\begin{matrix} {\\gamma\_{I} = \\gamma\_{I,y} = \\gamma\_{I,z} = \\int\_{- L}^{L}{\\left( {f\_{b}\\left( \\phi \\right) + \\frac{c\_{I}}{2}\\left( {\\partial\_{x}\\phi} \\right)^{2}} \\right)dx}} \\\\ {\\mspace{45mu} = \\int\_{- L}^{L}{c\_{I}\\left( {\\partial\_{x}\\phi} \\right)^{2}dx} = \\int\_{- L}^{L}{2f\_{b}\\left( \\phi \\right)dx = \\frac{2\\sqrt{2}}{3}\\sqrt{c\_{b}c\_{I}}}} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.38)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si137.png)(2.38) 

Comparing the previous equations, it is reasonable to assume that *τ*[*y**y*] = *τ*[*z**z*] = *c*[*I*](∂[*x*]*ϕ*)²![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si138.png). Thus we have the expression for *τ*[*i**j*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si139.png),

$\\begin{array}{l} {\\tau = c\_{I}\\left( {\\partial\_{x}\\phi} \\right)^{2}\\left( \\begin{array}{lll} 0 & 0 & 0 \\\\ 0 & 1 & 0 \\\\ 0 & 0 & 1 \\\\ \\end{array} \\right)} \\\\ {\\quad = c\_{I}\\left( {\\nabla\\phi \\cdot \\nabla\\phi} \\right)\\mathbf{I} - c\_{I}\\left( {\\partial\_{x}\\phi} \\right)^{2}\\left( \\begin{array}{lll} 1 & 0 & 0 \\\\ 0 & 0 & 0 \\\\ 0 & 0 & 0 \\\\ \\end{array} \\right)} \\\\ {\\quad = c\_{I}\\left( {\\nabla\\phi \\cdot \\nabla\\phi} \\right)\\mathbf{I} - c\_{I}\\nabla\\phi \\otimes \\nabla\\phi} \\\\ \\end{array}$ (2.39)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si140.png)(2.39) 

where the last line is indeed a tensor by a quick verification. From the stress expression *σ* =  − *p*[*x*]**I** + *τ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si141.png), we obtain

*σ* = (*c*[*I*](∇*ϕ*⋅∇*ϕ*)−*p*[*x*])**I** − *c*[*I*]∇*ϕ* ⊗ ∇*ϕ* (2.40)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si142.png)(2.40) 

The above stress tensor can be split into an isotropic term and an anisotropic term,

*σ* = *σ*[*i**s**o*] + *σ*[*a**n**i**s**o*] (2.41)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si143.png)(2.41) 

where *σ*[*i**s**o*] = (*c*[*I*](∇*ϕ*⋅∇*ϕ*)−*p*[*x*])**I**![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si144.png) and *σ*[*a**n**i**s**o*] =  − *c*[*I*]∇*ϕ* ⊗ ∇*ϕ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si145.png).

From the previous equation, it is clear to see that the isotropy-plus-anisotropy splitting is not unique. In incompressible fluid flow, specific forms of *σ*[*i**s**o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si146.png) do not matter, as the difference in varied forms will be absorbed to the Lagrange multiplier (interpreted as pressure). The anisotropic part *σ*[*a**n**i**s**o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si147.png) results from the gradient contribution of free energy, and it can model the effect of interfacial tension to flow.

Recall Cauchy’s equation of motion *ρ*(*D**v*[*i*]/*D**t*) = *ρ**g*[*i*] + (∂*σ*[*i**j*]/∂*x*[*j*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si148.png), which can lead to the single-phase N–S equation for incompressible fluid with constant shear viscosity,

$\\rho\\left( {\\frac{\\partial\\mathbf{v}}{\\partial t} + \\mathbf{v} \\cdot \\nabla\\mathbf{v}} \\right) = \\rho\\mathbf{g} - \\nabla p + \\eta\\nabla^{2}\\mathbf{v}$ (2.42)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si149.png)(2.42) 

Formulation [(2.42)](#chp002.xhtml#eqn42) does not contain the anisotropic term for modeling interfacial tension *σ*[*a**n**i**s**o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si150.png). Including it, the following (two-phase) N–S equation can be obtained,

$\\rho\\left( {\\frac{\\partial\\mathbf{v}}{\\partial t} + \\mathbf{v} \\cdot \\nabla\\mathbf{v}} \\right) = \\rho\\mathbf{g} - \\nabla p + \\eta\\nabla^{2}\\mathbf{v} + \\nabla \\cdot \\sigma\_{aniso}$ (2.43)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si151.png)(2.43) 

With the result *σ*[*a**n**i**s**o*] =  − *c*[*I*]∇*ϕ* ⊗ ∇*ϕ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si152.png), the (two-phase) N–S [Eq. (2.43)](#chp002.xhtml#eqn43) reads

$\\rho\\left( {\\frac{\\partial\\mathbf{v}}{\\partial t} + \\mathbf{v} \\cdot \\nabla\\mathbf{v}} \\right) = \\rho\\mathbf{g} - \\nabla p + \\eta\\nabla^{2}\\mathbf{v} - \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {c\_{I}\\nabla\\phi \\otimes \\nabla\\phi} \\right)$ (2.44)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si153.png)(2.44) 

Define *σ*[*v**i**s*] = *η*(∇**v**+∇**v**^(*T*))![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si154.png), [Eq. (2.44)](#chp002.xhtml#eqn44) becomes

$\\rho\\left( {\\frac{\\partial\\mathbf{v}}{\\partial t} + \\mathbf{v} \\cdot \\nabla\\mathbf{v}} \\right) = \\rho\\mathbf{g} - \\nabla p + \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\sigma\_{visc} + \\sigma\_{aniso}} \\right)$ (2.45)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si155.png)(2.45) 

or

$\\rho\\left( {\\frac{\\partial\\mathbf{v}}{\\partial t} + \\mathbf{v} \\cdot \\nabla\\mathbf{v}} \\right) = \\rho\\mathbf{g} - \\nabla p + \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\sigma\_{visc} - c\_{I}\\nabla\\phi \\otimes \\nabla\\phi} \\right)$ (2.46)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si156.png)(2.46) 

It should be noted that we can also get other expressions of ∇ ⋅ *σ*[*a**n**i**s**o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si157.png). It is not difficult to prove the following mathematical identity:

$\\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\nabla\\phi \\otimes \\nabla\\phi} \\right) = \\nabla\\phi\\nabla^{2}\\phi + \\nabla\\left( \\frac{\\nabla\\phi \\cdot \\nabla\\phi}{2} \\right)$ (2.47)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si158.png)(2.47) 

Recall ∇ ⋅ *σ*[*a**n**i**s**o*] = ∇ ⋅ (−*c*[*I*]∇*ϕ*⊗∇*ϕ*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si159.png), since we can absorb (*c*[*I*]∇*ϕ*⋅∇*ϕ*/2)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si160.png) to the pressure, the influence of the interfacial tension can also be written as

∇ ⋅ *σ*[*a**n**i**s**o*] =  − *c*[*I*]∇*ϕ*∇²*ϕ* (2.48)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si161.png)(2.48) 

Recall *μ* = *μ*[*b*] − *c*[*I*]∇²*ϕ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si162.png) and *μ*[*b*]∇*ϕ* = ∇*f*[*b*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si163.png), implying *μ*∇*ϕ* = ∇*f*[*b*] − *c*[*I*]∇*ϕ*∇²*ϕ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si164.png). Since *f*[*b*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si165.png) can be absorbed to the pressure, we also have

∇ ⋅ *σ*[*a**n**i**s**o*] = *μ*∇*ϕ* (2.49)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si166.png)(2.49)  

### 2.2.3 Consistency of the N–S interfacial term and C–H model

 

#### 2.2.3.1 Equilibrium condition and partial differential equation from Cahn–Hilliard model

Recall the Cahn–Hilliard equation without convection,

$\\frac{\\partial\\phi}{\\partial t} = \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {M\\nabla\\mu} \\right)$ (2.50)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si167.png)(2.50) 

where *μ* = *c*[*b*](*ϕ*³−*ϕ*) − *c*[*I*]∇²*ϕ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si168.png) and *M* is the mobility coefficient.

At equilibrium, [Eq. (2.50)](#chp002.xhtml#eqn50) together with homogeneous Neumann B.C. implies *μ* = *c**o**n**s**t*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si169.png). It is easy to see *μ* = *μ*[*b*] + *μ*[∇] = 0 + 0 = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si170.png) in the bulk phase region and thus the constant above must be zero; in other words, *μ* must vanish everywhere,

*μ* = 0 (2.51)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si171.png)(2.51) 

Thus Cahn–Hilliard equation at equilibrium becomes

*c*[*I*]∇²*ϕ* = *μ*[*b*](*ϕ*) = *c*[*b*](*ϕ*³−*ϕ*) (2.52)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si172.png)(2.52) 

Define

*p*[*b*] :  = *ϕ**μ*[*b*](*ϕ*) − *f*[*b*](*ϕ*) (2.53)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si173.png)(2.53) 

Here the definition [(2.53)](#chp002.xhtml#eqn53) has its motivation from thermodynamics. If we have a consistent equation of state (EOS) of compressible fluid, the abovementioned definition gives thermodynamic pressure in bulk phase regions. For double-well potential the definition is formal only, and it may not relate to (mechanical) pressure in the system. For the Cahn–Hilliard model,

$f = \\frac{c\_{b}}{4}\\left( {\\phi^{2} - 1} \\right)^{2} + \\frac{c\_{I}}{2}\\left| {\\nabla\\phi} \\right|^{2}$ (2.54)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si174.png)(2.54) 

Then we have

$\\mu = \\frac{\\delta f}{\\delta\\phi} = c\_{b}\\phi\\left( {\\phi^{2} - 1} \\right) - c\_{I}\\nabla^{2}\\phi$ (2.55)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si175.png)(2.55) 

$\\widehat{p} = - f + \\mu\\phi = - \\frac{c\_{b}}{4}\\left( {\\phi^{2} - 1} \\right)^{2} - \\frac{c\_{I}}{2}\\left| {\\nabla\\phi} \\right|^{2} + c\_{b}\\phi^{2}\\left( {\\phi^{2} - 1} \\right) - c\_{I}\\phi\\nabla^{2}\\phi$ (2.56)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si176.png)(2.56) 

 

#### 2.2.3.2 Implication of equilibrium partial differential equation

The Cahn–Hilliard equation at equilibrium implies

*ϕ**μ*[*b*](*ϕ*) = *c*[*I*]*ϕ*∇²*ϕ* (2.57)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si177.png)(2.57) 

The gradient of the bulk free energy *f*[*b*] becomes

$\\nabla f\_{b} = \\frac{\\partial f\_{b}}{\\partial\\phi}\\nabla\\phi = \\mu\_{b}\\nabla\\phi = c\_{I}\\nabla^{2}\\phi\\nabla\\phi$ (2.58)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si178.png)(2.58) 

The gradient of the previously defined then can be written as *p*[*b*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si179.png) becomes

∇*p*[*b*] = ∇(*ϕ**μ*[*b*]) − ∇*f*[*b*] = ∇(*c*[*I*]*ϕ*∇²*ϕ*) − *c*[*I*]∇²*ϕ*∇*ϕ* (2.59)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si180.png)(2.59) 

[Eq. (2.59)](#chp002.xhtml#eqn59) can be further simplified to

∇*p*[*b*] − *c*[*I*]*ϕ*∇(∇²*ϕ*) = 0 (2.60)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si181.png)(2.60)  

#### 2.2.3.3 Equation for mechanical equilibrium

With some algebraic manipulations (preferably using Einstein’s notation of summation), one can prove the following mathematical identity:

$\\phi\\nabla\\left( {\\nabla^{2}\\phi} \\right) = \\nabla\\left( {\\phi\\nabla^{2}\\phi} \\right) + \\nabla\\left( \\frac{\\nabla\\phi \\cdot \\nabla\\phi}{2} \\right) - \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\nabla\\phi \\otimes \\nabla\\phi} \\right)$ (2.61)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si182.png)(2.61) 

Substituting the abovementioned identity [(2.61)](#chp002.xhtml#eqn61) into the equilibrium [Eq. (2.60)](#chp002.xhtml#eqn60) yields

∇ ⋅ *σ* :  = ∇ ⋅ (−*p̂***I**−*c*[*I*]∇*ϕ*⊗∇*ϕ*) = 0 (2.62)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si183.png)(2.62) 

where $\\widehat{p}: = p\_{b} - c\_{I}\\phi\\nabla^{2}\\phi - \\frac{c\_{I}}{2}\\nabla\\phi \\cdot \\nabla\\phi$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si184.png).

 

#### 2.2.3.4 Consistency of equilibrium conditions

Recall the stress expression [(2.41)](#chp002.xhtml#eqn41) obtained previously, we see [Eq. (2.62)](#chp002.xhtml#eqn62) contains the same *σ*[*a**n**i**s**o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si185.png). In fact, one sees

$p\_{x} = \\widehat{p} + c\_{I}\\left( {\\nabla\\phi \\cdot \\nabla\\phi} \\right) = p\_{b} - c\_{I}\\phi\\nabla^{2}\\phi + \\frac{c\_{I}}{2}\\nabla\\phi \\cdot \\nabla\\phi$ (2.63)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si186.png)(2.63) 

Previous derivation shows that the mechanical equilibrium ∇ ⋅ *σ* = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si187.png) with *σ* :  =  − *p̂***I** − *c*[*I*]∇*ϕ* ⊗ ∇*ϕ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si188.png) is a consequence of the chemical equilibrium condition *μ* = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si189.png). In other words, chemical equilibrium implies mechanical equilibrium in Cahn–Hilliard model.

 

### 2.2.4 The N–S/C–H model with boundary and initial conditions

 

#### 2.2.4.1 No-slip boundary conditions

The no-slip B.C., that is, zero relative tangential velocity between the fluid and solid at the interface, serves as a cornerstone in continuum hydrodynamics. The no-slip B.C. works well for macroscopic flows at low flow rate; however, it has been well known that the no-slip B.C. is not applicable to the moving contact line where the fluid–fluid interface intersects the solid wall. In the two-phase immiscible flow where one fluid displaces another fluid, the contact line appears to “slip” at the solid surface, in direct violation of the no-slip B.C. Furthermore, the viscous stress diverges at the contact line if the no-slip B.C. is applied everywhere along the solid wall.

This stress divergence is best illustrated in the reference frame where the fluid–fluid interface is time-independent while the wall moves with velocity *U*. As the fluid velocity has to change from *U* at the wall (as required by the no-slip B.C.) to zero at the fluid–fluid interface (which is static), the viscous stress varies as *η**U*/*x*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si190.png), where *η*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si191.png) is the viscosity and *x* is the distance along the wall away from the contact line. Obviously, this stress diverges as *x*→0![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si192.png) because the distance over which the fluid velocity changes from *U* to zero tends to vanish as the contact line is approached. In particular, this stress divergence is nonintegrable (the integral of 1/*x* yields ln *x*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si193.png)), thus implying infinite viscous dissipation.

 

#### 2.2.4.2 Momentum and mass balances

With the result ∇ ⋅ *σ*[*a**n**i**s**o*] = *μ*∇*ϕ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si194.png) the (two-phase) N–S equation reads

$\\begin{matrix} {\\rho\\left( {\\frac{\\partial\\mathbf{v}}{\\partial t} + \\left( {\\mathbf{v} \\cdot \\nabla} \\right)\\mathbf{v}} \\right) = \\rho\\mathbf{g} - \\nabla p + \\eta\\nabla^{2}\\mathbf{v} + \\mu\\nabla\\phi,} & {in} & \\Omega \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.64)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si195.png)(2.64) 

The volume conservation is still applicable,

$\\begin{matrix} {\\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{v} = 0,} & {in} & \\Omega \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.65)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si196.png)(2.65) 

The transport of interface is modeled by the Cahn–Hilliard equation (with convection),

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{\\partial\\phi}{\\partial t} + \\mathbf{v} \\cdot \\nabla\\phi = M\\nabla^{2}\\mu,} & {in} & \\Omega \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.66)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si197.png)(2.66)  

#### 2.2.4.3 Generalized Navier boundary condition

We apply the generalized Navier B.C.,

$\\begin{matrix} {\\beta\\nu\_{\\tau}^{slip} = - \\eta\\left( {\\partial\_{n}\\nu\_{\\tau} + \\partial\_{\\tau}\\nu\_{n}} \\right) + L\\left( \\phi \\right)\\partial\_{\\tau}\\phi,} & \\begin{matrix} {on} & \\Gamma\_{slip} \\\\ \\end{matrix} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.67)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si198.png)(2.67) 

where *ν*[*τ*]^(*s**l**i**p*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si199.png) is the (tangential) slip velocity related to the wall; *L*(*ϕ*)∂[*τ*]*ϕ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si200.png) is the uncompensated Young stress with *L*(*ϕ*) = *c*[*I*]∂[*n*]*ϕ* + ((∂*γ*[*w**f*](*ϕ*))/∂*ϕ*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si201.png), *γ*[*w**f*](*ϕ*) =  − (1/2)*γ*cos (*θ*[*S*])sin ((*π*/2)*ϕ*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si202.png) is the fluid–solid interfacial free energy density, *θ*[*S*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si203.png) is the static contact angle; *v*[*n*] = **v** ⋅ **n**![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si204.png) and *v*[*τ*] = **v** ⋅ **τ**![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si205.png) are the velocities respectively along the normal and tangent directions of the slip boundary, where **n** and **τ**![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si206.png) are the unit normal and tangent vector of the slip boundary.

 

#### 2.2.4.4 Dynamic boundary conditions and nonpenetration boundary conditions

For the phase-field equation the following dynamic B.C. is imposed,

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{\\partial\\phi}{\\partial t} + u\_{\\tau}\\partial\_{\\tau}\\phi = - \\Gamma L\\left( \\phi \\right),} & \\begin{matrix} {on} & \\Gamma\_{slip} \\\\ \\end{matrix} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.68)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si207.png)(2.68) 

where *Γ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si208.png) is a (positive) phenomenological parameter.

The following nonpenetration B.Cs are adopted on the solid boundary,

$\\begin{matrix} {\\nu\_{n} = 0,} & \\begin{matrix} {on} & {\\Gamma\_{slip},} \\\\ \\end{matrix} \\\\ \\end{matrix}\\begin{matrix} & \\begin{matrix} {\\partial\_{n}\\mu = 0,} & \\begin{matrix} {on} & \\Gamma\_{slip} \\\\ \\end{matrix} \\\\ \\end{matrix} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.69)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si209.png)(2.69) 

The I.Cs are always given and are straightforward for velocity, pressure, and phase-field variable.

 

#### 2.2.4.5 Dimensionless modeling equations and boundary conditions

When the length is scaled by a reference length *L*[0]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si210.png), velocity is scaled by a reference velocity *V*, time is scaled by *L*[0]/*V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si211.png), and pressure is scaled by *η**V*/*L*[0]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si212.png), the following dimensionless governing equations can be obtained:

$\\begin{matrix} {R\\left( {\\frac{\\partial\\mathbf{v}}{\\partial t} + \\left( {\\mathbf{v} \\cdot \\nabla} \\right)\\mathbf{v}} \\right) = \\mathbf{g} - \\nabla p + \\nabla^{2}\\mathbf{v} + \\lambda\\mu\\nabla\\phi,} & {in} & \\Omega \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.70a)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si213.png)(2.70a) 

$\\begin{matrix} {\\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{v} = 0,} & {in} & \\Omega \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.70b)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si214.png)(2.70b) 

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{\\partial\\phi}{\\partial t} + \\mathbf{v} \\cdot \\nabla\\phi = L\_{d}\\nabla^{2}\\mu,} & {in} & \\Omega \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.70c)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si215.png)(2.70c) 

$\\begin{matrix} {\\mu = \\frac{1}{\\mathit{\\epsilon}}\\left( {\\phi^{3} - \\phi} \\right) - \\mathit{\\epsilon}\\nabla^{2}\\phi,} & {in} & \\Omega \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.70d)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si216.png)(2.70d) 

where *ϵ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si217.png) is the ratio between interface thickness *ζ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si218.png) and reference length *L*[0]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si219.png).

After the scaling the B.C.s along the solid slip boundary becomes

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{\\nu\_{\\tau}^{slip}}{L\_{S}} = - \\eta\\left( {\\partial\_{n}\\nu\_{\\tau} + \\partial\_{\\tau}\\nu\_{n}} \\right) + \\lambda L\\left( \\phi \\right)\\partial\_{\\tau}\\phi,} & \\begin{matrix} {on} & \\Gamma\_{slip} \\\\ \\end{matrix} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.71a)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si220.png)(2.71a) 

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{\\partial\\phi}{\\partial t} + u\_{\\tau}\\partial\_{\\tau}\\phi = - V\_{S}L\\left( \\phi \\right),} & \\begin{matrix} {on} & \\Gamma\_{slip} \\\\ \\end{matrix} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.71b)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si221.png)(2.71b) 

$\\begin{matrix} {\\nu\_{n} = 0,} & {\\partial\_{n}\\mu = 0,} \\\\ \\end{matrix}\\begin{matrix} \\\\ \\end{matrix}\\begin{matrix} {on} & \\Gamma\_{slip} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.71c)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si222.png)(2.71c) 

where $L\\left( \\phi \\right) = \\mathit{\\epsilon}\\partial\_{n}\\phi - \\left( {\\sqrt{2}/6} \\right)\\pi\\cos\\theta\_{s}\\cos\\left( {\\left( {\\pi/2} \\right)\\phi} \\right)$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si223.png).

The following six dimensionless parameters are involved in the abovementioned equations:

$L\_{d}: = \\frac{3M\\gamma}{2\\sqrt{2}VL\_{0}^{2}}, R: = \\frac{\\rho VL\_{0}}{\\eta},\\lambda: = \\frac{3\\gamma}{2\\sqrt{2}\\eta V}, V\_{s}: = \\frac{c\_{I}\\Gamma}{V}, L\_{S}: = \\frac{\\eta}{\\beta L\_{0}},\\mathit{\\epsilon}: = \\frac{\\zeta}{L\_{0}}$ (2.72)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si224.png)(2.72) 

where *R* is the well-known Reynolds number, the ratio of inertial forces to viscous forces within a fluid. The parameter is inversely proportional to the capillary number, *C**a* = *η**V*/*γ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si225.png).

 

## 2.3 Dynamic Van der Waals theory

 

### 2.3.1 Motivation

In most phase transition theories, the temperature *T* is a given parameter, which is independent of space and the fluctuation of *T* is assumed to be small that is ignorable. For instance, the Ginzburg–Landau theory is based on a free energy functional with homogeneous *T*. However, there can be cases in which phase transitions occur in inhomogeneous temperature. For example, wetting properties near the gas–liquid critical point are very sensitive to applied heat flux; boiling processes and droplet motion are significantly affected by applied heat flux. To consider effects of nonuniform distribution of temperature, 

 

### 2.3.2 Introduction of dynamic Van der Waals theory

The van der Waals theory presents a brief description of gas–liquid phase transitions in one-component fluids, which is an equilibrium mean-field theory for hard sphere particles with long-range attractive interaction. In a pioneering paper published in 1893, van der Waals introduced a gradient term in the Helmholtz free energy density to describe a gas–liquid interface. Such a gradient term began to be widely adopted in statistical mechanics of nonuniform states. In this part, we will first review the fundamental concepts of the van der Waals theory then introduce the gradient theory and equilibrium conditions proposed by Onuki.

 

#### 2.3.2.1 van der Waals theory

For monoatomic molecules the Helmholtz free density *f*(*n*,*T*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si226.png) can be written as a function of temperature *T* and number density *n*,

*f* = *k*[*B*]*T**n*\[ln(*λ*[*t**h*]^(*d*)*n*)−1−ln(1−*v*[0]*n*)\] − *ϵ**v*[0]*n*² (2.73)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si227.png)(2.73) 

where *v*[0]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si228.png) is molecular volume, *v*[0] = *a*^(*d*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si229.png) with molecular radius *a* and space dimensionality *d*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si230.png); *ϵ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si231.png) is magnitude of the attractive potential; *k*[*B*] is Boltzmann coefficient; *λ*[*t**h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si232.png) is thermal de Broglie length, *λ*[*t**h*] = *h*(2*π*/*m**T*)^(1/2)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si233.png), *m*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si234.png) is molecular mass.

As functions of *n* and *T*, the internal energy density *e*, the entropy *s* per particle, and the pressure *p* are respectively calculated by

*e* = *d**n**k*[*B*]*T*/2−*ϵ**v*[0]*n*² (2.74)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si235.png)(2.74) 

*s* =  − *k*[*B*]ln \[*λ*[*t**h*]^(*d*)*n*/(1−*v*[0]*n*)\] + *k*[*B*](*d*+2)/2 (2.75)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si236.png)(2.75) 

*p* = *n**k*[*B*]*T*/(1−*v*[0]*n*)−*ϵ**v*[0]*n*² (2.76)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si237.png)(2.76) 

van der Waals introduced the gradient free energy density,

$f\_{gra} = \\frac{1}{2}M\\left| {\\nabla n} \\right|^{2}$ (2.77)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si238.png)(2.77) 

Consider the equilibrium interface density profile *n* = *n*(*x*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si239.png) varying along the *x* axis, the chemical potential per particle *μ*(*n*,*T*) = (∂*f*/∂*n*)[*T*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si240.png) changes as

$\\mu - \\mu\_{cx} = \\frac{M}{2}\\frac{d^{2}n}{dx^{2}} + \\frac{d}{dx}\\frac{M}{2}\\frac{dn}{dx}$ (2.78)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si241.png)(2.78) 

where *μ*[*c**x*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si242.png) is the chemical potential on the coexistence curve.

Due to *d**p* = *n**d**μ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si243.png) at constant *T*, the van der Waals pressure in [Eq. (2.76)](#chp002.xhtml#eqn76) can be expressed as

$p - p\_{cx} = \\frac{M}{2}n\\frac{d^{2}n}{dx^{2}} + \\frac{d}{dx}\\frac{M}{2}n\\frac{dn}{dx} - M\\left( \\frac{dn}{dx} \\right)^{2}$ (2.79)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si244.png)(2.79) 

where *p*[*c**x*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si245.png) is the chemical potential on the coexistence curve.

The surface tension *γ*(*T*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si246.png) reads

$\\gamma = {\\int\_{- \\infty}^{+ \\infty}{dxM\\left( \\frac{dn}{dx} \\right)}}^{2}$ (2.80)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si247.png)(2.80) 

where in [Eqs. (2.77)](#chp002.xhtml#eqn77)– [(2.80)](#chp002.xhtml#eqn80), *M* is coefficient of the gradient term in Helmholtz free energy defined in equilibrium as *M*\=*CT*+*K*. The capillary pressure tensor *C*, which depends on the density gradient, incorporates the total pressure tensor. The gradient term represents the contribution of density nonuniformity to the pressure tensor as a function of density gradient, it explains the energy required to form and maintain the density nonuniformity.

 

#### 2.3.2.2 Gradient theory and equilibrium conditions

The gradient contributions to entropy and internal energy in van der Waals theory are computed by

$S\_{b} = \\int{d\\mathbf{r}}\\left\\lbrack {ns\\left( {n,e} \\right) - \\frac{1}{2}C\\left| {\\nabla n} \\right|^{2}} \\right\\rbrack$ (2.81)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si248.png)(2.81) 

$\\varepsilon\_{b} = \\int{d\\mathbf{r}}\\left\\lbrack {e + \\frac{1}{2}K\\left| {\\nabla n} \\right|^{2}} \\right\\rbrack$ (2.82)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si249.png)(2.82) 

where coefficients *K* and *C* are often set as *K*\=0 and *C* is independent of *n*, but to construct a general theory, *C* and *K* can be expressed as a function of *n*, *C* = *C*(*n*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si250.png), *K* = *K*(*n*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si251.png).

From [Eqs. (2.81)](#chp002.xhtml#eqn81) and [(2.82)](#chp002.xhtml#eqn82) the entropy density and internal energy density, including gradient contributions can thus be expressed as

$\\widehat{S} = ns - \\frac{1}{2}C\\left| {\\nabla n} \\right|^{2}$ (2.83)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si252.png)(2.83) 

$\\widehat{e} = e + \\frac{1}{2}K\\left| {\\nabla n} \\right|^{2}$ (2.84)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si253.png)(2.84) 

where the gradient terms represent a decrease of entropy and an increase of energy due to inhomogeneity of *n*, respectively. They are important in the interface region. Note that the gravitational energy is neglected here.

Define the local temperature *T*(*n*,*e*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si254.png) as follows,

$\\frac{1}{T} = \\left( {\\frac{\\delta}{\\delta e}S\_{b}} \\right)\_{n} = n\\left( \\frac{\\partial s}{\\partial e} \\right)\_{n}$ (2.85)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si255.png)(2.85) 

where *n* is fixed in the derivatives. This definition of *T* is analogous to that in a microcanonical ensemble, it can even be used in the situation with inhomogeneous *n* and *e* in nonequilibrium.

Also define a generalized chemical potential,

$\\widehat{\\mu} = - T\\left( \\frac{\\delta S\_{b}}{\\delta n} \\right)\_{\\widehat{e}} = \\mu - T\\nabla \\cdot \\frac{M}{T}\\nabla n + \\frac{M\\prime}{2}\\left| {\\nabla n} \\right|^{2}$ (2.86)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si256.png)(2.86) 

where the internal energy density *ê*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si257.png) is fixed and we set *δ**e* =  − *δ*(*K*|∇*n*|²/2)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si258.png) in the functional derivative; *μ* = (*e*+*p*)/*n* − *T**s*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si259.png) is the usual chemical potential per particle.

Suppose *S*[*b*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si260.png) is a function of *ê*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si261.png) and *n*, a small change of *ê*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si262.png) and *n* would yield an incremental variation of *S*[*b*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si263.png),

$\\delta S\_{b} = \\int{d\\mathbf{r}}\\left( {\\frac{1}{T}\\delta\\widehat{e} - \\frac{\\widehat{\\mu}}{T}\\delta n} \\right) - \\int{da}\\frac{M}{T}\\left( {\\mathbf{\\nu} \\cdot \\nabla n} \\right)\\delta n$ (2.87)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si264.png)(2.87) 

where the second term is the surface integral, *da* is the surface element, and **ν**![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si265.png) is the outward normal unit vector at the surface.

In equilibrium, we maximize *S*[*b*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si266.png) at a fixed particle number *N* = ∫*d***r***n*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si267.png) and a fixed energy $\\varepsilon\_{b} = \\int{d\\mathbf{r}\\widehat{\\mathbf{\\varepsilon}}}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si268.png) for a fluid confined in a cell. For the bulk equilibrium, introduce

$W = \\frac{S\_{b}}{k\_{B}} + \\mathbf{\\upsilon}N - \\beta\\varepsilon\_{b}$ (2.88)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si269.png)(2.88) 

where **υ**![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si270.png) and *β* are the Lagrange multipliers.

In the Ginzburg–Landau theory, *W* can be furthermore minimized with respect to *n* to obtain

*μ̂* = *k*[*B*]*T**ν* = const (2.89)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si271.png)(2.89) 

where *μ̂*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si272.png) is defined by [Eq. (2.86)](#chp002.xhtml#eqn86). In the thermodynamic limit *W* = *p**V*/*k*[*B*]*T*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si273.png) where *p* is the pressure and *V* is the total volume of the fluid.

The stress based on van der Waals pressure can be expressed as

$\\prod\_{ij} = p\\delta\_{ij} - CT\\left\\lbrack {n\\nabla^{2}n + \\frac{\\left( {\\nabla n} \\right)^{2}}{2}} \\right\\rbrack\\delta\_{ij} + CT\\nabla\_{i}n\\nabla\_{j}n$ (2.90)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si274.png)(2.90)  

### 2.3.3 Generalized hydrodynamic equations

Generalized hydrodynamic equations were developed by 

The mass density *ρ* = *m**n*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si275.png) (*m* is the particle mass) obeys the following continuum equation:

$\\frac{\\partial\\rho}{\\partial t} = - \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\rho\\mathbf{\\nu}} \\right)$ (2.91)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si276.png)(2.91) 

[Eq. (2.91)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn91) can be further written as

$\\frac{\\partial n}{\\partial t} = - \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {n\\mathbf{\\nu}} \\right)$ (2.92)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si277.png)(2.92) 

The momentum equation reads

$\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial t}\\rho\\mathbf{\\nu} = - \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\rho\\mathbf{\\nu}\\mathbf{\\nu}} \\right) - \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\prod\\limits^{\\leftrightarrow} - \\overset{\\leftrightarrow}{\\sigma}} \\right) - \\rho g\\mathbf{e}\_{z}$ (2.93)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si278.png)(2.93) 

where the last term stems from the gravity with **e**[*z*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si279.png) being the (upward) unit vector along *z* axis; $\\prod\\limits^{\\leftrightarrow} = \\left\\{ \\prod\_{ij} \\right\\}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si280.png) is the reversible stress tensor, including the gradient contributions, it can be calculated by [Eq. (2.90)](#chp002.xhtml#eqn90); $\\overset{\\leftrightarrow}{\\sigma} = \\left\\{ \\sigma\_{ij} \\right\\}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si281.png) is the dissipative stress tensor, *σ*[*i**j*] = *η*(∇[*i*]*ν*[*j*]+∇[*j*]*ν*[*i*]) + (*ζ*−2*η*/*d*)*δ*[*i**j*]∇ ⋅ **ν**![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si282.png) with ∇[*i*] = ∂/∂*x*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si283.png), *η*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si284.png) and *ζ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si285.png) are the shear and bulk viscosities.

The energy equation yields

$\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial t}e\_{T} = - \\nabla \\cdot \\left\\lbrack {e\_{T}\\mathbf{\\nu} + \\left( {\\prod\\limits^{\\leftrightarrow} - \\overset{\\leftrightarrow}{\\sigma}} \\right) \\cdot \\mathbf{\\nu}} \\right\\rbrack + \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\lambda\\nabla T} \\right) - \\rho gv\_{z}$ (2.94)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si286.png)(2.94) 

where *e*[*T*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si287.png) is the energy density, *e*[*T*] = *ê* + *ρ***ν**²/2![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si288.png); *λ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si289.png) is thermal conductivity.

 

## 2.4 Multiphase porous flow solvers

 

### 2.4.1 Incompressible two-phase flow solver

The governing equations of incompressible two-phase flows are introduced next. There are two conservation laws for incompressible two-phase flow, one for each phase,

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{\\partial\\left( {\\phi S\_{\\alpha}} \\right)}{\\partial t} + \\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{u}\_{\\alpha} = q\_{\\alpha},} & {\\alpha = n,w} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.95)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si290.png)(2.95) 

Darcy’s law one for each phase,

$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathbf{u}\_{\\alpha} = - \\frac{k\_{r\\alpha}\\left( S\_{w} \\right)}{\\mu\_{\\alpha}}\\mathbf{k}\\left( {\\nabla p\_{\\alpha} - \\rho\_{\\alpha}\\mathbf{g}} \\right),} & {\\alpha = n,w} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.96)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si291.png)(2.96) 

Summation constraint of the saturation,

*S*[*w*] + *S*[*n*] = 1 (2.97)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si292.png)(2.97) 

Equations of states,

$\\begin{matrix} {\\rho\_{w} = \\rho\_{w}^{given},} & {\\rho\_{n} = \\rho\_{n}^{given}} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.98)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si293.png)(2.98) 

Capillary pressure equation,

*p*[*n*] − *p*[*w*] = *p*[*c*](*S*[*w*]) (2.99)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si294.png)(2.99) 

The phase mobilities and the total (phase) mobility are defined as

$\\begin{matrix} {\\lambda\_{\\alpha}\\left( S\_{\\alpha} \\right): = \\frac{k\_{r\\alpha}}{\\mu\_{\\alpha}},} & {\\alpha = n,w} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.100)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si295.png)(2.100) 

*λ*[*t*](*S*[*w*]) :  = *λ*[*w*](*S*[*w*]) + *λ*[*n*](*S*[*w*]) (2.101)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si296.png)(2.101) 

The fractional flow functions are defined as

$f\_{w}\\left( S\_{w} \\right): = \\frac{\\lambda\_{w}\\left( S\_{w} \\right)}{\\lambda\_{t}\\left( S\_{w} \\right)},f\_{n}\\left( S\_{w} \\right): = \\frac{\\lambda\_{n}\\left( S\_{w} \\right)}{\\lambda\_{t}\\left( S\_{w} \\right)}$ (2.102)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si297.png)(2.102) 

The total (Darcy) velocity is defined as

**u**[*t*] :  = **u**[*w*] + **u**[*n*] (2.103)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si298.png)(2.103) 

Summing the two conservation laws yields

$\\frac{\\partial\\left\\lbrack {\\phi\\left( {S\_{w} + S\_{n}} \\right)} \\right\\rbrack}{\\partial t} + \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\mathbf{u}\_{w} + \\mathbf{u}\_{n}} \\right) = q\_{w} + q\_{n}$ (2.104)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si299.png)(2.104) 

Define the total volumetric injection rate *q*[*t*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si300.png) as follows:

*q*[*t*] :  = *q*[*w*] + *q*[*n*] (2.105)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si301.png)(2.105) 

It should be noted *q*[*t*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si302.png) can be either given or can be a function of pressure and saturation.

Substituting [Eqs. (2.97)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn97), [(2.103)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn103), [and (2.105)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn105) into [(2.104)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn104) gives

∇ ⋅ **u**[*t*] = *q*[*t*] (2.106)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si303.png)(2.106) 

which is similar to the incompressible single-phase flow.

Rewrite the Darcy’s law for phase *α*,

$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathbf{u}\_{\\alpha} = - \\lambda\_{\\alpha}\\left( S\_{w} \\right)\\mathbf{k}\\left( {\\nabla p\_{\\alpha} - \\rho\_{\\alpha}\\mathbf{g}} \\right),} & {\\alpha = n,w} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.107)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si304.png)(2.107) 

and sum the two Darcy’s laws (one for each phase),

**u**[*t*] =  − *λ*[*w*]**k**(∇*p*[*w*]−*ρ*[*w*]**g**) − *λ*[*n*]**k**(∇*p*[*n*]−*ρ*[*n*]**g**) (2.108)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si305.png)(2.108) 

[Eq. (2.108)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn108) can be reformulated as

**u**[*t*] =  − **k**\[(*λ*[*w*]∇*p*[*w*]+*λ*[*n*]∇*p*[*n*])−(*λ*[*w*]*ρ*[*w*]+*λ*[*n*]*ρ*[*n*])**g**\] (2.109)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si306.png)(2.109) 

where the term (*λ*[*w*]∇*p*[*w*]+*λ*[*n*]∇*p*[*n*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si307.png) represents the pressure gradient contribution to total Darcy’s velocity, while the term (*λ*[*w*]*ρ*[*w*]+*λ*[*n*]*ρ*[*n*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si308.png) represents the contribution of gravity to total Darcy’s s velocity. The phase mobilities are like the weight factors to sum individual contributions from both phases.

 

#### 2.4.1.1 Choice of primary variables

Without loss of generality, the nonwetting-phase pressure *p*[*n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si309.png) and wetting-phase saturation *S*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si310.png) can be selected as primary variables. And using the capillary pressure *p*[*c*], [Eq. (2.109)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn109) becomes

**u**[*t*] =  − **k**\[(*λ*[*w*]∇*p*[*n*]+*λ*[*n*]∇*p*[*n*]−*λ*[*w*]∇*p*[*c*])−(*λ*[*w*]*ρ*[*w*]+*λ*[*n*]*ρ*[*n*])**g**\] (2.110)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si311.png)(2.110) 

With the total mobility [(2.101)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn101) the total Darcy’s velocity [(2.110)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn110) becomes

**u**[*t*] =  − **k**\[(*λ*[*t*]∇*p*[*n*]−*λ*[*w*]∇*p*[*c*])−(*λ*[*w*]*ρ*[*w*]+*λ*[*n*]*ρ*[*n*])**g**\] (2.111)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si312.png)(2.111) 

Substituting [Eq. (2.111)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn111) into the conservation law [(2.106)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn106), we have

 − ∇ ⋅ \[**k**(*λ*[*t*]∇*p*[*n*]−*λ*[*w*]∇*p*[*c*])−**k**(*λ*[*w*]*ρ*[*w*]+*λ*[*n*]*ρ*[*n*])**g**\] = *q*[*t*] (2.112)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si313.png)(2.112) 

Rewrite [Eq. (2.112)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn112) to get only the unknown *p*[*n*] on the left-hand side of the equation

 − ∇ ⋅ (**k***λ*[*t*]∇*p*[*n*]) = *q*[*t*] − ∇ ⋅ \[**k**(*λ*[*w*]∇*p*[*c*]+(*λ*[*w*]*ρ*[*w*]+*λ*[*n*]*ρ*[*n*])**g**)\] (2.113)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si314.png)(2.113) 

which is known as the pressure equation for two-phase flow.

 

#### 2.4.1.2 Modeling of wells

First define following source and sink terms of the wells,

$\\begin{matrix} {q\_{\\alpha} = {\\sum\\limits\_{l.m}{q\_{\\alpha}^{l,m}\\delta\\left( {\\mathbf{x} - \\mathbf{x}^{l,m}} \\right)}},} & {\\alpha = n,w} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.114)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si315.png)(2.114) 

where *q*[*α*]^(*l*, *m*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si316.png) denotes the volume of the fluid produced or injected per unit time at the *l*th well and the *m*th perforated zone **x**^(*l*, *m*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si317.png) for phase *α*; *δ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si318.png) is the Dirac delta function.

Following 

$q\_{\\alpha}^{l,m} = \\frac{2\\pi\\overline{k}k\_{r\\alpha}\\Delta L^{l,m}}{\\mu\_{\\alpha}\\ln\\left( {r\_{e}^{l}/r\_{c}^{l}} \\right)}\\left( {p\_{bh}^{l} - p\_{\\alpha} - \\rho\_{\\alpha}g\\left( {Z\_{bh}^{l} - Z} \\right)} \\right)$ (2.115)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si320.png)(2.115) 

where *Δ**L*^(*l*, *m*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si321.png) represents the length (in the flow direction) of a grid block (containing the *l*th well) at the *m*th perforated zone; *p*[*b**h*]^(*l*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si322.png) denotes bottom hole pressure (BHP) at the datum level depth *Z*[*b**h*]^(*l*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si323.png); *r*[*e*]^(*l*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si324.png) denotes equivalent well radius; *r*[*c*]^(*l*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si325.png) is radius of the *l*th well; $\\overline{k}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si326.png) is some average of **k** at wells.

For a diagonal tensor $\\overline{k} = {diag}\\left( {k\_{11},k\_{22},k\_{33}} \\right)$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si327.png), for example, $\\overline{k} = k\_{11}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si328.png) at a vertical well, where *k*[22]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si329.png) and *k*[33]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si330.png) are the permeabilities in the horizontal and vertical directions, respectively. In this case the equivalent radius can be computed as follows:

*r*[*e*]^(*l*) = 0.14(*D**X*²+*D**Y*²)^(1/2) (2.116)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si331.png)(2.116) 

where DX and DY are the *x*\- and *y*\-dimensions of the grid block containing this vertical well.

For a horizontal well (e.g., in the *x*\-direction),

$\\overline{k} = \\sqrt{k\_{11}k\_{33}}$ (2.117)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si332.png)(2.117) 

$r\_{e}^{l} = \\frac{0.14\\left( {\\left( {k\_{33}/k\_{11}} \\right)^{1/2}{DX}^{2} + \\left( {k\_{11}/k\_{33}} \\right)^{1/2}{DZ}^{2}} \\right)^{1/2}}{0.5\\left( {\\left( {k\_{33}/k\_{11}} \\right)^{1/4} + \\left( {k\_{11}/k\_{33}} \\right)^{1/4}} \\right)}$ (2.118)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si333.png)(2.118) 

where DZ is the *z*\-dimension of the grid block, which contains this horizontal well.

 

#### 2.4.1.3 Pressure equation for two-phase flow

Recall we select the nonwetting-phase pressure and wetting-phase saturation as primary variables, the pressure equation for two-phase flow now reads

 − ∇ ⋅ \[**k***λ*[*t*](*S*[*w*])∇*p*[*n*]\] = *R**H**S*[*p**r**e**s*](*p*[*n*],*S*[*w*]) (2.119)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si334.png)(2.119) 

and

*R**H**S*[*p**r**e**s*](*p*[*n*],*S*[*w*]) = *q*[*t*](*p*[*n*],*S*[*w*]) − ∇ ⋅ \[**k**(*λ*[*w*](*S*[*w*])∇*p*[*c*](*S*[*w*])+(*λ*[*w*]*ρ*[*w*]+*λ*[*n*]*ρ*[*n*])**g**)\] (2.120)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si335.png)(2.120) 

where for given *S*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si336.png), *R**H**S*[*p**r**e**s*](*p*[*n*],*S*[*w*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si337.png) is a linear function of *p*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si338.png); for given *S*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si339.png), the pressure equation is linear with respect to *p*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si340.png).

 

#### 2.4.1.4 Saturation equation for two-phase flow

Recall the fractional flow functions $\\begin{matrix} {f\_{\\alpha}: = {\\lambda\_{\\alpha}/\\lambda\_{t}},} & {\\alpha = n,w} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si341.png). For the nonwetting-phase velocity we have

**u**[*n*] =  − **k**(*λ*[*n*]∇*p*[*n*]−*λ*[*n*]*ρ*[*n*]**g**) (2.121)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si342.png)(2.121) 

Noting that *λ*[*w*]*f*[*n*] = *λ*[*n*]*f*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si343.png), we have

**u**[*n*] = *f*[*n*]**u**[*t*] − **k***λ*[*w*]*f*[*n*]∇*p*[*c*] + **k***λ*[*w*]*f*[*n*](*ρ*[*n*] − *ρ*[*c*])**g** (2.122)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si344.png)(2.122) 

Similarly, for wetting-phase velocity, we have

**u**[*w*] = *f*[*w*]**u**[*t*] + **k***λ*[*n*]*f*[*w*]∇*p*[*c*] + **k***λ*[*n*]*f*[*w*](*ρ*[*w*]−*ρ*[*n*])**g** (2.123)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si345.png)(2.123) 

Assuming a time-independent porosity, we have the following conservation of volume for wetting phase,

$\\phi\\frac{\\partial S\_{w}}{\\partial t} + \\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{u}\_{w} = q\_{w}$ (2.124)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si346.png)(2.124) 

Substituting [Eq. (2.123)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn123) into [Eq. (2.124)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn124) yields the saturation equation shown next,

$\\phi\\frac{\\partial S\_{w}}{\\partial t} + \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {f\_{w}\\mathbf{u}\_{t}} \\right) + \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\mathbf{k}f\_{w}\\lambda\_{n}\\left( {\\nabla p\_{c} + \\left( {\\rho\_{w} - \\rho\_{n}} \\right)\\mathbf{g}} \\right)} \\right) = q\_{w}$ (2.125)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si347.png)(2.125) 

We note that

$\\nabla p\_{c} = \\left( \\frac{dp\_{c}\\left( S\_{w} \\right)}{dS\_{w}} \\right)\\nabla S\_{w}$ (2.126)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si348.png)(2.126) 

Substitute [Eq. (2.126)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn126) into [Eq. (2.125)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn125), the saturation equation now reads

$\\phi\\frac{\\partial S\_{w}}{\\partial t} = {RHS}\_{sat}\\left( {p\_{n},S\_{w}} \\right)$ (2.127)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si349.png)(2.127) 

where $\\begin{array}{l} {{RHS}\_{sat}\\left( {p\_{n},S\_{w}} \\right) = q\_{w}\\left( {p\_{n},S\_{w}} \\right) - \\nabla \\cdot \\left\\lbrack {f\_{w}\\left( S\_{w} \\right)\\mathbf{u}\_{t}\\left( {p\_{n},S\_{w}} \\right)} \\right\\rbrack} \\\\ {- \\nabla \\cdot \\left\\lbrack {\\mathbf{k}f\_{w}\\left( S\_{w} \\right)\\lambda\_{n}\\left( S\_{w} \\right)\\left( {\\left( {{dp\_{c}}/{dS\_{w}}} \\right)\\nabla S\_{w} + \\left( {\\rho\_{w} - \\rho\_{n}} \\right)\\mathbf{g}} \\right)} \\right\\rbrack} \\\\ \\end{array}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si350.png).

 

#### 2.4.1.5 The implicit pressure, explicit saturation formulation for incompressible two-phase flow

The IMPES (IMplicit Pressure, Explicit Saturation) method was developed by 

The phase formulation (pressure-saturation formulation) of incompressible two-phase flows consists of two main equations for the two primary unknowns (the nonwetting-phase pressure *p*[*n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si351.png) and the wetting-phase saturation *S*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si352.png)).

The pressure equation reads

 − ∇ ⋅ (**k***λ*[*t*](*S*[*w*])∇*p*[*n*]) = *R**H**S*[*p**r**e**s*](*p*[*n*],*S*[*w*]) (2.128)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si353.png)(2.128) 

The saturation equation reads

$\\phi\\frac{\\partial S\_{w}}{\\partial t} = {RHS}\_{sat}\\left( {p\_{n},S\_{w}} \\right)$ (2.129)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si354.png)(2.129) 

The standard IMPES formulation is stated as follows: Let *J* = (0,*T*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si355.png) be the time interval of interest, and for *N* ∈ *N*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si356.png), let 0=*t*[0]<*t*[1]…<*t*[*N*]\=*T* be a partition of *J*. In the pressure computation in IMPES algorithm, the saturation *S*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si357.png) is supposed to be known, and the pressure equation is implicitly solved for *p*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si358.png). That is, for each *n*\=0, 1,…,

 − ∇ ⋅ (**k***λ*[*t*](*S*[*w*]^((*n*)))∇*p*[*n*]^((*n*))) = *R**H**S*[*p**r**e**s*](*p*[*n*]^((*n*)),*S*[*w*]^((*n*))) (2.130)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si359.png)(2.130) 

In this classical IMPES the saturation equation is solved explicitly for *S*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si360.png); that is, for each *n*\=0, 1, …,

$\\phi\\frac{S\_{w}^{({n + 1})} - S\_{w}^{(n)}}{t\_{n + 1} - t\_{n}} = {RHS}\_{sat}\\left( {p\_{n}^{(n)},S\_{w}^{(n)}} \\right)$ (2.131)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si361.png)(2.131) 

The IMPES formulation offers several advantages, it is more convenient to implement than the fully implicit scheme. It successfully decouples pressure from saturation based on the fact that pressure depends on saturation only weakly, and the fact that pressure changes slowly with time. The total velocity **u**[*t*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si362.png) has a continuous normal component, which is retained in the standard IMPES formulation (this is clear in a mixed finite element IMPES formulation). The IMPES scheme is locally mass and volume conservative for the wetting phase, and it produces nonnegative wetting-phase saturation if the time step size is smaller than a certain value. Some disadvantages of IMPES method are presented here, such as it requires a quite small time step size for numerical stability (and for positivity). It cannot (locally nor globally) preserve conservation for the nonwetting phase, consequently it cannot (locally nor globally) preserve conservation for the total fluid mixture. The IMPES scheme may produce a wetting-phase saturation that is larger than one. For different capillary pressure functions, it cannot reproduce the correct saturation solution with discontinuity.

 

#### 2.4.1.6 A revised implicit pressure, explicit saturation formulation by Hoteit and Firoozabadi

To treat contrast in capillary pressure of heterogeneous permeable media, which can have a significant effect on the flow path in two-phase immiscible flow, Hoteit and Firoozabadi proposed a revised IMPES formulation, namely Hoteit–Firoozabadi (HF) IMPES scheme. First define the flow potential *Φ*[*α*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si363.png) of phase *α*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si364.png) as follows:

$\\begin{matrix} {\\Phi\_{\\alpha}: = p\_{\\alpha} + \\rho\_{\\alpha}gz,} & {\\alpha = n,w} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.132)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si365.png)(2.132) 

Define the capillary potential as the difference of the two flow potentials,

*Φ*[*c*] :  = *Φ*[*n*] − *Φ*[*w*] = *p*[*c*] + (*ρ*[*n*]−*ρ*[*w*])*g**z* (2.133)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si366.png)(2.133) 

With the flow potentials, Darcy’s law for the two phases becomes

$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathbf{u}\_{\\alpha} = - \\lambda\_{\\alpha}\\left( S\_{w} \\right)\\mathbf{k}\\nabla\\Phi\_{\\alpha},} & {\\alpha = n,w} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.134)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si367.png)(2.134) 

The “apparent” velocity **u**[*α*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si368.png) is the total Darcy velocity if both flow potentials take the value of *Φ*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si369.png),

**u**[*α*] :  =  − *λ*[*t*]**k**∇*Φ*[*w*] (2.135)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si370.png)(2.135) 

Note that apparent velocity **u**[*α*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si371.png) has the same driving force as the wetting-phase velocity but with a smoother mobility *λ*[*t*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si372.png) (*λ*[*t*] = *λ*[*n*] + *λ*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si373.png)) than the wetting-phase mobility. Define the “capillary” velocity **u**[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si374.png) as

**u**[*c*] :  =  − *λ*[*n*]**k**∇*Φ*[*c*] (2.136)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si375.png)(2.136) 

It is easy to obtain

**u**[*t*] = **u**[*n*] + **u**[*w*] = **u**[*a*] + **u**[*c*] (2.137)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si376.png)(2.137) 

In the HF-IMPES scheme, for each time step three calculation steps should be conducted,

1.  1. Given *S*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si377.png) and find **u**[*c*] ∈ **V**[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si378.png) such that,
    
    $\\begin{matrix} {\\left( {\\mathbf{u}\_{c},\\mathbf{v}} \\right) = \\left( {- \\lambda\_{n}\\mathbf{k}\\nabla\\Phi\_{c}\\left( S\_{w} \\right),\\mathbf{v}} \\right),} & \\forall \\\\ \\end{matrix}\\mathbf{v} \\in V\_{h}$ (2.138)
    
    ![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si379.png)(2.138) 
    
      
    This is one half of the standard mixed finite element method (FEM).
    
2.  2. Given *S*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si380.png) and **u**[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si381.png), and solve **u**[*α*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si382.png) and *Φ*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si383.png),
    
    $\\begin{matrix} {\\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{u}\_{a} = q\_{t} - \\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{u}\_{c},} & {\\mathbf{u}\_{a} = - \\lambda\_{t}\\left( S\_{w} \\right)\\mathbf{k}\\nabla\\Phi\_{w}} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.139)
    
    ![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si384.png)(2.139) 
3.  3. Given **u**[*α*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si385.png), *Φ*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si386.png) and current *S*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si387.png), and solve *S*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si388.png) at the next time,

$\\phi\\frac{\\partial S\_{w}}{\\partial t} + \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {f\_{w}\\left( S\_{w} \\right)\\mathbf{u}\_{a}} \\right) = q\_{w}\\left( {S\_{w},\\Phi\_{w}} \\right)$ (2.140)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si389.png)(2.140) 

For different capillary pressure functions, it reproduces the saturation solution with expected discontinuity. Like the standard IMPES, the HF-IMPES formulation is more convenient to implement than the fully implicit scheme. It successfully decouples flow potentials from saturation and the total velocity **u**[*t*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si390.png) sits the *H*(*d**i**v*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si391.png) space. The HF-IMPES scheme is locally mass and volume conservative for the wetting phase. However, it still requires a quite small time step size for numerical stability and for positivity, and the assumption that the “capillary” velocity **u**[*c*] =  − *λ*[*n*]**k**∇*Φ*[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si392.png) has a continuous normal component may not be true! Similar to standard IMPES, the HF-IMPES scheme cannot guarantee (local nor global) conservation for the nonwetting phase. As a result, the scheme is not (locally nor globally) conservative for the total fluid mixture. Same as standard IMPES, the HF-IMPES scheme might produce a wetting-phase saturation that is larger than one.

 

### 2.4.2 The implicit pressure, explicit saturation method for compressible two-phase porous flow

 

#### 2.4.2.1 Compressible two-phase flow equations

For the immiscible two-phase compressible flow in porous media (isothermal), the mass conservation,

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{\\partial\\left( {\\phi S\_{\\alpha}\\rho\_{\\alpha}} \\right)}{\\partial t} + \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\rho\_{\\alpha}\\mathbf{u}\_{\\alpha}} \\right) = q\_{m,\\alpha},} & {\\alpha = n,w} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.141)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si393.png)(2.141) 

Extended Darcy’s law,

$\\mathbf{u}\_{\\alpha} = - \\frac{k\_{r\\alpha}(S\_{w})}{\\mu\_{\\alpha}}\\mathbf{k}(\\nabla p\_{\\alpha} - \\rho\_{\\alpha}\\mathbf{g}),\\quad\\alpha = n,w$ (2.142)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si394.png)(2.142) 

Capillary pressure,

*p*[*n*] − *p*[*w*] = *p*[*c*](*S*[*w*]) (2.143)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si395.png)(2.143) 

EOS,

$\\begin{matrix} {\\rho\_{w} = \\rho\_{w}\\left( {p\_{w},T} \\right),} & {\\rho\_{n} = \\rho\_{n}\\left( {p\_{n},T} \\right)} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.144)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si396.png)(2.144) 

Saturation constraint,

*S*[*w*] + *S*[*n*] = 1 (2.145)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si397.png)(2.145) 

Together with proper B.Cs and I.Cs.

 

#### 2.4.2.2 Two-phase fluid compressibility

The fluid compressibility of each phase reads

$c\_{f,w} = - \\frac{1}{V\_{w}}\\left( \\frac{\\partial V\_{w}}{\\partial p\_{w}} \\right)\_{T} = \\frac{1}{\\rho\_{w}}\\left( \\frac{\\partial\\rho\_{w}}{\\partial p\_{w}} \\right)\_{T}$ (2.146)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si398.png)(2.146) 

$c\_{f,n} = - \\frac{1}{V\_{n}}\\left( \\frac{\\partial V\_{n}}{\\partial p\_{n}} \\right)\_{T} = \\frac{1}{\\rho\_{n}}\\left( \\frac{\\partial\\rho\_{n}}{\\partial p\_{n}} \\right)\_{T}$ (2.147)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si399.png)(2.147) 

Recall the rock compressibility,

$R = \\frac{1}{\\phi}\\left( \\frac{\\partial\\phi}{\\partial p} \\right)\_{T}$ (2.148)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si400.png)(2.148) 

where the pressure of one phase should be chosen.

Ignoring the capillary pressure, the compressibility of fluid mixture can be defined by

$c\_{f} = - \\frac{1}{V\_{f}}\\left( \\frac{\\partial V\_{f}}{\\partial p} \\right)\_{T}$ (2.149)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si401.png)(2.149) 

A quick manipulation reveals

$c\_{f} = - \\frac{1}{V\_{w} + V\_{n}}\\left( \\frac{\\partial\\left( {V\_{w} + V\_{n}} \\right)}{\\partial p} \\right)\_{T} = - \\frac{V\_{w}}{V\_{w} + V\_{n}}\\frac{\\left( {{\\partial V\_{w}}/{\\partial p}} \\right)\_{T}}{V\_{w}} - \\frac{V\_{n}}{V\_{w} + V\_{n}}\\frac{\\left( {{\\partial V\_{n}}/{\\partial p}} \\right)\_{T}}{V\_{n}} = c\_{f,w}S\_{w} + c\_{f,n}S\_{n}$ (2.150)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si402.png)(2.150) 

where the fluid compressibility of each phase is *c*[*f*, *α*] =  − (1/*V*[*α*])(∂*V*[*α*]/∂*p*)[*T*] = (1/*ρ*[*α*])(∂*ρ*[*α*]/∂*p*)[*T*], *α* = *w*, *n*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si403.png).

 

#### 2.4.2.3 The pressure equation for two-phase flow

The following pressure equation can also be derived for IMPES method:

$\\phi c\_{tot}\\frac{\\partial p}{\\partial t} + {\\sum\\limits\_{\\alpha = n,w}{\\frac{1}{\\rho\_{\\alpha}}\\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\rho\_{\\alpha}\\mathbf{u}\_{\\alpha}} \\right) = q\_{tot}}}$ (2.151)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si404.png)(2.151) 

where *c*[*t**o**t*] = *c*[*f*, *w*]*S*[*w*] + *c*[*f*, *n*]*S*[*n*] + *c*[*R*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si405.png); the rock compressibility *c*[*R*] = (1/*ϕ*)(∂*ϕ*/∂*p*)[*T*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si406.png); *q*[*t**o**t*] = *q*[*m*, *w*]/*ρ*[*w*] + *q*[*m*, *n*]/*ρ*[*n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si407.png).

It should be noted that it is a wrong way to form pressure equation by a simple summation,

${\\sum\\limits\_{\\alpha = w,n}\\frac{\\partial\\left( {\\phi S\_{\\alpha}\\rho\_{\\alpha}} \\right)}{\\partial t}} + {\\sum\\limits\_{\\alpha = w,n}{\\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\rho\_{\\alpha}\\mathbf{u}\_{\\alpha}} \\right)}} = {\\sum\\limits\_{\\alpha = w,n}q\_{m,\\alpha}}$ (2.152)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si408.png)(2.152) 

${\\sum\\limits\_{\\alpha = w,n}\\frac{\\partial\\left( {\\phi S\_{\\alpha}c\_{\\alpha}} \\right)}{\\partial t}} + {\\sum\\limits\_{\\alpha = w,n}{\\nabla \\cdot \\left( {c\_{\\alpha}\\mathbf{u}\_{\\alpha}} \\right)}} = {\\sum\\limits\_{\\alpha = w,n}q\_{\\alpha}}$ (2.153)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si409.png)(2.153) 

The correct way to form pressure equation is to apply weight ${\\overline{V}}\_{i}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si410.png). Note that p.m.v. of the immiscible two-phase mixture for oil (water) component is the same as the molar volume of pure oil (water) component,

${\\sum\\limits\_{\\alpha = w,n}{\\frac{1}{c\_{\\alpha}}\\frac{\\partial\\left( {\\phi S\_{\\alpha}c\_{\\alpha}} \\right)}{\\partial t}}} + {\\sum\\limits\_{\\alpha = w,n}{\\frac{1}{c\_{\\alpha}}\\nabla \\cdot \\left( {c\_{\\alpha}\\mathbf{u}\_{\\alpha}} \\right)}} = {\\sum\\limits\_{\\alpha = w,n}{\\frac{1}{c\_{\\alpha}}q\_{\\alpha}}}$ (2.154)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si411.png)(2.154) 

In this way, the derivative of saturations no longer appears.

First assume zero capillary pressure for simplicity, the summation weighted by partial molar volume is equivalent to [Eq. (2.151)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn151). In general form with nonzero capillary pressure, the pressure equation reads

$\\begin{array}{l} {\\frac{\\partial\\left( {\\phi\\rho\_{\\alpha}S\_{\\alpha}} \\right)}{\\partial t} = \\left( {\\frac{\\partial\\phi}{\\partial p\_{\\alpha}}\\rho\_{\\alpha} + \\phi\\frac{\\partial\\rho\_{\\alpha}}{\\partial p\_{\\alpha}}} \\right)\\frac{\\partial p\_{\\alpha}}{\\partial t}S\_{\\alpha} + \\phi\\rho\_{\\alpha}\\frac{\\partial S\_{\\alpha}}{\\partial t}} \\\\ {= \\phi\\rho\_{\\alpha}\\left( {c\_{R} + c\_{f,\\alpha}} \\right)\\frac{\\partial p\_{\\alpha}}{\\partial t}S\_{\\alpha} + \\phi\\rho\_{\\alpha}\\frac{\\partial S\_{\\alpha}}{\\partial t}} \\\\ {= q\_{m,a} - \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\rho\_{\\alpha}\\mathbf{u}\_{\\alpha}} \\right)} \\\\ \\end{array}$ (2.155)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si412.png)(2.155) 

Weighted summation yields

$\\phi\\left( {c\_{R} + c\_{f,w}S\_{w} + c\_{f,n}S\_{n}} \\right)\\frac{\\partial p\_{w}}{\\partial t} = {\\sum\\limits\_{\\alpha = n,w}q\_{\\alpha}} - \\phi c\_{f,n}S\_{n}\\frac{\\partial p\_{c}}{\\partial t} - {\\sum\\limits\_{\\alpha = n,w}\\frac{1}{\\rho\_{\\alpha}}}\\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\rho\_{\\alpha}\\mathbf{u}\_{\\alpha}} \\right)$ (2.156)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si413.png)(2.156) 

Thus the following pressure equation is obtained:

$\\phi c\_{tot}\\frac{\\partial p\_{w}}{\\partial t} + {\\sum\\limits\_{\\alpha = n,w}\\frac{1}{\\rho\_{\\alpha}}}\\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\rho\_{\\alpha}\\mathbf{u}\_{\\alpha}} \\right) = q\_{tot}$ (2.157)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si414.png)(2.157) 

where $q\_{tot} = {\\sum\\limits\_{\\alpha = n,w}q\_{\\alpha}} - \\phi c\_{f,n}S\_{n}\\left( {{\\partial p\_{c}}/{\\partial t}} \\right)$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si415.png).

Substituting the extended Darcy’s law [(2.72)](#chp002.xhtml#eqn72) into [Eq. (2.158)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn158) yields

$\\phi c\_{tot}\\frac{\\partial p\_{w}}{\\partial t} - {\\sum\\limits\_{\\alpha = n,w}\\frac{1}{\\rho\_{\\alpha}}}\\nabla \\cdot \\left\\lbrack {\\rho\_{\\alpha}\\lambda\_{\\alpha}\\mathbf{k}\\left( {\\nabla p\_{\\alpha} - \\rho\_{\\alpha}\\mathbf{g}} \\right)} \\right\\rbrack = q\_{tot}$ (2.158)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si416.png)(2.158) 

Rearranging [Eq. (2.158)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn158) gives

$\\phi c\_{tot}\\frac{\\partial p\_{w}}{\\partial t} - {\\sum\\limits\_{\\alpha = n,w}\\frac{1}{\\rho\_{\\alpha}}}\\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\rho\_{\\alpha}\\lambda\_{\\alpha}\\mathbf{k}\\nabla p\_{w}} \\right) = {RHS}\_{pres}\\left( {p\_{w},S\_{w}} \\right)$ (2.159)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si417.png)(2.159) 

where ${RHS}\_{pres} = q\_{tot} + \\left( {{\\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\rho\_{n}\\lambda\_{n}\\mathbf{k}\\nabla p\_{c}} \\right)}/\\rho\_{n}} \\right) - {\\sum\\limits\_{\\alpha = n,w}\\left( {1/\\rho\_{\\alpha}} \\right)}\\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\rho\_{\\alpha}\\lambda\_{\\alpha}\\mathbf{k}\\mathbf{g}} \\right)$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si418.png).

 

#### 2.4.2.4 The saturation equation for two-phase flow

Recall the saturation equation, we have

$\\frac{\\partial\\left( {\\phi\\rho\_{w}S\_{w}} \\right)}{\\partial t} = {RHS}\_{sat}\\left( {p\_{w},S\_{w}} \\right)$ (2.160)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si419.png)(2.160) 

where $\\begin{array}{l} {{RHS}\_{sat}\\left( {p\_{w},S\_{w}} \\right) = q\_{m,w}\\left( {p\_{w},S\_{w}} \\right) - \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {f\_{w}\\left( S\_{w} \\right)\\rho\_{w}\\mathbf{u}\_{t}(p\_{w},S\_{w})} \\right)} \\\\ {- \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\mathbf{k}f\_{w}\\left( S\_{w} \\right)\\rho\_{w}\\lambda\_{n}(S\_{w})\\left\\lbrack {\\left( {{dp\_{c}}/{dS\_{w}}} \\right)\\nabla S\_{w} + (\\rho\_{w} - \\rho\_{n})\\mathbf{g}} \\right\\rbrack} \\right)} \\\\ \\end{array}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si420.png).

 

#### 2.4.2.5 The implicit pressure, explicit saturation formulation for compressible two-phase flow

The IMPES method for compressible two-phase flows is the same as that for incompressible two-phase flows. The phase formulation of compressible two-phase flow consists of two main equations for the two primary unknowns *p*[*n*] and *S*[*w*].

The pressure equation,

$\\phi c\_{tot}\\frac{\\partial p\_{w}}{\\partial t} - {\\sum\\limits\_{\\alpha = n,w}\\frac{1}{\\rho\_{\\alpha}}}\\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\rho\_{\\alpha}\\lambda\_{\\alpha}\\mathbf{k}\\nabla p\_{w}} \\right) = {RHS}\_{pres}\\left( {p\_{w},S\_{w}} \\right)$ (2.161)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si421.png)(2.161) 

The saturation equation,

$\\frac{\\partial\\left( {\\phi\\rho\_{w}S\_{w}} \\right)}{\\partial t} = {RHS}\_{sat}\\left( {p\_{w},S\_{w}} \\right)$ (2.162)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si422.png)(2.162) 

In the pressure computation of the IMPES method, for each *n*\=0, 1…,

$\\phi^{(n)}c\_{tot}^{(n)}\\frac{p\_{w}^{({n + 1})} - p\_{w}^{(n)}}{t\_{n + 1} - t\_{n}} - {\\sum\\limits\_{\\alpha = n,w}\\frac{1}{\\rho\_{\\alpha}^{(n)}}}\\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\rho\_{\\alpha}^{(n)}\\lambda\_{\\alpha}^{(n)}\\mathbf{k}\\nabla p\_{w}^{({n + 1})}} \\right) = {RHS}\_{pres}\\left( {p\_{w}^{({n + 1})},S\_{w}^{(n)}} \\right)$ (2.163)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si423.png)(2.163) 

In this classical IMPES method, the saturation equation is solved explicitly for *S*[*w*]; that is, for each *n*\=0, 1…,

$\\frac{\\phi^{({n + 1})}\\rho\_{w}^{({n + 1})}S\_{w}^{({n + 1})} - \\phi^{(n)}\\rho\_{w}^{(n)}S\_{w}^{(n)}}{t\_{n + 1} - t\_{n}} = {RHS}\_{sat}\\left( {p\_{w}^{({n + 1})},S\_{w}^{(n)}} \\right)$ (2.164)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si424.png)(2.164)  

## 2.5 Wellbore modeling

 

### 2.5.1 Overview of well modeling

The reservoir simulation is a significant method to predict the fluids (typically, oil, water, and gas) flow through porous media, which has been widely used in petroleum industry in the development of new fields and in developed fields for improvement. From the perspective of well management, (1) for new fields, reservoir simulations may help development by identifying the number of wells required, the optimal completion of wells, and the expected production of oil, water and gas and (2) for ongoing reservoir management, reservoir simulations can help to improve the oil recovery by, for example, hydraulic fracturing, and highly deviated or horizontal wells can be represented.

The fundamental tasks in modeling wells include accurate modeling of flows into/from the wellbore and developing accurate well equations that allow the calculation of the BHP when a production or injection rate is given, or the calculation of the rate when BHP is known. The difficulty in modeling wells in the field-scale numerical simulation lies in that the region where pressure gradients are the largest is closest to a well and is far smaller than the spatial size of grid blocks. Using the local grid refinement around wells can alleviate this problem but can lead to an impractical restriction on time step sizes in reservoir simulations. There are also some great computational challenges. (1) Three regions of different scales (see [Fig. 2.5](#chp002_a.xhtml#f0030)): (a) wellbore (modeled by the function), (b) near-well region, and (c) far-field region. (2) Spatial multiscale physics: flow in the small region near a well differs from flow in the region far from the well. (3) Temporal multiscale physics: flow near the well is fast while it is slow far away. (4) Need to resolve coupling of the following two physics: (a) fast flow in a small region near the well and (b) slow flow in the large region far from the well. (5) Require an algorithm to treat multiscale physics: near the well, the analytical solutions are used; far away from the well, a numerical method, say cell-centered finite difference (CCFD) methods (other numerical methods, such as FEM, are also applicable), is used.

![Figure 2.5 Sketch map of three regions of different scales.](OEBPS/images/F000022f02-05-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 2.5 Sketch map of three regions of different scales.

 

### 2.5.2 Analytical solutions for flow near the well

Generally, the well flow equations are constructed based on the assumption that the flow is radial in a neighborhood of the well. It needs to use analytical formulas for radial flow. Here, the incompressible single-phase steady-state flow in homogeneous and isotropic reservoirs is considered.

Modeling equations (without the gravity term) are

∇ ⋅ (*ρ***u**) = *q̂**δ*, *o**r*∇ ⋅ **u** = *q**δ* (2.165)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si425.png)(2.165) 

$\\mathbf{u} = - \\frac{\\mathbf{k}}{\\mu}\\nabla p$ (2.166)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si426.png)(2.166) 

where *δ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si427.png) is the Dirac delta function, it represents a well placed at the origin; $q\\left( \\overset{\\frown}{q} \\right)$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si428.png) is the volumetric (mass) production/injection rate at this well.

In order to obtain the analytical solution to [Eqs. (2.165)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn165) and [(2.166)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn166) for the single-phase flow in a near-well region, the following assumptions are adopted:

1.  1. The flow is 2D in *x*[1]\- and *x*[2]\-directions (i.e., it is homogeneous in the *x*[3]\-direction, and gravity is neglected).
2.  2. The reservoir is homogeneous and isotropic, that is, **k** = *k***I**![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si429.png) and *k*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si430.png) is a constant.
3.  3. The dynamic viscosity *μ* and density *ρ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si431.png) are constant.
4.  4. The flow is radial in a small neighborhood of the well.
5.  5. The Dirac delta function *δ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si432.png) is used to represent the contribution of a well, and without loss of generality, the well is placed at the origin.

First recall the cylindrical coordinate system, suppose the three coordinates (*r*,*φ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si433.png), *z*) of a point *P* are defined as the radial distance *r*, the azimuth *φ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si434.png), and the height *z*. The del operator in cylindrical coordinate system yields the following expressions for gradient, divergence, curl and Laplacian:

$\\nabla f = \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial r}\\mathbf{r} + \\frac{1}{r}\\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial\\varphi}\\mathbf{\\varphi} + \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial z}\\mathbf{z}$ (2.167)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si435.png)(2.167) 

$\\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{A} = \\frac{1}{r}\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial r}\\left( {rA\_{r}} \\right) + \\frac{1}{r}\\frac{\\partial A\_{\\varphi}}{\\partial\\varphi} + \\frac{\\partial A\_{z}}{\\partial z}$ (2.168)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si436.png)(2.168) 

$\\nabla^{2}f = \\frac{1}{r}\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial r}\\left( {r\\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial r}} \\right) + \\frac{1}{r^{2}}\\frac{\\partial^{2}f}{\\partial\\varphi^{2}} + \\frac{\\partial^{2}f}{\\partial z^{2}}$ (2.169)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si437.png)(2.169) 

Since the single-phase flow is assumed to be radial in a small neighborhood of the well, near the well the velocity has following form:

$\\mathbf{u}\\left( {r,\\theta} \\right) = u\\left( r \\right)\\left( \\begin{array}{l} {\\cos\\theta} \\\\ {\\sin\\theta} \\\\ \\end{array} \\right)$ (2.170)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si438.png)(2.170) 

where (cos*θ*,sin*θ*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si439.png) represents polar coordinate system.

Since the well is placed at the origin, substituting [Eq. (2.170)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn170) into the conservation law gives

$\\frac{1}{r}\\frac{d}{dr}\\left( {ru} \\right) = 0,r > 0$ (2.171)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si440.png)(2.171) 

the solution of which is *u* = *C*/*r*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si441.png), *C* is constant and proportional to *q̂*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si442.png), where *q̂*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si443.png) represents the mass production/injection. For example, when the well is an injector, for any small neighborhood *B* of the origin (a small circle) *q̂*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si444.png) is the mass flux,

*q̂* = *h*[*z*]∫[*B*]*ρ***u** ⋅ **n***d**a*(**x**) = 2*π**ρ**h*[*z*]*C* (2.172)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si445.png)(2.172) 

where **n** is the outward unit normal to *B* and *h*[*z*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si446.png) is the reservoir thickness (or the height of the gridblock containing the well).

It is easy to get the integration constant,

$C = \\frac{\\widehat{q}}{2\\pi\\rho h\_{z}}$ (2.173)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si447.png)(2.173) 

With the integration constant the magnitude of the Darcy velocity (which is the same as the component of the Darcy velocity) has the following solution:

$u = \\frac{\\widehat{q}}{2\\pi\\rho h\_{z}r}$ (2.174)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si448.png)(2.174) 

Then the Darcy velocity has the following solution:

$\\mathbf{u} = u\\mathbf{n} = \\frac{\\widehat{q}}{2\\pi\\rho h\_{z}r}\\left( {\\cos\\theta,\\sin\\theta} \\right)^{T}$ (2.175)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si449.png)(2.175) 

where *u* = *C*/*r**B***n** = **r**/|**r**| = **r**/*r*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si450.png).

Recall the component of Darcy’s law,

$u = - \\frac{k}{\\mu}\\frac{\\partial p}{\\partial r}$ (2.176)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si451.png)(2.176) 

The following equation can be obtained by substituting [Eq. (2.175)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn175) into [Eq. (2.176)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn176) and integration:

$p\\left( r \\right) = p\\left( r^{O} \\right) - \\frac{\\mu\\widehat{q}}{2\\pi\\rho kh\_{z}}\\ln\\frac{r}{r^{O}}$ (2.177)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si452.png)(2.177) 

where (*r*^(*O*),0)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si453.png) is a reference point. The previous equation is an analytical flow model near the well, and on the basis of the this equation, well equations for various numerical methods are developed.

 

### 2.5.3 Modeling well using cell-centered finite difference methods

 

#### 2.5.3.1 Overview of Peaceman’s study: three different approaches

For the single-phase flow, 

 

#### 2.5.3.2 Isotropic media on square grids: simplified by symmetry

For a square grid, suppose the single-phase flow equation is solved with the well located in the center of a grid cell, say at *C* = (*x*[*i* − 0.5],*y*[*j* − 0.5])![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si460.png). The adjacent cells are the four cells centered at *W* = (*x*[*i* − 1.5],*y*[*j* − 0.5])![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si461.png), *E* = (*x*[*i* + 0.5],*y*[*j* − 0.5])![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si462.png), *S* = (*x*[*i* − 0.5],*y*[*j* − 1.5])![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si463.png), and *N* = (*x*[*i* − 0.5],*y*[*j* + 0.5])![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si464.png), respectively.

Application of the CCFD (a five-point stencil scheme) gives

$\\begin{array}{l} {\\frac{\\rho k}{\\mu}\\frac{p\_{C} - p\_{E}}{h\_{x}}h\_{y}h\_{z} + \\frac{\\rho k}{\\mu}\\frac{p\_{C} - p\_{W}}{h\_{x}}h\_{y}h\_{z} + \\frac{\\rho k}{\\mu}\\frac{p\_{C} - p\_{S}}{h\_{x}}h\_{y}h\_{z}} \\\\ {+ \\frac{\\rho k}{\\mu}\\frac{p\_{C} - p\_{N}}{h\_{x}}h\_{y}h\_{z} = \\int\_{S = h\_{x}h\_{y}}{\\delta\\widehat{q}}dA = \\widehat{q}} \\\\ \\end{array}$ (2.178)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si465.png)(2.178) 

Assuming square grids (*h*[*x*] = *h*[*y*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si466.png)), the following equation can be obtained:

$\\frac{\\rho kh\_{z}}{\\mu}\\left( {4p\_{i - 0.5,j - 0.5} - p\_{i - 1.5,j - 0.5} - p\_{i + 0.5,j - 0.5} - p\_{i - 0.5,j - 1.5} - p\_{i - 0.5,j + 0.5}} \\right) = \\widehat{q}$ (2.179)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si467.png)(2.179) 

Using the symmetry of the solution *p*, that is,

*p*[*i* − 1.5, *j* − 0.5] = *p*[*i* + 0.5, *j* − 0.5] = *p*[*i* − 0.5, *j* − 1.5] = *p*[*i* − 0.5, *j* + 0.5] (2.180)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si468.png)(2.180) 

[Eq. (2.179)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn179) can be simplified as

$\\frac{\\rho kh\_{z}}{\\mu}\\left( {p\_{i - 0.5,j - 0.5} - p\_{i + 0.5,j - 0.5}} \\right) = \\frac{\\widehat{q}}{4},orp\_{C} - p\_{E} = \\frac{\\mu\\widehat{q}}{4\\rho kh\_{z}}$ (2.181)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si469.png)(2.181)  

#### 2.5.3.3 Bottom hole pressure

A BHP or bottomhole pressure is the pressure at the bottom of the hole, it is usually measured in pounds per square inch (psi). In a static, fluid-filled wellbore BHP can be computed by

*B**H**P* = *M**W* × *d**e**p**t**h* × 0.052 (2.182)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si470.png)(2.182) 

where MW is the mud weight in pounds per gallon, *d**e**p**t**h*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si471.png) is the true vertical depth in feet, and 0.052 is a conversion factor if these units of measure are used.

 

#### 2.5.3.4 Link between bottom hole pressure and cell-centered pressure

Suppose the pressure at the adjacent cells is calculated accurately, which means the analytical well model can be an accurate approximation in the cell centered at ***E***. Recall that *r*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si472.png) is the well radius, if the BHP *P*[*b**h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si473.png) is given, then it follows from the analytical solution that

$p\_{E} = p\_{bh} - \\frac{\\mu\\widehat{q}}{2\\pi\\rho kh\_{z}}\\ln\\left( \\frac{h}{r\_{w}} \\right)$ (2.183)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si474.png)(2.183) 

It should be noted that the BHP should exceed the formation pressure to avoid an influx of formation fluid into the wellbore. However, a weak formation may fracture and cause a loss of wellbore fluids if BHP is too high.

Now the following equations can be obtained:

$p\_{C} - p\_{E} = \\frac{\\mu\\widehat{q}}{4\\rho kh\_{z}}$ (2.184)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si475.png)(2.184) 

$p\_{E} = p\_{bh} - \\frac{\\mu\\widehat{q}}{2\\pi\\rho kh\_{z}}\\ln\\left( \\frac{h}{r\_{w}} \\right)$ (2.185)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si476.png)(2.185) 

Summing [Eqs. (2.184)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn184) and [(2.185)](#chp002_a.xhtml#eqn185) yields

$p = p\_{C} = p\_{bh} + \\frac{\\mu\\widehat{q}}{2\\pi\\rho kh\_{z}}\\left( {\\ln\\left( \\frac{r\_{w}}{h} \\right) + \\frac{\\pi}{2}} \\right)$ (2.186)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si477.png)(2.186) 

The abovementioned equation can be written as $p = p\_{C} = p\_{bh} + \\frac{\\mu\\widehat{q}}{2\\pi\\rho kh\_{z}}\\ln\\left( {r\_{w}/r\_{e}} \\right)$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si478.png), if the equivalent radius is introduced,

*r*[*e*] :  = *α*[1]*h* = *e*^( − *π*/2)*h* ≈ 0.20788*h* ≈ 0.2*h* (2.187)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si479.png)(2.187) 

Then for an injection well,

$\\widehat{q} = \\frac{2\\pi\\rho kh\_{z}}{\\mu\\ln\\left( {r\_{e}/r\_{w}} \\right)}\\left( {p\_{bh} - p} \\right)$ (2.188)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si480.png)(2.188) 

When the well is a producer, the well rate is

$\\left| \\widehat{q} \\right| = - \\widehat{q} = \\frac{2\\pi\\rho kh\_{z}}{\\mu\\ln\\left( {r\_{e}/r\_{w}} \\right)}\\left( {p - p\_{bh}} \\right)$ (2.189)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si481.png)(2.189)  

### 2.5.4 Extensions of well modeling

 

#### 2.5.4.1 Extension to anisotropic media

The aforementioned well model can be extended to rectangular grids, incorporating effects of gravity force, anisotropic reservoirs, skin effects, horizontal wells, and multiphase flows. Here an extension of the well model to the first four effects is considered. With these effects for the single-phase flow and an anisotropic (but still diagonal, not full-tensor) permeability **k** = *d**i**a**g*(*k*[11],*k*[22],*k*[33])![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si482.png), the well model is extended to

$\\widehat{q} = \\frac{2\\pi\\rho h\_{z}\\sqrt{k\_{11}k\_{22}}}{\\mu\\left( {\\ln\\left( {r\_{e}/r\_{w}} \\right) + s\_{k}} \\right)}\\left( {p\_{bh} - p - \\rho g\\left( {z\_{bh} - z} \\right)} \\right)$ (2.190)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si483.png)(2.190) 

where *g* is the gravitational acceleration; *z* is the depth; *z*[*b**h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si484.png) is the well datum level depth; *r*[*e*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si485.png) is the equivalent radius, which is defined for nonsquare grids or anisotropic media in the following text; $\\sqrt{k\_{11}k\_{22}}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si486.png) is the factor arises from the coordinate transformation: ${x\_{1}}^{\\prime} = x\_{1}/\\sqrt{k\_{11}}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si487.png) and ${x\_{2}}^{\\prime} = x\_{2}/\\sqrt{k\_{22}}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si488.png); and *s*[*k*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si489.png) is the skin factor.

 

#### 2.5.4.2 Equivalent radius and well index

In the nonsquare grid and anisotropic media case, the equivalent radius *r*[*e*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si490.png) can be computed by

$r\_{e} = \\frac{0.14\\left( {\\left( {k\_{22}/k\_{11}} \\right)^{1/2}h\_{1}^{2} + \\left( {k\_{11}/k\_{22}} \\right)^{1/2}h\_{2}^{2}} \\right)^{1/2}}{0.5\\left( {\\left( {k\_{22}/k\_{11}} \\right)^{1/4} + \\left( {k\_{11}/k\_{22}} \\right)^{1/4}} \\right)}$ (2.191)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si491.png)(2.191) 

where *h*[1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si492.png) and *h*[2]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si493.png) are *x*[1]\- and *x*[2]\-grid sizes of the gridblock containing the vertical well.

Define below well index,

$WΙ = \\frac{2\\pi h\_{z}\\sqrt{k\_{11}k\_{22}}}{\\ln\\left( {r\_{e}/r\_{w}} \\right) + s\_{k}}$ (2.192)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si494.png)(2.192) 

With WI the following equation can be obtained:

$\\widehat{q} = WΙ\\frac{\\rho}{\\mu}\\left( {p\_{bh} - p - \\rho g\\left( {z\_{bh} - z} \\right)} \\right)$ (2.193)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si495.png)(2.193) 

Other extensions include horizontal wells, multiphase flow, off-centered wells, and multilayer well models.

 

## 2.6 Solute transport in porous media

 

### 2.6.1 Introduction on solute transport in porous media

 

#### 2.6.1.1 Terminologies

Before introducing the solute transport in porous media, first the fundamental definitions involved are presented here. Generally, solution is a homogeneous mixture of two or more substances which form a single phase, and it can be classified into solid solution (not considered in this book) and fluid solution (liquid/gaseous solution). Solute is the substance in a solution that has a small fraction of the mass and solvent is the substance has a large fraction of the mass, both solute and solvent are components of the solution (see [Fig. 2.6](#chp002_b.xhtml#f0035)). In this part, referring to solution instead of fluid indicates a focus that is more on chemical aspects than on physical ones.

![Figure 2.6 Solution, in which the light blue color represents the solvent and the red dot represents the solute.](OEBPS/images/F000022f02-06-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 2.6 Solution, in which the light blue color represents the solvent and the red dot represents the solute.

Transport in subsurface porous media refers to the transport of a fluid and/or its components through the Earth’s crust with or without chemical reactions. It is crucial to establish various transport models in order to better understand some phenomena in our life and engineering, including the composition of natural waters, the origin of economic mineral deposits, the formation and dissolution of rocks and minerals in geologic formations in response to injection of industrial wastes, steam, or carbon dioxide, and the generation of acidic waters and leaching of metals from mine wastes. For instance, the prediction of migration of contaminant plumes and the mobility of radionuclides in waste repositories are relying on the transport models.

 

#### 2.6.1.2 Subprocesses of solute transport in porous media

Solute transport in porous media consists of two aspects: (1) movement with the fluid.
