It originates from the fluid’s flow which in turn is determined by the fluid’s physical properties, geometry and physicochemical properties of the bounding solid, and in multiphase systems by the properties of the further fluids. This process refers to as convection (or advection) and (2) movement within the fluid. It results from the movement of entities of smaller scale, which include molecular diffusion (the thermal motion) and dispersion (or mechanical dispersion).

The difference between the dispersion and the explicitly represented convection depends on the scale of focus, as the convection may still vary at larger scales. It is still challenging to determine an optimal scale for the separation between convection and dispersion and quantifying the latter in the study of solute transport physics. Molecular diffusion is always active with spreading essentially a function of time, it is inherently isotropic, possibly restricted by phase boundaries. In contrast, the dispersion is a direct consequence of fluid flow in porous media with spreading basically a function of travel distance and mean velocity. It is anisotropic, depending on the direction of bulk flow. Molecular diffusion is usually scale independent, but dispersion is scale dependent.

 

#### 2.6.1.3 Solute transport modeling

The modeling of modern reactive transport has arisen from several separate schools of thought (hydrological models, geochemical models, and multicomponent reactive transport models). Hydrologists mainly focused on physical natures of mass transport with assumptions of relatively simple reaction formulations, such as linear distribution coefficients or linear decay terms, which can be easily incorporated into the advection–dispersion equation. For example, the advection–dispersion equation can be modified by a simple retardation factor and solved analytically by assuming a linear and equilibrium sorption. However, such analytical solutions are only limited to relatively simple flow systems and reactions.

Generally, the modeling of modern reactive transport involves complex physical and chemical processes: (1) mass transport: advection, molecular scale diffusion, hydrodynamic dispersion, colloid-facilitated transport; (2) heat transport: advection, conduction, convection; (3) medium deformation: compression or expansion of the domain, fracture formation; and (4) geochemical reactions: acid–base reactions; aqueous complexation; mineral dissolution and precipitation; reduction and oxidation (redox) reactions, including those catalyzed by enzymes, surfaces, and microorganisms; sorption, ion exchange, and surface complexation; gas dissolution and exsolution; stable isotope fractionation; radioactive decay.

 

### 2.6.2 Modeling equations for solute transport in porous media

 

#### 2.6.2.1 Simplified solute transport scenario

We assume that the transport of a solute within a fluid phase occupying the whole void space in porous media is considered. Obviously, it is a mass transport together with a single-phase flow in porous media. To simplify the solute transport process, the effects of chemical reactions between different components (species) in the fluid phase, biodegradation, or growth due to bacterial activities that cause the quantity of this component to decrease or increase are all ignored here. Adsorption, desorption, and radioactive decay will be consider later in this book.

 

#### 2.6.2.2 Solute transport equation: advection and diffusion–dispersion

 

1.  1. Solute transport equation  
    The mass conservation of a solute in the fluid phase is given by
    
    $\\frac{\\partial\\left( {\\phi c\\rho} \\right)}{\\partial t} + \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {c\\rho\\mathbf{u} - \\rho\\mathbf{D}\\nabla c} \\right) = q\_{c}^{-} + q\_{c}^{+}$ (2.194)
    
    ![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si496.png)(2.194) 
    
    where *c* is the component concentration (volumetric fraction in the fluid phase); **D** is the diffusion–dispersion tensor,
    
    **D**(**u**) = *ϕ*(*d*[*m*]**Ι**+|**u**|(*d*[*l*]**E**(**u**)+*d*[*t*]**E**^⊥(**u**))) (2.195)
    
    ![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si497.png)(2.195) 
    
    where *d*[*m*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si498.png), *d*[*l*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si499.png), and *d*[*t*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si500.png) are respectively the molecular, longitudinal, and transverse dispersion coefficients. Physically, *d*[*l*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si501.png) is usually considerably larger than *d*[*t*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si502.png) because the tensor dispersion is more significant than the molecular diffusion; |**u**|![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si503.png) is the Euclidean norm of **u** = (*u*[1],*u*[2],*u*[3])![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si504.png), $\\left| \\mathbf{u} \\right| = \\sqrt{u\_{1}^{2} + u\_{2}^{2} + u\_{3}^{2}}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si505.png); **E**^⊥(**u**) = **I** − **E**(**u**)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si506.png), **E**(**u**)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si507.png) is the orthogonal projection along the velocity,
    
    $\\mathbf{E}\\left( \\mathbf{u} \\right) = \\frac{1}{\\left| \\mathbf{u} \\right|^{2}}\\left( \\begin{array}{l} {u\_{1}^{2}u\_{1}u\_{2}u\_{1}u\_{3}} \\\\ {u\_{2}u\_{1}u\_{2}^{2}u\_{2}u\_{3}} \\\\ {u\_{3}u\_{1}u\_{3}u\_{2}u\_{3}^{2}} \\\\ \\end{array} \\right)$ (2.196)
    
    ![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si508.png)(2.196) 
    
      
    *q*[*c*]^−![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si509.png) and *q*[*c*]^+![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si510.png) in [Eq. (2.194)](#chp002_b.xhtml#eqn194) are computed by
    
    $q\_{c}^{-} = - {\\sum\\limits\_{i}q\_{1}^{(i)}}\\left( {\\mathbf{x}^{(i)},t} \\right)\\delta\\left( {\\mathbf{x} - \\mathbf{x}^{(i)}} \\right)\\left( {\\rho c} \\right)\\left( {\\mathbf{x},t} \\right)$ (2.197)
    
    ![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si511.png)(2.197) 
    
    $q\_{c}^{+} = {\\sum\\limits\_{j}q\_{2}^{(i)}}\\left( {\\mathbf{x}^{(j)},t} \\right)\\delta\\left( {\\mathbf{x} - \\mathbf{x}^{(j)}} \\right)\\left( {\\rho^{(j)}c^{(j)}} \\right)\\left( {\\mathbf{x},t} \\right)$ (2.198)
    
    ![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si512.png)(2.198) 
    
    where *q*[1]^((*i*))![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si513.png) is the production rate; *q*[2]^((*j*))![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si514.png) is the injection rate; *c*^((*j*))![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si515.png) is the specified concentration at source points.
    
2.  2. Flow equation

Darcy’s law for the fluid reads

$\\mathbf{u} = - \\frac{1}{\\mu}\\mathbf{k}\\left( {\\nabla p - \\rho g} \\right)$ (2.199)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si516.png)(2.199) 

The mass balance of the fluid is computed by

$\\frac{\\partial\\left( {\\phi\\rho} \\right)}{\\partial t} + \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\rho\\mathbf{u}} \\right) = q^{-} + q^{+}$ (2.200)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si517.png)(2.200) 

where $q^{-} = - {\\sum\\limits\_{i}{\\rho q\_{1}^{(i)}}}\\left( {\\mathbf{x}^{(i)},t} \\right)\\delta\\left( {\\mathbf{x} - \\mathbf{x}^{(i)}} \\right)$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si518.png), $q^{+} = {\\sum\\limits\_{j}{\\rho^{(j)}q\_{2}^{(j)}}}\\left( {\\mathbf{x}^{(j)},t} \\right)\\delta\\left( {\\mathbf{x} - \\mathbf{x}^{(j)}} \\right)$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si519.png).

 

#### 2.6.2.3 A coupled system in *c* and *p*

The relationships of density and viscosity with *p* and *c* are given next,

*ρ* = *ρ*(*p*,*c*), *μ* = *μ*(*p*,*c*) (2.201)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si520.png)(2.201) 

A coupled system of two equations in *c* and *p* can be established after the substitution of Darcy’s law into the two conservation laws (one for the solute and the other for the total fluid). Note that the equations described here is applicable to the problem of miscible displacement of one fluid by another in porous media.

 

### 2.6.3 Advection

 

#### 2.6.3.1 Advection and its properties

In engineering, physics, and earth sciences, advection refers to the transport of a substance by bulk motions. A vivid example of advection is the transport of pollutants or silt in a river by bulk water flow downstream. During advection, a fluid transports conserved quantity or material via bulk motions. The properties that are carried with the advected substance are conserved properties such as energy. Another commonly advected quantity is energy or enthalpy. In general, any substance or conserved, extensive quantity can be advected by a fluid that holds or contains the quantity or substance.

 

#### 2.6.3.2 Advection equation for a conserved quantity

The advection equation for a conserved quantity described by a scalar field *ψ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si521.png) is expressed mathematically by a continuity equation as shown next,

$\\frac{\\partial\\psi}{\\partial t} + \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\psi\\mathbf{u}} \\right) = 0$ (2.202)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si522.png)(2.202) 

where ∇⋅![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si523.png) is the divergence operator and **u** is the velocity vector field.

 

#### 2.6.3.3 Advection equation for incompressible/steady flow

Due to the fact that the incompressible flow in considered in most cases, thus the velocity field satisfies

∇ ⋅ **u** = 0 (2.203)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si524.png)(2.203) 

and **u** is said to be solenoidal. If this is so, [Eq. (2.203)](#chp002_b.xhtml#eqn203) can be rewritten as

$\\frac{\\partial\\psi}{\\partial t} + \\mathbf{u} \\cdot \\nabla\\psi = 0$ (2.204)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si525.png)(2.204) 

If the flow is steady,

**u** ⋅ ∇*ψ* = 0 (2.205)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si526.png)(2.205) 

It indicates that *ψ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si527.png) is constant along a streamline, this is because the direction of **u** must be perpendicular to the direction of ∇*ψ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si528.png), or ∂[*β*]∂*ψ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si529.png), where *β*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si530.png) is the direction of the velocity **u**. In addition, ∂*ψ*/∂*t* = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si531.png), so *ψ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si532.png) does not vary in time.

 

#### 2.6.3.4 Difficulty in solving advection equations

It should be noted that the advection equation is not easy to be numerically solved due to the hyperbolic PDE system, and interest typically centers on discontinuous “shock” solutions, but the “shock” solutions are notoriously hard to handle. Even with 1D and a constant velocity field, the system still remains difficult to solve,

$\\frac{\\partial\\psi}{\\partial t} + u\_{x}\\frac{\\partial\\psi}{\\partial x} = 0$ (2.206)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si533.png)(2.206) 

where *ψ* = *ψ*(*x*,*t*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si534.png) is a scalar field being advected and *u*[*x*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si535.png) is the velocity component of **u**(*u*[*x*],0,0)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si536.png).

 

### 2.6.4 Upwind-biased schemes

 

#### 2.6.4.1 Upwind scheme

In computational fluid dynamics, the upwind scheme denotes a class of numerical discretization methods for solving hyperbolic PDEs. Generally, the upwind-biased schemes or simply upwind schemes use an adaptive or solution-sensitive finite difference (FD) stencil to numerically simulate the direction of information propagation in a flow field. This type of scheme attempts to discretize hyperbolic PDEs by using differencing biased in the direction determined by the sign of characteristic speeds.

Consider 1D linear advection equation next as an example,

$\\frac{\\partial c}{\\partial t} + u\\frac{\\partial c}{\\partial x} = 0$ (2.207)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si537.png)(2.207) 

It describes a wave propagating along the *x*\-axis with a velocity *u*. If *u* is positive, the traveling wave solution of the equation abovementioned propagates toward the right, the left side of *i* is called upwind side and the right side is the downwind side. Similarly, if *u* is negative the traveling wave solution propagates toward the left (then left=downwind side and right=upwind side) (see [Fig. 2.7](#chp002_b.xhtml#f0040)).

![Figure 2.7 The sketch map of upwind scheme.](OEBPS/images/F000022f02-07-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 2.7 The sketch map of upwind scheme.

Since the wave is coming from its upwind side, it makes more sense to believe that its upwind side contains more information than its downwind side. Thus it also makes more sense to approximate ∂*u*/∂*x*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si538.png) using more points in the upwind side than its downwind side. If the FD scheme for the spatial derivative ∂*u*/∂*x*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si539.png) contains more points in the upwind side, the scheme is called an upwind scheme.

 

#### 2.6.4.2 The first-order upwind finite difference scheme

The first-order upwind scheme is the simplest upwind scheme, which is given by

$\\frac{c\_{i}^{n + 1} - c\_{i}^{n}}{\\Delta t} + u\\frac{c\_{i}^{n} - c\_{i - 1}^{n}}{\\Delta x} = 0\\quad{for} u > 0$ (2.208a)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si540.png)(2.208a) 

$\\frac{c\_{i}^{n + 1} - c\_{i}^{n}}{\\Delta t} + u\\frac{c\_{i + 1}^{n} - c\_{i}^{n}}{\\Delta x} = 0\\quad{for} u < 0$ (2.208b)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si541.png)(2.208b) 

The compact form of the first-order upwind scheme reads

*c*[*i*]^(*n* + 1) = *c*[*i*]^(*n*) − *Δ**t*(*u*^+*c*[*x*]^−+*u*^−*c*[*x*]^+) (2.209)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si542.png)(2.209) 

where *u*^+ = max (*u*,0)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si543.png), *u*^− = min (*u*,0)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si544.png), *c*[*x*]^− = (*c*[*i*]^(*n*)−*c*[*i* − 1]^(*n*))/*Δ**x*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si545.png) and *c*[*x*]^+ = (*c*[*i* + 1]^(*n*)−*c*[*i*]^(*n*))/*Δ**x*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si546.png).

Scheme [(2.209)](#chp002_b.xhtml#eqn209) has the following properties:

1.  1. The scheme is stable if CFL condition given next is satisfied,
    
    $\\alpha = \\left| \\frac{u\\Delta t}{\\Delta x} \\right| \\leq 1$ (2.210)
    
    ![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si547.png)(2.210) 
2.  2. The Taylor series analysis shows the scheme is first-order accurate in space and time.
3.  3. The modified wavenumber analysis shows the scheme can introduce severe numerical diffusion/dissipation in the solution where large gradients exist due to necessity of high wavenumbers to represent sharp gradients.

 

## 2.7 Dynamic sorption in porous media

 

### 2.7.1 The phenomenon of adsorption

 

#### 2.7.1.1 Adsorption

Adsorption is the phenomenon in which part of the mass of a chemical species present in a fluid (adsorbate) that occupies the void space, or part of it accumulates on the solid matrix (adsorbent) at the liquid–solid interface (see [Fig. 2.8](#chp002_b.xhtml#f0045)). It is caused by the attraction of the species to the solid surface, or by reactions of one or more species present in the liquid with the solid, it may also take place between a solid and a gaseous phase. Adsorption is the opposite of desorption, it can be classified into physical adsorption and chemical adsorption. In hydrology, researchers mainly focus on the adsorption of a species onto a solid in contact with a liquid phase. In general, sorption can narrowly mean adsorption, or it means adsorption and desorption in this book.

![Figure 2.8 The sketch map of adsorption in porous media.](OEBPS/images/F000022f02-08-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 2.8 The sketch map of adsorption in porous media.

 

#### 2.7.1.2 Equilibrium adsorption versus kinetic adsorption

A simple and commonly used method to study adsorption is using the concept of an adsorption isotherm. In the concept of an adsorption isotherm, it is assumed that adsorption is under equilibrium conditions and the amount of adsorbed species on a solid (within an REV) is solely a function of the species concentration in the liquid. This assumption of equilibrium adsorption is valid as long as the concentration of all other dissolved species influencing adsorption does not change substantially in time. Generally, however, the equilibrium condition does not hold and a more complicated analysis is required, involving the analysis of reactions at the solid surface. Examples of such reactions are ion exchange and surface complexation.

 

#### 2.7.1.3 Effecting factors of sorption

The reasons contributing to adsorption are complex, one possible driving force for adsorption is the lyophobic (solute disliking) nature of a solute relative to that of the solvent. Another possible reason is the high affinity of the solute for the solid. The affinity of a dissolved species to the solid surface can attribute to various physical causes, such as electrical attraction, van der Waals attraction, that is, intermolecular forces of attraction between molecules of the solid and those of adsorbed species, and chemisorption, that is, chemical interaction between the solid and adsorbed species. The most crucial influencing factor is the solubility degree of the dissolved species. Other factors that may influence the adsorption and desorption of a chemical species are the physical, electrical, and chemical characteristics of the species and of the surface of the solid, temperature, and the presence of other species in the fluid phase.

 

#### 2.7.1.4 The role of diffusion and the rate-limiting step

Here we distinct adsorption between diffusion from the perspective of physical process. Note that at a pore scale **u** ⋅ **n** = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si548.png) even if the slippage B.C. is adopted, thus the advective fluid flow within this boundary layer is negligible. To reach the solid surface, the adsorbate has first to pass from the bulk solution through this layer by molecular diffusion, and then the adsorbate can interact with the solid. The desorbed species can return to the bulk solution in an opposite way. In a double porosity medium an adsorbate must first diffuse into the (liquid saturated) solid matrix, then diffuse within the small pores constituting the pore space of the latter, and finally adsorb on its surface area.

When discussing the adsorption rate or the characteristic time involved, in many cases the rate-limiting step is not the chemical interaction with the solid but the diffusion through the film and (in the case of a porous matrix) through the tiny pores within the solid matrix. In some theories the solid is always supposed to be covered by a thin fluid boundary layer or film that has properties and composition different from those of the bulk fluid. When considering “equilibrium,” it means the equilibrium between the adsorbed species and the species concentration in that film.

 

### 2.7.2 Adsorption isotherms

 

#### 2.7.2.1 Partitioning coefficient (distribution coefficient)

When adsorption of a dissolved species takes place in saturated flows, its total mass within each REV of the porous medium is partitioned between the solid matrix and the solution. Any increase in the quantity of species in the liquid is associated with an appropriate increase in its quantity on the solid, and vice versa. In unsaturated flows the partition of the total mass of dissolved species in the REV, among the solid, the liquid, and the gaseous phases, should be considered carefully. An adsorption isotherm is a function relating the quantity of a species adsorbed on the solid to its quantity in the liquid phase that occupies the void space under equilibrium conditions at a fixed temperature.

Let *F* denotes the mass of a species (adsorbate) adsorbed on the solid (adsorbent), per unit mass of the latter. Here *F* can be measured in kg/kg, or in moles/kg, while the concentration *c* in the liquid is measured in kg/L, or in moles/L. Although it seems to be more natural to define the quantity of the species on the solid per unit surface area of the solid, the reference to “unit mass of solid” arises from the way that this quantity is measured in the laboratory by performing a batch adsorption experiment.

 

#### 2.7.2.2 Linear isotherm

The linear isotherm usually has the following form:

*F* = *K*[*d*]*c* (2.211)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si549.png)(2.211) 

where *K*[*d*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si550.png) is distribution coefficient or partitioning coefficient representing the affinity of species for the solid relative to that for the fluid. It gives the mass of the species on the solid, per unit mass of the latter, per unit concentration of the species in the liquid phase at every instant.

Sometimes, *K*[*d*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si551.png) for the adsorption process differs from that for the desorption one, which indicates that the sorption process is not completely reversible often due to the surface catalysis. Another observation is that there exists a limit to the adsorptive capacity of a solid surface especially in chemisorption, and it requires to modify the isotherm form. In unsaturated flows, part of the surface of the solid is less readily accessible to pore water as the larger pores are occupied by air. In this case, *K*[*d*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si552.png) may be a function of the saturation. On the other hand, it is well known that water is a wetting liquid and it is everywhere adjacent to the solid surface, albeit at some places as a very thin film with diffusion of chemical species through it.

 

#### 2.7.2.3 Freundlich isotherm

*F* = *b**c*^(*m*) (2.212)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si553.png)(2.212) 

where *c* is the adsorbate concentration in the solution; *b* and *m* are constant coefficients depending on temperature. The situation *m*<1 means that as *F* increases, it becomes more difficult to adsorb additional quantities of the adsorbate. The opposite case is described by *m*\>1, when *m*\>1 the isotherm reduces to linear isotherm.

 

#### 2.7.2.4 Langmuir isotherm

$F = \\frac{k\_{3}c}{1 + k\_{4}c}$ (2.213)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si554.png)(2.213) 

where *k*[3]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si555.png) and *k*[4]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si556.png) are constant coefficients, usually *k*[3] > 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si557.png) and *k*[4] > 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si558.png). Thus as *F* increases, it becomes more difficult to adsorb additional quantities of the adsorbate. It should be noted that *F*→*c**o**n**s**t*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si559.png) as *c*→∞![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si560.png), thus unlike Freundlich isotherm, the adsorption can reach a certain saturated value in Langmuir isotherm.

 

#### 2.7.2.5 Lindstrom–van Genuchten isotherm

*F* = *k*[5]*c*exp (−2*k*[6]*F*) (2.214)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si561.png)(2.214) 

where *k*[5]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si562.png) and *k*[6]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si563.png) are constant coefficients. It indicates that *k*[*d*] = *F*/*c*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si564.png) is a function of the concentration in the solid. [Eq. (2.214)](#chp002_b.xhtml#eqn214) reduces to linear isotherm when *k*[6] = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si565.png). Usually *k*[6] > 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si566.png), thus as *F*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si567.png) increases, it becomes more difficult to adsorb additional quantities of the adsorbate.

 

### 2.7.3 Modeling of transport with sorption

 

#### 2.7.3.1 General equations for solute transport with sorption

Without sorption, the mass conservation of a solute in an incompressible fluid phase is given by

$\\frac{\\partial\\left( {\\phi c} \\right)}{\\partial t} + \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {c\\mathbf{u} - \\mathbf{D}\\nabla c} \\right) = q\_{c}$ (2.215)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si568.png)(2.215) 

*q*[*c*] = *q*[*c*]^−(*c*) + *q*[*c*]^+ (2.216)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si569.png)(2.216) 

where *c* is the concentration (mass fraction in the fluid phase) of the component; **D**![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si570.png) is the diffusion–dispersion tensor, it is a function of the Darcy velocity **D** = **D**(**u**)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si571.png); *q*[*c*]^−![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si572.png) and *q*[*c*]^+![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si573.png) are source terms representing production and injection wells, and *q*[*c*]^−![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si574.png) is a function of residence concentration.

Consider sorption, the mass conservation of a solute in the fluid phase reads

$\\frac{\\partial\\left( {\\phi c} \\right)}{\\partial t} + \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {c\\mathbf{u} - \\mathbf{D}\\nabla c} \\right) = q\_{c}\\left( c \\right) - r\_{sorp}\\left( {c,c\_{s}} \\right)$ (2.217)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si575.png)(2.217) 

where *r*[*s**o**r**p*](*c*,*c*[*s*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si576.png) is the net rate of sorption (modeled using a kinetic approach), that is, *r*[*s**o**r**p*] = *r*[*a**d**s**o**r**p*] − *r*[*d**e**s**o**r**p*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si577.png).

The mass conservation of the solute in the solid phase is given by

$\\frac{\\partial\\left( {\\rho\_{b}c\_{s}} \\right)}{\\partial t} = r\_{sorp}\\left( {c,c\_{s}} \\right)$ (2.218)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si578.png)(2.218) 

Here to calculate *r*[*s**o**r**p*](*c*,*c*[*s*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si579.png), a linear kinetic model *r*[*a**d**s**o**r**p*] = *k*[*a*]*c*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si580.png) and *r*[*d**e**s**o**r**p*] = *k*[*a*]*c*[*s*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si581.png) can be used.

Summing [Eqs. (2.217)](#chp002_b.xhtml#eqn217) and [(2.218)](#chp002_b.xhtml#eqn218) yields

$\\frac{\\partial\\left( {\\phi c + \\rho\_{b}c\_{s}} \\right)}{\\partial t} + \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {c\\mathbf{u} - \\mathbf{D}\\nabla c} \\right) = q\_{c}\\left( c \\right)$ (2.219)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si582.png)(2.219) 

If sorption is assumed to be equilibrium, *c*[*s*] = *K*[*d*]*c*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si583.png) with a given partitioning coefficient *K*[*d*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si584.png). Under the assumption of equilibrium sorption, the total mass conservation of the solute (in both fluid and solid) [(2.219)](#chp002_b.xhtml#eqn219) becomes

$\\frac{\\partial\\left( {\\phi + \\rho\_{b}K\_{d}} \\right)c}{\\partial t} + \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {c\\mathbf{u} - \\mathbf{D}\\nabla c} \\right) = q\_{c}\\left( c \\right)$ (2.220)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si585.png)(2.220) 

Define retardation coefficient as follows:

$R\_{d} = 1 + \\frac{\\rho\_{b}K\_{d}}{\\phi}$ (2.221)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si586.png)(2.221) 

With the retardation coefficient, [Eq. (2.220)](#chp002_b.xhtml#eqn220) can be simplified as

$\\frac{\\partial\\left( {\\phi R\_{d}c} \\right)}{\\partial t} + \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {c\\mathbf{u} - \\mathbf{D}\\nabla c} \\right) = q\_{c}\\left( c \\right)$ (2.222)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si587.png)(2.222) 

It looks like that both advection and diffusion–dispersion are slowed down by a factor of *R*[*d*] > 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si588.png). For nonlinear isotherms, *R*[*d*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si589.png) is still a function of *c*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si590.png) or/and *c*[*s*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si591.png).

 

#### 2.7.3.2 Linear sorption: equilibrium versus kinetics

A linear kinetic model for adsorption rate and desorption rate is defined as

*r*[*a**d**s**o**r**p*] = *k*[*a*]*c* < *r*[*d**e**s**o**r**p*] = *k*[*d*]*c*[*s*] (2.223)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si592.png)(2.223) 

Then the net rate of sorption can be calculated as

*r*[*s**o**r**p*] = *r*[*a**d**s**o**r**p*] − *r*[*d**e**s**o**r**p*] = *k*[*a*]*c* − *k*[*d*]*c*[*s*] (2.224)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si593.png)(2.224) 

At equilibrium the net sorption is zero, that is, *r*[*s**o**r**p*] = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si594.png). Consequently, we have *k*[*a*]*c* = *k*[*d*]*c*[*s*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si595.png),

$K\_{d} = \\frac{c\_{s}}{c} = \\frac{k\_{a}}{k\_{d}}$ (2.225)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si596.png)(2.225)  

#### 2.7.3.3 Langmiur sorption: equilibrium versus kinetics

For Langmiur sorption, the forward (adsorption) and the reverse (desorption) reaction rates can be modeled by

*r*[*a**d**s**o**r**p*] = *k*[*f*]*c*(1−*θ*), *r*[*d**e**s**o**r**p*] = *k*[*r*]*θ* (2.226)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si597.png)(2.226) 

where *k*[*f*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si598.png) and *k*[*r*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si599.png) are the respective rate constants; *θ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si600.png) is the surface coverage, or the fraction of available sites being covered by the sorbed component on the solid phase.

At equilibrium the net sorption rate *r*[*s**o**r**p*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si601.png) is zero and thus *r*[*a**d**s**o**r**p*] = *r*[*d**e**s**o**r**p*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si602.png). Substituting and solving for the coverage gives

$\\theta = \\frac{\\left( {k\_{f}/k\_{r}} \\right)c}{1 + \\left( {k\_{f}/k\_{r}} \\right)c}$ (2.227)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si603.png)(2.227) 

The forward (adsorption) rate expression *r*[*a**d**s**o**r**p*] = *k*[*f*]*c*(1−*θ*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si604.png) says that the rate of desorption depends linearly on the equilibrium concentration in the liquid and the density of the uncovered or bare sites. Sorption onto already sorbed sites is not allowed (the so-called monolayer coverage). The desorption reaction *r*[*d**e**s**o**r**p*] = *k*[*r*]*θ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si605.png) says that the rate of desorption depends linearly only on how many sites are occupied. Neither rate expression contains information about the specific nature of the sorbed component (e.g., its charge). The linearities are what make the Langmiur isotherm a traditional surrogate for physical sorption.

 

### 2.7.4 Numerical methods for transport with sorption

To solve [Eq. (2.222)](#chp002_b.xhtml#eqn222) of solute transport with equilibrium sorption, we can just use the same locally conservative algorithms. Attention should be paid that *R*[*d*] cannot be divided if *R*[*d*] = *R*[*d*](*c*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si606.png) or *R*[*d*] = *R*[*d*](*c*[*s*]).![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si607.png) For solving [Eq. (2.219)](#chp002_b.xhtml#eqn219) of solute transport with kinetic sorption, it can be treated like an advection–diffusion–reaction system.

 

## 2.8 Black oil model

 

### 2.8.1 Introduction of black oil model

 

#### 2.8.1.1 Main idea of black oil model

As we know, in immiscible two-phase (e.g., oil–water) flows, the assumption that mass does not transfer between phases is held. To relax this assumption, in this part the black oil model is introduced. In black oil model, it is now assumed that the hydrocarbon components are divided into the gas component and oil component in a stock tank at standard pressure and temperature, and mass transfer is allowed between the gas phase and oil phase. However, no mass transfer occurs between the water phase and the other two phases (oil and gas). The gas component mainly consists of methane and ethane and the water phase is just the water component.

To avoid confusion, one needs carefully distinguish between phases and components. In this book, we use the lowercase and uppercase letter subscripts to represent phases and components, respectively, and the subscript *s* indicates standard conditions. The notation *ρ*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si608.png) denotes the mass density of water phase; gas phase *g*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si609.png) contains gas component *G*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si610.png) only, and *ρ*[*g*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si611.png) denotes the mass density of gas phase; oil phase *o*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si612.png) contains both oil component *O*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si613.png) and gas component *G*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si614.png); *ρ*[*O**o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si615.png) and *ρ*[*G**o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si616.png) are used to denote the partial densities of the oil and gas components in the oil phase, respectively (see [Fig. 2.9](#chp002_b.xhtml#f0050)).

![Figure 2.9 The sketch map of black oil model.](OEBPS/images/F000022f02-09-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 2.9 The sketch map of black oil model.

 

#### 2.8.1.2 Mass conservation of each component

In black oil model, due to the mass interchange between the oil phase and gas phase, mass is not conserved within each phase, but the total mass of each component is conserved in the whole system.

For the water component,

$\\frac{\\partial\\left( {\\phi\\rho\_{w}S\_{w}} \\right)}{\\partial t} = - \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\rho\_{w}\\mathbf{u}\_{w}} \\right) + q\_{W}$ (2.228)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si617.png)(2.228) 

For the oil component,

$\\frac{\\partial\\left( {\\phi\\rho\_{Oo}S\_{o}} \\right)}{\\partial t} = - \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\rho\_{Oo}\\mathbf{u}\_{o}} \\right) + q\_{O}$ (2.229)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si618.png)(2.229) 

where *ρ*[*O**o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si619.png) indicates the partial density of oil component in the oil phase.

For the gas component,

$\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial t}\\left( {\\phi\\left( {\\rho\_{Go}S\_{o} + \\rho\_{g}S\_{g}} \\right)} \\right) = - \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\rho\_{Go}\\mathbf{u}\_{o} + \\rho\_{g}\\mathbf{u}\_{g}} \\right) + q\_{G}$ (2.230)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si620.png)(2.230) 

where oil phase *o*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si621.png) contains both oil component *O*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si622.png) and gas component *G*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si623.png); that is, gas component *G*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si624.png) exists in both gas phase and oil phase; *ρ*[*G**o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si625.png) indicates the partial density of gas component in the oil phase.

 

#### 2.8.1.3 Darcy’s law and other equations

The extended Darcy’s law reads

$\\mathbf{u}\_{\\alpha} = - \\frac{\\mathbf{K}k\_{ra}}{\\mu\_{\\alpha}}(\\nabla p\_{\\alpha} - \\rho\_{a}\\mathbf{g}),\\alpha = w,o,g$ (2.231)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si626.png)(2.231) 

The three phases jointly fill the void space, thus the saturation equation is given by

*S*[*w*] + *S*[*o*] + *S*[*g*] = 1 (2.232)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si627.png)(2.232) 

The phase pressures are related by capillary pressures,

*p*[*c**o**w*] = *p*[*o*] − *p*[*w*], *p*[*c**g**w*] = *p*[*g*] − *p*[*o*] (2.233)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si628.png)(2.233)  

#### 2.8.1.4 The gas solubility

In the black oil model, it is convenient to work with conservation equations on “standard volume” instead of on “mass.” The volume fractions of oil and gas components in the oil phase can be determined by gas solubility, *R*[*s**o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si629.png) (also called dissolved gas/oil ratio). *R*[*s**o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si630.png) is the gas volume (measured at standard conditions) dissolved at a given pressure and reservoir temperature in an unit volume of stock tank oil,

$R\_{so}\\left( {p,T} \\right) = \\frac{V\_{Gs}}{V\_{Os}}$ (2.234)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si631.png)(2.234) 

Note that

$V\_{Os} = \\frac{W\_{O}}{\\rho\_{Os}}, V\_{Gs} = \\frac{W\_{G}}{\\rho\_{Gs}}$ (2.235)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si632.png)(2.235) 

where *W*[*O*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si633.png) is the weight of oil component and *W*[*G*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si634.png) is the weight of gas components.

Substitute [Eq. (2.235)](#chp002_b.xhtml#eqn235) into [(2.236)](#chp002_b.xhtml#eqn236), the dissolved gas/oil ratio *R*[*s**o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si635.png) can be written as

$R\_{so} = \\frac{W\_{G}\\rho\_{Os}}{W\_{O}\\rho\_{Go}}$ (2.236)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si636.png)(2.236)  

#### 2.8.1.5 The oil formation volume factor

The oil formation volume factor *B*[*o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si637.png) is defined as the ratio of the volume *V*[*o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si638.png) of oil phase (measured at reservoir conditions) to the volume *V*[*o**s*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si639.png) of oil component (measured at standard conditions),

$B\_{o}\\left( {p,T} \\right) = \\frac{V\_{o}\\left( {p,T} \\right)}{V\_{Os}}$ (2.237)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si640.png)(2.237) 

where *V*[*o*] = (*W*[*O*]+*W*[*G*])/*ρ*[*o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si641.png).

One can also derive that

$B\_{o} = \\frac{\\left( {W\_{O} + W\_{G}} \\right)\\rho\_{Os}}{W\_{O}\\rho\_{o}}$ (2.238)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si642.png)(2.238)  

#### 2.8.1.6 Mass fractions of components

The mass fractions of oil and gas components in oil phase are given by

$C\_{Oo} = \\frac{W\_{O}}{W\_{O} + W\_{G}} = \\frac{\\rho\_{Os}}{B\_{o}\\rho\_{o}}, C\_{Go} = \\frac{W\_{G}}{W\_{O} + W\_{G}} = \\frac{R\_{so}\\rho\_{Gs}}{B\_{o}\\rho\_{o}}$ (2.239)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si643.png)(2.239) 

The abovementioned equations together with *C*[*O**o*] + *C*[*G**o*] = 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si644.png) yield

$\\rho\_{o} = \\frac{R\_{so}\\rho\_{Gs} + \\rho\_{Os}}{B\_{o}}$ (2.240)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si645.png)(2.240)  

#### 2.8.1.7 The gas formation volume factor

Similar to the definition of *B*[*o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si646.png), the gas formation volume factor *B*[*g*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si647.png) is defined as the ratio of the volume of gas phase (measured at reservoir conditions) to the volume of gas component (measured at standard conditions),

$B\_{g}\\left( {p,T} \\right) = \\frac{V\_{g}\\left( {p,T} \\right)}{V\_{Gs}}$ (2.241)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si648.png)(2.241) 

We have *V*[*g*] = *W*[*G*]/*ρ*[*g*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si649.png) and *V*[*G**s*] = *W*[*G*]/*ρ*[*G**s*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si650.png), thus the density of gas phase can be computed by

$\\rho\_{g} = \\frac{\\rho\_{Gs}}{B\_{g}}$ (2.242)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si651.png)(2.242)  

#### 2.8.1.8 The water formation volume factor

Similarly, the water formation volume factor *B*[*w*] is defined by

$B\_{w} = \\frac{\\rho\_{Ws}}{\\rho\_{w}}$ (2.243)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si652.png)(2.243) 

The flow rates in [Eqs. (2.228)](#chp002_b.xhtml#eqn228)– [(2.230)](#chp002_b.xhtml#eqn230) are defined by

$q\_{W} = \\frac{q\_{Ws}\\rho\_{Ws}}{B\_{w}}, q\_{O} = \\frac{q\_{Os}\\rho\_{Os}}{B\_{o}}, q\_{G} = \\frac{q\_{Gs}\\rho\_{Gs}}{B\_{g}} + \\frac{q\_{Os}R\_{so}\\rho\_{Gs}}{B\_{o}}$ (2.244)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si653.png)(2.244) 

where *q*[*W**s*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si654.png), *q*[*O**s*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si655.png), and *q*[*G**s*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si656.png) are the flows rates at standard conditions.

 

#### 2.8.1.9 Equations on standard volumes

Define below fluid gravities,

*γ*[*α*] = *ρ*[*α*]*g*, *α* = *w*, *o*, *g* (2.245)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si657.png)(2.245) 

Furthermore, introduce below transmissibility,

$\\mathbf{T}\_{\\alpha} = \\frac{k\_{r\\alpha}}{\\mu\_{\\alpha}B\_{\\alpha}}\\mathbf{k},\\alpha = w,o,g$ (2.246)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si658.png)(2.246) 

Substitute the above flow rates, fluid gravities, and transmissibility expressions into the conservation laws and divide the resultant equations respectively by *ρ*[*W**s*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si659.png), *ρ*[*O**s*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si660.png), and *ρ*[*G**s*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si661.png), the conservation equations on standard volumes can be obtained,

$\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial t}\\left( \\frac{\\phi S\_{w}}{B\_{w}} \\right) = \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\mathbf{T}\_{w}(\\nabla p\_{w} - \\gamma\_{w}\\nabla z)} \\right) + \\frac{q\_{Ws}}{B\_{w}}$ (2.247a)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si662.png)(2.247a) 

$\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial t}\\left( \\frac{\\phi S\_{O}}{B\_{o}} \\right) = \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\mathbf{T}\_{o}(\\nabla p\_{o} - \\gamma\_{o}\\nabla z)} \\right) + \\frac{q\_{Os}}{B\_{o}}$ (2.247b)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si663.png)(2.247b) 

$\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial t}\\left( {\\phi\\left( {\\frac{S\_{g}}{B\_{g}} + \\frac{R\_{so}S\_{O}}{B\_{o}}} \\right)} \\right) = \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\mathbf{T}\_{g}(\\nabla p\_{g} - \\gamma\_{g}\\nabla z)} \\right) + R\_{so}\\mathbf{T}\_{o}(\\nabla p\_{o} - \\gamma\_{g}\\nabla z) + \\frac{q\_{Gs}}{B\_{g}} + \\frac{q\_{Os}}{B\_{o}}$ (2.247c)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si664.png)(2.247c)  

### 2.8.2 Treatment of the wells in black oil model

The volumetric flow rates at wells (at standard conditions) are computed by

$q\_{Ws} = \\sum\\limits\_{\\nu = 1}^{N\_{w}}{\\sum\\limits\_{m = 1}^{M\_{wv}}q\_{Ws,m}^{(\\nu)}}\\delta(\\mathbf{x} - \\mathbf{x}\_{m}^{(\\nu)})$ (2.248a)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si665.png)(2.248a) 

$q\_{Os} = \\sum\\limits\_{\\nu = 1}^{N\_{w}}{\\sum\\limits\_{m = 1}^{M\_{wv}}q\_{Os,m}^{(\\nu)}}\\delta(\\mathbf{x} - \\mathbf{x}\_{m}^{(\\nu)})$ (2.248b)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si666.png)(2.248b) 

$q\_{Gs} = \\sum\\limits\_{\\nu = 1}^{N\_{w}}{\\sum\\limits\_{m = 1}^{M\_{wv}}q\_{Gs,m}^{(\\nu)}}\\delta(\\mathbf{x} - \\mathbf{x}\_{m}^{(\\nu)})$ (2.248c)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si667.png)(2.248c) 

With

$q\_{Ws,m}^{(\\nu)} = WI\_{m}^{(\\nu)}\\frac{k\_{rw}}{\\mu\_{w}}\\left( {p\_{bh}^{(\\nu)} - p\_{w} - \\gamma\_{w}(z\_{bh}^{(\\nu)} - z)} \\right)$ (2.249a)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si668.png)(2.249a) 

$q\_{Os,m}^{(\\nu)} = WI\_{m}^{(\\nu)}\\frac{k\_{ro}}{\\mu\_{o}}\\left( {p\_{bh}^{(\\nu)} - p\_{o} - \\gamma\_{o}(z\_{bh}^{(\\nu)} - z)} \\right)$ (2.249b)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si669.png)(2.249b) 

$q\_{Gs,m}^{(\\nu)} = WI\_{m}^{(\\nu)}\\frac{k\_{rg}}{\\mu\_{g}}\\left( {p\_{bh}^{(\\nu)} - p\_{g} - \\gamma\_{g}(z\_{bh}^{(\\nu)} - z)} \\right)$ (2.249c)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si670.png)(2.249c) 

where the well parameter ${WI}\_{m}^{(\\nu)} = \\left. {{2\\pi\\overline{k}\\Delta h}/{\\ln\\left( {r\_{e}/r\_{w}} \\right)}} \\right|\_{m}^{(\\nu)}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si671.png); *δ*(*x*) is the Dirac delta function; *N*[*w*] is the total well number; *M*[*wv*] is the total number of perforated zones of the *v*th well; *Δ**h*[*m*]^((*υ*))![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si672.png) and **x**[*m*]^((*υ*))![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si673.png) are the segment length and central location of the *m*th perforated zone of the *v*th well; $\\overline{k}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si674.png) is an average of *k* at the well; *r*[*w*]^((*ν*))![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si675.png) represents wellbore radius of the *v*th well; *r*[*e*, *m*]^((*ν*))![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si676.png) denotes the drainage radius of the *v*th well at the gridblock in which **x**[*m*]^((*ν*))![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si677.png) is located; *p*[*b**h*]^((*ν*))![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si678.png) denotes the BHP of the *v*th well at the well datum *z*[*b**h*]^((*ν*))![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si679.png).

 

### 2.8.3 Models for three-phase relative permeabilities

Many reservoir processes involve simultaneous flow of three phases. To model these processes, three-phase relative permeabilities are mandatory. However, the measurement of three-phase relative permeabilities is much rarer than that of two-phase relative permeabilities, and there are more uncertainties in the reported three-phase data. Current efforts in three-phase relative permeability studies are weighted toward identification of models for extrapolating two-phase relative permeability data to three-phase applications.

 

#### 2.8.3.1 Stone I for three phases

Stone started this trend in 1970 with a model that is now known as the Stone I model. In this model for water-wet porous media, the three-phase water relative permeability *k*[rw, wog]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si680.png) depends only on water saturation and is identical to *k*[rw, wo]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si681.png) measured in water/oil displacements,

*k*[rw, wog](*S*[*w*]) = *k*[rw, wo](*S*[*w*]) (2.250)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si682.png)(2.250) 

Similarly, the three-phase gas relative permeability *k*[rw, wog]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si683.png) depends only on gas saturation and is identical to *k*[rg, go]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si684.png) measured in gas/oil displacements,

*k*[rg, wog](*S*[*g*]) = *k*[rg, go](*S*[*g*]) (2.251)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si685.png)(2.251) 

The equality of water and gas relative permeabilities in two-phase and three-phase flows is supported by much of the three-phase data in the literature for water-wet media. On the other hand, the three-phase oil relative permeability *k*[ro, wog]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si686.png) depends nonlinearly on water and gas saturations,

$k\_{\\text{ro},\\text{wog}}(S\_{w},S\_{g}) = \\frac{S\_{oS}}{k\_{\\text{ro},\\text{wo}}(S\_{wc})}\\frac{k\_{\\text{ro},\\text{wo}}(S\_{w})}{1 - S\_{wS}}\\frac{k\_{\\text{ro},\\text{og}}(S\_{g})}{1 - S\_{gS}}$ (2.252)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si687.png)(2.252) 

with the oil, water, and gas saturations scaled as follows:

$S\_{oS}: = \\frac{S\_{o} - S\_{om}}{1 - S\_{wc} - S\_{om}},S\_{wS}: = \\frac{S\_{w} - S\_{wc}}{1 - S\_{wc} - S\_{om}},S\_{gS}: = \\frac{S\_{g}}{1 - S\_{wc} - S\_{om}}$ (2.253)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si688.png)(2.253) 

According to the study of Stone, the minimum oil saturation *S*[*o**m*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si689.png) should be in the range of 0.25 *S*[*w**c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si690.png) to 0.5 *S*[*w**c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si691.png) and should be less than or equal to the smaller of *S*[*o**r**w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si692.png) or *S*[*o**r**g*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si693.png), the residual oil saturations for waterflooding and gasflooding, respectively.

The minimum oil saturation can be computed by (

*S*[*o**m*] = *α**S*[*o**r**w*] + (1 − *α*)*S*[*o**r**g*] (2.254)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si694.png)(2.254) 

with *α* = 1 − *S*[*g*]/(1 − *S*[*w**c*] − *S*[*o**r**g*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si695.png).

Or it can be calculated following the next formula,

$S\_{om} = S\_{orw}\\left( \\frac{S\_{w} - S\_{wc}}{1 - S\_{owc} - S\_{orw}} \\right)^{\\alpha} + S\_{org}\\left( \\frac{S\_{g}}{1 - S\_{owc} - S\_{orw}} \\right)^{\\beta}$ (2.255)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si696.png)(2.255) 

Note that to account for hysteresis effects in three-phase flow, Stone recommended to use appropriate two-phase relative permeabilities.

 

#### 2.8.3.2 Stone II for three phases

In 1973 Stone developed another model that was known as the Stone II model. The difference of Stone II and Stone I model lies in the oil relative permeability. In Stone II model the oil relative permeability is defined as

$k\_{\\text{ro},\\text{wog}}(S\_{w},S\_{g}) = k\_{\\text{ro},\\text{wo}}(S\_{wc})\\left\\lbrack {\\left( {\\frac{k\_{\\text{ro},\\text{wo}}}{k\_{\\text{ro},\\text{wo}}(S\_{wc})} + k\_{\\text{rw},\\text{wo}}} \\right)\\left( {\\frac{k\_{\\text{ro},\\text{og}}}{k\_{\\text{ro},\\text{wo}}(S\_{wc})} + k\_{\\text{rg},\\text{go}}} \\right) - (k\_{\\text{rw},\\text{wo}} + k\_{\\text{rg},\\text{go}})} \\right\\rbrack$ (2.256)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si697.png)(2.256) 

where the water/oil and the gas/oil relative permeabilities in [Eq. (2.257)](#chp002_b.xhtml#eqn257) are functions of water saturation and gas saturation, respectively.

 

### 2.8.4 Rock and fluid properties

 

#### 2.8.4.1 Rock properties

The porosity *ϕ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si698.png) is assumed to have the form,

*ϕ* = *ϕ*⁰\[1+*c*[*R*](*p*−*p*⁰)\] (2.257)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si699.png)(2.257) 

where *ϕ*⁰![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si700.png) is the porosity at a reference pressure *p*⁰![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si701.png); *c*[*R*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si702.png) is the rock compressibility.

 

#### 2.8.4.2 Fluid properties

Three phases and three components are included in the black oil model. The water component and the oil component exist solely in water phase and oil phase, respectively. The gas component is divided into two parts: one part in the gas phase that is called free gas with density *ρ*[*g*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si703.png) and the other part in the oil phase that is termed the solution gas with density *ρ*[*G**o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si704.png).

The water viscosity *μ*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si705.png) is treated as constant and the water density *ρ*[*W**s*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si706.png) at standard conditions is determined using water salinities, while the water phase density *ρ*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si707.png) is given by

$\\rho\_{w} = \\frac{\\rho\_{Ws}}{B\_{wi}}(1 + c\_{w}(p - p^{0}))$ (2.258)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si708.png)(2.258) 

where *B*[*w**i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si709.png) is the water formation volume factor at the initial formation pressure *p*⁰![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si710.png), and *c*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si711.png) is the water compressibility.

The oil phase density *ρ*[*o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si712.png) is determined by

*ρ*[*o*] = *ρ*[*O**o*] + *ρ*[*G**o*] (2.259)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si713.png)(2.259) 

where the *ρ*[*O**o*] = *ρ*[*O**s*]/*B*[*o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si714.png), *B*[*o*] = *B*[*o**b*](*p*[*b*])\[1+*c*[*o*](*p*−*p*[*b*])\]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si715.png) with *B*[ob] being the formation volume factor at the bubble point pressure *p*[*b*] and *c*[*o*] is the oil compressibility.

The oil viscosity *μ*[*o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si716.png) is calculated by

*μ*[*o*] = *μ*[*o**b*](*p*[*b*])\[1+*c*[*μ*](*p*−*p*[*b*])\] (2.260)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si717.png)(2.260) 

where *μ*[*o**b*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si718.png) is the oil viscosity at *p*[*b*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si719.png) and *c*[*μ*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si720.png) denotes the oil viscosity compressibility.

The gas viscosity *μ*[*g*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si721.png) is a function of *p*,

*μ*[*g*] = *μ*[*g*](*p*) (2.261)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si722.png)(2.261) 

The solution gas density *ρ*[*G**o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si723.png) is determined by

$\\rho\_{Go} = \\frac{R\_{so}\\rho\_{Gs}}{B\_{o}}$ (2.262)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si724.png)(2.262) 

The free gas density *ρ*[*g*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si725.png) is defined by

$\\rho\_{g} = \\frac{\\rho\_{Gs}}{B\_{g}}$ (2.263)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si726.png)(2.263) 

where *ρ*[*G**s*] = *Y*[*G*]*ρ*[*a**i**r*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si727.png), and *B*[*g*] = (*Z**T*/*p*)(*p*[*s*]/*T*[*s*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si728.png) with *Y*[*G*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si729.png) being raw gas density, *ρ*[*a**i**r*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si730.png) is the air density and *Z* is the gas deviation factor.

 

### 2.8.5 Phase states and choice of the primary unknowns

 

#### 2.8.5.1 Phase state

In the secondary recovery of oil, the flow is in two-phase state if the reservoir pressure is above the bubble point pressure of the oil phase; however, the flow is of black oil type if the pressure drops below the bubble point pressure. In real reservoir engineering, the bubble point pressure is not a constant due to the frequent changes in injection and production in a reservoir. If all three phases coexist, the reservoir is referred as in the saturated state. When all gas dissolves into the oil phase, there is no gas phase present and the reservoir is said to be in the undersaturated state. The critical pressure at which the saturated state changes to the undersaturated state, or vice versa, is the bubble point pressure.

1.  1. For saturated state: *S*[*g*] ≠ 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si731.png) and *p*[*b*] = *p*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si732.png), the densities and viscosities depend only on pressure *p*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si733.png),
    
    $\\rho\_{Oo}(p) = \\frac{\\rho\_{Os}}{B\_{ob}(p)},\\rho\_{Go}(p) = \\frac{R\_{so}(p)\\rho\_{Gs}}{B\_{ob}(p)},\\rho\_{g}(p) = \\frac{\\rho\_{Gs}}{B\_{g}(p)}$ (2.264)
    
    ![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si734.png)(2.264) 
    
    *μ*[*o*] = *μ*[*o*](*p*), *μ*[*g*] = *μ*[*g*](*p*) (2.265)
    
    ![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si735.png)(2.265) 
    
2.  2. For undersaturated state: *S*[*g*] = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si736.png) and *p*[*b*] < *p*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si737.png), the densities and viscosities in the oil phase depend on both *p*![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si738.png) and *p*[*b*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si739.png),

$\\rho\_{Oo}(p,p\_{b}) = \\frac{\\rho\_{Os}}{B\_{ob}(p\_{b})}\\left\\lbrack {1 + c\_{o}(p - p\_{b})} \\right\\rbrack$ (2.266a)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si740.png)(2.266a) 

$\\rho\_{Go}(p,p\_{b}) = \\frac{R\_{so}(p\_{b})\\rho\_{Gs}}{B\_{ob}(p\_{b})}(1 + c\_{o}(p - p\_{b}))$ (2.266b)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si741.png)(2.266b) 

$\\rho\_{g}(p) = \\frac{\\rho\_{Gs}}{B\_{g}(p)}$ (2.266c)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si742.png)(2.266c) 

*μ*[*o*](*p*, *p*[*b*]) = *μ*[*o**b*](*p*[*b*])\[1+*c*[*μ*](*p*−*p*[*b*])\], *μ*[*g*] = *μ*[*g*](*p*) (2.267)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si743.png)(2.267)  

#### 2.8.5.2 Choice of the primary unknowns

The choice of primary unknowns depends on the states in the black oil model (1) In the saturated state, *p* = *p*[*o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si744.png), *S*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si745.png) and *S*[*o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si746.png) can be selected as the primary unknowns and (2) in the undersaturated state, *p* = *p*[*o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si747.png), *p*[*b*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si748.png) and *S*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si749.png) are always chosen as the primary unknowns. Consequently, the I.C.s are either,

*p*(**x**, 0) = *p*⁰(**x**), *S*[*w*](**x**, 0) = *S*[*w*]⁰(**x**), *S*[*o*](**x**, 0) = *S*[*o*]⁰(**x**), *x* ∈ *Ω* (2.268)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si750.png)(2.268) 

or

*p*(**x**, 0) = *p*⁰(**x**), *S*[*w*](**x**, 0) = *S*[*w*]⁰(**x**), *p*[*b*](**x**, 0) = *p*[*b*]⁰(**x**), *x* ∈ *Ω* (2.269)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si751.png)(2.269) 

depending on the initial state of a reservoir.

 

### 2.8.6 Treatment of initial conditions

The I.C.s for the black oil model include the specification of phase pressures and/or saturations for each grid block at the beginning of each simulation. Differences in phase gravities and capillary pressures cause fluids to segregate until the reservoir system reaches gravity/capillary equilibrium. There exist up to five different fluid zones vertically from the top of the reservoir to its bottom: gas cap, gas/oil transition, oil, oil/water transition, and water zone. In general, the specification of initial data depends on the gravity/capillary equilibrium and the nature of the fluids occupying different zones. For a continuous phase the initial pressure is directly calculated from a hydrostatic relation, while for a discontinuous phase, the initial pressure is determined from the capillary pressure function evaluated at the endpoint saturation. The initial saturation of a continuous phase is calculated from either the capillary pressure function or the saturation relation, and the initial saturation of a discontinuous phase is given at the endpoint saturation.

 

#### 2.8.6.1 Gas cap

Initially, only the gas phase is continuous in the gas cap zone. Therefore the vertical distribution of the gas pressure can be computed by

$\\frac{dp\_{g}}{dz} = \\gamma\_{g}$ (2.270)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si752.png)(2.270) 

with

*S*[*w*] = *S*[*i**w*], *S*[*g*] = 0 (2.271)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si753.png)(2.271) 

where *S*[*i**w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si754.png) is the irreducible water saturation. Other variables can be determined by *S*[*g*] = 1 − *S*[*w*] − *S*[*o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si755.png) and *p*[*w*] = *p*[*o*] − *p*[*c**o**w*](*S*[*i**w*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si756.png).

 

#### 2.8.6.2 Gas/oil transition

In the gas/oil transition zone, both the gas and oil phases are continuous, thus their vertical pressure distributions can be directly obtained from the hydrostatic relations,

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{dp\_{g}}{dz} = \\gamma\_{g},} & {\\frac{dp\_{o}}{dz} = \\gamma\_{o}} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.272)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si757.png)(2.272) 

In addition,

*S*[*w*] = *S*[*i**w*] (2.273)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si758.png)(2.273) 

From the abovementioned conditions, the following variables can be obtained,

*S*[*g*] = *p*[*c**g**o*]^( − 1)(*p*[*g*] − *p*[*o*]), *p*[*w*] = *p*[*o*] − *p*[*c**o**w*](*S*[*i**w*]), *S*[*o*] = 1 − *S*[*g*] − *S*[*w*] (2.274)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si759.png)(2.274) 

where we assume the *p*[*c**g**o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si760.png) has an inverse *p*[*c**g**o*]^( − 1)![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si761.png).

 

#### 2.8.6.3 Oil

The oil phase is the only continuous phase in the oil zone,

$\\frac{dp\_{o}}{dz} = \\gamma\_{o}$ (2.275)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si762.png)(2.275) 

with,

*S*[*w*] = *S*[*i**w*], *S*[*g*] = 0 (2.276)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si763.png)(2.276) 

Then we can get

*p*[*g*] = *p*[*o*] + *p*[*c**g**o*](0), *p*[*w*] = *p*[*o*] − *p*[*c**o**w*](*S*[*i**w*]), *S*[*o*] = 1 − *S*[*g*] − *S*[*w*] (2.277)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si764.png)(2.277)  

#### 2.8.6.4 Oil/water transition

Both the oil and water phases are continuous in the oil/water zone,

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{dp\_{o}}{dz} = \\gamma\_{o},} & {\\frac{dp\_{w}}{dz} = \\gamma\_{w}} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (2.278)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si765.png)(2.278) 

In addition,

*S*[*g*] = 0 (2.279)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si766.png)(2.279) 

From the conditions mentioned previously, it can be obtained that

*S*[*w*] = *p*[*c**o**w*]^( − 1)(*p*[*o*] − *p*[*w*]), *S*[*o*] = 1 − *S*[*g*] − *S*[*w*], *p*[*g*] = *p*[*o*] + *p*[*c**g**o*](0) (2.280)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si767.png)(2.280) 

where *p*[*c**o**w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si768.png) is assumed to be invertible.

 

#### 2.8.6.5 Water zone

In the water zone, only the water phase is continuous,

$\\frac{dp\_{w}}{dz} = \\gamma\_{w}$ (2.281)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si769.png)(2.281) 

We also have

*S*[*g*] = *S*[*o*] = 0 (2.282)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si770.png)(2.282) 

From these initial data we can get

*p*[*o*] = *p*[*w*] + *p*[*c**o**w*](*S*[*w*, max ]), *p*[*g*] = *p*[*o*] + *p*[*c**g**o*](0), *S*[*w*] = 1 (2.283)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si771.png)(2.283) 

where *S*[*w*, max ]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si772.png) is the maximum water saturation in the original water zone.

 

#### 2.8.6.6 Determination of initial conditions

In reservoir simulation the depths of the water/oil contact and the oil/gas contact are given. Then the initial pressure and saturation at all gridblocks can be uniquely determined if a reference pressure (e.g., datum pressure) and a reference depth (e.g., datum depth) are given. For an undersaturated reservoir, the reference depth and pressure are arbitrary and can be specified in any of the five fluid zones. For a saturated reservoir the reference depth must be the depth of the oil/gas contact, and the reference pressure must be the initial bubble point pressure.

When the hydrostatic conditions are adopted to obtain the initial pressure, the simulation model will initialize to equilibrium if the depths in the initialization part are the same as those in the reservoir layers. However, if a simulation model is not in an initial hydrostatic equilibrium, an initialization algorithm should be performed in several time steps (without source/sink terms) to allow the model to reach the equilibrium state. If capillary pressures (*p*[*c**o**w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si773.png) and/or *p*[*c**g**o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si774.png)) are ignored, the initial phase saturations must be imposed, but the pressures can be obtained from the reference pressure. In this situation, no transition zone is generally assumed to exist in the reservoir.

 

### 2.8.7 Solution techniques

 

#### 2.8.7.1 Simultaneous solution techniques

The simultaneous solution technique is the most natural solution method for the black oil model. It was originally proposed by 

 

#### 2.8.7.2 Sequential solution techniques

The basic idea of sequential solution technique (

 

#### 2.8.7.3 Iterative implicit pressure, explicit saturation solution techniques

The IMPES approach discussed for two-phase flow is also useful for the solution of black oil system. When IMPES is couple with a Newton–Raphson iteration, it is called iterative IMPES. In iterative IMPES the pressure equation is calculated implicitly and the other two (saturation and bubble point pressure) equations are evaluated explicitly. All saturation functions *k*[*r**w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si780.png), *k*[*r**o*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si781.png), *k*[*r**g*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si782.png), *p*[*c**w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si783.png), and *p*[*c**g*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000022si784.png) are evaluated at the saturation values of the previous time step in a Newton–Raphson iteration, and the fluid formation volume factors and viscosities in the transmissibilities, phase potentials, and well terms are calculated using the previous Newton–Raphson iteration values.

Chapter three 

# Recent progress in pore scale reservoir simulation

 

## Abstract

Flow and transport in geological formation are usually described within the continuum hypothesis at Darcy’s scale. However, Darcy’s continuum hypothesis lacks, in many cases, rigorous derivation from basic principles. This has been highlighted by the many ad hoc terms and coefficients that have been imposed into the governing equations to lump the complexities of pore-scale phenomena. Recently, there has been extensive research work trying to bridge the gap between scales and to calculate these terms based on pore-scale modeling. At a pore scale, there are a number of complexities that one needs to overcome in order to be able to carry out simulations. As an example, phenomena involving the movement of multiphase systems with either phase change and/or composition partition are among the problems that have not been very well comprehended. Currently, many oil companies, including Saudi Aramco and Schlumberger, are making great efforts in researching pore-scale flow and transport in geological formation. In this chapter, we will start from the key effort to construct the mathematical model governing the compositional multiphase flow to determine the phase compositions of the fluid mixture, and then calculate other related physical properties. Especially, capillary effect, which is often ignored in conventional reservoir phase equilibrium calculation, needs to be considered in shale and tight formations, as the nano-sized pores yield a large capillary pressure and confinement effect. Newly proposed thermodynamic consistent diffuse interface models and algorithms based on realistic equation of state will be generated to describe multicomponent multiphase flow and we will show the approach to consider partial miscibility common seen in carbon dioxide flooding.

 

### Keywords

Phase equilibria; NVT flash calculation; diffuse interface models; SAV method; partial miscibility
