## 3.1 Phase equilibria in subsurface reservoirs

 

### 3.1.1 Peng–Robinson equation of state

The Peng–Robinson (PR) equation was developed in 1976 at The University of Alberta by 

Typical forms of the PR-EOS are conclude as following equations, as a result of the long development history:

$p = \\frac{\\mathit{RT}}{v-b}-\\frac{a\\left( T \\right)}{v^{2} + 2\\mathit{bv}-b^{2}}.$ (3.1)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si34.png)(3.1) 

$p = \\frac{\\mathit{RT}}{v-b}-\\frac{a\\left( T \\right)}{v\\left( v + b \\right) + b\\left( v-b \\right)}.$ (3.2)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si35.png)(3.2) 

$\\left( p + \\frac{a\\left( T \\right)}{v\\left( v + b \\right) + b\\left( v-b \\right)} \\right)\\left( v-b \\right) = \\mathit{RT}.$ (3.3)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si36.png)(3.3) 

$\\left( p + \\frac{a\\left( T\_{c} \\right)\\alpha\\left( T\_{r},\\omega \\right)}{v\\left( v + b \\right) + b\\left( v-b \\right)} \\right)\\left( v-b \\right) = \\mathit{RT}.$ (3.4)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si37.png)(3.4) 

Parameters can be related to critical properties, in particular to *T*[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si38.png) and *P*[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si39.png)

$a\\left( T\_{c} \\right) = \\frac{0.45724R^{2}T\_{c}^{2}}{p\_{c}}\\mathit{;b} = b\\left( T\_{c} \\right) = \\frac{0.07780RT\_{c}}{p\_{c}}.$ (3.5)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si40.png)(3.5) 

The attraction parameter can be modeled by

*a*(*T*) = *a*(*T*[*c*])*α*(*T*[*r*],*ω*) = *a*(*T*[*c*])(1+*m*(1−*T*[*r*]^(0.5)))², (3.6)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si41.png)(3.6) 

where

*m* = 0.37464 + 1.54226*ω* − 0.26992*ω*², *f**o**r*0 < *ω* < 0.5

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si42.png)

*m* = 0.3796 + 1.485*ω* − 0.1644*ω*², *f**o**r*0.1 < *ω* < 2.0

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si43.png)

and the acentric factor *ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si44.png) is a conceptual number introduced by Kenneth Pitzer in 1955, proven to be very useful in the description of matter. It has become a standard for the phase characterization of single and pure components. The acentric factor is said to be a measure of the nonsphericity (centricity) of molecules. As it increases, the vapor curve is “pulled” down, resulting in higher boiling points. This factor can be calculated as

*ω* =  − log[10](*p*[*r*]^(*s**a**t*)) − 1, *a**t**T*[*r*] = 0.7, (3.7)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si45.png)(3.7) 

where *T*[*r*] = *T*/*T*[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si46.png) is the reduced temperature and *p*[*r*]^(*s**a**t*) = *p*^(*s**a**t*)/*p*[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si47.png) is the reduced saturation vapor pressure. For many monatomic fluids *p*[*r*]^(*s**a**t*)at*T*[*r*] = 0.7![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si48.png) is close to 0.1, which will result in *ω*→0![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si49.png). In fact, it has been found in many cases that *T*[*r*] = 0.7![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si50.png) lies above the boiling temperature of liquids at atmospheric pressure. Values of *ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si51.png) can be determined for any fluid from accurate experimental vapor pressure data. Preferably, these data should first be regressed against a reliable vapor pressure equation such as the following:

$\\ln\\left( p^{sat} \\right) = A + \\frac{B}{T} + C\\ln\\left( T \\right) + DT^{6}.$ (3.8)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si52.png)(3.8) 

If we assume a two-parameter vapor pressure equation such as ln(*p*^(*s**a**t*)) = *A* + (*B*/*T*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si53.png), we need only the normal boiling point together with critical properties to determine the acentric factor. If we assume log[10](*p*^(*s**a**t*)) = *A* + (*B*/*T*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si54.png), we can have log[10](*p*[*a**t**m*]) = *A* + (*B*/*T*[*b*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si55.png), log[10](*p*[*c*]) = *A* + (*B*/*T*[*c*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si56.png), and log[10](*p*^(*s**a**t*)|[*T*[*r*] = 0.7]) = *A* + (*B*/0.7*T*[*c*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si57.png). It yields that log[10](*p*[*c*]/*p*^(*s**a**t*)|[*T*[*r*] = 0.7]) = *B*/*T*[*c*](1−(1/0.7))![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si58.png) and log[10](*p*[*c*]/*p*[*a**t**m*]) = (*B*/*T*[*c*]) − (*B*/*T*[*b*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si59.png). Knowing the normal boiling point *T*[*b*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si60.png), we can solve for *B*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si61.png) as

$B = \\frac{T\_{b}T\_{c}}{T\_{b}-T\_{c}}\\log\_{10}\\left( \\frac{p\_{c}}{p\_{atm}} \\right).$ (3.9)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si62.png)(3.9) 

Noting that 1 atm=14.6959 psi, finally we can get a more common used equation for

$\\omega = -\\log\_{10}\\left( {p\_{r}^{sat}|}\_{T\_{r} = 0.7} \\right)-1 = \\log\_{10}\\left( \\frac{p\_{c}}{{p^{sat}|}\_{T\_{r} = 0.7}} \\right)-1 = \\frac{3}{7}\\left( \\frac{\\log\_{10}\\left( {p\_{c}/p\_{atm}} \\right)}{\\left( {\\mathit{Tc}/T\_{b}} \\right)-1} \\right)-1.$ (3.10)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si63.png)(3.10) 

A polynomial form is often used in the calculation of PR-EOS,

*Z*³ − (1−*B*)*Z*² + (*A*−2*B*−3*B*²) − (*A**B*−*B*²−*B*³) = 0, (3.11)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si64.png)(3.11) 

where

$A = \\frac{a\\left( T\_{c} \\right)\\alpha p}{R^{2}T^{2}},B = \\frac{\\mathit{bp}}{\\mathit{RT}}.$ (3.12)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si65.png)(3.12) 

For a single component fluid, the critical compressibility factor *Z*[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si66.png) can be obtained by setting the first- and second-order derivatives of pressure w.r.t. molar volume to be zero, and calculated as *Z*[*c*] = (*p*[*c*]*v*[*c*]/*R**T*[*c*]) = 0.307![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si67.png).

Using Clausius–Clapeyron equation, we can get

$\\frac{\\mathit{dp}}{\\mathit{dT}} = \\frac{p\\Delta h}{RT^{2}\\Delta Z} \\approx \\frac{p\\Delta h}{RT^{2}}.$ (3.13)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si68.png)(3.13) 

Assuming *Δ**h*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si69.png) remains the same with various temperature and pressure, we could have *d*ln *p* =  − (*Δ**h*/*R*)*d*(1/*T*),![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si70.png) which yields

$\\ln p\_{2}-\\ln p\_{1} = \\frac{\\Delta h}{R}\\left( {\\frac{1}{T\_{1}}-\\frac{1}{T\_{2}}} \\right).$ (3.14)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si71.png)(3.14)  

### 3.1.2 Redlich–Kwong and Soave–Redlich–Kwong equation of state

In 1949 the Redlich–Kwong (RK)-EOS was a considerable improvement at the time:

$p = \\frac{\\mathit{RT}}{v-b}-\\frac{a}{\\sqrt{T}v\\left( {v + b} \\right)\\prime}$ (3.15)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si72.png)(3.15) 

where

$a = \\frac{0.42748R^{2}{T\_{c}}^{5/2}}{p\_{c}}; b = \\frac{0.08664RT\_{c}}{p\_{c}}$ (3.16)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si73.png)(3.16) 

While superior to the van der Waals EOS, it performs poorly with respect to the liquid phase and thus cannot be used for accurately calculating vapor–liquid equilibria. However, it can be used in conjunction with separate liquid-phase correlations for vapor–liquid equilibria.

In 1972 Soave replaced the $a/\\sqrt{T}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si74.png) term of the RK equation with a function *α*(*T*,*ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si75.png):

$p = \\frac{\\mathit{RT}}{v-b}-\\frac{a\\left( T\_{c} \\right)\\alpha}{v\\left( v + b \\right)},$ (3.17)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si76.png)(3.17) 

where *a*(*T*[*c*]) = (0.427*R*²*T*[*c*]²/*P*[*c*])*;* *b* = (0.08664*R**T*[*c*]/*P*[*c*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si77.png); and *α* = (1+(0.48508+1.55171*ω*−0.15613*ω*²)(1−*T*[*r*]^(0.5)))².![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si78.png) When one compares the performance of PR-EOS and Soave–Redlich–Kwong (SRK)-EOS, they are pretty close to a tie, except for a slightly better behavior by PR-EOS at the critical point. A slightly better performance around critical conditions makes PR-EOS somewhat better suited to gas/condensate systems.

A general form has been proposed to describe the thermodynamic rules with the capability of modeling most of the popular EOS:

$p = \\frac{\\mathit{RT}}{v + \\delta\_{1}}-\\frac{a\\left( T \\right)}{\\left( v + \\delta\_{2} \\right)\\left( v + \\delta\_{3} \\right)}.$ (3.18)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si79.png)(3.18) 

The parameters can be chosen as in [Table 3.1](#chp003.xhtml#t0010) to recover each EOS.

Table 3.1

Parameters of different equation of state (EOS).

 
| EOS || *δ*[1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si2.png) || *δ*[2]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si3.png) || *δ*[3]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si4.png) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| van der Waals ||  − *b*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si5.png) || 0 || 0 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| SRK ||  − *b*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si6.png) || 0 || *b*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si7.png) |
| Peng–Robinson ||  − *b*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si8.png) || $\\left( {1 + \\sqrt{2}} \\right)b$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si9.png) || $\\left( {1-\\sqrt{2}} \\right)b$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si10.png) |
| SRK–Peneloux ||  − *b*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si11.png) || *c*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si12.png) || *b* + 2*c*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si13.png) |
| PR–Peneloux ||  − *b*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si14.png) || $c + \\left( {1 + \\sqrt{2}} \\right)\\left( {b + c} \\right)$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si15.png) || $c + \\left( {1-\\sqrt{2}} \\right)\\left( {b + c} \\right)$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si16.png) |
| Adachi–Lu–Sugie ||  − *b*[1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si17.png) ||  − *b*[2]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si18.png) || *b*[3]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si19.png) |

![Image](OEBPS/images/T000034tabt0010.png)

*PR*, Peng–Robinson; *SRK*, Soave–Redlich–Kwong.

 

### 3.1.3 Extension to mixture

For pure substance, *a* = *a*(*T*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si80.png) depends on the type of species and temperature, *b*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si81.png) depends on the type of species only. For mixture, it is easy to understand that these parameters depend on compositions as well. The mixing rule is written as

$a = \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}{\\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{M}{x\_{i}x\_{j}a\_{\\mathit{ij}}}}, b = \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}{x\_{i}b\_{i}},$ (3.18)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si82.png)(3.18) 

where

*a*[*i**j*] = (1−*k*[*i**j*])(*a*[*i*]*a*[*j*])^(1/2); *k*[*i**j*] = *k*[*j**i*]; *k*[*i**i*] = 0, (3.19)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si83.png)(3.19) 

and *k*[*i**j*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si84.png) is the interaction parameter between components *i*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si85.png) and *j*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si86.png). The binary interaction parameter is assumed to be independent of pressure and composition and generally independent of temperature.

The fugacity coefficient of component *i*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si87.png) in PR fluid mixture can be defined as

$\\ln\\varphi\_{i} = \\frac{b\_{i}}{b}\\left( Z-1 \\right)-\\ln\\left( Z-B \\right)-\\frac{A}{2\\sqrt{2}B}\\left( {\\frac{2\\sum\_{j = 1}^{M}{y\_{j}a\_{\\mathit{ij}}}}{a}-\\frac{b\_{i}}{b}} \\right)\\ln\\frac{Z + \\left( \\sqrt{2} + 1 \\right)B}{Z-\\left( \\sqrt{2}-1 \\right)B},$ (3.20)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si88.png)(3.20) 

where *A* = *a*(*T*)*p*/*R*²*T*², *B* = *b**p*/*R**T*.![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si89.png) This equation is very important in the thermodynamics of phase equilibrium. The condition of the equality of the fugacity of equilibrium provides the phase composition. To make the computations easier, a more general *V*\-integrating formula for fugacity coefficients is

$\\ln\\varphi\_{i} = \\int\_{V}^{\\infty}\\left( {\\frac{1}{\\mathit{RT}}\\left( \\frac{\\partial P}{\\partial N\_{i}} \\right)\_{T,V,\\mathbf{N}\_{\\neq \\mathbf{i}}}-\\frac{1}{V}} \\right)\\mathit{dV}-\\ln Z.$ (3.21)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si90.png)(3.21) 

Derive the following term and then make substitution:

$\\left( \\frac{\\partial P}{\\partial N\_{i}} \\right)\_{T,V,\\mathbf{N}\_{\\neq \\mathbf{i}}} = \\frac{\\mathit{RT}\\left( v-b + b\_{i} \\right)}{N\\left( v-b \\right)^{2}}-\\frac{2\\sum\_{j = 1}^{M}{y\_{j}a\_{\\mathit{ij}}-\\left( {{ab\_{i}}/b} \\right)}}{N\\left( v^{2} + 2\\mathit{bv}-b^{2} \\right)}-\\frac{ab\_{i}\\left( v^{2} + b^{2} \\right)}{\\mathit{Nb}\\left( v^{2} + 2\\mathit{bv}-b^{2} \\right)^{2}}.$ (3.22)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si91.png)(3.22) 

The parameter can be chosen on the basis of [Table 3.2](#chp003.xhtml#t0015) for various components common seen in reservoir fluids.

Table 3.2

Data table for parameters of common reservoir species.

 
| Component || *T*[*c*] (K) || *P*[*c*] (MPa) || *ρ*[*c*] (g/cm³) || *T*[*b*] (K) || *Z*[*c*] |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| H[2]O || 647.4 || 22.104 || 0.400 || 373.30 || 0.229 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| CO[2] || 304.4 || 7.398 || 0.461 || 194.80 || 0.274 |
| N[2] || 126.2 || 3.392 || 0.311 || 77.50 || 0.290 |
| C[1] || 190.58 || 4.604 || 0.162 || 111.63 || 0.288 |
| C[2] || 305.42 || 4.880 || 0.203 || 184.55 || 0.285 |
| C[3] || 369.82 || 4.250 || 0.217 || 231.05 || 0.281 |
| *n*C[4] || 425.18 || 3.797 || 0.228 || 272.64 || 0.274 |
| *n*C[5] || 469.7 || 3.369 || 0.230 || 309.22 || 0.271 |
| *n*C[6] || 507.3 || 3.014 || 0.233 || 341.88 || 0.264 |
| *n*C[7] || 540.1 || 2.734 || 0.233 || 371.57 || 0.262 |
| *n*C[8] || 568.7 || 2.495 || 0.232 || 398.82 || 0.260 |
| *n*C[9] || 594.6 || 2.280 || 0.231 || 423.97 || 0.256 |
| *n*C[10] || 617.7 || 2.099 || 0.228 || 447.30 || 0.255 |
| *n*C[11] || 638.8 || 1.948 || 0.227 || 469.08 || 0.253 |
| *n*C[12] || 658.4 || 1.810 || 0.226 || 489.47 || 0.249 |
| *n*C[13] || 675.9 || 1.679 || 0.224 || 508.62 || 0.246 |
| *n*C[14] || 692.3 || 1.573 || 0.222 || 526.73 || 0.244 |
| *n*C[15] || 707.8 || 1.479 || 0.220 || 543.84 || 0.242 |
| *n*C[16] || 722.6 || 1.401 || 0.219 || 560.01 || 0.241 |
| *n*C[17] || 735.6 || 1.342 || 0.218 || 575.17 || 0.242 |
| *n*C[18] || 774.2 || 1.292 || 0.214 || 589.50 || 0.247 |

![Image](OEBPS/images/T000034tabt0015.png)

The Helmholtz free energy for PR fluid mixture can be written as

*F*(*T*,*V*,**N**) = *F*[*i**d**e**a**l*](*T*,*V*,**N**) + *F*[*e**x**c**e**s**s*](*T*,*V*,**N**). (3.23)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si92.png)(3.23) 

The ideal gas contribution can be computed by (noting that $N = \\sum\\limits\_{i}N\_{i}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si93.png))

$F\_{ideal}\\left( T,V,\\mathbf{N} \\right) = \\mathit{RT}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}{N\_{i}\\ln\\left( \\frac{N\_{i}}{V} \\right)} + NC\_{intg}\\left( T \\right).$ (3.24)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si94.png)(3.24) 

The excess part of Helmholtz free energy can be computed by

$F\_{excess}\\left( T,V,\\mathbf{N} \\right) = \\frac{a\\left( T \\right)N}{2\\sqrt{2}b}\\ln{\\left( \\frac{V + \\left( 1-\\sqrt{2} \\right)\\mathit{bN}}{V + \\left( 1 + \\sqrt{2} \\right)\\mathit{bN}} \\right)}-\\mathit{NRT}\\ln\\left( {1-\\frac{\\mathit{Nb}}{V}} \\right).$ (3.25)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si95.png)(3.25) 

 

### 3.1.4 Volume-translation technique

In the PR and SRK equations, no parameter is adjusted for density. As a result, these two equations have a density-prediction deficiency. The SRK-EOS underestimates the liquid density of many substances; the PR-EOS overestimates the density to *ω* = 0.35![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si96.png), and then underestimates the density of *n*\-alkanes heavier than *n*C[8]. The deviation is nearly constant up to a reduced temperature of 0.75, which motivates the volume-translation technique.

The volume-translation technique is to translate along the volume axis

*v*^(*t**r**u**e*) = *v*^(*E**O**S*) + *c*, (3.26)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si97.png)(3.26) 

where *c*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si98.png) is the volume-translation parameter. The volume-translation technique separates vapor–liquid equilibriua from density calculations. Extension to multicomponent mixture by the mixing rule:

$c = \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}{x\_{i}c\_{i}.}$ (3.27)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si99.png)(3.27) 

The following additional correction was suggested in 

$v^{true} = v^{EOS} + c + f\_{c}\\left( \\frac{\\lambda}{\\lambda-\\left( {v^{2}/\\mathit{RT}} \\right)\\left( {{\\partial P}/{\\partial v}} \\right)\_{T}} \\right).$ (3.28)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si100.png)(3.28) 

The term (−*v*²/*R**T*)(∂*P*/∂*v*)[*T*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si101.png) is a dimensionless quantity related to the inverse of the compressibility. It is zero at critical point, while high at low temperature. This modified volume-translation technique forces the EOS to pass through *T*[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si102.png), *p*[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si103.png), and *v*[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si104.png). In PR-type fluid the value of *λ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si105.png) can be determined by regressing data for many substances and then a widely accepted result is 0.41.

The simple volume-translation expression *v*^(*t**r**u**e*) = *v*^(*E**O**S*) + *c*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si106.png), where $c = \\sum\_{i = 1}^{M}x\_{i}c\_{i}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si107.png), does not affect the prediction of phase compositions, while only the phase densities will be affected.

 

### 3.1.5 Solutions of Peng–Robinson equation of state

To solve the PR-EOS, there are currently two main approaches. The first one is to solve for *Z*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si108.png) in

*Z*³ − (1−*B*)*Z*² + (*A*−2*B*−3*B*²) − (*A**B*−*B*²−*B*³) = 0, (3.29)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si109.png)(3.29) 

and the other one is to solve for *v*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si110.png) in

$\\left( {p + \\frac{a\\left( T \\right)}{\\left( v-\\left( \\sqrt{2}-1 \\right)b \\right)\\left( v + \\left( \\sqrt{2} + 1 \\right)b \\right)}} \\right)\\left( v-b \\right) = \\mathit{RT}.$ (3.30)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si111.png)(3.30) 

Plot of *p*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si112.png) as a function of *v*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si113.png) shows three vertical asymptotes: *v* = *b*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si114.png), $v = \\left( \\sqrt{2}-1 \\right)b$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si115.png), and $v = -\\left( \\sqrt{2} + 1 \\right)b$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si116.png). The branches for which *v* < *b*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si117.png) have no physical meaning lead us to seek solutions for *v* > *b*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si118.png) only.

In algebra a cubic function (cubic polynomial) is a function of the form *f*(*x*) = *a**x*³ + *b**x*² + *c**x* + *d*,![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si119.png) in which *a*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si120.png) is nonzero. Setting *f*(*x*) = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si121.png) produces a cubic equation of the following scheme:

*a**x*³ + *b**x*² + *c**x* + *d* = 0. (3.31)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si122.png)(3.31) 

If all of the coefficients *a*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si123.png), *b*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si124.png), *c*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si125.png), and *d*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si126.png) of the cubic equation are real numbers, then it has at least one real root (due to the fundamental theorem of algebra). All of the roots of the cubic equation can be found algebraically (due to the Abel–Ruffini theorem). Abel’s impossibility theorem (also known as the Abel–Ruffini theorem) states that there is no algebraic solution—that is, solution in radicals—to the general polynomial equations of degree five or higher with arbitrary coefficients. A cubic formula is a closed-form solution for a cubic equation, similar to the quadratic formula for a quadratic equation. In elementary algebra the quadratic formula is the solution of the quadratic equation. For a general quadratic equation

*a**x*² + *b**x* + *c* = 0, (3.32)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si127.png)(3.32) 

the quadratic formula can be written as the following equation to satisfy the quadratic equation by inserting the former into the latter:

$x = \\frac{-b \\pm \\sqrt{b^{2}-4\\mathit{ac}}}{2a}.$ (3.33)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si128.png)(3.33)  

### 3.1.6 Phase split calculation

Two-phase (multiphase) splitting is a key problem in phase equilibrium calculation. Currently, there are two main approaches to do that: NPT flash calculation and NVT flash calculation. In NPT flash calculation the given data are the moles of feed *F*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si129.png), feed composition *z*[*i*], *i* = 1, …, *M*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si130.png), temperature *T*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si131.png), and pressure *p*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si132.png) and the target to determine are the moles of liquid and vapor phases *L*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si133.png) and *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si134.png), vapor-phase composition *y*[*i*], *i* = 1, …, *M*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si135.png), and liquid-phase composition *x*[*i*], *i* = 1, …, *M*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si136.png). In NVT flash calculation the given data are the moles of feed *F*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si137.png), feed composition *z*[*i*], *i* = 1, …, *M*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si138.png), temperature *T*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si139.png), and total volume *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si140.png) and the target to determine are the moles of liquid and vapor phases *L*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si141.png) and *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si142.png), vapor-phase composition *y*[*i*], *i* = 1, …, *M*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si143.png), and liquid-phase composition *x*[*i*], *i* = 1, …, *M*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si144.png).

The chemical or diffusive equilibrium can be represented by

*f*[*i*]^(*L*)(*T*,*p*,*x*[1],…,*x*[*M*]) = *f*[*i*]^(*V*)(*T*,*p*,*y*[1],…,*y*[*M*]), *i* = 1, …, *M*. (3.34)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si145.png)(3.34) 

which is the same as

*p**x*[*i*]*φ*[*i*]^(*L*)(*T*,*p*,*x*[1],*x*[2],…,*x*[*M*]) = *p**y*[*i*]*φ*[*i*]^(*V*)(*T*,*p*,*y*[1],*y*[2],…,*y*[*M*]), (3.35)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si146.png)(3.35) 

and also equivalent to

*y*[*i*] = *K*[*i*]*x*[*i*], *i* = 1, 2, …, *M*, (3.36)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si147.png)(3.36) 

where the vapor–liquid equilibrium ratio *K*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si148.png) can be written as

$K\_{i} = K\_{i}\\left( T,p,x\_{1},\\ldots,x\_{M},y\_{1},\\ldots,y\_{M} \\right) = \\frac{\\varphi\_{i}^{L}\\left( T,p,x\_{1},x\_{2},\\ldots,x\_{M} \\right)}{\\varphi\_{i}^{V}\\left( T,p,y\_{1},y\_{2},\\ldots,y\_{M} \\right)}.$ (3.37)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si149.png)(3.37) 

It is also known as the *K*\-vale, or the phase equilibrium constant, or the distribution coefficient and the partition coefficient. Recall the fugacity equation for vapor and liquid phase:

$\\ln\\varphi\_{i}^{L} = \\frac{b\_{i}^{L}}{b^{L}}\\left( Z^{L}-1 \\right)-\\ln\\left( Z^{L}-B^{L} \\right)-\\frac{A^{L}}{2\\sqrt{2}B^{L}}\\left( {\\frac{2\\sum\_{j = 1}^{M}{x\_{j}a\_{\\mathit{ij}}^{L}}}{a^{L}}-\\frac{b\_{i}^{L}}{b^{L}}} \\right)\\ln\\frac{Z^{L} + 2.414B^{L}}{Z^{L}-0.414B^{L}},$ (3.38)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si150.png)(3.38) 

$\\ln\\varphi\_{i}^{V} = \\frac{b\_{i}^{V}}{b^{V}}\\left( Z^{V}-1 \\right)-\\ln\\left( Z^{V}-B^{V} \\right)-\\frac{A^{V}}{2\\sqrt{2}B^{V}}\\left( {\\frac{2\\sum\_{j = 1}^{M}{y\_{j}a\_{\\mathit{ij}}^{V}}}{a^{V}}-\\frac{b\_{i}^{V}}{b^{V}}} \\right)\\ln\\frac{Z^{V} + 2.414B^{V}}{Z^{V}-0.414B^{V}}.$ (3.39)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si151.png)(3.39) 

To compute *x*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si152.png) and *y*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si153.png), the following correlation is often used:

$x\_{i} = \\frac{z\_{i}}{1 + \\beta\\left( K\_{i}-1 \\right)}, y\_{i} = \\frac{K\_{i}z\_{i}}{1 + \\beta\\left( K\_{i}-1 \\right)}.$ (3.40)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si154.png)(3.40) 

and the Rachford–Rice (RR) equation is applied to calculate the *β*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si155.png):

$\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}\\frac{\\left( K\_{i}-1 \\right)z\_{i}}{1 + \\beta\\left( K\_{i}-1 \\right)} = 0.$ (3.41)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si156.png)(3.41) 

Total number of equations can be concluded as 3*M* + 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si157.png) that matches the total 3*M* + 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si158.png) unkowns: *x*[*i*], *y*[*i*], *K*[*i*], *i* = 1, 2, …, *M*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si159.png) and *β*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si160.png).

Successive substitution method (SSM), also known as successive substitution iteration, is a popular approach for the NPT-type flash calculation. The iterative scheme can be modeled as

Step 1. An initial guess of the *K*\-values near the equilibrium solution to speed up the convergence procedure. Often the Wilson correlation (

$K\_{i}^{Wilson} = \\frac{p\_{c,i}}{p}\\exp\\left( 5.37\\left( 1 + \\omega\_{i} \\right)\\left( 1-\\frac{T\_{c,i}}{T} \\right) \\right).$ (3.42)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si161.png)(3.42) 

Step 2. Calculate *β*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si162.png) with the RR procedure (i.e., by solving the RR equation). Once the *K*\-values for each component are specified, the RR equation is used to estimate the phase mole fractions. The RR equation can be solved by a simple Newton–Raphson iteration, or the false position method, or the bisection method (note that the RR function is monotonic).

Step 3. Calculate the cubic EOS parameters (e.g., *a*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si163.png) and *b*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si164.png)). The critical temperatures, pressures, and acentric factors for each component are needed to calculate the EOS parameters.

Step 4. Solve the cubic EOS for the phase molar volumes *v*^(*L*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si165.png) and *v*^(*V*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si166.png). This step requires solution of the cubic EOS for the compressibility factor of the vapor and liquid. Because the compositions of the vapor and liquid are different, two separate solutions for the roots of the cubic EOS are required. A cubic equation-solver or iteration method should be used to obtain the roots of the EOS. The procedure for this step is more complex than for a pure fluid because six roots of the cubic EOS are calculated (i.e., three roots for the liquid and three for the vapor). Among the six roots (three for vapor; three for liquid), the middle roots for vapor/liquid are discarded because they lead to unstable phases, similar to pure fluids. One of the remaining two liquid roots is paired with one of the other vapor roots to calculate component fugacities and equilibrium. If the wrong root pairing is selected, the solution could be false in that an unstable or metastable solution could be obtained. The correct equilibrium solution is the one that minimizes the total Gibbs energy compared with the other possible root pairings. For most cases the correct root for liquid is the one that gives the smallest molar volume, and the correct root for vapor is the one that gives the largest molar volume.

Step 5. Calculate the component fugacities of each component in each phase *f*[*i*]^(*V*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si167.png) and *f*[*i*]^(*L*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si168.png).

Step 6. Check to see if equilibrium has been reached. A good criterion is |(*f*[*i*]^(*V*)/*f*[*i*]^(*L*)) − 1| < 10^( − 5)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si169.png) for all components. If the criteria are satisfied, equilibrium has been obtained.

Step 7. If the criteria have not been satisfied, the *K*\-values should be updated and steps 2–6 repeated. This step is also very important; it affects both the rate of convergence and whether the iteration converges at all. One procedure that works well is the simple successive substitution scheme that relies on *φ*[*i*]^(*V*) = (*f*[*i*]^(*V*)/*y*[*i*]*p*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si170.png) and *φ*[*i*]^(*L*) = (*f*[*i*]^(*L*)/*x*[*i*]*p*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si171.png). We update the *K*\-values by *K*[*i*]^(*n**e**w*) = (*f*[*i*]^(*L*)/*f*[*i*]^(*V*))*K*[*i*]^(*o**l**d*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si172.png). Once the new *K*\-values are determined, steps 2–6 are repeated until convergence in step 6 is achieved. Convergence from successive substitutions can be slow near the critical region. Other methods may be required when convergence is slow.

Except for the previously stated criterion in Step 6, other commonly used criteria include the following:

$\\frac{1}{M}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}\\left( {\\ln\\left( \\frac{f\_{i}^{L}}{f\_{i}^{V}} \\right)} \\right)^{2} < 10^{-12}.$ (3.43)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si173.png)(3.43) 

$\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}\\left( {\\frac{f\_{i}^{L}}{f\_{i}^{V}}-1} \\right)^{2} < 10^{-14}.$ (3.44)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si174.png)(3.44) 

When the system is close to the critical point and fugacities are strongly composition-dependent, a slowing-down of the convergence rate of the SSM is to be expected. In an attempt to avoid slow convergence problems, some methods have been proposed. Among the most popular are the Minimum Variable Newton Raphson method and the accelerated and stabilized SSM (ASSM). Such procedure is implemented to accelerate the calculation of *K*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si175.png)\-values, especially in the region close to critical point where the use of the SSM alone will not be efficient.

The classical ASSM technique consists of the following steps:

Step 1. Use the SSM technique to initiate the updating of the *K*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si176.png)\-values at the first time point.

Step 2. Check all following criteria at every step during iterations using the SSM: $\\left( {{\\sum\\limits\_{i}{R\_{i}^{new}-1}^{2}}/{\\sum\\limits\_{i}{R\_{i}^{old}-1}^{2}}} \\right) > 0.8,|\\beta^{new}-\\beta^{old}| < 0.1,10^{-5} < \\sum\\limits\_{i}\\left( R\_{i}^{new}-1 \\right)^{2} < 10^{-3},$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si177.png) and 0 < *β*^(*n**e**w*) < 1.![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si178.png) These criteria show that you have sufficient proximity to the conditions to ensure the efficiency of the method. *R*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si179.png) is the ratio of liquid fugacity to gas fugacity of the *i*th component.

Step 3. If the system satisfies *all* the previous criteria, the iteration technique is then switched from the SSM to the ASSM. Otherwise, SSM is used for the update of the *K*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si180.png)\-values. The following expressions are used to update *K*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si181.png)\-values in ASSM:

*K*[*i*]^(*n**e**w*) = *K*[*i*]^(*o**l**d*)*R*[*i*]^(*λ*[*i*]), (3.45)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si182.png)(3.45) 

where *λ*[*i*] = (*R*[*i*]^(*o**l**d*) − 1/*R*[*i*]^(*o**l**d*) − *R*[*i*]^(*n**e**w*)).![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si183.png) In some cases, using a constant acceleration value of *λ*[*i*] = 2![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si184.png) is good enough for the accuracy and convergence requirement.

Step 4. Once all the criteria in step 2 are satisfied, skip step 2 for the subsequent iterations and use the ASSM technique to update *K*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si185.png)\-values until convergence is attained, unless it does not give acceptable new estimates.

Step 5. When ASSM is used, it must always be tested to show that it leads to an improved solution (i.e., that it brings fugacity ratios closer to unity). If not, it must be rejected and switched back to SSM.

A checklist of data needed for an NPT-type flash calculation is provided in [Table 3.3](#chp003.xhtml#t0020) for readers’ reference.

Table 3.3

Data checklist for Peng–Robinson equation of state–based NPT flash calculation.

 
| Data || Notation |
| --- | --- |
| Number of species || *M* |
| --- | --- |
| Name of each species || Methane, … |
| Molecular weights || *W* |
| Critical properties || *T*[*c*], *p*[*c*], *v*[*c*], *Z*[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si20.png) |
| Normal boiling temperature || *T*[*b*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si21.png) |
| Acentric factor || *ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si22.png) |
| Binary interaction coefficients || *k*[*i**j*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si23.png) |

![Image](OEBPS/images/T000034tabt0020.png)

 

### 3.1.7 A successive substitution iteration example

We set the number of components *M* = 2![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si186.png), species={C[1], *n*C[10]}, *z*[1] = 0.4, *z*[2] = 0.6.![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si187.png) The subscript 1 is defined for C[1] and 2 is defined for *n*C[10]. The temperature is set as *T* = 344.26*K*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si188.png) and the pressure is set as *p* = 10![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si189.png) MPa. Parameters of the components can be selected from [Table 3.4](#chp003.xhtml#t0025).

Table 3.4

Parameters of components.

 
| Component || *T*[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si24.png) (K) || *T*[*b*](*K*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si25.png) || *p*[*c*](*M**P**a*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si26.png)) || Molecular weight |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| C[1] || 190.58 || 111.63 || 4.604 || 16 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| *n*C[10] || 617.7 || 447.3 || 2.099 || 142 |

![Image](OEBPS/images/T000034tabt0025.png)

Following the procedure in [Section 3.1.6](#chp003.xhtml#s0040), the flash calculation can be performed:

*Iteration no. 1*

Step 1. Let us calculate *ω*[1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si190.png) and *ω*[2]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si191.png) first: *ω*[1] = (3/7)(log[10](4.604/0.101325)/(190.58/111.63−1)) − 1 = 0.004348,![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si192.png) and similarly we can get *ω*[2] = 0.489![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si193.png). Wilson’s correlation gives *K*[1] = 5.1921![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si194.png) and *K*[2] = 0.000368![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si195.png).

Step 2. Solving the RR equation, we obtain *β* = 0.25703![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si196.png).

Step 3. Compute *x*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si197.png) and *y*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si198.png): *x*[1] = (0.4/(1+(5.1921−1)0.25703)) = 0.1925![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si199.png), *x*[2] = 0.8075![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si200.png) and *y*[1] = *K*[1]*x*[1] = 0.999677![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si201.png), *y*[2] = *K*[2]*x*[2] = 0.000297![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si202.png).

Step 4. Calculate *φ*[*i*]^(*L*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si203.png) and *φ*[*i*]^(*G*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si204.png), and then update *K*[1](*T*,*p*,**x**,**y**) = (*φ*[1]^(*L*)/*φ*[1]^(*V*)) = 3.2889,![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si205.png) *K*[2](*T*,*p*,**x**,**y**) = (*φ*[2]^(*L*)/*φ*[2]^(*V*)) = 0.00407.![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si206.png)

*Iteration no. 2*

Step 1. Solving the RR equation, we obtain *β* = 0.13948![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si207.png).

Step 2. Compute *x*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si208.png) and *y*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si209.png) to get: (*x*[1],*x*[2]) = (0.3032,0.6968)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si210.png), (*y*[1],*y*[2]) = (0.99719,0.00284)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si211.png).

Step 3. Update the *K*\-values: (*K*[1],*K*[2]) = (3.1371,0.00431)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si212.png).

*Iteration no. 3*

Step 1. Solving the RR equation, we obtain *β* = 0.12099![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si213.png).

Step 2. Compute *x*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si214.png) and *y*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si215.png) to get: (*x*[1],*x*[2]) = (0.3178,0.6822), (*y*[1],*y*[2])=(0.99704,0.00294)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si216.png).

Step 3. Update the *K*\-values: (*K*[1],*K*[2]) = (3.1146,0.00433)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si217.png).

*Iteration no. 4*

Step 1. Solving the RR equation, we obtain *β* = 0.11800![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si218.png).

Step 2. Compute *x*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si219.png) and *y*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si220.png) to get: (*x*[1],*x*[2]) = (0.3201,0.6799), (*y*[1],*y*[2]) = (0.99706,0.00294)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si221.png).

Step 3. Update the *K*\-values: (*K*[1],*K*[2]) = (3.111,0.00433)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si222.png).

*Iteration No. 5*

Step 1. Solving the RR equation, we obtain *β* = 0.11751![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si223.png).

Step 2. Compute *x*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si224.png) and *y*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si225.png) to get: (*x*[1],*x*[2]) = (0.3205,0.6795), (*y*[1],*y*[2])=(0.99707,0.00295)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si226.png).

Step 3. Update the *K*\-values: (*K*[1],*K*[2]) = (3.11041,0.00433)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si227.png).

*Iteration no. 6*

Step 1. Solving the RR equation, we obtain *β* = 0.11745![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si228.png).

Step 2. Compute *x*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si229.png) and *y*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si230.png) to get: (*x*[1],*x*[2]) = (0.3205,0.6795), (*y*[1],*y*[2]) = (0.99704,0.00295)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si231.png).

Step 3. Update the *K*\-values: (*K*[1],*K*[2]) = (3.11034,0.00433)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si232.png).

*Iteration no. 7*

Step 1. Solving the RR equation, we obtain *β* = 0.11742![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si233.png).

Step 2. Compute *x*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si234.png) and *y*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si235.png) to get: (*x*[1],*x*[2]) = (0.3206,0.6794), (*y*[1],*y*[2]) = (0.99707,0.00295)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si236.png).

Step 3. Update the *K*\-values: (*K*[1],*K*[2]) = (3.11030,0.00433)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si237.png).

*Iteration no. 8*

Step 1. Solving the RR equation, we obtain *β* = 0.11742![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si238.png).

Step 2. Compute *x*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si239.png) and *y*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si240.png) to get: (*x*[1],*x*[2]) = (0.3206,0.6794), (*y*[1],*y*[2]) = (0.99707,0.00295)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si241.png).

Step 3. Update the *K*\-values: (*K*[1],*K*[2]) = (3.11030,0.00433)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si242.png).

As the results of Iterations 7 and 8 meet well, we can consider the system has converged. Molar volume of each phase can be then calculated as *V*^(oil) = 1.6636 × 10^( − 4)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si243.png) and *V*^(gas) = 2.5769 × 10^( − 4)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si244.png). Molar density of each phase

$c^{\\text{oil}} = \\frac{N^{\\text{oil}}}{V^{\\text{oil}}} = \\frac{1}{1.6636 \\times 10^{-4}} = 6011.06\\text{mol}/\\text{m}^{3}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si245.png) 

$c^{\\text{gas}} = \\frac{N^{\\text{gas}}}{V^{\\text{gas}}} = \\frac{1}{2.5769 \\times 10^{-4}} = 3880.63\\text{mol}/\\text{m}^{3}.$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si246.png)

The overall molar volume of the mixture is

 

*v* = 1.6636 × 10^( − 4) × 0.8826 + 2.5769 × 10^( − 4) × 0.1174 = 1.7708 × 10^( − 4)m³/mol.

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si247.png)

The final saturations of each phase can be calculated as

 

$S^{oil} = \\frac{V^{\\text{oil}}}{V^{\\text{oil}} + V^{\\text{gas}}} = \\frac{1.6636 \\times 10^{-4}}{1.6636 \\times 10^{-4} + 2.5769 \\times 10^{-4}} = 0.3923,$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si248.png) 

$S^{gas} = \\frac{V^{\\text{gas}}}{V^{\\text{oil}} + V^{\\text{gas}}} = \\frac{2.5769 \\times 10^{-4}}{1.6636 \\times 10^{-4} + 2.5769 \\times 10^{-4}} = 0.6077.$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si249.png)

Here, another example will be provided for the readers’ exercise with only the final result. They are expected to complete the whole procedure. ## 3.2 Stable dynamic NVT algorithm with capillarity

 

### 3.2.1 Thermodynamic preparation

Recall the total Helmholtz free energy, denoted by *F*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si254.png), expressed as

*F* = *f*(**n**^(*G*))*V*^(*G*) + *f*(**n**^(*L*))*V*^(*L*), (3.46)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si255.png)(3.46) 

where **n**^(*G*) = **N**^(*G*)/*V*^(*G*), **n**^(*L*) = **N**^(*L*)/*V*^(*L*).![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si256.png) The total entropy *S*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si257.png) is a summation of two contributions: *S* = *S*[sys] + *S*[env]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si258.png), where *S*[sys]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si259.png) is the entropy of the system, and *S*[env]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si260.png) is the entropy of the environment. The environmental entropy can be calculated by

$\\begin{matrix} {dS\_{\\text{env}} = -\\frac{d\\text{\\\_}Q}{T},} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.47)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si261.png)(3.47) 

where *Q*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si262.png) is the heat transfer defined from the environment to the system in order to keep the temperature in the system.

The internal energy of the mixture system can be defined as *U*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si263.png). A common treatment in phase equilibria problems is to see the interface between the two phases as a sharp interface, which means that no width is considered upon the interphase. The total internal energy is always treated as the summation of internal energies of the two phases occurring in the mixture. It can be stated from the first law of thermodynamics as

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{dU}{dt} = \\frac{d\\text{\\\_}Q}{dt} + \\frac{dW}{dt}.} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.48)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si264.png)(3.48) 

The correlation between the total internal energy *U*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si265.png) and the total Helmholtz free energy can be expressed as

*U* = *F* + *T**S*[sys] (3.49)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si266.png)(3.49) 

Substituting [Eq. (3.49)](#chp003.xhtml#eqn49) into [Eq. (3.48)](#chp003.xhtml#eqn48), we can get

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{d\\text{\\\_}Q}{dt} = \\frac{dF}{dt} + T\\frac{dS\_{\\text{sys}}}{dt} + p\_{c}\\frac{dV^{G}}{dt}.} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.50)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si267.png)(3.50) 

The entropy change respecting to time can be then derived as

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{dS}{dt} = \\frac{dS\_{\\text{sys}}}{dt} + \\frac{dS\_{\\text{env}}}{dt}} \\\\ {= \\frac{dS\_{\\text{sys}}}{dt}-\\frac{1}{T}\\frac{d\\text{\\\_}Q}{dt}} \\\\ {= -\\frac{1}{T}\\frac{dF}{dt}-\\frac{p\_{c}}{T}\\frac{dV^{G}}{dt}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.51)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si268.png)(3.51) 

The partial derivatives of Helmholtz free energy can be calculated as

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{\\partial F\\left( \\mathbf{N}^{G},V^{G} \\right)}{\\partial N\_{i}^{G}} = \\mu\_{i}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{G} \\right)-\\mu\_{i}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{L} \\right),} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.52)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si269.png)(3.52) 

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{\\partial F\\left( \\mathbf{N}^{G},V^{G} \\right)}{\\partial V^{G}} = p\_{L}-p\_{G},} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.53)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si270.png)(3.53) 

where *μ*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si271.png) is the chemical potential of component *i*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si272.png), and *p*[*L*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si273.png) and *p*[*G*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si274.png) represent, respectively, the gas and liquid pressures. It should be noted that if no capillary pressure is accounted, these two pressures can be viewed as the same. Using the chain rule, we can get

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{dF}{dt} = \\frac{\\partial F}{\\partial V^{G}}\\frac{\\partial V^{G}}{\\partial t} + \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}{\\frac{\\partial F}{\\partial N\_{i}^{G}}\\frac{\\partial N\_{i}^{G}}{\\partial t}}} \\\\ {= \\left( {p\_{L}-p\_{G}} \\right)\\frac{\\partial V^{G}}{\\partial t} + \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}{\\left( {\\mu\_{i}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{G} \\right)-\\mu\_{i}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{L} \\right)} \\right)\\frac{\\partial N\_{i}^{G}}{\\partial t}}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.54)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si275.png)(3.54) 

With this expression of time derivative of Helmholtz free energy, the change of entropy over time can be calculated as

$\\begin{matrix} \\frac{dS}{dt} & = & {\\frac{1}{T}\\left( {p\_{G}-p\_{L}-p\_{c}} \\right)\\frac{\\partial V^{G}}{\\partial t} + \\frac{1}{T}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}{\\left( {\\mu\_{i}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{L} \\right)-\\mu\_{i}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{G} \\right)} \\right)\\frac{\\partial N\_{i}^{G}}{\\partial t}}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.55)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si276.png)(3.55) 

According to Onsager’s reciprocal principle, a symmetrical matrix **Ψ** = (*ψ*[*i*, *j*])[*i*, *j* = 1]^(*M* + 1)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si277.png) can be introduced and the time derivative can be expressed as

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{\\partial N\_{i}^{G}}{\\partial t} = \\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{M}{\\psi\_{i,j}\\left( {\\mu\_{j}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{L} \\right)-\\mu\_{j}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{G} \\right)} \\right) + \\psi\_{i,M + 1}\\left( {p\_{G}-p\_{L}-p\_{c}} \\right)}, 1 \\leq i \\leq M} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.56)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si278.png)(3.56) 

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{\\partial V^{G}}{\\partial t} = \\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{M}{\\psi\_{M + 1,j}\\left( {\\mu\_{j}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{L} \\right)-\\mu\_{j}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{G} \\right)} \\right) + \\psi\_{M + 1,M + 1}\\left( {p\_{G}-p\_{L}-p\_{c}} \\right)}} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.57)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si279.png)(3.57) 

Based on the second law of thermodynamics, the total entropy shall not decrease with time, which yields that the matrix **Ψ**![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si280.png) must be positive definite. A diagonal positive definite matrix is often chosen to meet this requirement as

$\\begin{matrix} {\\psi\_{i,i} = \\frac{D\_{i}N\_{i}^{t}}{\\mathit{RT}},i = 1,\\ldots,M,\\psi\_{M + 1,M + 1} = \\frac{C\_{V}^{G}C\_{V}^{L}V^{t}}{C\_{V}^{L}p\_{G} + C\_{V}^{G}p\_{L}},} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.58)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si281.png)(3.58) 

and the diffusion coefficient of component *i*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si282.png) is noted as *D*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si283.png) and nonzero restrictions are needed for *C*[*V*]^(*G*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si284.png) and *C*[*V*]^(*L*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si285.png). An obvious difference between NVT- and NPT-type flash calculation is that the pressure in NVT system can be negative so that this nonzero restrictions can ensure that *ψ*[*M* + 1, *M* + 1] > 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si286.png). The evolutionary formula for the moles and volume can be expressed as

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{\\partial N\_{i}^{G}}{\\partial t} = \\frac{D\_{i}N\_{i}^{t}}{RT}\\left( {\\mu\_{i}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{L} \\right)-\\mu\_{i}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{G} \\right)} \\right),\\quad i = 1,\\ldots,M.} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.59)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si287.png)(3.59) 

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{\\partial V^{G}}{\\partial t} = \\frac{C\_{V}^{G}C\_{V}^{L}V^{t}}{C\_{V}^{L}p\_{G} + C\_{V}^{G}p\_{L}}\\left( {p\_{G}-p\_{L}-p\_{c}} \\right).} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.60)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si288.png)(3.60)  

### 3.2.2 Capillarity effect

Capillary pressure, denoted by *p*[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si289.png), can be defined as the difference between the liquid and vapor phase, which is caused due to the capillarity effect. Namely, the capillary pressure can be expressed as

$\\begin{matrix} {p\_{c} = p\_{G}-p\_{L}.} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.61)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si290.png)(3.61) 

Capillary pressure can be calculated by the Young–Laplace equation and Weinaug–Katz correlation or sometimes simply represented by a constant. At the pore scale the capillary pressure can be formulated by Young–Laplace equation:

$\\begin{matrix} {p\_{c} = \\frac{2\\sigma\\cos\\theta}{r},} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.62)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si291.png)(3.62) 

where *σ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si292.png) stands for the interfacial tension, *θ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si293.png) is the contact angle, and *r*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si294.png) represents the pore radius. The contact angle is affected by the wettability. The interfacial tension *σ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si295.png) can be provided by experimental data or calculated by a semiempirical relation. In order to apply the laws of thermodynamics, we need to describe the work done by the capillary pressure. For the pore scale, we consider a closed cylinder container with the fixed volume *V*^(*t*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si296.png) under a constant temperature. We assume that this container is fully filled by a mixture with the overall moles, and this mixture may be split into the gas and liquid phases with a sharp interface, which occupy the volumes *V*^(*G*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si297.png) and *V*^(*L*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si298.png), respectively, satisfying *V*^(*G*) + *V*^(*L*) = *V*^(*t*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si299.png). Correspondingly, the moles in the gas and liquid cells are denoted by *N*^(*G*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si300.png) and *N*^(*L*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si301.png), respectively. We use *W*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si302.png) to represent the total work done by the capillary pressure. When considering the work done by capillary pressure, we assume that for a specified time, the molar density is invariable but volume may change at this time. In this case, *W*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si303.png) has the following form:

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{dW}{dt} = -\\int\_{I}{p\_{c}\\mathbf{u} \\cdot \\mathbf{\\nu}dI},} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.63)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si304.png)(3.63) 

where *t*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si305.png) is the time, *I*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si306.png) stands for the contact region between two phases, **u**![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si307.png) is the velocity on the contact surface, and **ν**![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si308.png) is a normal unit outward vector to *I*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si309.png). For any given time, we assume that *p*[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si310.png) has a unique value on the interface, and **u**![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si311.png) is a constant vector in space, being parallel along cylinder toward from liquid to gas. The divergence theorem gives

$\\begin{matrix} {\\int\_{I}{\\mathbf{u} \\cdot \\mathbf{\\nu}dI}-\\int\_{A}{\\mathbf{u} \\cdot \\mathbf{\\nu}dA} = 0,} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.64)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si312.png)(3.64) 

where *A*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si313.png) is the cross-sectional area of this cylinder container. Consequently, assuming *p*[*c*] is spatially constant, we derive

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{dW}{dt} = -p\_{c}A\\mathbf{u} \\cdot \\mathbf{\\nu} = -p\_{c}\\frac{dV^{G}}{dt}.} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.65)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si314.png)(3.65) 

For the scale of porous media, we assume that the contact surface is flat, so that the previous equation is still suitable.

 

### 3.2.3 Thermodynamic stable numerical method

The system composed in [Section 3.2.1](#chp003.xhtml#s0055) describes the dynamic process from an initial state, which is nonequilibrium state to an equilibrium state in the presence of capillarity effect at the fixed moles, volume, and temperature. For NPT-based phase equilibrium model, only temperature or pressure can be specified for a pure substance, while both are usually required to be specified for multicomponent mixtures. The specified thermodynamical variables are unified (always *N*, *V*, and *T*) for both a pure substance and a multicomponent mixture.

[Eq. (3.55)](#chp003.xhtml#eqn55) can be written in another form with the capillarity effect involved as follows:

$\\begin{matrix} \\frac{dS}{dt} & = & {\\frac{1}{T}\\frac{C\_{V}^{G}C\_{V}^{L}V^{t}}{C\_{V}^{L}p\_{G} + C\_{V}^{G}p\_{L}}\\left( {p\_{G}-p\_{L}-p\_{c}} \\right)^{2} + \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}{\\frac{D\_{i}N\_{i}^{t}}{RT^{2}}\\left( {\\mu\_{i}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{L} \\right)-\\mu\_{i}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{G} \\right)} \\right)^{2}}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.66)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si315.png)(3.66) 

At the equilibrium state the entropy attains the maximum value and the state does not change, that is, *d**S*/*d**t*, ∂*N*[*i*]^(*G*)/∂*t*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si316.png), and ∂*V*^(*G*)/∂*t*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si317.png) are all equal to zero. For both multicomponent mixture and pure substance, the equilibrium state can be expressed by

$\\begin{matrix} {\\mu\_{i}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{L} \\right)-\\mu\_{i}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{G} \\right) = 0,} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.67)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si318.png)(3.67) 

$\\begin{matrix} {p\_{G}-p\_{L}-p\_{c} = 0,} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.68)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si319.png)(3.68) 

Entropy stability implies that the total Helmholtz free energy is dissipated with time. It is well-known that both of fully explicit and implicit Euler’s method fail to preserve the energy dissipation unless the time step size is restricted to be sufficiently small. So a semiimplicit scheme is a preferable choice to preserve the entropy stability and admit reasonable large time steps. The effectiveness of convex–concave splitting techniques has been demonstrated in numerical simulation of the realistic fluids. For the pure substance, it can be easily proved that the Helmholtz free energy density can be split into convex and concave parts. For the multicomponent mixture, we use the additional ideal term to construct a strict convex–concave splitting (

$\\begin{matrix} {f\\left( \\mathbf{n} \\right) = f^{\\text{convex}}\\left( \\mathbf{n} \\right) + f^{\\text{concave}}\\left( \\mathbf{n} \\right),} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.69)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si322.png)(3.69) 

$\\begin{matrix} {\\left. f^{\\text{convex}}(\\mathbf{n} \\right) = \\left( {1 + \\lambda} \\right)f^{\\text{ideal}}\\left( \\mathbf{n}) + f^{\\text{repulsion}}\\left( \\mathbf{n}), \\right. \\right.} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.70)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si323.png)(3.70) 

$\\begin{matrix} {f^{\\text{concave}}\\left( \\mathbf{n} \\right) = f^{\\text{attraction}}\\left( \\mathbf{n} \\right)-\\lambda f^{\\text{ideal}}\\left( \\mathbf{n} \\right),} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.71)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si324.png)(3.71) 

where *λ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si325.png) is a nonzero coefficient. Correspondingly, the chemical potential can be expressed as the sum of two parts as

$\\begin{matrix} {\\mu\_{i}\\left( \\mathbf{n} \\right) = \\mu\_{i}^{\\text{convex}}\\left( \\mathbf{n} \\right) + \\mu\_{i}^{\\text{concave}}\\left( \\mathbf{n} \\right),} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.72)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si326.png)(3.72) 

where $\\begin{matrix} {\\mu\_{i}^{\\text{convex}} = \\left( {{\\partial f^{\\text{convex}}}/{\\partial n\_{i}}} \\right)\_{T,n\_{j},j \\neq i},\\mu\_{i}^{\\text{concave}} = \\left( {{\\partial f^{\\text{concave}}}/{\\partial n\_{i}}} \\right)\_{T,n\_{j},j \\neq i}.} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si327.png)

It has been shown in 

$\\begin{matrix} {F\\left( \\mathbf{N}^{G},V^{G} \\right) = F\_{c}\\left( \\mathbf{N}^{G},V^{G} \\right) + F\_{a}\\left( \\mathbf{N}^{G},V^{G} \\right),} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.73)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si330.png)(3.73) 

where

$\\begin{matrix} {F\_{c}\\left( \\mathbf{N}^{G},V^{G} \\right) = f^{\\text{convex}}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{G} \\right)V^{G} + f^{\\text{convex}}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{L} \\right)V^{L},} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.74)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si331.png)(3.74) 

$\\begin{matrix} {F\_{a}\\left( \\mathbf{N}^{G},V^{G} \\right) = f^{\\text{concave}}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{G} \\right)V^{G} + f^{\\text{concave}}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{L} \\right)V^{L}.} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.75)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si332.png)(3.75) 

The second-order partial derivatives of *F*[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si333.png) with respect to *N*[*i*]^(*G*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si334.png) and *N*[*j*]^(*G*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si335.png) are calculated as

$\\begin{matrix} \\frac{\\partial^{2}F\_{c}\\left( \\mathbf{N}^{G},V^{G} \\right)}{\\partial N\_{i}^{G}\\partial N\_{j}^{G}} & = & {\\frac{\\partial^{2}f^{\\text{convex}}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{G} \\right)}{\\partial n\_{i}^{G}\\partial n\_{j}^{G}}\\frac{1}{V^{G}} + \\frac{\\partial^{2}f^{\\text{convex}}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{L} \\right)}{\\partial n\_{i}^{L}\\partial n\_{j}^{L}}\\frac{1}{V^{L}}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.76)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si336.png)(3.76) 

Owing to the convexity of *f*^(convex)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si337.png), using the features of the positive definite matrix, we can conclude that *F*[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si338.png) is convex with respect to **N**^(*G*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si339.png). Moreover, *F*[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si340.png) is also convex with respect to *V*^(*G*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si341.png) on account of the second-order partial derivative of *F*[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si342.png) with respect to *V*^(*G*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si343.png)

$\\begin{matrix} \\frac{\\partial^{2}F\_{c}\\left( \\mathbf{N}^{G},V^{G} \\right)}{\\partial V^{G}\\partial V^{G}} & = & {\\sum\\limits\_{i,j = 1}^{M}{\\frac{\\partial^{2}f^{\\text{convex}}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{G} \\right)}{\\partial n\_{i}^{G}\\partial n\_{j}^{G}}\\frac{n\_{i}^{G}n\_{j}^{G}}{V^{G}}} + \\sum\\limits\_{i,j = 1}^{M}{\\frac{\\partial^{2}f^{\\text{convex}}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{L} \\right)}{\\partial n\_{i}^{L}\\partial n\_{j}^{L}}\\frac{n\_{i}^{L}n\_{j}^{L}}{V^{L}}}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.77)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si344.png)(3.77) 

Applying the similar analysis approaches to *F*[*a*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si345.png), we can derive that it is concave with respect to **N**^(*G*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si346.png) or *V*^(*G*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si347.png).

 

### 3.2.4 Semiimplicit numerical scheme

In the semiimplicit numerical scheme, the mass conservation equations (in moles) and the volume equation will be solved separately by the mixed explicit–implicit schemes. The total time interval is chosen as *I* = (0,*T*[*f*]\]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si348.png), where *T*[*f*] > 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si349.png). The molar densities at the *k*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si350.png)th time step are calculated as **n**^(*G*, *k*) = (**N**^(*G*, *k*)/*V*^(*G*, *k*))![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si351.png) and **n**^(*L*, *k*) = (**N**^(*L*, *k*)/*V*^(*L*, *k*))![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si352.png). Furthermore, the notations of **n**^(*G*, *k* + (1/2))![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si353.png) and **n**^(*L*, *k* + (1/2))![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si354.png) are used to represent

$\\mathbf{n}^{G,k + \\frac{1}{2}} = \\frac{\\mathbf{N}^{G,k + 1}}{V^{G,k}},\\mathbf{n}^{L,k + \\frac{1}{2}} = \\frac{\\mathbf{N}^{L,k + 1}}{V^{L,k}}.$ (3.78)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si355.png)(3.78) 

The temporal discrete equation for moles of component *i*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si356.png) (*i* = 1, …, *M*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si357.png)) is expressed as

$\\begin{matrix} \\frac{N\_{i}^{G,k + 1}-N\_{i}^{G,k}}{\\delta t\_{k}} & = & {\\frac{D\_{i}N\_{i}^{t}}{\\mathit{RT}}\\left( {\\mu\_{i}^{L,k + (1/2)}-\\mu\_{i}^{G,k + (1/2)}} \\right),} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.79)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si358.png)(3.79) 

where *μ*[*i*]^(*G*, *k* + (1/2)) = *μ*[*i*]^(convex)(**n**^(*G*, *k* + (1/2))) + *μ*[*i*]^(concave)(**n**^(*G*, *k*)),![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si359.png) *μ*[*i*]^(*L*, *k* + (1/2)) = *μ*[*i*]^(convex)(**n**^(*L*, *k* + (1/2))) + *μ*[*i*]^(concave)(**n**^(*L*, *k*)).![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si360.png) This is obviously a nonlinear system, unknowns of which are the gas-phase moles of all components **N**^(*G*, *k* + 1)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si361.png) at the (*k*+1)*t**h*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si362.png) time step, but this system is independent of the gas volume *V*^(*G*, *k* + 1)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si363.png) at the (*k*+1)*t**h*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si364.png) time step. The following discrete volume evolutionary equation to calculate the gas volume at the (*k*+1)*t**h*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si365.png) time step can be computed as

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{V^{G,k + 1}-V^{G,k}}{\\delta t\_{k}} = \\frac{C\_{V}^{G}C\_{V}^{L}V^{t}}{C\_{V}^{L}p\_{G}^{k} + C\_{V}^{G}p\_{L}^{k}}\\left( {p\_{G}^{k + (1/2)}-p\_{L}^{k + (1/2)}-p\_{c}^{k + (1/2)}} \\right),} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.80)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si366.png)(3.80) 

where

$\\begin{matrix} {p\_{G}^{k + (1/2)} = \\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{M}\\left( {n\_{j}^{G,k + 1}\\mu\_{j}^{\\text{convex}}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{G,k + 1} \\right) + n\_{j}^{G,k + \\frac{1}{2}}\\mu\_{j}^{\\text{concave}}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{G,k + \\frac{1}{2}} \\right)} \\right)} \\\\ {-f^{\\text{convex}}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{G,k + 1} \\right) + f^{\\text{concave}}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{G,k + \\frac{1}{2}} \\right),} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.81)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si367.png)(3.81) 

$\\begin{matrix} {p\_{L}^{k + (1/2)} = \\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{M}\\left( {n\_{j}^{L,k + 1}\\mu\_{j}^{\\text{convex}}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{L,k + 1} \\right) + n\_{j}^{L,k + (1/2)}\\mu\_{j}^{\\text{concave}}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{L,k + (1/2)} \\right)} \\right)} \\\\ {-f^{\\text{convex}}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{L,k + 1} \\right) + f^{\\text{concave}}\\left( \\mathbf{n}^{L,k + (1/2)} \\right),} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.82)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si368.png)(3.82) 

$\\begin{matrix} {p\_{c}^{k + \\frac{1}{2}} = p\_{c}\\left( {\\mathbf{N}^{G,k + 1},V^{G,k}} \\right).} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.83)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si369.png)(3.83)  

### 3.2.5 Thermodynamical stability

We first introduce the definition of thermodynamical stability, which is desired by an efficient scheme. According to the second law of thermodynamics, the total entropy increases over time, that is, *d**S*/*d**t* ≥ 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si370.png), and thereby it is derived as

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{dF}{dt} + p\_{c}\\frac{dV^{G}}{dt} \\leq 0.} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.84)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si371.png)(3.84) 

Integrating over the time interval (*t*[*k*],*t*[*k* + 1]\])![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si372.png) gives

$\\begin{matrix} {F^{k + 1}-F^{k} + \\int\_{t\_{k}}^{t\_{k + 1}}{p\_{c}\\frac{\\partial V^{G}}{\\partial t} \\leq 0}.} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.85)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si373.png)(3.85) 

If *p*[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si374.png) is a given constant, then the previous inequality is reduced into

$\\begin{matrix} {F^{k + 1}-F^{k} + p\_{c}\\left( {V^{G,k + 1}-V^{G,k}} \\right) \\leq 0.} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.86)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si375.png)(3.86) 

If *p*[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si376.png) is a function of **N**^(*G*)*a**n**d**V*^(*G*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si377.png), then we apply the quadrature rule to the inequality and easily get

$\\begin{matrix} {F^{k + 1}-F^{k} + p\_{c}^{\*}\\left( {V^{G,k + 1}-V^{G,k}} \\right) \\leq 0,} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.87)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si378.png)(3.87) 

where *p*[*c*]^(\*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si379.png) is the capillary pressure that is equal to certain value between the *k*th time step and (*k*+1)*t**h*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si380.png) time step. For a specific discretization of capillary pressure, if a discrete scheme satisfies the inequality, then we call this scheme as a thermodynamically stable scheme. If capillarity is neglected, then the thermodynamical stability becomes the energy stability.

Multiplying [Eq. (3.79)](#chp003_a.xhtml#eqn79) by (*μ*[*i*]^(*G*, *k* + (1/2))−*μ*[*i*]^(*L*, *k* + (1/2)))![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si381.png), we can get

$\\left( {\\mu\_{i}^{G,k + (1/2)}-\\mu\_{i}^{L,k + (1/2)}} \\right)\\frac{N\_{i}^{G,k + 1}-N\_{i}^{G,k}}{\\delta t\_{k}} = -\\frac{D\_{i}N\_{i}^{t}}{\\mathit{RT}}\\left( {\\mu\_{i}^{L,k + (1/2)}-\\mu\_{i}^{G,k + (1/2)}} \\right)^{2}.$ (3.88)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si382.png)(3.88) 

Summing the previous equation from *i* = 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si383.png) to *M*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si384.png) and considering the convex–concave property, it is easy to obtain

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{F\\left( \\mathbf{N}^{G,k + 1},V^{G,k} \\right)-F\\left( \\mathbf{N}^{G,k},V^{G,k} \\right)}{\\delta t\_{k}} \\leq -\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}{\\frac{D\_{i}N\_{i}^{t}}{\\mathit{RT}}\\left( {\\mu\_{i}^{L,k + (1/2)}-\\mu\_{i}^{G,k + (1/2)}} \\right)^{2}}.} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.89)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si385.png)(3.89) 

Multiplying [Eq. (3.80)](#chp003_a.xhtml#eqn80) by (*p*[*L*]^(*k* + (1/2))−*p*[*G*]^(*k* + (1/2))+*p*[*c*]^(*k* + (1/2)))![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si386.png) and we can easily get

$\\begin{matrix} {\\left( {p\_{L}^{k + (1/2)}-p\_{G}^{k + (1/2)} + p\_{c}^{k + (1/2)}} \\right)\\frac{V^{G,k + 1}-V^{G,k}}{\\delta t\_{k}}} & {= -\\frac{C\_{V}^{G}C\_{V}^{L}V^{t}}{C\_{V}^{L}p\_{G}^{k} + C\_{V}^{G}p\_{L}^{k}}\\left( {p\_{G}^{k + (1/2)}-p\_{L}^{k + (1/2)}-p\_{c}^{k + (1/2)}} \\right)^{2}} & \\\\ & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.90)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si387.png)(3.90) 

Using the convex–concave property of the Helmholtz free energy with respect to the gas volume, it is easy to get that

$\\begin{matrix} {F\\left( \\mathbf{N}^{G,k + 1},V^{G,k + 1} \\right)-F\\left( \\mathbf{N}^{G,k + 1},V^{G,k} \\right) \\leq \\left( {p\_{L}^{k + (1/2)}-p\_{G}^{k + (1/2)}} \\right)\\left( {V^{G,k + 1}-V^{G,k}} \\right)} & & \\\\ & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.91)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si388.png)(3.91) 

Finally, we can prove the thermodynamic stability of the dynamic scheme as

$\\begin{matrix} {F^{k + 1}-F^{k} + p\_{c}^{k + (1/2)}\\left( {V^{G,k + 1}-V^{G,k}} \\right) \\leq -\\frac{D\_{i}N\_{i}^{t}}{\\mathit{RT}}\\left( {\\mu\_{i}^{L,k + (1/2)}-\\mu\_{i}^{G,k + (1/2)}} \\right)^{2}} \\\\ {-\\frac{C\_{V}^{G}C\_{V}^{L}V^{t}}{C\_{V}^{L}p\_{G}^{k} + C\_{V}^{G}p\_{L}^{k}}\\left( {p\_{G}^{k + (1/2)}-p\_{L}^{k + (1/2)}-p\_{c}^{k + (1/2)}} \\right)^{2}} \\\\ {\\leq 0.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.92)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si389.png)(3.92) 

It can be easily referred from [Eq. (3.92)](#chp003_a.xhtml#eqn92) that the entropy shall increase with time steps even for large time step sizes until it reaches a maximum. When the entropy attains its maximum, the system reaches the equilibrium state. This means that the proposed scheme obeys the second law of thermodynamics, so the proposed scheme has the thermodynamical stability (entropy stability) for any time step size.

 

### 3.2.6 Phase stability analysis

The appropriate initial conditions, including initial values of moles and volumes, are required for the proposed dynamical model. To determine the initial conditions, we need to carry out the phase stability analysis with capillarity. The goal of phase stability is to ascertain whether the fluid system remains in a single phase or splits into more phases at specified moles, volume, and temperature under consideration of capillarity effect.

In the cases caring the changes of primal thermodynamical variables when a single-phase system splits into two phases, the change of total entropy, denoted by *Δ**S*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si390.png), should be positive definite based on the second law of thermodynamics. Similarly, the entropy change can be divided into two parts: change of the system *Δ**S*[sys]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si391.png) and change of the environment *Δ**S*[env]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si392.png), which can be calculated by

$\\begin{matrix} {\\Delta S\_{env} = -\\frac{\\Delta\\mathit{\\\_ Q}}{T}.} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.93)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si393.png)(3.93) 

It can be stated from the first law of thermodynamics that

$\\begin{matrix} {\\Delta U = \\Delta\\text{\\\_}Q + \\Delta W.} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.94)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si394.png)(3.94) 

It can be easily expressed in another form as

$\\begin{matrix} {\\Delta\\text{\\\_}Q = \\Delta F + T\\Delta S\_{\\text{sys}} + p\_{c}\\Delta V^{G},} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.95)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si395.png)(3.95) 

The total entropy change can be derived as

$\\begin{matrix} {\\Delta S = \\Delta S\_{\\text{sys}}-\\frac{1}{T}\\Delta\\text{\\\_}Q = -\\frac{1}{T}\\Delta F-\\frac{p\_{c}}{T}\\Delta V^{G}.} & & \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.96)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si396.png)(3.96) 

If the feed mixture is assumed to be gas, then the trial phase is liquid. In this case, taking *V*^′ = *V*^(*L*) > 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si397.png) and **N**^′ = **N**^(*L*) > 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si398.png), and the total Helmholtz free energy change can be expressed by

$\\begin{matrix} {\\Delta F = F\\left( {V^{L},T,\\mathbf{N}^{L}} \\right) + F\\left( {V^{t}-V^{L},T,\\mathbf{N}^{t}-\\mathbf{N}^{L}} \\right)-F\\left( {V^{t},T,\\mathbf{N}^{t}} \\right)} \\\\ {= \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}{\\left( {\\mu\_{i}\\left( {V^{L},T,\\mathbf{N}^{L}} \\right)-\\mu\_{i}\\left( {V^{t},T,\\mathbf{N}^{t}} \\right)} \\right)N\_{i}^{L}}} \\\\ {-\\left( {p\\left( {V^{L},T,\\mathbf{N}^{L}} \\right)-p\\left( {V^{t},T,\\mathbf{N}^{t}} \\right)} \\right)V^{L} + R\_{1}\\left( {V^{L},T,\\mathbf{N}^{L}} \\right),} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.97)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si399.png)(3.97) 

where the pressure (*p*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si400.png)) and chemical potentials (*μ*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si401.png)) are treated as functions of volume, temperature, and moles, and *R*[1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si402.png) represents the reminder in the Taylor’s expansion after the first-order terms.

If the feed mixture is assumed to be liquid, then the trial phase is gas. In this case, we take *V*^′ = *V*^(*G*) > 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si403.png), **N**^′ = **N**^(*G*) > 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si404.png), and the change of total Helmholtz free energy is similarly derived as

$\\begin{matrix} {\\Delta F = \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}{\\left( {\\mu\_{i}\\left( {V^{G},T,\\mathbf{N}^{G}} \\right)-\\mu\_{i}\\left( {V^{t},T,\\mathbf{N}^{t}} \\right)} \\right)N\_{i}^{G}}} \\\\ {-\\left( {p\\left( {V^{G},T,\\mathbf{N}^{G}} \\right)-p\\left( {V^{t},T,\\mathbf{N}^{t}} \\right)} \\right)V^{G} + R\_{1}\\left( {V^{G},T,\\mathbf{N}^{G}} \\right).} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.98)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si405.png)(3.98) 

A combining formula of [Eqs. (3.97)](#chp003_a.xhtml#eqn97) and [(3.98)](#chp003_a.xhtml#eqn98) can be written as

$\\begin{matrix} {-T\\Delta S = \\Delta F + p\_{c}\\Delta V^{G}} \\\\ {= \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}{\\left( {\\mu\_{i}\\left( {V^{\\prime},T,\\mathbf{N}^{\\prime}} \\right)-\\mu\_{i}\\left( {V^{t},T,\\mathbf{N}^{t}} \\right)} \\right)N\_{i}^{\\prime}}} \\\\ {-\\left( {p\\left( {V^{\\prime},T,\\mathbf{N}^{\\prime}} \\right)-p\\left( {V^{t},T,\\mathbf{N}^{t}} \\right)} \\right)V^{\\prime} + \\gamma\_{s}p\_{c}V^{\\prime} + R\_{1}\\left( {V^{\\prime},T,\\mathbf{N}^{\\prime}} \\right).} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.99)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si406.png)(3.99) 

In the previous equality, if the feed mixture is assumed to be liquid and the trial phase is gas, then we take *γ*[*s*]\=![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si407.png)1; otherwise, that is, the feed mixture is gas and the trial phase is liquid, then we take *γ*[*s*] = −![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si408.png)1.

It can be stated from the second law of thermodynamics that the single phase is stable if *Δ**S* ≤ 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si409.png), which is equal to state that the single phase is stable if

$\\begin{matrix} {\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}\\left( {\\mu\_{i}\\left( {V^{\\prime},T,\\mathbf{N}^{\\prime}} \\right)-\\mu\_{i}\\left( {V^{t},T,\\mathbf{N}^{t}} \\right)} \\right)N\_{i}^{\\prime}} \\\\ {-\\left( {p\\left( {V^{\\prime},T,\\mathbf{N}^{\\prime}} \\right)-p\\left( {V^{t},T,\\mathbf{N}^{t}} \\right)} \\right)V^{\\prime} + \\gamma\_{s}p\_{c}V^{\\prime} \\geq 0.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.100)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si410.png)(3.100) 

If we denote the overall molar density $\\mathbf{n}^{t} = \\frac{\\mathbf{N}^{t}}{V^{t}}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si411.png) and the trial phase molar density $\\mathbf{n}^{\\prime} = \\frac{\\mathbf{N}^{\\prime}}{V^{\\prime}}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si412.png), the tangent plane distance function can be defined as

$\\left. \\Psi(\\mathbf{n}^{\\prime},T \\right) = \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}{n\_{i}^{\\prime}\\left( {\\mu\_{i}\\left( {T,\\mathbf{n}^{\\prime}} \\right)-\\mu\_{i}\\left( {T,\\mathbf{n}^{t}} \\right)} \\right)-\\left( {p\\left( {T,\\mathbf{n}^{\\prime}} \\right)-p\\left( {T,\\mathbf{n}^{t}} \\right)} \\right) + \\gamma\_{s}p\_{c}},$ (3.101)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si413.png)(3.101) 

noting that the pressure and chemical potentials are the homogeneous functions of degree zero with respect to volume and moles. As a result, the single phase is table if and only if *Ψ*(**n**^′,*T*) ≥ 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si414.png). This statement holds for all admissible molar density.

 

### 3.2.7 Staggered-grid finite difference methods

In [Sections 3.1](#chp003.xhtml#s0010) and [3.2](#chp003.xhtml#s0050), phase equilibria calculations and dynamic models to predict the phase splitting process in fluid mixtures have been presented. In reservoir simulation, numerical discretization and algorithm implementation are also needed to complete a full research. Staggered-grid finite difference method, also known as SGFD, is a commonly used approach in the numerical simulation of subsurface reservoirs with a long history.

 

### 3.2.8 Staggered grid

A rectangular mesh is defined by *x*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si415.png), *i* = 0, 1, 2, …, *m*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si416.png), and *y*[*j*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si417.png), *j* = 0, 1, 2, …, *n*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si418.png). For example, a 20 × 15![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si419.png) uniform mesh can be illustrated in [Fig. 3.1](#chp003_a.xhtml#f0010).

![Figure 3.1 A 20 \\times 15image uniform mesh.](OEBPS/images/F000034f03-01-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 3.1 A 20 × 15![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si1.png) uniform mesh.

Nodal points can be defined as (*x*[*i*],*y*[*j*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si420.png), *i* = 0, 1, 2, …, *m*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si421.png), *j* = 0, 1, 2, …, *n*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si422.png) and cells defined as \[*x*[*i* − 1],*x*[*i*]\] × \[*y*[*j* − 1],*y*[*j*]\]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si423.png), *i* = 1, 2, …, *m*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si424.png), *j* = 1, 2, …, *n*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si425.png). The mesh center is defined as $x\_{i-(1/2)}: = \\frac{x\_{i} + x\_{i-1}}{2}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si426.png), and $y\_{j-(1/2)}: = \\frac{y\_{j} + y\_{j-1}}{2}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si427.png) and the cell center is defined as (*x*[*i* − (1/2)],*y*[*j* − (1/2)])![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si428.png), *i* = 1, 2, …, *m*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si429.png), *j* = 1, 2, …, *n*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si430.png), as shown in [Fig. 3.2](#chp003_a.xhtml#f0015).

![Figure 3.2 Cell centers in a mesh.](OEBPS/images/F000034f03-02-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 3.2 Cell centers in a mesh.

Similarly, we can define the centers of *x*\-edges as (*x*[*i*],*y*[*j* − (1/2)])![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si431.png), *i* = 0, 1, …, *m*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si432.png), *j* = 1, …, *n*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si433.png) and centers of *y*\-edges as (*x*[*i* − (1/2)],*y*[*j*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si434.png), *i* = 1, …, *m*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si435.png), *j* = 0, 1, …, *n*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si436.png), as shown in [Fig. 3.3](#chp003_a.xhtml#f0020).

![Figure 3.3 Edge centers in a mesh.](OEBPS/images/F000034f03-03-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 3.3 Edge centers in a mesh.

In a staggered grid, we place pressure unknowns in cell centers, but velocity unknowns in edge centers, as shown in [Fig. 3.4](#chp003_a.xhtml#f0025).

![Figure 3.4 A staggered grid.](OEBPS/images/F000034f03-04-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 3.4 A staggered grid.

 

### 3.2.9 Staggered-grid finite difference for the stokes equation

Recall the vector form of the Navier–Stokes (N–S) equation:

$\\rho\\left( {\\frac{\\partial\\mathbf{v}}{\\partial t} + \\mathbf{v} \\cdot \\nabla\\mathbf{v}} \\right) = \\rho\\mathbf{g}-\\nabla\\widehat{p} + \\mu\\nabla^{2}\\mathbf{v} + \\left( {\\frac{\\mu}{3} + \\beta} \\right)\\nabla\\left( \\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{v} \\right).$ (3.102)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si437.png)(3.102) 

It is to couple with the continuity equation:

$\\frac{\\partial\\rho}{\\partial t} + \\nabla \\cdot \\left( \\rho\\mathbf{v} \\right) = 0.$ (3.103)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si438.png)(3.103) 

For incompressible fluid with constant shear viscosity:

$\\rho\\left( {\\frac{\\partial\\mathbf{v}}{\\partial t} + \\mathbf{v} \\cdot \\nabla\\mathbf{v}} \\right) = \\rho\\mathbf{g}-\\nabla p + \\mu\\nabla^{2}\\mathbf{v}.$ (3.104)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si439.png)(3.104) 

The continuity equation could be written as

∇ ⋅ **v** = 0. (3.105)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si440.png)(3.105) 

In reservoir simulation, Stokes’ flow is a typical type of fluid flow where advective inertial forces are small compared with viscous forces. The steady state Stokes equation can be written as

*μ**Δ***v** = ∇*p* − *ρ***g**, **x** ∈ *Ω*, (3.106)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si441.png)(3.106) 

and the rectangular domain in 2D is defined as *Ω* :  = (0,*L*[*x*]) × (0,*L*[*y*]).![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si442.png) The components of position, velocity, and body force density vectors are denoted by **x** = (*x*[1],*x*[2]) = (*x*,*y*),![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si443.png) **v** = (*v*[1],*v*[2]) = (*u*,*v*),![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si444.png) and **g** = (*g*[1],*g*[2]) = (*g*[*x*],*g*[*y*]).![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si445.png) Numerical solutions of Stokes’ flow as special cases of N–S.

In mathematics, finite-difference methods (FDMs) are numerical methods for solving differential equations by approximating them with difference equations, in which finite differences approximate the derivatives. Today, FDMs are the dominant approach to numerical solutions of partial differential equations. FDMs are simpler to implement than finite element methods (FEM). FDMs can be designed to be both locally conservative and energy stable. FDMs are the dominant approach adopted in reservoir simulation software in industries (Eclipse, CMG, etc.).

Forward finite difference formula is

$f^{\\prime}\\left( a \\right) \\approx \\frac{f\\left( a + h \\right)-f\\left( a \\right)}{h}.$ (3.107)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si446.png)(3.107) 

Central finite difference formula is

$f^{\\prime}\\left( a \\right) \\approx \\frac{f\\left( a + \\frac{h}{2} \\right)-f\\left( a-\\frac{h}{2} \\right)}{h}.$ (3.108)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si447.png)(3.108) 

Backward finite difference formula is

$f^{\\prime}\\left( a \\right) \\approx \\frac{f\\left( a \\right)-f\\left( a-h \\right)}{h}.$ (3.109)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si448.png)(3.109) 

The Stokes equation in a 2D rectangular domain can be written as

$\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial x}\\left( {\\mu\\frac{\\partial u}{\\partial x}} \\right) + \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial y}\\left( {\\mu\\frac{\\partial u}{\\partial y}} \\right) = \\frac{\\partial p}{\\partial x}-\\rho g\_{x},\\left( {EqMmX} \\right)} \\\\ {\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial x}\\left( {\\mu\\frac{\\partial v}{\\partial x}} \\right) + \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial y}\\left( {\\mu\\frac{\\partial v}{\\partial y}} \\right) = \\frac{\\partial p}{\\partial y}-\\rho g\_{y},\\left( {EqMmY} \\right)} \\\\ {\\frac{\\partial u}{\\partial x} + \\frac{\\partial v}{\\partial y} = 0.\\left( {EqCnt} \\right)} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (3.110)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si449.png)(3.110) 

It can be easily counted that we have (3*m**n*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si450.png) unknowns in the system:

*u*[*i*, *j* − 0.5], *v*[*i* − 0.5, *j*], *a**n**d**p*[*i* − 0.5, *j* − 0.5], *i* = 1, 2, …, *m*, *j* = 1, 2, …, *n* (3.111)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si451.png)(3.111) 

if a periodic boundary conditions treatment is used:

**v**(0,*y*) = **v**(*L*[*x*],*y*), **v**(*x*,0) = **v**(*x*,*L*[*y*]), *p*(0,*y*) = *p*(*L*[*x*],*y*), *p*(*x*,0) = *p*(*x*,*L*[*y*]). (3.112)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si452.png)(3.112)
