First, we consider an ideal reversible process to get the form of the reversible stress, and the reversibility implies that there exist no effects of viscosity and friction. In this case the entropy shall be conserved, so the diffusions vanish, that is, **J**[*i*] = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si792.png), and the total tress **σ**![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si793.png) becomes equal to the reversible stress, denoted by **σ**[rev]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si794.png), which must have the form

$\\mathbf{\\sigma}\_{\\text{rev}} = p\\mathbf{I} + \\sum\\limits\_{i,j = 1}^{M}{c\_{ij}\\left( {\\nabla n\_{i} \\otimes \\nabla n\_{j}} \\right)}.$ (3.247)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si795.png)(3.247) 

The last term on the right-hand side of [Eq. (3.246)](#chp003_c_split_001.xhtml#eqn246) shall also be zero as

$\\rho\\frac{d\\mathbf{u}}{\\mathit{dt}} + \\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{\\sigma}\_{\\text{rev}} = 0.$ (3.248)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si796.png)(3.248) 

For the realistic irreversible multicomponent chemical systems, the driving force for diffusion of each component is the gradient of chemical potentials, so we express the diffusion flux for each component as

$\\mathbf{J}\_{i} = -\\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{M}{\\mathcal{M}\_{\\mathit{ij}}\\nabla\\mu\_{j}}, i = 1,\\ldots,M,$ (3.249)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si797.png)(3.249) 

where ℳ = (ℳ[*i**j*])[*i*, *j* = 1]^(*M*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si798.png) is the mobility. Consequently, the mole balance equation for component *i*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si799.png) is stated as

$\\frac{\\partial n\_{i}}{\\partial t} + \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\mathbf{u}n\_{i}} \\right)-\\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{M}{\\nabla \\cdot \\mathcal{M}\_{\\mathit{ij}}\\nabla\\mu\_{j}} = 0,$ (3.250)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si800.png)(3.250) 

where *i* = 1, …, *M*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si801.png). The mobility matrix ℳ![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si802.png) shall be symmetric in terms of Onsager’s reciprocal principle. The second law of thermodynamics requires

$\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}{\\mathbf{J}\_{i} \\cdot \\nabla\\mu\_{i} \\leq 0},$ (3.251)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si803.png)(3.251) 

which ensures the nonnegativity of the first term on the right-hand side of [Eq. (3.246)](#chp003_c_split_001.xhtml#eqn246). A few choices of the mobility are provided to help readers select for different cases. In the following mobility formulations, *R*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si804.png) stands for the universal gas constant.

**Mobility 1** We take ℳ![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si805.png) as a diagonal positive definite matrix with diagonal elements ℳ[*i**i*] = *D*[*i*]*n*[*i*]/*R**T*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si806.png), and then we have the following diffusion flux **J**[*i*] =  − (*D*[*i*]*n*[*i*]/*R**T*)∇*μ*[*i*],![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si807.png) wehre *D*[*i*] > 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si808.png) is the diffusion coefficient of component. This mobility choice apparently satisfies Onsager’s reciprocal principle and the condition stated in [Eq. (3.251)](#chp003_c_split_001.xhtml#eqn251). At constant temperature and pressure, it holds that $\\sum\_{i = 1}^{M}{n\_{i}\\nabla\\mu\_{i}} = 0$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si809.png) due to the Gibbs–Duhem equation, and furthermore, if taking *D*[1] = ⋯ = *D*[*M*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si810.png), we derive $\\sum\_{i = 1}^{M}\\mathbf{J}\_{i} = 0$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si811.png) at constant temperature and pressure.

**Mobility 2** We take the mobility using molar-average velocity as

$\\mathcal{M}\_{\\mathit{ii}} = \\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{M}\\frac{\\mathcal{D}\_{\\mathit{ij}}n\_{i}n\_{j}}{\\mathit{nRT}},\\mathcal{M}\_{\\mathit{ij}} = -\\frac{\\mathcal{D}\_{\\mathit{ij}}n\_{i}n\_{j}}{\\mathit{nRT}}, j \\neq i,$ (3.252)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si812.png)(3.252) 

where $n = \\sum\_{j = 1}^{M}n\_{j}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si813.png) and the mole diffusion coefficients 𝒟[*i**j*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si814.png) satisfy 𝒟[*i**i*] = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si815.png) and 𝒟[*i**j*] = 𝒟[*j**i*] > 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si816.png) for *i* ≠ *j*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si817.png). This means that $\\mathcal{M}\_{\\mathit{ii}} = -\\sum\_{j = 1,j \\neq i}^{M}\\mathcal{M}\_{\\mathit{ij}}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si818.png), so we have $\\sum\_{j = 1}^{M}\\mathcal{M}\_{\\mathit{ij}} = 0$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si819.png) for any 1 ≤ *i* ≤ *M*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si820.png) and thus $\\sum\_{i = 1}^{M}\\mathbf{J}\_{i} = -\\sum\_{i = 1}^{M}{\\sum\_{j = 1}^{M}\\mathcal{M}\_{\\mathit{ij}}}\\nabla\\mu\_{j} = -\\sum\_{i = 1}^{M}{\\nabla\\mu\_{i}}\\sum\_{j = 1}^{M}\\mathcal{M}\_{\\mathit{ij}} = 0.$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si821.png) This mobility is symmetric and thus it satisfies Onsager’s reciprocal principle.

**Mobility 3** We denote *ρ*[*i*] = *n*[*i*]*M*[*w*, *i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si822.png) and take the mobility for mass-average velocity as

$\\mathcal{M}\_{\\mathit{ii}} = \\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{M}\\frac{\\mathcal{D}\_{\\mathit{ij}}n\_{i}\\rho\_{j}}{M\_{w,i}\\rho\\mathit{RT}},\\mathcal{M}\_{\\mathit{ij}} = -\\frac{\\mathcal{D}\_{\\mathit{ij}}n\_{i}n\_{j}}{\\rho\\mathit{RT}}, j \\neq i,$ (3.253)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si823.png)(3.253) 

where the mass diffusion coefficients 𝒟[*i**j*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si824.png) satisfy 𝒟[*i**i*] = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si825.png) and 𝒟[*i**j*] = 𝒟[*j**i*] > 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si826.png) for *i* ≠ *j*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si827.png). It is obvious that this mobility is symmetric and Onsager’s reciprocal principle is satisfied. We further denote $\\overline{\\mathcal{M}} = \\left( {\\overline{\\mathcal{M}}}\_{\\mathit{ij}} \\right)\_{i,j = 1}^{M}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si828.png) as ${\\overline{\\mathcal{M}}}\_{\\mathit{ii}} = M\_{w,i}^{2}\\mathcal{M}\_{\\mathit{ii}} = \\sum\_{j = 1}^{M}\\left( \\mathcal{D}\_{\\mathit{ij}}\\rho\_{i}\\rho\_{j}/\\rho\\mathit{RT} \\right),{\\overline{\\mathcal{M}}}\_{\\mathit{ij}} = M\_{w,i}M\_{w,j}\\mathcal{M}\_{\\mathit{ij}} = -\\left( \\mathcal{D}\_{\\mathit{ij}}\\rho\_{i}\\rho\_{j}/\\rho\\mathit{RT} \\right),j \\neq i.$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si829.png) Apparently, $\\overline{\\mathcal{M}}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si830.png) is symmetric and it satisfies $\\sum\_{i = 1}^{M}{{\\overline{\\mathcal{M}}}\_{\\mathit{ij}} = 0}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si831.png) for any 1 ≤ *j* ≤ *M*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si832.png), so we obtain $\\sum\_{i = 1}^{M}{M\_{w,i}\\mathbf{J}\_{i}} = -\\sum\_{i = 1}^{M}{\\sum\_{j = 1}^{M}{M\_{w,i}\\mathcal{M}\_{\\mathit{ij}}\\nabla\\mu\_{j}}} = -\\sum\_{i = 1}^{M}{\\sum\_{j = 1}^{M}{{\\overline{\\mathcal{M}}}\_{\\mathit{ij}}\\left( \\nabla\\mu\_{j}/M\_{w,j} \\right)}} = -\\sum\_{j = 1}^{M}\\left( \\nabla\\mu\_{j}/M\_{w,j} \\right)\\sum\_{i = 1}^{M}{{\\overline{\\mathcal{M}}}\_{\\mathit{ij}} = 0}.$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si833.png)

For the realistic viscous flow the total stress can be split into two parts: reversible part and irreversible part

**σ** = **σ**[rev] + **σ**[irrev]. (3.254)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si834.png)(3.254) 

The irreversible part can be calculated using Newtonian fluid theory as

$\\mathbf{\\sigma}\_{\\text{irrev}} = -\\eta\\left( {\\nabla\\mathbf{u} + \\nabla\\mathbf{u}^{T}} \\right)-\\left( {\\left( {\\xi-\\frac{2}{3}\\eta} \\right)\\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{u}} \\right)\\mathbf{I},$ (3.255)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si835.png)(3.255) 

So that the complete momentum balance equation can be written as

$\\rho\\left( {\\frac{\\partial\\mathbf{u}}{\\partial t} + \\mathbf{u} \\cdot \\nabla\\mathbf{u}} \\right) + \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}M\_{w,i}\\mathbf{J}\_{i} \\cdot \\nabla\\mathbf{u} = \\nabla\\left( {\\left( {\\xi-\\frac{2}{3}\\eta} \\right)\\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{u}-p} \\right) + \\nabla \\cdot \\eta\\left( {\\nabla\\mathbf{u} + \\nabla\\mathbf{u}^{T}} \\right)-\\sum\\limits\_{i,j = 1}^{M}\\nabla \\cdot \\left( {c\_{\\mathit{ij}}\\nabla n\_{i} \\otimes \\nabla n\_{j}} \\right),$ (3.256)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si836.png)(3.256) 

The conservation form can be formulated as

$\\frac{\\partial\\left( {\\rho\\mathbf{u}} \\right)}{\\partial t} + \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\rho\\mathbf{u} \\otimes \\mathbf{u}} \\right) + \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}{M\_{w,i}\\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\mathbf{u} \\otimes \\mathbf{J}\_{i}} \\right)} = \\nabla\\left( {\\left( {\\xi-\\frac{2}{3}\\eta} \\right)\\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{u}-p} \\right) + \\nabla \\cdot \\eta\\left( {\\nabla\\mathbf{u} + \\nabla\\mathbf{u}^{T}} \\right)-\\sum\\limits\_{i,j = 1}^{M}{\\nabla \\cdot \\left( {c\_{\\mathit{ij}}\\nabla n\_{i} \\otimes \\nabla n\_{j}} \\right)}.$ (3.257)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si837.png)(3.257)  

### 3.4.3 Thermodynamical consistency

The gradients of the pressure and chemical potentials have the following relation:

$\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}n\_{i}\\nabla\\mu\_{i} = \\nabla p + \\sum\\limits\_{i,j = 1}^{M}\\nabla \\cdot \\left( {c\_{\\mathit{ij}}\\nabla n\_{i} \\otimes \\nabla n\_{j}} \\right).$ (3.258)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si838.png)(3.258) 

Thus the momentum equation formulated in [Eq. (3.257)](#chp003_c_split_001.xhtml#eqn257) can be reformulated as

$\\rho\\left( {\\frac{\\partial\\mathbf{u}}{\\partial t} + \\mathbf{u} \\cdot \\nabla\\mathbf{u}} \\right) + \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}{M\_{w,i}\\mathbf{J}\_{i} \\cdot \\nabla\\mathbf{u}} = -\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}{n\_{i}\\nabla\\mu\_{i} + \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\eta\\left( {\\nabla\\mathbf{u} + \\nabla\\mathbf{u}^{T}} \\right) + \\left( {\\xi-\\frac{2}{3}\\eta} \\right)\\left( {\\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{u}} \\right)\\mathbf{I}} \\right),}$ (3.259)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si839.png)(3.259) 

Considering the mass balance [Eq. (3.234)](#chp003_c_split_000.xhtml#eqn234), we can formulate

$\\frac{\\partial E}{\\partial t} = \\left( {\\rho\\frac{\\partial\\mathbf{u}}{\\partial t},\\mathbf{u}} \\right) + \\frac{1}{2}\\left( {\\frac{\\partial\\rho}{\\partial t},{|\\mathbf{u}|}^{2}} \\right) = \\left( {\\rho\\frac{\\partial\\mathbf{u}}{\\partial t},\\mathbf{u}} \\right)-\\frac{1}{2}\\left( {\\nabla \\cdot \\left( \\rho\\mathbf{u} \\right) + \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}{M\_{w,i}\\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{J}\_{i},{|\\mathbf{u}|}^{2}}} \\right) = \\left( {\\rho\\frac{\\partial\\mathbf{u}}{\\partial t} + \\rho\\mathbf{u} \\cdot \\nabla\\mathbf{u} + \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}{M\_{w,i}\\mathbf{J}\_{i} \\cdot \\nabla\\mathbf{u},\\mathbf{u}}} \\right) = -\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}\\left( {n\_{i}\\nabla\\mu\_{i},\\mathbf{u}} \\right) + \\left( {\\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\eta\\left( {\\nabla\\mathbf{u} + \\nabla\\mathbf{u}^{T}} \\right) + \\left( {\\xi-\\frac{2}{3}\\eta} \\right)\\left( {\\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{u}} \\right)\\mathbf{I}} \\right),\\mathbf{u}} \\right) = -\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}\\left( {n\_{i}\\nabla\\mu\_{i},\\mathbf{u}} \\right)-\\left( {\\eta\\left( {\\nabla\\mathbf{u} + \\nabla\\mathbf{u}^{T}} \\right) + \\left( {\\xi-\\frac{2}{3}\\eta} \\right)\\left( {\\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{u}} \\right)\\mathbf{I},\\nabla\\mathbf{u}} \\right) = -\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}{\\left( {n\_{i}\\nabla\\mu\_{i},\\mathbf{u}} \\right)-\\left( {\\eta\\left( {\\nabla\\mathbf{u} + \\nabla\\mathbf{u}^{T}} \\right),\\nabla\\mathbf{u}} \\right)}-\\left( {\\left( {\\xi-\\frac{2}{3}\\eta} \\right)\\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{u},\\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{u}} \\right) = -\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}\\left( {n\_{i}\\nabla\\mu\_{i},\\mathbf{u}} \\right)-\\frac{1}{2}\\left\\| {\\eta^{1 \\slash 2}\\left( {\\nabla\\mathbf{u} + \\nabla\\mathbf{u}^{T}} \\right)} \\right\\|^{2}-\\left\\| {\\left( {\\xi-\\frac{2}{3}\\eta} \\right)^{1 \\slash 2}\\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{u}} \\right\\|^{2}.$ (3.260)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si840.png)(3.260) 

Multiplying both sides of [Eq. (3.233)](#chp003_c_split_000.xhtml#eqn233) by *μ*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si841.png) and integrating it over *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si842.png), we obtain

$\\left( {\\frac{\\partial n\_{i}}{\\partial t},\\mu\_{i}} \\right) + \\left( {\\nabla \\cdot \\left( n\_{i}\\mathbf{u} \\right),\\mu\_{i}} \\right) = \\left( {\\mathbf{J}\_{i},\\nabla\\mu\_{i}} \\right).$ (3.261)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si843.png)(3.261) 

Applying the formulation of *μ*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si844.png), we derive the summation of the first term on the left-hand side:

$\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}\\left( {\\frac{\\partial n\_{i}}{\\partial t},\\mu\_{i}} \\right) = \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}\\left( {\\frac{\\partial n\_{i}}{\\partial t},\\mu\_{i}^{b}-\\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{M}{\\nabla \\cdot \\left( {c\_{\\mathit{ij}}\\nabla n\_{j}} \\right)}} \\right) = \\left( {\\frac{\\partial f\_{b}}{\\partial t},1} \\right)-\\sum\\limits\_{i,j = 1}^{M}{\\left( {\\frac{\\partial n\_{i}}{\\partial t},\\nabla \\cdot \\left( {c\_{\\mathit{ij}}\\nabla n\_{j}} \\right)} \\right) = \\left( {\\frac{\\partial f\_{b}}{\\partial t},1} \\right)} + \\sum\\limits\_{i,j = 1}^{M}{\\left( {\\nabla\\frac{\\partial n\_{i}}{\\partial t},c\_{\\mathit{ij}}\\nabla n\_{j}} \\right) = \\left( {\\frac{\\partial f\_{b}}{\\partial t},1} \\right)} + \\frac{1}{2}\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial t}\\sum\\limits\_{i,j = 1}^{M}{\\left( {c\_{\\mathit{ij}}\\nabla n\_{i},\\nabla n\_{j}} \\right) = \\frac{\\partial F}{\\partial t}}.$ (3.262)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si845.png)(3.262) 

We denote **μ** = \[*μ*[1],…,*μ*[*M*]\]^(*T*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si846.png), and further define ${\\|\\nabla\\mathbf{\\mu}\\|}\_{M}^{2}≔-\\sum\_{i = 1}^{M}\\left( {\\mathbf{J}\_{i},\\nabla\\mu\_{i}} \\right) = \\sum\_{i,j = 1}^{M}\\left( \\mathcal{M}\_{\\mathit{ij}}\\nabla\\mu\_{i},\\nabla\\mu\_{j} \\right).$![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si847.png) Taking the summation of [Eq. (3.261)](#chp003_c_split_001.xhtml#eqn261) from *i* = 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si848.png) to *M*![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si849.png) as

$\\frac{\\partial F}{\\partial t} = -\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{M}\\left( {\\nabla \\cdot \\left( n\_{i}\\mathbf{u} \\right),\\mu\_{i}} \\right)-{\\|\\nabla\\mathbf{\\mu}\\|}\_{M}^{2}.$ (3.263)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si850.png)(3.263) 

Finally, we can obtain that

$\\frac{\\partial\\left( {F + E} \\right)}{\\partial t} = -{\\|\\nabla\\mathbf{\\mu}\\|}\_{M}^{2}-\\frac{1}{2}\\|\\eta^{1 \\slash 2}\\left( {\\nabla\\mathbf{u} + \\nabla\\mathbf{u}^{T}} \\right)\\|^{2}-\\left\\| {\\left( {\\xi-\\frac{2}{3}\\eta} \\right)^{1 \\slash 2}\\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{u}} \\right\\|^{2},$ (3.264)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si851.png)(3.264) 

and it is easy to see that (∂(*F*+*E*))/∂*t* ≤ 0.![image](OEBPS/images/F000034si852.png) It can be stated that the sum of the Helmholtz free energy and kinetic energy is dissipated with time.

 

## References

1\. Kou J, Sun S. Numerical methods for a multicomponent two-phase interface model with geometric mean influence parameters. *SIAM J Sci Comput.* 2015;37(4):B543–B569.

2\. Kou J, Sun S. Unconditionally stable methods for simulating multi-component two-phase interface models with Peng–Robinson equation of state and various boundary conditions. *J Comput Appl Math*. 2016;291:158–182.

3\. Kou J, Sun S. Thermodynamically consistent modeling and simulation of multi-component two-phase flow with partial miscibility. *Comput Method Appl Mech Eng*. 2018;331:623–649.

4\. Li Y, Johns RT. Rapid flash calculations for compositional simulation. *SPE Reserv Eval Eng*. 2006;9(5):521–529.

5\. Li X, Shen J, Rui H. Energy stability and convergence of SAV block-centered finite difference method for gradient flows. *Math Comput.* 2019;88(319):2047–2068.

6\. Li Y, Zhang T, Sun S. Acceleration of the NVT Flash Calculation for Multicomponent Mixtures Using Deep Neural Network Models. *Ind Eng Chem*. 2019;58(27):12312–12322.

7\. Liu C, Shen J, Yang X. Decoupled Energy Stable Schemes for a Phase-Field Model of Two-Phase Incompressible Flows with Variable Density. *J Sci Comput*. 2015;62(2):601–622.

8\. Mathias PM, Copeman TW. Extension of the Peng-Robinson equation of state to complex mixtures: evaluation of the various forms of the local composition concept. *Fluid Phase Equilibr*. 1983;13:91–108.

9\. Mathias PM, Naheiri T, Oh EM. A density correction for the Peng—Robinson equation of state. *Fluid Phase Equilib.* 1989;47(1):77–87.

10\. Nichita DV, Broseta D, Leibovici CF. Reservoir fluid applications of a pseudo-component delumping new analytical procedure. *J Petrol Sci Eng*. 2007;59(1-2):59–72.

11\. Qiao Z, et al. A new multi-component diffuse interface model with peng-robinson equation of state and its scalar auxiliary variable (SAV) approach. *Commun Comput Phys.* 2019;26:1597–1616.

Chapter four 

# Recent progress in Darcy’s scale reservoir simulation

 

## Abstract

Reservoir simulation on Darcy’s scale provides an upscale view of the various processes that are involved in engineering applications. This point of view facilitates greatly the description and the simulation on this relatively direct level. Several algorithms and corresponding solvers have been proposed in the petroleum industry to model and calculate phenomena seen at Darcy’s scale, and plenty of software have been developed using these solvers to help process managing and production estimations. In this chapter, we will go over the notations and model equations of finite element methods (FEMs), first, that are well accepted in Darcy’s scale reservoir simulation, and we will show the equivalence between FEMs and finite-difference methods (FDMs).

 

### Keywords

Galerkin finite element methods; discontinuous Galerkin; IMPES; Darcy’s scale; reactive transport

## 4.1 Introductions on popular finite element methods

 

### 4.1.1 Galerkin finite element methods: general statement

We consider a stationary problem in two dimensions:

 − *Δ**p* = *f*, *i**n**Ω*,

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1.png)

*p* = 0, *o**n**Γ*, (4.1)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si2.png)(4.1) 

where *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si3.png) is a bounded domain in the plane with boundary *Γ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si4.png), *f*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si5.png) is a given real-valued piecewise continuous bounded function in *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si6.png), and the Laplacian operator *Δ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si7.png) is defined by

$\\Delta p = \\frac{\\partial^{2}p}{\\partial x\_{1}^{2}} + \\frac{\\partial^{2}p}{\\partial x\_{2}^{2}}.$ (4.2)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si8.png)(4.2) 

which is an elastic membrane fixed at its boundary and subject to a transversal load of intensity *f*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si9.png).

We define the linear vector space:

$V = \\left\\{ \\mathit{v:v}{is} a{continuous}{function}{on}\\Omega,\\frac{\\partial v}{\\partial x\_{1}}{and}\\frac{\\partial v}{\\partial x\_{x}}{is}{piecewise}{continuous}{and}{bounded}{on}\\Omega,{and} v = 0{on}\\Gamma \\right\\}.$ (4.3)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si10.png)(4.3) 

Let us recall Green’s formula. For a vector-valued function, **b** = (*b*[1],*b*[2])![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si11.png), the divergence theorem reads:

∫[*Ω*]∇ ⋅ **b** = ∫[*Γ*]**b** ⋅ **n***d**l* (4.4)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si12.png)(4.4) 

Using Green’s formula:

$\\int\_{\\Omega}{\\Delta\\mathit{vwd}\\mathbf{x}} = \\int\_{\\Gamma}{\\frac{\\partial v}{\\partial\\mathbf{n}}\\mathit{wdl}} - \\int\_{\\Omega}{\\nabla v \\cdot \\nabla\\mathit{wd}\\mathbf{x}},$ (4.5)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si13.png)(4.5) 

where the normal derivative is expressed by $\\frac{\\partial v}{\\partial\\mathbf{n}} = \\frac{\\partial v}{\\partial x\_{1}}n\_{1} + \\frac{\\partial v}{\\partial x\_{2}}n\_{2}.$![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si14.png)

We introduce the notation

*a*(*p*,*v*) :  = ∫[*Ω*]∇*p* ⋅ ∇*v**d***x**, *L*(*v*) :  = (*f*,*v*) :  = ∫[*Ω*]*f**v**d***x**. (4.6)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si15.png)(4.6) 

The form *a*(⋅,⋅)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si16.png) is called a bilinear form on *V* × *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si17.png). We also define the functional *F* *:* *V*→ℝ![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si18.png) by

$F\\left( v \\right) = \\frac{1}{2}a\\left( v,v \\right) - L\\left( v \\right),v \\in V.$ (4.7)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si19.png)(4.7) 

The PDE (partial differential equation) can be formulated as the minimization problem

*F**i**n**d**p* ∈ *V**s**u**c**h**t**h**a**t**F*(*p*) ≤ *F*(*v*), ∀*v* ∈ *V*. (4.8)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si20.png)(4.8) 

Multiplying the PDE by *v* ∈ *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si21.png) and integrating over *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si22.png), we see that

 − ∫[*Ω*]*Δ**p**v**d***x** = ∫[*Ω*]*f**v**d***x**. (4.9)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si23.png)(4.9) 

Applying Green’s formula to this equation and using the homogeneous boundary condition lead to

∫[*Ω*]∇*p* ⋅ ∇*v**d***x** = ∫[*Ω*]*f**v**d***x**, ∀*v* ∈ *V*. (4.10)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si24.png)(4.10) 

Thus we reach the Galerkin variational form

*F**i**n**d**p* ∈ *V**s**u**c**h**t**h**a**t**a*(*p*,*v*) = *L*(*v*), ∀*v* ∈ *V*. (4.11)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si25.png)(4.11) 

We now construct the finite element method (FEM) (

${diam}\\left( K \\right) = {the}{longest}{edge}{of}\\overline{K}\\mathit{;h} = \\max\\limits\_{K \\in K\_{h}}{diam}\\left( K \\right).$ (4.12)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si32.png)(4.12) 

and the finite element space can be defined as

*V*[*h*] = *{**v* *:* *v**i**s**a**c**o**n**t**i**n**u**o**u**s**f**u**n**c**t**i**o**n**o**n**Ω*, *v**i**s**l**i**n**e**a**r**o**n**e**a**c**h**t**r**i**a**n**g**l**e**K* ∈ *K*[*h*], *a**n**d**v* = 0*o**n**Γ*}. (4.13)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si33.png)(4.13) 

Notice that *V*[*h*] ⊂ *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si34.png). The FEM for the PDE is formulated as

*F**i**n**d**p*[*h*] ∈ *V*[*h*]*s**u**c**h**t**h**a**t**a*(*p*[*h*],*v*) = *L*(*v*), ∀*v* ∈ *V*[*h*]. (4.14)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si35.png)(4.14) 

One can check that the abovementioned finite element formulation is equivalent to a discrete minimization problem:

*F**i**n**d**p*[*h*] ∈ *V*[*h*]*s**u**c**h**t**h**a**t**F*(*p*[*h*]) ≤ *F*(*v*), ∀*v* ∈ *V*[*h*]. (4.15)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si36.png)(4.15) 

which is also called the Ritz FEM.

Denote the vertices (nodes) of the triangles in *K*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si37.png) by **x**[1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si38.png), **x**[2]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si39.png), …, **x**[*N*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si40.png). The basis functions *φ*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si41.png) in *V*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si42.png), *i* = 1, 2, …, *N*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si43.png) are defined by

*φ*[*i*](**x**[*j*]) = 1*i**f**i* = *j*, *φ*[*i*](**x**[*j*]) = 0*i**f**i* ≠ *j*. (4.16)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si44.png)(4.16) 

The support of *φ*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si45.png), that is, the set of **x**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si46.png) where *φ*[*i*](**x**) ≠ 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si47.png), consists of the triangles with the common node **x**[*i*],![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si48.png) and the function *φ*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si49.png) is also called a hat or chapeau function.

Let *N*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si50.png) be the number of interior vertices in *K*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si51.png). Any function *v* ∈ *V*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si52.png) has the unique representation:

$v\\left( \\mathbf{x} \\right) = \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{N}{v\_{i}\\varphi\_{i}\\left( \\mathbf{x} \\right)},\\mathbf{x} \\in \\Omega,$ (4.17)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si53.png)(4.17) 

where *v*[*i*] = *v*(**x**[*i*]).![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si54.png) Due to the boundary condition, we exclude the vertices on the boundary of *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si55.png).

The Galerkin formulation can be written in matrix form **A****p** = **f**,![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si56.png) where the stiffness matrix **A**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si57.png) and vectors **p**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si58.png) and **f**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si59.png) are given by

**A** = (*a*[*i**j*]), **p** = (*p*[*j*]), **f** = (*f*[*j*]), (4.18)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si60.png)(4.18) 

and it can be checked that the stiffness matrix **A**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si61.png) is symmetric positive definite. In particular, it is nonsingular. Consequently, the FEM has a unique solution. Also, notice that **A**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si62.png) is sparse from the construction of the basis functions. In practical computations the entries *a*[*i**j*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si63.png) in **A**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si64.png) are obtained by summing the contributions from different triangles *K* ∈ *K*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si65.png):

$a\_{\\mathit{ij}} = a\\left( \\varphi\_{i},\\varphi\_{j} \\right) = \\sum\\limits\_{K \\in K\_{h}}{a^{K}\\left( \\varphi\_{i},\\varphi\_{j} \\right)} = \\sum\\limits\_{K \\in K\_{h}}a\_{\\mathit{ij}}^{K},$ (4.19)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si66.png)(4.19) 

where *a*[*i**j*]^(*K*) :  = *a*^(*K*)(*φ*[*i*],*φ*[*j*]) :  = ∫[*K*]∇*φ*[*i*] ⋅ ∇*φ*[*j*]*d***x**.![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si67.png) Using the definition of the basis functions, we see that *a*[*i**j*]^(*K*) = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si68.png) unless nodes **x**[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si69.png) and **x**[*j*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si70.png) are both vertices of *K*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si71.png).

For single-phase flow in porous media the problem model can be written as

$\\begin{matrix} {- \\nabla \\cdot K\\nabla p} & {= q{in}\\Omega,} \\\\ p & {= p\_{b}{on}\\Gamma\_{D},} \\\\ {- K\\nabla p \\cdot \\mathbf{n}} & {= u\_{b}{on}{\\Gamma}\_{N}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.20)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si72.png)(4.20) 

The weak form reads: to seek *p* ∈ *H*[0, *Γ*[*D*]](*Ω*) + *E*(*p*[*b*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si73.png) such that (with *p*[*b*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si74.png) essential boundary condition (BC) and *u*[*b*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si75.png) natural BC):

(*K*∇*p*,∇*v*) + ⟨*u*[*b*], *v*⟩[*Γ*[*N*]] = (*q*,*v*), ∀*v* ∈ *H*[0, *Γ*[*D*]](*Ω*). (4.21)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si76.png)(4.21) 

The Galerkin FEMs reads: to seek *p*[*h*] ∈ *V*[*h*] + *E*(*p*[*b*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si77.png) such that

(*K*∇*p*[*h*],∇*v*) + ⟨*u*[*b*], *v*⟩[*Γ*[*N*]] = (*q*,*v*), ∀*v* ∈ *V*[*h*](*Ω*). (4.22)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si78.png)(4.22)  

### 4.1.2 Abstract minimization and variational problems

We consider two problems:

1.  1. Abstract minimization formulation:
    
    *F**i**n**d**p* ∈ *V**s**u**c**h**t**h**a**t**F*(*p*) ≤ *F*(*v*), ∀*v* ∈ *V*, (4.23)
    
    ![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si79.png)(4.23) 
2.  2. Abstract variational formulation:
    
    *F**i**n**d**p* ∈ *V**s**u**c**h**t**h**a**t**a*(*p*,*v*) = *L*(*v*), ∀*v* ∈ *V*. (4.24)
    
    ![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si80.png)(4.24) 
    
      
    To analyze these two problems, we first assume the following four properties:
    
    *   • *a*(⋅,⋅)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si81.png) is symmetric, if *a*(*u*,*v*) = *a*(*v*,*u*), ∀*u*, *v* ∈ *V*.![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si82.png)
    *   • *a*(⋅,⋅)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si83.png) is continuous or bounded in the norm ∥ ⋅ ∥[*V*],![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si84.png) if there is a constant *a* \*  > 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si85.png) such that *|**a*(*u*,*v*)| ≤ *a*^(\*)∥*u*∥[*V*]∥*v*∥[*V*], ∀*u*, *v* ∈ *V*.![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si86.png)
    *   • *a*(⋅,⋅)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si87.png) is *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si88.png)\-elliptic or coercive, if there exists a constant *a* \*  > 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si89.png) such that *|**a*(*v*,*v*)| ≥ *a*[\*]∥*v*∥[*V*]², ∀*v* ∈ *V*.![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si90.png)
    *   • *L*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si91.png) is bounded in the norm ||⋅||[*V*]:|*L*(*v*)|≤*L̃*||*v*||[*V*],∀*v*∈*V*.![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si92.png)

*Riesz representation theorem*: Let *H*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si93.png) be a Hilbert space with the scalar product (⋅, ⋅ )[*H*],![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si94.png) then for any continuous linear functional *L*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si95.png) on *H*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si96.png), there is a unique *u* ∈ *H*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si97.png) such that *L*(*v*) = (*u*, *v*)[*H*].![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si98.png)

*Lax–Milgram theorem*: Under assumptions of symmetry, boundedness, and *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si99.png)\-ellipticity of *a*(⋅,⋅)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si100.png), and boundedness of *L*(⋅)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si101.png), the variational problem has a unique solution *p* ∈ *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si102.png) that satisfies the bound ||*p*||[*V*]≤(*L̃*/*a*[\*]).![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si103.png)

*Proof of Lax–Milgram theorem*: Since the bilinear form *a*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si104.png) is symmetric and *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si105.png)\-elliptic, it induces a scalar product in *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si106.png): \[*u*,*v*\] = *a*(*u*,*v*), *u*, *v* ∈ *V*.![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si107.png) By *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si108.png)\-ellipticity and boundedness of *a*(⋅,⋅)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si109.png), we see that

*a*[\*]∥*v*∥[*V*]² ≤ \[*v*,*v*\] ≤ *a*^(\*)∥*v*∥[*V*]², ∀*v* ∈ *V*. (4.25)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si110.png)(4.25) 

That is, the norm induced by \[⋅,⋅\]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si111.png) is equivalent to ∥ ⋅ ∥[*V*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si112.png). To this new norm, *L*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si113.png) is still a continuous linear functional. Thus according to the Riesz representation theorem, there is a unique *p* ∈ *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si114.png) such that \[*p*,*v*\] = *L*(*v*), ∀*v* ∈ *V*,![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si115.png) which show *p*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si116.png) is the solution.

To show stability, we take *v* = *p*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si117.png) in the finite element (FE) formulation and use *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si118.png)\-ellipticity of *a*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si119.png) and boundedness of *L*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si120.png) to see that

*a*[\*]||*p*||[*V*]²≤*a*(*p*,*p*)=*L*(*p*)≤*L̃*||*p*||[*V*], (4.26)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si121.png)(4.26) 

which yields ∥*p*∥[*V*] ≤ (*L̃*/*a*[\*]).![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si122.png)

Under the four abovementioned assumptions, it can be shown that the minimization problem and the variational problem are equivalent. One can check that the variational problem still possesses a unique solution even without the symmetry assumption. In this case, however, there is no corresponding minimization problem.

Suppose that *V*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si123.png) is a finite element (finite-dimensional) subspace of *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si124.png). The discrete counterpart of the abstract minimization problem is

*F**i**n**d**p*[*h*] ∈ *V*[*h*]*s**u**c**h**t**h**a**t**F*(*p*[*h*]) ≤ *F*(*v*), ∀*v* ∈ *V*[*h*], (4.27)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si125.png)(4.27) 

The discrete counterpart of the abstract variational problem is

*F**i**n**d**p*[*h*] ∈ *V*[*h*]*s**u**c**h**t**h**a**t**F*(*p*[*h*]) ≤ *F*(*v*), ∀*v* ∈ *V*[*h*], (4.28)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si126.png)(4.28) 

The previous Lax–Milgram theorem remains valid for discrete problems under the four assumptions. Moreover, the solution *p*[*h*] ∈ *V*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si127.png) satisfies ||*p*[*h*]||[*V*]≤*L̃*/*a*[\*].![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si128.png)

*Cea’s lemma (error estimate)*: Under the four assumptions mentioned earlier, if *p*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si129.png) and *p*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si130.png) are the respective solutions to the abstract variational problem and its discrete counterpart, then

$\\| p - p\_{h}\\|\_{V} \\leq \\frac{a^{\*}}{a\_{\*}}\\| p - v\\|\_{V},\\forall v \\in V\_{h}.$ (4.29)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si131.png)(4.29) 

*Proof of Cea’s lemma*: Subtracting the abstract variational problem by its discrete counterpart, we see that

*a*(*p*−*p*[*h*],*w*) = 0, ∀*w* ∈ *V*[*h*]. (4.30)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si132.png)(4.30) 

Using boundedness and *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si133.png)\-ellipticity of *a*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si134.png), for any *v* ∈ *V*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si135.png), it follows that

*a*[\*]∥*p* − *p*[*h*]∥[*V*]² ≤ *a*(*p*−*p*[*h*],*p*−*p*[*h*]) = *a*(*p*−*p*[*h*],*p*−*v*) ≤ *a*^(\*)∥*p* − *p*[*h*]∥[*V*]∥*p* − *v*∥[*V*], (4.31)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si136.png)(4.31) 

which implies our result.

 

### 4.1.3 Galerkin finite element methods: settings and notations

*The Riemann integral*, proposed by Bernhard Riemann (1826–66), is a broadly successful attempt to provide such a foundation. Riemann’s definition starts with the construction of a sequence of easily calculated areas that converge to the integral of a given function. This definition is successful in the sense that it gives the expected answer for many already solved problems and gives useful results for many other problems. However, Riemann integration does not interact well with taking limits of sequences of functions, making such limiting processes difficult to analyze. This is important, for instance, in the study of Fourier series, Fourier transforms, and other topics.

*The Lebesgue integral* is more useful in describing how and when it is possible to take limits under the integral sign (via the powerful monotone convergence theorem and dominated convergence theorem). While the Riemann integral considers the area under a curve as made out of vertical rectangles, the Lebesgue definition considers horizontal slabs that are not necessarily just rectangles, and so it is more flexible. For example, the Dirichlet function, which is 0 where its argument is irrational and 1 otherwise, has a Lebesgue integral (of zero value) but does not have a Riemann integral. (Recall the intuition that when picking a real number uniformly at random from the unit interval, the probability of picking a rational number should be zero.)

For a real-valued function *v*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si137.png) on *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si138.png), we use the notation ∫[*Ω*]*v*(**x**)*d***x**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si139.png) to denote the integral of *f*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si140.png) in the sense of Lebesgue. For 1 ≤ *q* < ∞![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si141.png), we define the norm

∥*v*∥[*L*^(*q*)(*Ω*)] :  = (∫[*Ω*]*|**v*(**x**)|^(*q*)*d***x**)^(1/*q*). (4.32)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si142.png)(4.32) 

For *q* = ∞![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si143.png), we set

∥*v*∥[*L*^∞(*Ω*)] :  = *e**s**s**s**u**p**{**|**v*(**x**)*|**:***x** ∈ *Ω*}, (4.33)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si144.png)(4.33) 

where *e**s**s**s**u**p*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si145.png) denotes the essential supremum.

For 1 ≤ *q* ≤ ∞,![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si146.png) we define the ***Lebesgue spaces***

*L*^(*q*)(*Ω*) :  = *{**v* *:* *v**i**s**d**e**f**i**n**e**d**o**n**Ω**a**n**d*∥*v*∥[*L*^(*q*)(*Ω*)] < ∞}. (4.34)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si147.png)(4.34) 

For *q* = 2![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si148.png), for example, *L*²(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si149.png) consists of all square-integrable functions on *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si150.png) (in the sense of Lebesgue). A linear space *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si151.png) endowed with a norm ∥ ⋅ ∥![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si152.png) is called a normed linear space. *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si153.png) is termed complete if every Cauchy sequence {*v*[*i*]}![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si154.png) in *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si155.png) has a limit *v*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si156.png) that is an element of *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si157.png). The Cauchy sequence {*v*[*i*]}![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si158.png) means that ∥*v*[*i*]−*v*[*j*]∥→0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si159.png) as *i*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si160.png), *j*→∞![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si161.png), and completeness says that ∥*v*[*i*]−*v*∥→0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si162.png) as *i*→∞![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si163.png).

A linear space *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si164.png), together with an inner product (⋅,⋅)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si165.png) defined on it, is called an inner product space and is represented by (*V*,(⋅,⋅))![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si166.png). With the inner product (⋅,⋅)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si167.png), there is an associated norm defined on *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si168.png): $\\| v\\|: = \\sqrt{\\left( v,v \\right)},v \\in V.$![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si169.png) Hence an inner product space can be always made to be a normed linear space. A normed linear space (*V*,∥⋅∥)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si170.png) is called a *Banach space*, if it is complete with respect to the norm ∥ ⋅ ∥![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si171.png). For 1 ≤ *q* ≤ ∞![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si172.png) the space *L*^(*q*)(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si173.png) is a Banach space. An inner product space (*V*,∥⋅∥)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si174.png) is called a *Hilbert space*, if it is complete with respect to the norm $\\| v\\|: = \\sqrt{\\left( v,v \\right)}.$![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si175.png)

We introduce the multiindex partial derivative notation:

$D^{\\alpha}v = \\frac{\\partial^{|\\alpha|}v}{\\partial x\_{1}^{\\alpha\_{1}}\\partial x\_{2}^{\\alpha\_{2}}\\cdots\\partial x\_{d}^{\\alpha\_{d}}},$ (4.35)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si176.png)(4.35) 

where *α* = (*α*[1],*α*[2],…,*α*[*d*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si177.png) is a multiindex (called a *d*\-tuple), with *α*[1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si178.png), *α*[2]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si179.png), …, *α*[*d*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si180.png) nonnegative integers, and |*α*| = *α*[1] + *α*[2] + ⋯ + *α*[*d*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si181.png) is the length of *α*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si182.png). In calculus, derivatives of a function are defined pointwise. The variational formulation in the FEM is given globally, that is, in terms of integrals on *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si183.png). Hence it is natural to introduce a global definition of derivative more suitable to the Lebesgue spaces.

For *Ω* ⊂ ℝ^(*d*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si184.png), we indicate by 𝒟(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si185.png) or *C*[0]^∞(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si186.png) the subset of *C*^∞(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si187.png) (i.e., infinitely differentiable) functions that have compact (i.e., bounded and closed) support in *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si188.png). In order to introduce weak derivatives, we define

*L*[*l**o**c*]¹(*Ω*) :  = *{**v* *:* *v* ∈ *L*¹(*K*)*f**o**r**a**n**y**c**o**m**p**a**c**t**K**i**n**s**i**d**e**Ω*}. (4.36)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si189.png)(4.36) 

We note that *C*⁰(*Ω*) ⊂ *L*[*l**o**c*]¹(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si190.png) and functions in *L*[*l**o**c*]¹(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si191.png) can behave arbitrarily badly near the boundary. A function *v* ∈ *L*[*l**o**c*]¹(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si192.png) is said to have a weak (generalized) derivative, *D*[*w*]^(*α*)*v**:* = *u*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si193.png), if there is *u* ∈ *L*[*l**o**c*]¹(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si194.png) such that

∫[*Ω*]*u*(**x**)*φ*(**x**)*d***x** = ( − 1)^(|*α*|)∫[*Ω*]*v*(**x**)*D*^(*α*)*φ*(**x**)*d***x**, ∀*φ* ∈ 𝒟(*Ω*). (4.37)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si195.png)(4.37) 

For any multiindex *α*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si196.png), if *v* ∈ *C*^(|*α*|)(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si197.png), the weak derivative *D*[*w*]^(*α*)*v*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si198.png) exists and equals *D*^(*α*)*v*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si199.png). Consequently, we will ignore the difference in the definition of *D*[*w*]^(*α*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si200.png) and *D*^(*α*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si201.png). Namely, if classical derivatives do not exist, the differentiation symbol *D*^(*α*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si202.png) will refer to weak derivatives. For *r* ∈ ℕ![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si203.png) and *v* ∈ *L*[*l**o**c*]¹(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si204.png), we define the *Sobolev norm*:

$\\| v\\|\_{W^{r,q}(\\Omega)}: = \\left( {\\sum\\limits\_{|\\alpha| \\leq r}{\\| D^{\\alpha}v\\|\_{L^{q}(\\Omega)}^{q}}} \\right)^{1/q},$ (4.38)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si205.png)(4.38) 

if 1 ≤ *q* < ∞![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si206.png). For *q* = ∞![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si207.png), we define

$\\| v\\|\_{W^{r,\\infty}(\\Omega)}: = \\max\\limits\_{|\\alpha| \\leq r}\\| D^{\\alpha}v\\|\_{L^{\\infty}(\\Omega)}.$ (4.39)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si208.png)(4.39) 

The *Sobolev spaces* are defined by

*W*^(*r*, *q*)(*Ω*) :  = *{**v* ∈ *L*[*l**o**c*]¹(*Ω*) : ∥*v*∥[*W*^(*r*, *q*)(*Ω*)] < ∞}, 1 ≤ *q* ≤ ∞. (4.40)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si209.png)(4.40) 

One can check that ∥ ⋅ ∥[*W*^(*r*, *q*)(*Ω*)]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si210.png) is indeed a norm; moreover, the Sobolev space *W*^(*r*, *q*)(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si211.png) is a Banach space. We denote by *W*[0]^(*r*, *q*)(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si212.png) the completion of 𝒟(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si213.png) with respect to the norm ∥ ⋅ ∥[*W*^(*r*, *q*)(*Ω*)]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si214.png). Furthermore, for *q* = 2![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si215.png), we will utilize the symbols

*H*^(*r*)(*Ω*) :  = *W*^(*r*, 2)(*Ω*), *H*[0]^(*r*)(*Ω*) :  = *W*[0]^(*r*, 2)(*Ω*), *r* = 1, 2, ⋯. (4.41)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si216.png)(4.41)  

### 4.1.4 Mixed finite element methods

In numerical analysis the mixed FEM (MFEM), also known as the hybrid FEM, is a type of FEM in which extra independent variables are introduced as unknown variables during the discretization of a PDE problem. The extra independent variables are constrained by using the Lagrange multipliers. To be distinguished from the MFEM, usual FEMs that do not introduce such extra independent variables are also called irreducible FEMs. The MFEM is efficient for some problems that would be numerically ill-posed if discretized by using the irreducible FEM; one example of such problems is to compute the stress and strain fields in an almost incompressible elastic body (locking effects in the Galerkin FEM).

The reason for using the mixed method is, among others, that in some applications a vector variable (e.g., a fluid velocity) is the primary variable in which one is interested. This is particularly true for flow problems. Then, the mixed method is developed to approximate both the vector variable and a scalar variable (e.g., a pressure) simultaneously and to give a high-order approximation of both variables. Instead of a single finite element space used in the standard FEM, the MFEM employs two different spaces, which suggests the name mixed. For flow problems, another obvious advantage of the MFEM is the mass (or volume) that is conserved locally in each element. In other words, it is locally conservative.

We need one approximation space for the scalar variable (e.g., pressure) and another for the vector variable (e.g., the Darcy velocity). These two spaces must satisfy an inf–sup condition for the mixed method to be stable. 

We consider a stationary problem for the unknown *p*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si217.png):

$\\begin{matrix} {- \\nabla \\cdot \\left( \\mathbf{K}\\nabla p \\right)} & {= f,{in}\\Omega,} \\\\ p & {= p\_{b}{on}\\Gamma = \\partial\\Omega,} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.42)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si218.png)(4.42) 

where *Ω* ⊂ ℝ^(*d*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si219.png) (*d* = 2![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si220.png) or 3) is a bounded 2D or 3D domain with boundary *Γ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si221.png), and *f* ∈ *L*²(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si222.png) is a given function. The mixed variational form reads: Find **u** ∈ **V** = **H**(*d**i**v*,*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si223.png) and *p* ∈ *W* = *L*²(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si224.png) such that

$\\begin{matrix} {\\left( \\mathbf{K}^{- 1}\\mathbf{u},\\mathbf{v} \\right) - \\left( \\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{v},p \\right)} & {= - \\int\_{\\Gamma}{p\_{b}\\mathbf{v} \\cdot \\nu}\\mathit{ds},\\forall\\mathbf{v} \\in \\mathbf{V},} \\\\ \\left( \\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{u},w \\right) & {= \\left( f,w \\right),\\forall w \\in W.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.43)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si225.png)(4.43) 

Conductivity **K**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si226.png) is assumed to be bounded, symmetric, and uniformly positive definite in **x**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si227.png): ∀**x** ∈ *Ω*, ∀*η* ∈ ℝ^(*d*) ∖ 0,![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si228.png)

$0 < a\_{\*} \\leq {|\\eta|}^{- 2}\\sum\\limits\_{i,j = 1}^{d}{K\_{\\mathit{ij}}\\left( \\mathbf{x} \\right)\\eta\_{i}\\eta\_{j}} \\leq a^{\*} < \\infty.$ (4.44)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si229.png)(4.44) 

There is *C*[1] > 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si230.png) such that the inf–sup condition holds

$\\underset{0 \\neq \\mathbf{v} \\in \\mathbf{V}}{\\mathit{\\sup}}\\frac{|\\left( \\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{v},w \\right)|}{\\|\\mathbf{v}\\|\_{\\mathbf{V}}} \\geq C\_{1}\\| w\\|,\\forall w \\in W.$ (4.45)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si231.png)(4.45) 

Because of the property on *K*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si232.png) (bounded and spd) and the inf–sup condition, the mixed variational form has a unique solution **u** ∈ **V**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si233.png) and *p* ∈ *W*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si234.png).

Let **V**[*h*] ⊂ **V**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si235.png) and *W*[*h*] ⊂ *W*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si236.png) be certain finite-dimensional subspaces. The discrete version of the previous “mixed variational form” is: Find **u**[*h*] ∈ **V**[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si237.png) and *p*[*h*] ∈ *W*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si238.png) such that

$\\begin{matrix} {\\left( \\mathbf{K}^{- 1}\\mathbf{u}\_{h},\\mathbf{v} \\right) - \\left( \\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{v},p\_{h} \\right)} & {= - \\int\_{\\Gamma}{p\_{b}\\mathbf{v} \\cdot \\nu}\\mathit{ds},\\forall\\mathbf{v} \\in \\mathbf{V}\_{h},} \\\\ \\left( \\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{u}\_{h},w \\right) & {= \\left( f,w \\right),\\forall w \\in W\_{h}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.46)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si239.png)(4.46) 

For this problem to have a unique solution, it is natural to impose a discrete inf–sup condition:

$\\underset{0 \\neq \\mathbf{v} \\in \\mathbf{V}\_{h}}{\\mathit{\\sup}}\\frac{|\\left( \\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{v},w \\right)|}{\\|\\mathbf{v}\\|\_{\\mathbf{V}}} \\geq C\_{2}\\| w\\|\_{W},\\forall w \\in W\_{h},$ (4.47)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si240.png)(4.47) 

where *C*[2] > 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si241.png) is a constant independent of *h*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si242.png).

The condition next is for the solvability of MFEM:

$\\sup\\limits\_{0 \\neq \\mathbf{v} \\in \\mathbf{V}\_{h}}\\frac{|\\left( \\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{v},w \\right)|}{\\|\\mathbf{v}\\|\_{\\mathbf{V}}} \\geq C\_{2}\\| w\\|\_{W},\\forall w \\in W\_{h}.$ (4.48)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si243.png)(4.48) 

The condition can be rewritten as

$\\inf\\limits\_{0 \\neq w \\in W\_{h}}\\sup\\limits\_{0 \\neq \\mathbf{v} \\in \\mathbf{V}\_{h}}\\frac{|\\left( \\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{v},w \\right)|}{\\|\\mathbf{v}\\|\_{\\mathbf{V}}\\| w\\|\_{W}} \\geq C\_{2} > 0.$ (4.49)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si244.png)(4.49) 

It is the (discrete) stability condition, also known as the discrete inf–sup condition. It is also called the Babuska–Brezzi condition or sometimes the Ladyshenskaja–Babuska–Brezzi condition.

For *Ω* ⊂ ℝ²![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si245.png), let 𝒦[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si246.png) be a partition of *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si247.png) into triangles such that adjacent elements completely share their common edge. For a triangle *K* ∈ 𝒦[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si248.png), let

*P*[*r*](*K*) = *{**v* *:* *v**i**s**a**p**o**l**y**n**o**m**i**a**l**o**f**d**e**g**r**e**e**a**t**m**o**s**t**r**o**n**K**}*, (4.50)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si249.png)(4.50) 

where *r* ≥ 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si250.png) is an integer. Mixed finite element spaces **V**[*h*] × *W*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si251.png) are defined locally on each element *K* ∈ 𝒦[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si252.png), so let

**V**[*h*](*K*) = **V**[*h*]|[*K*] (4.51)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si253.png)(4.51) 

(the restriction of **V**[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si254.png) to *K*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si255.png)) and

*W*[*h*](*K*) = *W*[*h*]|[*K*]. (4.52)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si256.png)(4.52) 

*Raviart-Thomas (RT) spaces on triangles* are the first mixed finite element spaces introduced by 

**V**[*h*](*K*) = (*P*[*r*](*K*))² ⊕ ((*x*[1],*x*[2])*P*[*r*](*K*)), *W*[*h*](*K*) = *P*[*r*](*K*), (4.53)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si258.png)(4.53) 

where the notation ⊕![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si259.png) indicates a direct sum. For *r* = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si260.png), we observe that **V**[*h*](*K*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si261.png) has the form

**V**[*h*](*K*) = {**v** : **v** = (*b*[*K*]*x*[1]+*a*[*K*],*b*[*K*]*x*[2]+*c*[*K*]), *a*[*K*], *b*[*K*], *c*[*K*] ∈ ℝ}, (4.54)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si262.png)(4.54) 

and its dimension is three. In the case *r* = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si263.png), as the degrees of freedom (DOF) to describe the functions in **V**[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si264.png), we use the values of normal components of the functions at the midpoints of edges in 𝒦[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si265.png), and the DOF for *W*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si266.png) can be the averages of functions over *K*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si267.png). In general, for *r* ≥ 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si268.png), the dimensions of **V**[*h*](*K*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si269.png) and *W*[*h*](*K*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si270.png) are

$\\dim\\left( \\mathbf{V}\_{h}\\left( K \\right) \\right) = \\left( r + 1 \\right)\\left( r + 3 \\right),\\dim\\left( W\_{h}\\left( K \\right) \\right) = \\frac{\\left( r + 1 \\right)\\left( r + 2 \\right)}{2}.$ (4.55)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si271.png)(4.55) 

The DOF for the space **V**[*h*](*K*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si272.png), with *r* ≥ 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si273.png), are given by

(**v** ⋅ *ν*, *w*)[*e*], ∀*w* ∈ *P*[*r*](*e*), *e* ∈ ∂*K*, (4.56)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si274.png)(4.56) 

(**v**, **w**)[*K*], ∀**w** ∈ (*P*[*r* − 1](*K*))². (4.57)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si275.png)(4.57) 

This is indeed a legitimate choice; that is, a function in **V**[*h*](*K*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si276.png) is uniquely determined by these DOF. It is a square system and uniqueness implies existence.

We now consider the case where *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si277.png) is a rectangular domain and 𝒦[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si278.png) is a partition of *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si279.png) into rectangles such that the horizontal and vertical edges of rectangles are parallel to the *x*[1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si280.png)\- and *x*[2]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si281.png)\-coordinate axes, respectively, and adjacent elements completely share their common edge. Define

$Q\_{l,r}\\left( K \\right): = \\left\\{ {\\mathit{v:v}\\left( \\mathbf{x} \\right) = \\sum\\limits\_{i = 0}^{l}{\\sum\\limits\_{j = 0}^{r}{v\_{\\mathit{ij}}x\_{1}^{i}x\_{2}^{j}}},\\mathbf{x} = \\left( x\_{1},x\_{2} \\right) \\in K,v\_{\\mathit{ij}} \\in \\mathbb{R}} \\right\\}.$ (4.58)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si282.png)(4.58) 

That is, *Q*[*l*, *r*](*K*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si283.png) is the space of polynomials of degree at most *l*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si284.png) in *x*[1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si285.png) and *r*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si286.png) in *x*[2]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si287.png), *l*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si288.png),*r* ≥ 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si289.png).

The *RT spaces on rectangles* are an extension of the RT spaces on triangles to rectangles (

$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathbf{V}\_{h}(K) = Q\_{r + 1,r}(K) \\times Q\_{r,r + 1}(K),} \\\\ {W\_{h}\\left( K \\right) = Q\_{r,r}\\left( K \\right).} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.59)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si291.png)(4.59) 

In the case *r* = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si292.png), **V**[*h*](*K*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si293.png) takes the form:

**V**[*h*](*K*) = {**v** : **v** = (*a*[*K*]¹+*a*[*K*]²*x*[1],*a*[*K*]³+*a*[*K*]⁴*x*[2]), *a*[*K*]^(*i*) ∈ ℝ, *i* = 1, 2, 3, 4}. (4.60)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si294.png)(4.60) 

and its dimension is four. The DOF for **V**[*h*](*K*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si295.png) are the values of normal components of functions at the midpoint on each edge in 𝒦[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si296.png). For a general *r* ≥ 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si297.png) the dimensions of **V**[*h*](*K*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si298.png) and *W*[*h*](*K*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si299.png) are

$\\begin{array}{l} {\\dim(\\mathbf{V}\_{h}(K)) = 2(r + 1)(r + 2),} \\\\ {\\dim(W\_{h}(K)) = {(r + 1)}^{2}.} \\\\ \\end{array}$ (4.61)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si300.png)(4.61) 

The DOF for **V**[*h*](*K*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si301.png) are given by

$\\begin{matrix} {{(\\mathbf{v} \\cdot \\nu,w)}\_{e},\\forall w \\in P\_{r}(e),e \\in \\partial K,} \\\\ {{(\\mathbf{v},\\mathbf{w})}\_{K},\\forall\\mathbf{w} \\in Q\_{r - 1,r}(K) \\times Q\_{r,r - 1}(K).} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.62)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si302.png)(4.62) 

The *BDM (or Brezzi–Douglas–Marini) spaces* (Brezzi et al., 1985) on rectangles differ considerably from the RT spaces on rectangles: The vector elements are based on augmenting the space of vector polynomials of total degree *r*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si303.png) by exactly two additional vectors in place of augmenting the space of vector tensor–products of polynomials of degree *r*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si304.png) by 2*r* + 2![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si305.png) polynomials of higher degree. Besides, a lower dimensional space for the scalar variable is used. The BDM spaces, for any *r* ≥ 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si306.png) are given by

$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathbf{V}\_{h}(K) = {(P\_{r}(K))}^{2} \\oplus {span}\\left\\{ \\mathbf{c}\\mathbf{u}\\mathbf{r}\\mathbf{l}(x\_{1}^{r + 1}x\_{2}),\\mathbf{c}\\mathbf{u}\\mathbf{r}\\mathbf{l}(x\_{1}x\_{2}^{r + 1}) \\right\\},} \\\\ {W\_{h}(K) = P\_{r - 1}(K).} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.63)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si307.png)(4.63) 

In the case *r* = 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si308.png), **V**[*h*](*K*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si309.png) can be represented by

$\\begin{matrix} \\left\\{ \\mathbf{v}:\\mathbf{v} = (v\_{1},v\_{2}),v\_{1} = (a\_{K}^{1} + a\_{K}^{2}x\_{1} + a\_{K}^{3}x\_{2} - a\_{K}^{4}x\_{1}^{2} - 2a\_{K}^{5}x\_{1}x\_{2}), \\right. \\\\ \\left. v\_{2} = (a\_{K}^{6} + a\_{K}^{7}x\_{1} + a\_{K}^{8}x\_{2} + 2a\_{K}^{4}x\_{1}x\_{2} + a\_{K}^{5}x\_{2}^{2}), \\right\\} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.64)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si310.png)(4.64) 

and its dimension is eight. The DOF for **V**[*h*](*K*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si311.png) are the values of normal components of functions at the two quadratic Gauss points on each edge in 𝒦[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si312.png). For any *r* ≥ 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si313.png), the dimensions of **V**[*h*](*K*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si314.png) and *W*[*h*](*K*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si315.png) are

$\\begin{matrix} {\\dim(\\mathbf{V}\_{h}(K)) = (r + 1)(r + 2) + 2,} \\\\ {\\dim(W\_{h}(K)) = \\frac{r(r + 1)}{2}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.65)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si316.png)(4.65) 

The DOF for **V**[*h*](*K*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si317.png) are

$\\begin{matrix} {{(\\mathbf{v} \\cdot \\nu,w)}\_{e},\\forall w \\in P\_{r}(e),e \\in \\partial K,} \\\\ {{(\\mathbf{v},\\mathbf{w})}\_{K},\\forall\\mathbf{w} \\in {(P\_{r - 2}(K))}^{2}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.66)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si318.png)(4.66) 

The MFEM can be recast in matrix form:

$\\begin{bmatrix} \\mathbf{A} & \\mathbf{B} \\\\ \\mathbf{B}^{T} & 0 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}\\begin{pmatrix} \\mathbf{u} \\\\ \\mathbf{p} \\\\ \\end{pmatrix} = \\begin{pmatrix} \\mathbf{g} \\\\ {- \\mathbf{f}} \\\\ \\end{pmatrix}.$ (4.67)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si319.png)(4.67) 

The overall matrix

$M = \\begin{bmatrix} \\mathbf{A} & \\mathbf{B} \\\\ \\mathbf{B}^{T} & 0 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}$ (4.68)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si320.png)(4.68) 

is nonsingular under the inf–sup condition, but it is not positive definite. This limits application of many iterative algorithms to the resultant algebraic equations.

The Uzawa algorithm (

$\\begin{array}{l} {\\mathbf{A}\\mathbf{u}^{k} = \\mathbf{g} - \\mathbf{B}\\mathbf{p}^{k - 1},} \\\\ {\\mathbf{p}^{k} = \\mathbf{p}^{k - 1} + \\alpha(\\mathbf{B}^{T}\\mathbf{u}^{k} + \\mathbf{f}),} \\\\ \\end{array}$ (4.69)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si324.png)(4.69) 

where *α*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si325.png) is a given real number. To see convergence of The Uzawa algorithm, we define the residual:

**e**^(*k*) :  =  − **B**^(*T*)**u**^(*k*) − **f**. (4.70)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si326.png)(4.70) 

We note that

**B**^(*T*)**A**^( − 1)**B****p** = **B**^(*T*)**A**^( − 1)**g** + **f**. (4.71)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si327.png)(4.71) 

Using the abovementioned equation and the algorithm, we see that

**e**^(*k*) =  − **B**^(*T*)**A**^( − 1)(**g**−**B****p**^(*k* − 1)) − **f** =  − **B**^(*T*)**A**^( − 1)**B**(**p**−**p**^(*k* − 1)). (4.72)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si328.png)(4.72) 

So

**p**^(*k*) − **p**^(*k* − 1) =  − *α***e**^(*k*) = *α***B**^(*T*)**A**^( − 1)**B**(**p**−**p**^(*k* − 1)). (4.73)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si329.png)(4.73)  

### 4.1.5 Mixed–hybrid finite element methods

The usual mixed formulation requires the solution of a linear system in the form of a saddle point problem, which can be expensive to solve. An alternate approach is proposed then involving the hybrid (or the Lagrange multiplier) form of the equations. In this method, one eliminates the pressure and velocity unknowns in terms of the Lagrange multipliers. There are more overall unknowns: the lowest order RT spaces have one the Lagrange multiplier unknown per edge if *d* = 2![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si330.png) or per face if *d* = 3![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si331.png). It is simple to implement and requires the solution of a sparse, positive semidefinite linear system.

For convenience of presentation, we assume *p*[*b*] = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si332.png) in the model problem. That is, we now consider a stationary problem for the unknown *p*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si333.png):

$\\begin{matrix} {- \\nabla \\cdot \\left( \\mathbf{K}\\nabla p \\right)} & {= f,{in}\\Omega,} \\\\ p & {= 0{on}\\Gamma = \\partial\\Omega,} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.74)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si334.png)(4.74) 

where *Ω* ⊂ ℝ^(*d*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si335.png) (*d* = 2![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si336.png) or 3) is a bounded 2D or 3D domain with boundary *Γ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si337.png), and *f* ∈ *L*²(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si338.png) is a given function.

We recall

**V**[*h*] :  = {**v** ∈ **V** : **v**|[*K*] ∈ **V**[*h*](*K*)∀*K* ∈ 𝒦[*h*]}. (4.75)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si339.png)(4.75) 

The constraint **V**[*h*] ⊂ **V**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si340.png) implies that the normal components of the functions in **V**[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si341.png) are continuous across the interior boundaries in 𝒦[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si342.png). Following 

${\\widetilde{\\mathbf{V}}}\_{h}: = \\left\\{ \\mathbf{v} \\in \\left( {L^{2}\\left( \\Omega \\right)} \\right)^{d}:\\mathbf{v}|\_{K} \\in \\mathbf{V}\_{h}\\left( K \\right)\\forall K \\in \\mathcal{K}\_{h} \\right\\}.$ (4.76)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si345.png)(4.76) 

We note that **V**[*h*] ⊂ **V**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si346.png) and $\\mathbf{V}\_{h} \\subset {\\widetilde{\\mathbf{V}}}\_{h}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si347.png), but ${\\widetilde{\\mathbf{V}}}\_{h} ⊄ \\mathbf{V}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si348.png).

We need to introduce the Lagrange multipliers to enforce the required continuity on ${\\widetilde{\\mathbf{V}}}\_{h}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si349.png). In order to do this, we define

$L\_{h}: = \\{\\mu \\in L^{2}\\left( \\bigcup\\limits\_{e \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}e \\right):{\\mu|}\_{e} \\in {\\mathbf{V}\_{h} \\cdot \\nu|}\_{e}\\forall e \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}\\},$ (4.77)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si350.png)(4.77) 

where ℰ[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si351.png) indicates the set of all edges or faces in 𝒦[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si352.png).

The hybrid form of the mixed method is: Find (**u**[*h*],*p*[*h*],*λ*[*h*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si353.png) $\\in {\\widetilde{\\mathbf{V}}}\_{h} \\times W\_{h} \\times L\_{h}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si354.png) such that

$\\begin{matrix} {\\left( {\\mathbf{u}\_{h},\\mathbf{v}} \\right) - \\sum\\limits\_{K \\in \\mathcal{K}\_{h}}\\left( {\\left( {\\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{v},p\_{h}} \\right)\_{K} - \\mathbf{v} \\cdot \\nu\_{K},\\lambda\_{h\\partial K \\smallsetminus \\Gamma}} \\right) = 0,\\quad\\forall v \\in {\\widetilde{\\mathbf{V}}}\_{h},} \\\\ {\\sum\\limits\_{K \\in \\mathcal{K}\_{h}}\\left( {\\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{u}\_{h},w} \\right)\_{K} = \\left( {f,w} \\right),\\quad\\forall w \\in W\_{h},} \\\\ {\\sum\\limits\_{K \\in \\mathcal{K}\_{h}}\\left\\langle {\\mathbf{u}\_{h} \\cdot \\nu\_{K},\\mu} \\right\\rangle\_{\\partial K \\smallsetminus \\Gamma} = 0,\\quad\\forall\\mu \\in L\_{h},} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.78)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si355.png)(4.78) 

where *ν*[*K*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si356.png) denotes the outward unit normal to *K*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si357.png). Note that the abovementioned third equation enforces the continuity requirement on **u**[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si358.png). In fact, we have **u**[*h*] ∈ **V**[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si359.png). The matrix form could be expressed as

$\\begin{bmatrix} \\mathbf{A} & \\mathbf{B} & \\mathbf{C} \\\\ \\mathbf{B}^{T} & 0 & 0 \\\\ \\mathbf{C}^{T} & 0 & 0 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}\\begin{pmatrix} \\mathbf{u} \\\\ \\mathbf{p} \\\\ \\lambda \\\\ \\end{pmatrix} = \\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\\\ {- \\mathbf{f}} \\\\ 0 \\\\ \\end{pmatrix},$ (4.79)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si360.png)(4.79) 

where *λ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si361.png) is the DOF of *λ*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si362.png). The advantage of the system previously is that the matrix **A**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si363.png) is block diagonal, with each block corresponding to a single element. Hence **A**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si364.png) is easily inverted at the element level. Taking advantage of the fact that the matrix **A**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si365.png) is block diagonal (hence **A**^( − 1)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si366.png) can be easily obtained), the first equation in the matrix form of the mixed–hybrid FEM (MHFEM) leads to

**u** =  − **A**^( − 1)**B****p** − **A**^( − 1)**C***λ*. (4.80)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si367.png)(4.80) 

Substituting it into the second and third equations in the matrix form of the MFHEM, we see that

$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathbf{B}^{T}\\mathbf{A}^{- 1}\\mathbf{B}\\mathbf{p} + \\mathbf{B}^{T}\\mathbf{A}^{- 1}\\mathbf{C}\\lambda = \\mathbf{f},} \\\\ {\\mathbf{C}^{T}\\mathbf{A}^{- 1}\\mathbf{B}\\mathbf{p} + \\mathbf{C}^{T}\\mathbf{A}^{- 1}\\mathbf{C}\\lambda = \\mathbf{f}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.81)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si368.png)(4.81) 

We note **B**^(*T*)**A**^( − 1)**B**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si369.png) is symmetric and positive definite, so the first equation of the algebraic equation (AE) system in [Eq. (4.81)](#chp004_a.xhtml#eqn81) yields:

**p** = (**B**^(*T*)**A**^( − 1)**B**)^( − 1)**f** − (**B**^(*T*)**A**^( − 1)**B**)^( − 1)**B**^(*T*)**A**^( − 1)**C***λ*. (4.82)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si370.png)(4.82) 

Substituting this equation into the second equation of the AE system in [Eq. (4.81)](#chp004_a.xhtml#eqn81) implies the linear system for *λ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si371.png):

(**C**^(*T*)**A**^( − 1)**C** − (**C**^(*T*)**A**^( − 1)**B**)(**B**^(*T*)**A**^( − 1)**B**)^( − 1)(**B**^(*T*)**A**^( − 1)**C**))*λ* =  − (**C**^(*T*)**A**^( − 1)**B**)(**B**^(*T*)**A**^( − 1)**B**)^( − 1)**f**. (4.83)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si372.png)(4.83) 

This system for *λ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si373.png) is symmetric, positive definite, and sparse. We can solve it for *λ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si374.png) (via e.g., an iterative algorithm), recover **p**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si375.png), and then recover **u**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si376.png).

From another point of view, we note that, after reordering the unknowns within **u**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si377.png) and within **p**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si378.png), the matrix $\\begin{bmatrix} \\mathbf{A} & \\mathbf{B} \\\\ \\mathbf{B}^{T} & 0 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si379.png) becomes a block-diagonal matrix (denoted as **D**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si380.png)), with each block corresponding to a single element. We thus can invert the matrix **D**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si381.png) (and thus the 2 × 2![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si382.png) block matrix above) easily at the element level. The unknowns **u**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si383.png) and **p**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si384.png) can be solved by the equation system next, if *λ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si385.png) is given:

$\\begin{bmatrix} \\mathbf{A} & \\mathbf{B} \\\\ \\mathbf{B}^{T} & 0 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}\\begin{pmatrix} \\mathbf{u} \\\\ \\mathbf{p} \\\\ \\end{pmatrix} + \\begin{bmatrix} \\mathbf{C} \\\\ 0 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}\\begin{pmatrix} \\lambda \\\\ \\end{pmatrix} = \\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\\\ {- \\mathbf{f}} \\\\ \\end{pmatrix}.$ (4.84)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si386.png)(4.84) 

After local computation, we can obtain the unknowns **u**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si387.png) and **p**,![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si388.png) if *λ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si389.png) is given:

$\\begin{pmatrix} \\mathbf{u} \\\\ \\mathbf{p} \\\\ \\end{pmatrix} = - \\begin{bmatrix} \\mathbf{A} & \\mathbf{B} \\\\ \\mathbf{B}^{T} & 0 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}^{- 1}\\begin{pmatrix} {\\mathbf{C}\\lambda} \\\\ \\mathbf{f} \\\\ \\end{pmatrix}.$ (4.85)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si390.png)(4.85) 

It can be noted that inverting the matrix $\\begin{bmatrix} \\mathbf{A} & \\mathbf{B} \\\\ \\mathbf{B}^{T} & 0 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si391.png) involves only local computation at the element level. Substituting this equation into the third equation of the AE system (as listed in [Eq. 4.81](#chp004_a.xhtml#eqn81)) implies the linear system for *λ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si392.png).

 

## 4.2 Links between finite-difference methods and finite element methods

 

### 4.2.1 Model problem

We consider a stationary problem for the unknown *p*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si393.png),

$\\begin{matrix} {- \\nabla \\cdot \\left( \\mathbf{K}\\nabla p \\right)} & {= f,{in}\\Omega,} \\\\ p & {= 0{on}\\Gamma = \\partial\\Omega,} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.86)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si394.png)(4.86) 

where *Ω* ⊂ ℝ^(*d*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si395.png) is a bounded one-dimensional (1D), 2D, or 3D domain with boundary *Γ*,![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si396.png) and *f* ∈ *L*²(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si397.png) is a given function. Conductivity **K**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si398.png) is assumed to be bounded, symmetric, and uniformly positive definite in **x**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si399.png): ∀**x** ∈ *Ω*, ∀*η* ∈ ℝ^(*d*) ∖ 0,![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si400.png)

$0 < a\_{\*} \\leq {|\\eta|}^{- 2}\\sum\\limits\_{i,j = 1}^{d}{K\_{\\mathit{ij}}\\left( \\mathbf{x} \\right)\\eta\_{i}\\eta\_{j}} \\leq a^{\*} < \\infty.$ (4.87)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si401.png)(4.87) 

We assume that *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si402.png) is a rectangular domain. Let *T*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si403.png) be a partition of *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si404.png) into nonoverlapping (open) elements *E*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si405.png) that are intervals (1D), rectangles (2D), or rectangles (3D). Conductivity **K**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si406.png) is assumed to be a diagonal tensor with cell-wise constant coefficients. For example, if 2D, we have

$\\mathbf{K} = \\begin{bmatrix} K^{\\mathit{xx}} & 0 \\\\ 0 & K^{\\mathit{yy}} \\\\ \\end{bmatrix},$ (4.88)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si407.png)(4.88) 

where *K*^(*x**x*) = *K*^(*x**x*)(*x*,*y*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si408.png) and *K*^(*y**y*) = *K*^(*y**y*)(*x*,*y*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si409.png) are cell-wise constant functions.

 

### 4.2.2 Equivalence between Galerkin finite element methods and point-centered finite-difference methods

We let *V* = *H*[0]¹(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si410.png), and the (Galerkin) weak form reads: Find *p* ∈ *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si411.png) such that

(**K**∇*p*,∇*v*) = (*f*,*v*), ∀*v* ∈ *V*. (4.89)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si412.png)(4.89) 

Furthermore, if we set *V*[*h*] ⊂ *V*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si413.png) be a certain finite-dimensional subspace. The Galerkin FEM reads: Find *p*[*h*] ∈ *V*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si414.png) such that

(**K**∇*p*[*h*],∇*v*) = (*f*,*v*), ∀*v* ∈ *V*[*h*]. (4.90)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si415.png)(4.90) 

The Galerkin FEM solution has the unique representation

$p\\left( \\mathbf{x} \\right) = \\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{m - 1}{p\_{j}\\varphi\_{j}\\left( \\mathbf{x} \\right)},\\mathbf{x} \\in \\Omega.$ (4.91)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si416.png)(4.91) 

We set *v* = *φ*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si417.png) in the Galerkin FEM and obtain

$\\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{m - 1}{\\left( \\mathbf{K}\\nabla\\varphi\_{j},\\nabla\\varphi\_{i} \\right)p\_{j}} = \\left( {f,\\varphi\_{i}} \\right),i = 1,2,\\ldots,m - 1.$ (4.92)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si418.png)(4.92) 

The resultant algebraic equation system becomes

$\\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{m - 1}{a\_{\\mathit{ij}}p\_{j}} = b\_{i},i = 1,2,\\cdots,m - 1.$ (4.93)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si419.png)(4.93) 

where *a*[*i**j*] = (**K***φ*[*j*]^′,*φ*[*i*]^′), *b*[*i*] = (*f*,*φ*[*i*]), *i*, *j* = 1, 2, …, *m* − 1.![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si420.png) We recall that if *x*[*i* − 1] ≤ *x* ≤ *x*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si421.png), *φ*[*i*](*x*) = ((*x*−*x*[*i* − 1])/(*x*[*i*]−*x*[*i* − 1]))![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si422.png), if *x*[*i*] ≤ *x* ≤ *x*[*i* + 1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si423.png), then *φ*[*i*](*x*) = ((*x*−*x*[*i* + 1])/(*x*[*i*]−*x*[*i* + 1]))![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si424.png), and *φ*[*i*](*x*) = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si425.png) elsewhere. Afterward, we can obtain that if *x*[*i* − 1] ≤ *x* ≤ *x*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si426.png), *φ*[*i*]^′(*x*) = (1/(*x*[*i*]−*x*[*i* − 1]))![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si427.png), if *x*[*i*] ≤ *x* ≤ *x*[*i* + 1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si428.png), *φ*[*i*]^′(*x*) =  − (1/(*x*[*i* + 1]−*x*[*i*]))![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si429.png), and *φ*[*i*]^′(*x*) = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si430.png) elsewhere.

To calculate the coefficient matrix, we set *h*[*i* − 0.5] = *x*[*i*] − *x*[*i* − 1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si431.png) and then calculate

$\\begin{matrix} a\_{ii} & {= {(\\mathbf{K}{\\varphi\\prime}\_{i},{\\varphi\\prime}\_{i})}\_{E\_{i - 0.5}} + {(\\mathbf{K}{\\varphi\\prime}\_{i},{\\varphi\\prime}\_{i})}\_{E\_{i + 0.5}}} \\\\ & \\left. = \\middle| E\_{i - 0.5} \\middle| K\_{i - 0.5}\\left( \\frac{1}{h\_{i - 0.5}} \\right)^{2} + \\middle| E\_{i + 0.5} \\middle| K\_{i + 0.5}\\left( {- \\frac{1}{h\_{i + 0.5}}} \\right)^{2} \\right. \\\\ & {= \\frac{K\_{i - 0.5}}{h\_{i - 0.5}} + \\frac{K\_{i + 0.5}}{h\_{i + 0.5}}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.94)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si432.png)(4.94) 

Similarly, we have

$\\begin{matrix} a\_{i,i - 1} & {= {(\\mathbf{K}{\\varphi\\prime}\_{i - 1},{\\varphi\\prime}\_{i})}\_{E\_{i - 0.5}} = - \\frac{K\_{i - 0.5}}{h\_{i - 0.5}},} \\\\ a\_{i,i + 1} & {= {(\\mathbf{K}{\\varphi\\prime}\_{i + 1},{\\varphi\\prime}\_{i})}\_{E\_{i + 0.5}} = - \\frac{K\_{i + 0.5}}{h\_{i + 0.5}}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.95)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si433.png)(4.95) 

To calculate the right-hand side of [Eq. (4.93)](#chp004_a.xhtml#eqn93), using the trapezoidal quadrature rule \[denoted as (⋅, ⋅ )[*Q*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si434.png)\] next, we can have

$b\_{i} = (f,\\varphi\_{i}) \\approx {(f,\\varphi\_{i})}\_{Q} = \\frac{h\_{i - 0.5} + h\_{i + 0.5}}{2}f\_{i}$ (4.96)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si435.png)(4.96) 

For *i* = 2, 3, …, *m* − 2![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si436.png), [Eq. (4.93)](#chp004_a.xhtml#eqn93) becomes

$- \\frac{K\_{i - 0.5}}{h\_{i - 0.5}}p\_{i - 1} + \\left( {\\frac{K\_{i - 0.5}}{h\_{i - 0.5}} + \\frac{K\_{i + 0.5}}{h\_{i + 0.5}}} \\right)p\_{i} - \\frac{K\_{i + 0.5}}{h\_{i + 0.5}}p\_{i + 1} = \\frac{h\_{i - 0.5} + h\_{i + 0.5}}{2}f\_{i}.$ (4.97)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si437.png)(4.97) 

which is exactly the same as the point-centered finite-difference method:

$- \\frac{K\_{i + 0.5}\\left( \\left( p\_{i + 1} - p\_{i} \\right)/\\left( x\_{i + 1} - x\_{i} \\right) \\right) - K\_{i - 0.5}\\left( \\left( p\_{i} - p\_{i - 1} \\right)/\\left( x\_{i} - x\_{i - 1} \\right) \\right)}{x\_{i + 0.5} - x\_{i - 0.5}} = f\_{i}, i = 2,\\ldots,m - 2.$ (4.98)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si438.png)(4.98)  

### 4.2.3 Equivalence between mixed finite element methods and cell-centered finite-difference methods

The mixed variational form reads: Find **u** ∈ **V** = **H**(*d**i**v*,*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si439.png) and *p* ∈ *W* = *L*²(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si440.png) such that

$\\begin{matrix} {\\left( \\mathbf{K}^{- 1}\\mathbf{u},\\mathbf{v} \\right) - \\left( \\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{v},p \\right)} & {= 0,\\forall\\mathbf{v} \\in \\mathbf{V},} \\\\ \\left( \\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{u},w \\right) & {= \\left( f,w \\right),\\forall w \\in W.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.99)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si441.png)(4.99) 

Let **V**[*h*] ⊂ **V**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si442.png) and *W*[*h*] ⊂ *W*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si443.png) be certain finite-dimensional subspaces. The MFEM reads: Find **u**[*h*] ∈ **V**[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si444.png) and *p*[*h*] ∈ *W*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si445.png) such that

$\\begin{matrix} {\\left( \\mathbf{K}^{- 1}\\mathbf{u}\_{h},\\mathbf{v} \\right) - \\left( \\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{v},p\_{h} \\right)} & {= 0,\\forall\\mathbf{v} \\in \\mathbf{V}\_{h},} \\\\ \\left( \\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{u}\_{h},w \\right) & {= \\left( f,w \\right),\\forall w \\in W\_{h}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.100)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si446.png)(4.100) 

The inf–sup conditions reads:

*   • There is *C*[1] > 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si447.png) such that
    
    $\\underset{0 \\neq \\mathbf{v} \\in \\mathbf{V}}{\\mathit{\\sup}}\\frac{|\\left( \\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{v},w \\right)|}{\\|\\mathbf{v}\\|\_{\\mathbf{V}}} \\geq C\_{1}\\| w\\|\_{W},\\forall w \\in W.$ (4.101)
    
    ![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si448.png)(4.101) 
*   • There is *C*[2] > 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si449.png), a constant independent of *h*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si450.png), such that

$\\underset{0 \\neq \\mathbf{v} \\in \\mathbf{V}\_{h}}{\\mathit{\\sup}}\\frac{|\\left( \\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{v},w \\right)|}{\\|\\mathbf{v}\\|\_{\\mathbf{V}}} \\geq C\_{2}\\| w\\|\_{W},\\forall w \\in W\_{h}.$ (4.102)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si451.png)(4.102)  

*   – which could be rewritten as

$\\underset{0 \\neq w \\in W\_{h}}{\\mathit{\\inf}}\\underset{0 \\neq \\mathbf{v} \\in \\mathbf{V}\_{h}}{\\mathit{\\sup}}\\frac{|\\left( \\nabla \\cdot \\mathbf{v},w \\right)|}{\\|\\mathbf{v}\\|\_{\\mathbf{V}}\\| w\\|\_{W}} \\geq C\_{2} > 0.$ (4.103)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si452.png)(4.103) 

We now consider the case where *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si453.png) is a rectangular domain and 𝒦[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si454.png) is a partition of *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si455.png) into rectangles such that the horizontal and vertical edges of rectangles are parallel to the *x*[1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si456.png)\- and *x*[2]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si457.png)\-coordinate axes, respectively, and adjacent elements completely share their common edge. Define

$Q\_{l,r}\\left( K \\right): = \\left\\{ {\\mathit{v:v}\\left( \\mathbf{x} \\right) = \\sum\\limits\_{i = 0}^{l}{\\sum\\limits\_{j = 0}^{r}{v\_{\\mathit{ij}}x\_{1}^{i}x\_{2}^{j},\\mathbf{x} = \\left( {x\_{1},x\_{2}} \\right) \\in K,v\_{\\mathit{ij}} \\in \\mathbb{R}}}} \\right\\}.$ (4.104)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si458.png)(4.104) 

which is the space of polynomials of degree at most *l*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si459.png) in *x*[1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si460.png) and *r*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si461.png) in *x*[2]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si462.png), and *l*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si463.png),*r* ≥ 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si464.png)

These spaces are an extension of the RT spaces on triangles to rectangles, and for each *r* ≥ 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si465.png) are defined by

**V**[*h*](*K*) = *Q*[*r* + 1, *r*](*K*) × *Q*[*r*, *r* + 1](*K*), (4.105)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si466.png)(4.105) 

*W*[*h*](*K*) = *Q*[*r*, *r*](*K*). (4.106)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si467.png)(4.106) 

In the case *r* = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si468.png), **V**[*h*](*K*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si469.png) takes the form

**V**[*h*](*K*) = {**v** : **v** = (*a*[*K*]¹+*a*[*K*]²*x*[1],*a*[*K*]³+*a*[*K*]⁴*x*[2]), *a*[*K*]^(*i*) ∈ ℝ, *i* = 1, 2, 3, 4}. (4.107)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si470.png)(4.107) 

The MFEM can be recast in matrix form:

$\\begin{bmatrix} \\mathbf{A} & \\mathbf{B} \\\\ \\mathbf{B}^{T} & 0 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}\\begin{pmatrix} \\mathbf{u} \\\\ \\mathbf{p} \\\\ \\end{pmatrix} = \\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\\\ {- \\mathbf{f}} \\\\ \\end{pmatrix}.$ (4.108)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si471.png)(4.108) 

The coefficient matrix $\\begin{bmatrix} \\mathbf{A} & \\mathbf{B} \\\\ \\mathbf{B}^{T} & 0 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si472.png) is nonsingular under the inf–sup condition, but it is not positive definite. This limits application of many iterative algorithms to the resultant algebraic equations. With the trapezoidal quadrature rule the coefficients in 1D problem becomes
