where ∇⋅![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si869.png) is the divergence operator, *v*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si870.png) is the Darcy flux, *ϕ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si871.png) is the porosity, *ρ*[*f*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si872.png) is the fluid density, Γ[*s*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si873.png) is volumetric source/sink term, and *t*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si874.png) is time. The velocity of single-phase fluid flowing in subsurface porous media is governed by Darcy’s law: *v* =  − (*k*/*μ*)(∇*P*−*ρ*[*f*]*g*∇*z*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si875.png).

Typically, the voids of the geological formation in which CO[2]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si876.png) is injected are occupied by more than one fluid. For the multiphase fluid flow the mass conservation equation can be modeled as

$\\frac{\\partial\\left( S\_{\\alpha}\\phi\\rho\_{\\alpha} \\right)}{\\partial t} = \\nabla \\cdot \\left( \\rho\_{\\alpha}v\_{\\alpha} \\right) + \\rho\_{\\alpha}\\text{Γ}\_{s,\\alpha}$ (4.243)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si877.png)(4.243) 

where *S*[*α*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si878.png) is the saturation of phase *α*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si879.png), *ρ*[*α*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si880.png) is the density of the *α*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si881.png) phase fluid, *v*[*α*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si882.png) is the Darcy velocity of phase *α*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si883.png), and Γ[*s*, *α*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si884.png) is the volumetric source/sink term of the *α*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si885.png) phase. The extended Darcy’s law can be used to calculate the multiphase fluid flow in porous media:

$v\_{\\alpha} = - \\frac{kk\_{r,\\alpha}}{\\mu}\\left( \\nabla P\_{\\alpha} - \\rho\_{\\alpha}g\\nabla z \\right)$ (4.244)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si886.png)(4.244) 

where *k*[*r*, *α*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si887.png) is the relative permeability.

The conservation equations can be written separately for the aqueous species and mineral species in the following as

$\\frac{\\partial\\left( \\phi c\_{j} \\right)}{\\partial t} + L\_{j}\\left( c\_{j} \\right) = \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{N\_{e}}{\\nu\_{i,j}^{e}r\_{i}^{e}} + \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{N\_{k}}{\\nu\_{i,j}^{k}r\_{i}^{k},}\\left( j = \\text{aqueous}\\text{species} \\right)$ (4.245)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si888.png)(4.245) 

$\\frac{\\partial\\left( \\rho\_{s}\\left( 1 - \\phi \\right)m\_{j} \\right)}{\\partial t} = \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{N\_{k}}{\\nu\_{i,j}^{k}r\_{i}^{k},}\\left( j = \\text{mineralspecies} \\right)$ (4.246)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si889.png)(4.246) 

where *c*[*j*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si890.png) is the molarity of species *j* in all phases and can be calculated as

$c\_{j} = \\sum\\limits\_{\\alpha}{\\rho\_{\\alpha}S\_{\\alpha}c\_{j,\\alpha}}$ (4.247)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si891.png)(4.247) 

The transport operator *L*[*j*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si892.png) can be calculated as

*L*[*j*] = ∇ ⋅ (*v**c*[*j*]) − ∇ ⋅ (*D*∇*c*[*j*]) + *q*^(*j*), (*j*\=1,…,*n*[*a**q*]) (4.248)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si893.png)(4.248) 

We define $U = \\underset{n\_{1} \\times n\_{\\mathit{aq}}}{\\underset{︸}{\\left\\lbrack \\underset{n\_{1}\\times n\_{1}}{\\underset{︸}{I}}|\\underset{n\_{1}\\times N\_{e}}{\\underset{︸}{-\\left( V\_{1}^{e} \\right)^{T}}} \\right\\rbrack}}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si894.png) and $\\underset{n\_{aq} \\times N\_{e}}{\\underset{︸}{{(V^{e})}^{T}}} = \\left\\lbrack \\frac{\\overset{n\_{1} \\times N\_{e}}{\\overset{︷}{{(V\_{1}^{e})}^{T}}}}{\\underset{n\_{2} \\times N\_{e}}{\\underset{︸}{I}}} \\right\\rbrack$![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si895.png) and rewrite the governing equations for this system as

$\\{\\begin{matrix} {\\frac{\\partial\\left( \\phi c\_{1} \\right)}{\\partial t} - \\frac{\\partial\\left( \\phi\\left( V\_{1}^{e} \\right)^{T}c\_{2} \\right)}{\\partial t} + \\mathit{UL} = U\\left( V\_{\\mathit{aq}}^{k} \\right)^{T}r^{k}} \\\\ {\\log c\_{2} = - V\_{1}^{e}\\log c\_{1} + \\log K^{e} - V\_{1}^{e}\\log\\gamma\_{1} - \\log\\gamma\_{2}} \\\\ {\\frac{\\partial\\left( \\rho\_{s}\\left( 1 - \\phi \\right)m \\right)}{\\partial t} = \\left( V\_{m}^{k} \\right)^{T}r^{k}} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.249)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si896.png)(4.249) 

Mineral dissolution and precipitation continuously alter the void volume of the porous medium and thereby change the porosity. The changed porosity is calculated as follows:

$\\phi = \\phi\_{0} - \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{N\_{m}}\\frac{m\_{i} - m\_{i,0}}{\\rho\_{i}}$ (4.250)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si897.png)(4.250) 

where *ϕ*[0]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si898.png) is the initial porosity, *m*[*i*, 0]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si899.png) is the initial moles of mineral *i* per bulk volume (moles per *m*^( − 3)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si900.png) medium), *ρ*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si901.png) is the mole density of mineral *i*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si902.png) (moles per *m*^( − 3)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si903.png) medium).

The absolute permeability also varies with mineral dissolution and precipitation. A variety of correlations are available in the literature to relate the permeability to porosity. Here we give the most commonly used Kozeny–Carman equation as

$\\frac{k}{k\_{0}} = \\left( \\frac{\\phi}{\\phi\_{0}} \\right)^{3}\\left( \\frac{1 - \\phi\_{0}}{1 - \\phi} \\right)^{2}$ (4.251)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si904.png)(4.251)  

### 4.5.4 Algorithm

In the absence of mineral dissolution/precipitation, the conservation equation can be reduced as

$\\frac{\\partial\\left( \\phi u \\right)}{\\partial t} + \\mathit{UL} = 0$ (4.252)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si905.png)(4.252) 

If the mineral dissolution or precipitation cannot be ignored, the nonlinear reactive-transport equations need to be modeled as

$\\frac{\\partial\\left( \\phi u \\right)}{\\partial t} + \\mathit{UL} = U\\left( V\_{\\mathit{aq}}^{k} \\right)^{T}r^{k}$ (4.253)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si906.png)(4.253) 

The mathematical description of sequential process can be modeled by the **sequential method** using two steps:

${Transport}{step}\\phi\\frac{u^{\*} - u^{n}}{\\text{Δ}t} = - \\mathit{UL}$ (4.254)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si907.png)(4.254) 

${Reaction}{step}\\frac{\\left( \\phi u \\right)^{n + 1} - \\left( \\phi u \\right)^{\*}}{\\text{Δ}t} = U\\left( V\_{\\mathit{aq}}^{k} \\right)^{T}r^{k}$ (4.255)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si908.png)(4.255) 

Within each time step, once the concentrations of the components *u*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si909.png) are obtained, the concentrations of all species *c*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si910.png) can be calculated immediately. This procedure is referred to as speciation calculation and is achieved by solving the equations of component definition and mass action law:

*u* = *c*[1] − (*V*[1]^(*e*))^(*T*)*c*[2] (4.256)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si911.png)(4.256) 

log *c*[2] =  − *V*[1]^(*e*)log *c*[1] + log *K*^(*e*) − *V*[1]^(*e*)log *γ*[1] − log *γ*[2] (4.257)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si912.png)(4.257) 

A **global implicit method**, also referred to as the fully coupled or one-step method, in which the flow and reactive-transport equations are solved simultaneously. According to the difference in the choice of unknowns, the global implicit methods are divided into the following two categories: one is to directly solve the component concentration in any iteration and followed by a speciation calculation to determine the concentrations of all species. Another method is the direct substitution approach, in which the equilibrium equations and kinetic rate equations are directly substituted into the transport equations to yield a set of nonlinear PDEs. Then, these equations are solved simultaneously using the Newton–Raphson iterative method.

 

## 4.6 Discontinuous Galerkin methods

DG methods have recently gained popularity for a wide variety of problems (

 

### 4.6.1 Mathematical model

We assume that the Darcy velocity field **u**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si916.png) is given, is time independent, and satisfies ∇ ⋅ **u** = *q*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si917.png), where *q*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si918.png) is the imposed external total flow rate in an advection–diffusion–reaction flow problem. We denote by *Γ*[*i**n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si919.png) the inflow boundary and by *Γ*[*o**u**t*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si920.png) the outflow/no-flow boundary as

$\\begin{matrix} & {\\Gamma\_{in} = \\mathit{\\{ x} \\in \\partial\\Omega\\text{:}\\mathbf{u}\\cdot\\mathbf{n} < 0\\},} \\\\ & {\\Gamma\_{out} = \\mathit{\\{ x} \\in \\partial\\Omega\\text{:}\\mathbf{u}\\cdot\\mathbf{n}\\geqslant 0\\},} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.258)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si921.png)(4.258) 

where **n**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si922.png) denotes the unit outward normal vector to ∂*Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si923.png), and *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si924.png) is a polygonal and bounded domain in ℝ^(*d*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si925.png). Let *T*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si926.png) be the final simulation time. The classical advection–diffusion–reaction equation for a single flowing phase in porous media is given by

$\\frac{\\partial\\phi c}{\\partial t} + \\nabla\\cdot\\left( \\mathbf{u}c - \\mathbf{D}\\left( \\mathbf{u} \\right)\\nabla c \\right) = \\mathit{qc}^{\*} + r\\left( c \\right),\\left( x,t \\right) \\in \\Omega \\times \\left( 0,T \\right\\rbrack,$ (4.259)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si927.png)(4.259) 

where the unknown variable *c*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si928.png) is the concentration of a species (amount per volume). Here, *ϕ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si929.png) is the effective porosity and is assumed to be time independent, uniformly bounded above and below by positive numbers; **D**(**u**)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si930.png) is the dispersion/diffusion tensor and is assumed to be uniformly symmetric positive definite and bounded from above; *r*(*c*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si931.png) is the reaction term; *q**c*^(\*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si932.png) is the source/sink term, where the imposed external total flow rate q is a sum of sources (injection) and sinks (extraction); and *c*^(\*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si933.png) is the injected concentration *c*[*w*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si934.png) if *q* ≥ 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si935.png) or is the resident concentration *c*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si936.png) if *q* < 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si937.png). We consider the following boundary conditions for this problem:

(**u***c*−**D**(**u**)∇*c*) ⋅ **n** = *c*[*B*]**u** ⋅ **n**, (*x*,*t*) ∈ *Γ*[*i**n*] × (0,*T*\], (4.260)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si938.png)(4.260) 

(−**D**(**u**)∇*c*) ⋅ **n** = 0, (*x*,*t*) ∈ *Γ*[*o**u**t*] × (0,*T*\], (4.261)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si939.png)(4.261) 

where *c*[*B*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si940.png) is the inflow concentration. The initial concentration is specified as

*c*(*x*,0) = *c*[0](*x*), *x* ∈ *Ω*. (4.262)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si941.png)(4.262) 

Let ℰ[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si942.png) be a family of nondegenerate, quasiuniform and possibly nonconforming partitions of *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si943.png) composed of triangles or quadrilaterals, if *d* = 2![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si944.png), or tetrahedra, prisms, or hexahedra if *d* = 3![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si945.png). We assume that no element crosses the boundaries in *Γ*[*i**n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si946.png) and *Γ*[*o**u**t*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si947.png). The set of all interior edges (for a 2D domain) or faces (for a 3D domain) for ℰ[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si948.png) is denoted by *Γ*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si949.png).

For *s* ≥ 3![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si950.png), we define

*H*^(*s*)(ℰ[*h*]) = {*ϕ* ∈ *L*²(*Ω*):*ϕ*|[*E*] ∈ *H*^(*s*)(*E*), *E* ∈ ℰ[*h*]}. (4.263)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si951.png)(4.263) 

The usual Sobolev norm on *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si952.png) is denoted by ∥⋅∥[*m*, *Ω*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si953.png). The broken norms are defined, for *m*![ent](OEBPS/images/geqslant_10878.png)0, as

$\\mathit{|||}\\phi\\mathit{||}|\_{m}^{2} = \\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}{\\|\\phi\\|\_{m,E}^{2}}.$ (4.264)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si954.png)(4.264) 

We now define the average and jump for *ϕ* ∈ *H*^(*s*)(ℰ[*h*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si955.png), *s* ≥ 1/2![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si956.png). Let *E*[*i*], *E*[*j*] ∈ ℰ[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si957.png), and we denote

$\\{\\phi\\} = \\frac{1}{2}\\left( \\left( \\phi|\_{E\_{i}} \\right)|\_{\\gamma} + \\left( \\phi|\_{E\_{j}} \\right)|\_{\\gamma} \\right),$ (4.265)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si958.png)(4.265) 

\[*ϕ*\] = (*ϕ*|[*E*[*i*]])|[*γ*] − (*ϕ*|[*E*[*j*]])|[*γ*]. (4.266)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si959.png)(4.266) 

Denote the upwind value of the concentration *c*^(\*)|[*γ*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si960.png) as follows:

$c^{\*}|\_{\\gamma} = \\left\\{ \\begin{matrix} {c|\_{E\_{i}}} & {{if}\\mathbf{u}\\cdot\\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma}\\geqslant 0} \\\\ {c|\_{E\_{j}}} & {{if}\\mathbf{u}\\cdot\\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma} < 0.} \\\\ \\end{matrix} \\right.$ (4.267)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si961.png)(4.267) 

The discontinuous finite element space is taken to be

𝒟[*r*](ℰ[*h*]) ≡ {*ϕ* ∈ *L*²(*Ω*):*ϕ*|[*E*] ∈ ℙ[*r*](*E*), *E* ∈ ℰ[*h*]}, (4.268)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si962.png)(4.268) 

where ℙ[*r*](*E*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si963.png) denotes the space of polynomials of (total) degree less than or equal to *r*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si964.png) on *E*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si965.png). The “cutoff” operator ℳ![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si966.png) is defined as

ℳ(*c*)(*x*) = min (*c*(*x*),*M*), (4.269)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si967.png)(4.269) 

where *M*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si968.png) is a large positive constant. This operator is uniformly Lipschitz continuous with a Lipschitz constant of one; that is,

∥ℳ(*c*) − ℳ(*w*)∥[*L*^∞(*Ω*)] ≤ ∥*c* − *w*∥[*L*^∞(*Ω*)]. (4.270)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si969.png)(4.270) 

Let *E* ∈ ℰ[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si970.png) and *ϕ* ∈ *H*^(*S*)(*E*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si971.png), and let *h*[*E*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si972.png) denote the diameter of *E*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si973.png). There exists a constant *K*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si974.png), independent of *ϕ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si975.png), *r*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si976.png), and *h*[*E*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si977.png), and a sequence of, *r*\=1, 2, …, such that

$\\left\\{ \\begin{matrix} {\\|\\phi - z\_{r}^{h}\\|\_{q,E}\\leqslant K\\frac{h\_{E}^{\\mu - q}}{r^{s - q}}\\|\\phi\\|\_{s,E}} & {0\\leqslant q < \\mu,} \\\\ {\\|\\phi - z\_{r}^{h}\\|\_{q,\\partial E}\\leqslant K\\frac{h\_{E}^{\\mu - q - (1/2)}}{r^{s - q - (1/2)}}\\|\\phi\\|\_{s,E}} & {0\\leqslant q < \\mu - \\frac{1}{2},} \\\\ \\end{matrix} \\right.$ (4.271)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si978.png)(4.271) 

where *μ* = min (*r*+1,*s*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si979.png). We let *E* ∈ ℰ[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si980.png) and *v* ∈ ℙ[*r*](*E*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si981.png). There exists a constant *K*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si982.png), independent of *v*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si983.png), *r*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si984.png), and *h*[*E*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si985.png), such that

$\\left\\{ \\begin{matrix} {\\| D^{q}v\\|\_{0,\\partial E}\\leqslant K\\frac{r}{h\_{E}^{1/2}}\\| D^{q}v\\|\_{0,E},q\\geqslant 0} \\\\ {\\| D^{q + 1}v\\|\_{0,E}\\leqslant K\\frac{r^{2}}{h\_{E}}\\| D^{q}v\\|\_{0,E}q\\geqslant 0.} \\\\ \\end{matrix} \\right.$ (4.272)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si986.png)(4.272) 

We introduce the bilinear form *B*(*c*,*w*;**u**)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si987.png) defined as

$\\begin{matrix} {B(c,w;\\mathbf{u}) = \\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}\\int\_{E}{(\\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla c - c\\mathbf{u}) \\cdot \\nabla wdx} - \\int\_{\\Omega}{cq^{-}wdx} - \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\int\_{\\gamma}{\\left\\{ \\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla c \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma} \\right\\}\\left\\lbrack w \\right\\rbrack ds}} \\\\ {- s\_{form}\\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\int\_{\\gamma}\\left\\{ \\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla w \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma} \\right\\}\\left\\lbrack c \\right\\rbrack ds + \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\int\_{\\gamma}{c\*\\mathbf{u} \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma}\\left\\lbrack w \\right\\rbrack ds} +} \\\\ {\\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h,{out}}}\\int\_{\\gamma}{c\\mathbf{u} \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma}wds + J\_{0}^{\\sigma}(c,w)},} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.273)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si988.png)(4.273) 

where *s*[*f**o**r**m*] =  − 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si989.png) for the nonsymmetric formulation and *s*[*f**o**r**m*] = 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si990.png) for the symmetric scheme. *q*^+![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si991.png) and *q*^−![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si992.png) are the injection and extraction source terms, respectively, that is,

*q*^+ = max (*q*,0), *q*^− = min (*q*,0). (4.274)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si993.png)(4.274) 

By definition, we have *q* = *q*^+ + *q*^−![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si994.png). In addition, we define the interior penalty term *J*[0]^(*σ*)(*c*,*w*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si995.png) as

$J\_{0}^{\\sigma}\\left( c,w \\right) = \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\frac{r^{2}\\sigma\_{\\gamma}}{h\_{\\gamma}}\\int\\limits\_{\\gamma}\\left\\lbrack c \\right\\rbrack\\left\\lbrack w \\right\\rbrack\\mathit{ds},$ (4.275)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si996.png)(4.275) 

where *σ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si997.png) is a discrete positive function that takes the constant value *σ*[*γ*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si998.png) on the edge or face *γ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si999.png).

The linear functional *L*(*w*;**u**,*c*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1000.png) is defined by

$L\\left( w\\text{;}\\mathbf{u},c \\right) = \\int\\limits\_{\\Omega}{r\\left( \\mathcal{M}\\left( c \\right) \\right)}\\mathit{wdx} + \\int\\limits\_{\\Omega}{c\_{w}q^{+}}\\mathit{wdx} - \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h,{in}}}{\\int\\limits\_{\\gamma}c\_{B}}\\mathbf{u}\\cdot\\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma}\\mathit{wds}.$ (4.276)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1001.png)(4.276) 

The weak formulation of the reactive-transport problem can be modeled as

$\\left( {\\frac{\\partial\\phi c}{\\partial t},w} \\right) + B\\left( {c,w\\text{;}\\mathbf{u}} \\right) = L\\left( {w\\text{;}\\mathbf{u},c} \\right)\\forall w \\in H^{s}\\left( \\mathcal{E}\_{h} \\right),s > \\frac{3}{2}\\forall t \\in \\left( {0,T} \\right),$ (4.277)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1002.png)(4.277) 

if *c*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1003.png) is a solution of [Eq. (4.259)](#chp004_c_split_000.xhtml#eqn265) and essentially bounded. The continuous-in-time DG approximation *C*^(*D**G*) ∈ *W*^(1, ∞)(0,*T*;𝒟[*r*](ℰ[*h*]))![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1004.png) is defined by

$\\left( {\\frac{\\partial\\phi C^{DG}}{\\partial t},w} \\right) + B\\left( C^{DG},w\\text{;}\\mathbf{u} \\right) = L\\left( w\\text{;}\\mathbf{u},C^{DG} \\right),w \\in \\mathcal{D}\_{r}\\left( \\mathcal{E}\_{h} \\right),t \\in \\left( 0,T \\right\\rbrack,$ (4.278)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1005.png)(4.278) 

(*ϕ**C*^(*D**G*),*w*) = (*ϕ**c*[0],*w*), *w* ∈ 𝒟[*r*](ℰ[*h*]), *t* = 0. (4.279)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1006.png)(4.279)  

### 4.6.2 Properties of discontinuous Galerkin

Assume that the reaction rate is a locally Lipschitz continuous function of the concentration. Then the DG scheme has a unique solution for all *t* > 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1007.png). Let *c*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1008.png) be the solution, and assume *c* ∈ *L*²(0,*T*;*H*^(*s*)(ℰ[*h*]))![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1009.png), ∂*c*/∂*t* ∈ *L*²(0,*T*;*H*^(*s* − 1)(ℰ[*h*]))![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1010.png) and *c*[0] ∈ *H*^(*s* − 1)(ℰ[*h*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1011.png). We further assume that *c*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1012.png), *u*,![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1013.png) and *q*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1014.png) are essentially bounded and that the reaction rate is a locally Lipschitz continuous function of *c*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1015.png). If the constant *M*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1016.png) for the “cutoff” operator and the penalty parameter *σ*[0]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1017.png) are sufficiently large, there exists a constant *K*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1018.png), independent of *h*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1019.png) and *r*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1020.png), such that

$\\| C^{DG} - c\\|\_{L^{\\infty}(0,T\\text{;}L^{2})} + \\mathit{|||}\\mathbf{D}^{1/2}\\left( \\mathbf{u} \\right)\\nabla\\left( C^{DG} - c \\right)\\mathit{||}|\_{L^{2}(0,T\\text{;}L^{2})} + \\left( {\\int\\limits\_{0}^{T}{J\_{0}^{\\sigma}\\left( C^{DG} - c,C^{DG} - c \\right)\\mathit{dt}}} \\right)^{1/2}\\leqslant K\\frac{h^{\\mu - 1}}{r^{s - 1 - (\\delta/2)}}\\mathit{|||c||}|\_{L^{2}(0,T\\text{;}H^{s})} + K\\frac{h^{\\mu - 1}}{r^{s - 1}}\\left( \\mathit{|||}\\partial c/\\partial\\mathit{t||}|\_{L^{2}(0,T\\text{;}H^{s - 1})} + \\mathit{|||}c\_{0}\\mathit{||}|\_{s - 1} \\right).$ (4.280)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1021.png)(4.280) 

If we only consider the symmetric DG formulation (i.e., *s*[*f**o**r**m*] = 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1022.png)), we make additional assumptions on *ϕ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1023.png) and *D*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1024.png): *ϕ* ∈ *W*^(2, ∞)(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1025.png) and **D**[*i**j*] ∈ *W*[∞]^(1, 0)((0,*T*)×*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1026.png), where

$\\begin{matrix} & {W\_{\\infty}^{r,s}\\left( \\left( 0,T \\right) \\times \\Omega \\right) \\equiv \\mathit{\\{ f} \\in L^{2}\\left( \\left( 0,T \\right) \\times \\Omega \\right)|\\| f\\|\_{W\_{\\infty}^{r,s}} < \\infty\\},} \\\\ & {\\| f\\|\_{W\_{\\infty}^{r,s}} \\equiv \\sum\\limits\_{|\\alpha|\\leqslant r,\\beta\\leqslant s}{{ess}\\sup\_{(0,T) \\times \\Omega}\\left( |D\_{x}^{\\alpha}\\mathit{f|} + |D\_{t}^{\\beta}\\mathit{f|} \\right).}} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.281)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1027.png)(4.281) 

**Theorem:** Consider the parabolic equation

$\\begin{matrix} & {\\frac{\\partial\\phi\\Phi}{\\partial t} + \\nabla\\cdot\\left( \\mathbf{u}\\Phi - \\mathbf{D}\\nabla\\Phi \\right) + a\\Phi = f,x \\in \\Omega,t \\in \\left( 0,T \\right\\rbrack,} \\\\ & {\\mathbf{D}\\nabla\\Phi\\cdot\\mathbf{n} = 0,x \\in \\partial\\Omega,t \\in \\left( 0,T \\right\\rbrack,} \\\\ & {\\Phi = 0,x \\in \\Omega,t = 0.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.282)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1028.png)(4.282) 

There exists a unique solution *Φ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1029.png) satisfying the abovementioned equation and the regularity bounds given by

∥*Φ*∥[*L*^∞(0, *T*;*H*¹)] + ∥*Φ*∥[*L*²(0, *T*;*H*²)] ≤ *K*∥*f*∥[*L*²(0, *T*;*L*²)], (4.283)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1030.png)(4.283) 

where *K*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1031.png) is a constant independent of the input data *f*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1032.png).

We define residual quantities that depend only on the approximate solution and the data. The residuals consist of the interior residual *R*[*I*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1033.png), the zeroth-order boundary residual *R*[*B*0]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1034.png), and the first-order boundary residual *R*[*B*1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1035.png), as defined next:

$R\_{I} = \\mathit{qC}^{\\mathit{DG}\*} + r\\left( \\mathcal{M}\\left( C^{DG} \\right) \\right) - \\phi\\frac{\\partial C^{DG}}{\\partial t} - \\nabla\\cdot\\left( C^{DG}\\mathbf{u} - \\mathbf{D}\\left( \\mathbf{u} \\right)\\nabla C^{DG} \\right),$ (4.284)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1036.png)(4.284) 

$R\_{B0} = \\left\\{ \\begin{matrix} {\\left\\lbrack C^{DG} \\right\\rbrack,} & {x \\in \\gamma,\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h},} \\\\ {0,} & {x \\in \\partial\\Omega,} \\\\ \\end{matrix} \\right.$ (4.285)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1037.png)(4.285) 

$R\_{B1} = \\left\\{ \\begin{matrix} {\\left\\lbrack \\mathbf{D}\\left( \\mathbf{u} \\right)\\nabla C^{DG} \\right\\rbrack\\cdot\\mathbf{n},} & {x \\in \\gamma,\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h},} \\\\ {\\mathbf{D}\\left( \\mathbf{u} \\right)\\nabla C^{DG}\\cdot\\mathbf{n},} & {x \\in \\partial\\Omega.} \\\\ \\end{matrix} \\right.$ (4.286)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1038.png)(4.286) 

We remark that all the abovementioned quantities *R*[*I*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1039.png), *R*[*B*0],![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1040.png) and *R*[*B*1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1041.png) are computed directly and efficiently from the DG solution. The interior residual *R*[*I*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1042.png) is the PDE residual of the DG solution, and it is defined at every interior point of all mesh elements. The zeroth order boundary residual *R*[*B*0]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1043.png) is the numerical (nonphysical) discontinuity or Dirichlet boundary condition residual of the DG solution, and it is defined at almost every point on the mesh element boundaries. The first-order boundary residual *R*[*B*1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1044.png) is the numerical (nonphysical) discontinuity of the DG normal flux or Neumann boundary condition residual of the DG solution, and it is also defined at almost every point on the mesh element boundaries.

We further assume that *ϕ* ∈ *W*^(2, ∞)(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1045.png), **D**[*i**j*] ∈ *W*[∞]^(1, 0)((0,*T*)×*Ω*), **u** ∈ *L*^∞(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1046.png) (**u**![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1047.png) being independent of time), and *c*[0] ∈ 𝒟[*r*](ℰ[*h*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1048.png). Then there exists a constant *K*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1049.png), independent of *h*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1050.png) and *r*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1051.png), such that

$\\| C^{DG} - c\\|\_{L^{2}(0,T\\text{;}L^{2})}\\leqslant K\\left( {\\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}\\eta\_{E}^{2}} \\right)^{1/2},$ (4.287)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1052.png)(4.287) 

where

$\\begin{matrix} {\\eta\_{E}^{2} = \\frac{h\_{E}^{4}}{r^{4}}\\left\\| R\_{I} \\right\\|\_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(E))}^{2} + \\frac{1}{2}\\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\partial E\\backslash\\partial\\Omega}\\left( {\\frac{h\_{\\gamma}}{r} + \\delta rh\_{\\gamma}} \\right)\\left\\| R\_{B0} \\right\\|\_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\\gamma))}^{2}} \\\\ {+ \\frac{1}{2}\\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\partial E\\backslash\\partial\\Omega}\\frac{h\_{\\gamma}^{3}}{r^{3}}\\left\\| R\_{B1} \\right\\|\_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\\gamma))}^{2} + \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\partial E \\cap \\partial\\Omega}\\frac{h\_{\\gamma}^{3}}{r^{3}}\\left\\| R\_{B1} \\right\\|\_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\\gamma))}^{2}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.288)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1053.png)(4.288) 

It can be quickly proved as:

We denote the error in the DG method by *ξ*:

*ξ* = *C*^(*D**G*) − *c*.![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1054.png)

Subtracting the DG scheme equation by the weak formulation, we have for any *w* ∈ 𝒟[*r*](ℰ[*h*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1055.png) the following orthogonality equation:

$\\left( {\\frac{\\partial\\phi\\xi}{\\partial t},w} \\right) + B\_{S}\\left( \\xi,w\\text{;}\\mathbf{u} \\right) = L\\left( w\\text{;}\\mathbf{u},C^{DG} \\right) - L\\left( w\\text{;}\\mathbf{u},c \\right).$![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1056.png)

Now we consider the “backward” or adjoint parabolic equation:

 

$\\begin{matrix} & {- \\frac{\\partial\\phi\\Phi}{\\partial t} + \\nabla\\cdot\\left( - \\mathbf{u}\\Phi - \\mathbf{D}\\left( \\mathbf{u} \\right)\\nabla\\Phi \\right) + \\left( a + q^{+} \\right)\\Phi = \\xi,x \\in \\Omega,t \\in \\left\\lbrack 0,T \\right),} \\\\ & {\\mathbf{D}\\nabla\\Phi\\cdot\\mathbf{n} = 0,x \\in \\partial\\Omega,t \\in \\left\\lbrack 0,T \\right),} \\\\ & {\\Phi = 0,x \\in \\Omega,t = T,} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1057.png)

where *a* is defined by

$a\\left( x,t \\right) = - \\frac{r\\left( \\mathcal{M}\\left( C^{DG}\\left( x,t \\right) \\right) \\right) - r\\left( \\mathcal{M}\\left( c\\left( x,t \\right) \\right) \\right)}{C^{DG}\\left( x,t \\right) - c\\left( x,t \\right)}{if} C^{DG}\\left( x,t \\right) - c\\left( x,t \\right) \\neq 0$![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1058.png)

We note that

$L\\left( w\\text{;}\\mathbf{u},C^{DG} \\right) - L\\left( w\\text{;}\\mathbf{u},c \\right) = - \\int\\limits\_{\\Omega}{\\left( C^{DG} - c \\right)\\mathit{adx}} = - \\int\\limits\_{\\Omega}{a\\xi\\mathit{dx}}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1059.png)

and

$\\| a\\|\_{L^{2}(0,T\\text{;}L^{\\infty})}\\leqslant\\sqrt{T}\\| a\\|\_{L^{\\infty}(0,T\\text{;}L^{\\infty})}\\leqslant K\_{L} < \\infty,$![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1060.png)where *K*[*L*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1061.png) is a fixed constant. From the solution existence theorem, it can be referred that

∥*Φ*∥[*L*^∞(0, *T*;*H*¹)] + ∥*Φ*∥[*L*²(0, *T*;*H*²)] ≤ *K*∥*ξ*∥[*L*²(0, *T*;*L*²)].![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1062.png)

The *L*²![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1063.png) norm of the error can be written as

$\\begin{matrix} {\\left\\| \\xi \\right\\|\_{0,\\Omega}^{2} = \\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}{(\\xi,\\xi)}\_{E} = \\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}\\left( {\\xi, - \\frac{\\partial\\phi\\Phi}{\\partial t}} \\right)\_{E}} \\\\ {+ \\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}{(\\xi,\\nabla \\cdot ( - \\mathbf{u}\\Phi - \\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla\\Phi))}\_{E} + \\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}{(\\xi,(a + q^{+})\\Phi)}\_{E}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1064.png)

Integrating by parts, we can observe that

$\\begin{matrix} {\\left\\| \\xi \\right\\|\_{0,\\Omega}^{2} = - \\frac{d}{dt}\\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}{(\\xi,\\phi\\Phi)}\_{E} + \\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}\\left( {\\phi\\frac{\\partial\\xi}{\\partial t},\\Phi} \\right)\_{E} + \\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}{((a - q^{-})\\xi,\\Phi)}\_{E}} \\\\ {+ \\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}{(\\nabla\\xi,\\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla\\Phi)}\_{E} - \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\int\_{\\gamma}{\\left\\{ \\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla\\Phi \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma} \\right\\}\\left\\lbrack \\xi \\right\\rbrack ds} - \\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}{(\\xi,\\mathbf{u} \\cdot \\nabla\\Phi)}\_{E}} \\\\ {= - \\frac{d}{dt}\\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}{(\\xi,\\phi\\Phi)}\_{E} + \\left( {\\phi\\frac{\\partial\\xi}{\\partial t},\\Phi} \\right) + (a\\xi,\\Phi) + B\_{S}(\\xi,\\Phi;\\mathbf{u}).} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1065.png)

Applying the orthogonality, we can obtain

$\\left| \\middle| \\xi \\middle| |\_{0,\\Omega}^{2} = - \\frac{d}{dt}\\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}{(\\xi,\\phi\\mathit{\\Phi})}\_{E} + \\left( {\\phi\\frac{\\partial\\xi}{\\partial t},\\mathit{\\Phi} - \\widehat{\\mathit{\\Phi}}} \\right) + (a\\xi,\\mathit{\\Phi} - \\widehat{\\mathit{\\Phi}}) + B\_{S}(\\xi,\\mathit{\\Phi} - \\widehat{\\mathit{\\Phi}};\\mathbf{u}), \\right.$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1066.png)

where $\\widehat{\\mathit{\\Phi}} \\in \\mathcal{D}\_{r}(\\mathcal{E}\_{h})$![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1067.png) is an interpolant satisfying [Eq. (4.271)](#chp004_c_split_000.xhtml#eqn277) element wise. The bilinear term may be expanded as follows:

$\\begin{matrix} {B\_{S}(\\xi,\\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi};\\mathbf{u}) = \\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}\\int\_{E}{(\\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla\\xi - \\xi\\mathbf{u}) \\cdot \\nabla(\\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi})dx} - \\int\_{\\Omega}{\\xi q^{-}(\\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi})dx}} \\\\ {- \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\int\_{\\gamma}{\\left\\{ \\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla\\xi \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma} \\right\\}\\left\\lbrack \\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi} \\right\\rbrack ds} - \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\int\_{\\gamma}\\left\\{ {\\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla\\left( {\\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi}} \\right) \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma}} \\right\\}\\left\\lbrack \\xi \\right\\rbrack ds} \\\\ {+ \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\int\_{\\gamma}{\\xi^{\*}\\mathbf{u} \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma}\\left\\lbrack \\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi} \\right\\rbrack ds + J\_{0}^{\\sigma}(\\xi,\\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi})}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1068.png)

Applying a further integration by parts to the bilinear term, we have

$\\begin{matrix} {B\_{S}(\\xi,\\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi};\\mathbf{u}) = \\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}\\int\_{E}{\\nabla \\cdot ( - \\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla\\xi + \\xi\\mathbf{u})(\\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi})dx} + \\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}\\int\_{\\partial E}{(\\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla\\xi - \\xi\\mathbf{u})}} \\\\ {\\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\partial E}(\\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi})ds - \\int\_{\\Omega}{\\xi q^{-}(\\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi})dx} - \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\int\_{\\gamma}{\\left\\{ \\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla\\xi \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma} \\right\\}\\left\\lbrack \\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi} \\right\\rbrack ds}} \\\\ {- \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\int\_{\\gamma}\\left\\{ {\\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla\\left( {\\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi}} \\right) \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma}} \\right\\}\\left\\lbrack \\xi \\right\\rbrack ds + \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\int\_{\\gamma}{\\xi^{\*}\\mathbf{u} \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma}\\left\\lbrack \\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi} \\right\\rbrack ds} + J\_{0}^{\\sigma}(\\xi,\\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi}).} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1069.png)

It can be further transformed into

$\\begin{matrix} {B\_{S}(\\xi,\\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi};\\mathbf{u}) = \\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}\\int\_{E}{\\nabla \\cdot ( - \\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla\\xi + \\xi\\mathbf{u})(\\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi})dx} - \\int\_{\\Omega}{\\xi q^{-}(\\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi})dx}} \\\\ {- \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\int\_{\\gamma}\\left( {\\Phi - 2\\left\\{ \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi} \\right\\} + \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi}\*} \\right)\\mathbf{u} \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma}\\left\\lbrack \\xi \\right\\rbrack ds + \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\partial\\Omega}\\int\_{\\gamma}{\\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla\\xi \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma}(\\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi})ds}} \\\\ {- \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\int\_{\\gamma}\\left\\{ {\\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla\\left( {\\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi}} \\right) \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma}} \\right\\}\\left\\lbrack \\xi \\right\\rbrack ds + \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\int\_{\\gamma}{\\left\\lbrack \\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla\\xi \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma} \\right\\rbrack\\left\\{ \\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi} \\right\\} ds} + J\_{0}^{\\sigma}(\\xi,\\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi}).} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1070.png)

Thus by employing the residual notations, ∥*ξ*∥[0, *Ω*]²![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1071.png) can be formulated as

$\\begin{matrix} {\\left\\| \\xi \\right\\|\_{0,\\Omega}^{2} = - \\frac{d}{dt}\\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}{(\\xi,\\phi\\Phi)}\_{E} - \\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}\\int\_{E}{R\_{I}(\\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi})dx} - \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\int\_{\\gamma}{R\_{B0}\\mathbf{u} \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma}}\\left( \\Phi \\right.} \\\\ {\\left. {- 2\\left\\{ \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi} \\right\\} + \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi}\*} \\right)ds + \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\partial\\Omega}\\int\_{\\gamma}{R\_{B1}(\\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi})ds} - \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\int\_{\\gamma}\\left\\{ {\\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla\\left( {\\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi}} \\right) \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma}} \\right\\} R\_{B0}ds} \\\\ {+ \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\int\_{\\gamma}{R\_{B1}\\left\\{ \\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi} \\right\\} ds} + \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\frac{r^{2}\\sigma\_{\\gamma}}{h\_{\\gamma}}\\int\_{\\gamma}{R\_{B0}\\left\\lbrack \\Phi - \\overset{\\hat{}}{\\Phi} \\right\\rbrack ds},} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1072.png)

where we have used the fact

$\\begin{array}{l} {R\_{I} = qC^{{DG}\*} + r(\\mathcal{M}(C^{DG})) - \\phi\\frac{\\partial C^{DG}}{\\partial t}} \\\\ {- \\nabla \\cdot (C^{DG}\\mathbf{u} - \\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla C^{DG}) = \\xi q^{-} - a\\xi - \\phi\\frac{\\partial\\xi}{\\partial t} - \\nabla \\cdot (\\xi\\mathbf{u} - \\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla\\xi),} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1073.png)

which may be obtained by using the fact that the exact solution possesses a zero residual. Integrating ∥*ξ*∥[0, *Ω*]²![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1074.png) over time interval \[0,*T*\]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1075.png), applying the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we can get

$\\begin{array}{l} {\\left\\| \\xi \\right\\|\_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}^{2} \\leqslant K\\left\\| \\Phi \\right\\|\_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{2})}} \\\\ \\left( {\\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}\\frac{h\_{E}^{4}}{r^{4}}\\left\\| R\_{I} \\right\\|\_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(E))}^{2} + \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\frac{h\_{\\gamma}^{3}}{r^{3}}\\left\\| R\_{B0} \\right\\|\_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\\gamma))}^{2}} \\right. \\\\ {+ \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\partial\\Omega}\\frac{h\_{\\gamma}^{3}}{r^{3}}\\left\\| R\_{B1} \\right\\|\_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\\gamma))}^{2} + \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\frac{h\_{\\gamma}}{r}\\left\\| R\_{B0} \\right\\|\_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\\gamma))}^{2} + \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\frac{h\_{\\gamma}^{3}}{r^{3}}\\left\\| R\_{B1} \\right\\|\_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\\gamma))}^{2}} \\\\ {\\left. {+ \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}rh\_{\\gamma}\\left\\| R\_{B0} \\right\\|\_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\\gamma))}^{2}} \\right)^{1/2}.} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1076.png)

We note that for triangles or tetrahedra, we may choose a continuous interpolant $\\widehat{\\mathit{\\Phi}}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1077.png) such that the $\\int\_{0}^{T}{J\_{0}^{\\sigma}(\\xi,\\mathit{\\Phi} - \\widehat{\\mathit{\\Phi}})dt}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1078.png) term disappears. Thus we have

$\\begin{array}{l} \\left\\| \\xi \\right\\|\_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}^{2} \\\\ {\\leqslant K\\left\\| \\Phi \\right\\|\_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{2})}\\left( {\\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}\\frac{h\_{E}^{4}}{r^{4}}\\left\\| R\_{I} \\right\\|\_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(E))}^{2} + \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\left( {\\frac{h\_{\\gamma}}{r} + \\delta rh\_{\\gamma}} \\right)\\left\\| R\_{B0} \\right\\|\_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\\gamma))}^{2}} \\right.} \\\\ {\\left. {+ \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\partial\\Omega}\\frac{h\_{\\gamma}^{3}}{r^{3}}\\left\\| R\_{B1} \\right\\|\_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\\gamma))}^{2} + \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\frac{h\_{\\gamma}^{3}}{r^{3}}\\left\\| R\_{B1} \\right\\|\_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\\gamma))}^{2}} \\right)^{1/2},} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1079.png)

Finally, the inequality (4.287) can be proved by using the regularity of the adjoint problem.

For the bilinear form defined as

$\\begin{array}{l} {B(c,w;\\mathbf{u}): = \\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}\\int\_{E}{\\left( {\\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla c - c\\mathbf{u}} \\right) \\cdot \\nabla w}} \\\\ {- \\int\_{\\Omega}{cq^{-}w} - \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\int\_{\\gamma}{\\left\\{ {\\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla c \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma}} \\right\\}\\left\\lbrack w \\right\\rbrack}} \\\\ {- s\_{form}\\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\int\_{\\gamma}{\\left\\{ {\\mathbf{D}(\\mathbf{u})\\nabla w \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma}} \\right\\}\\left\\lbrack c \\right\\rbrack}} \\\\ {+ \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h}}\\int\_{\\gamma}{c\*\\mathbf{u} \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma}\\left\\lbrack w \\right\\rbrack} + \\sum\\limits\_{\\gamma \\in \\Gamma\_{h,{out}}}\\int\_{\\gamma}{c\\mathbf{u} \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\_{\\gamma}w + J\_{0}^{\\sigma}\\left( {c,w} \\right)},} \\\\ \\end{array}$ (4.289)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1080.png)(4.289) 

we have *s*[*f**o**r**m*] =  − 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1081.png) for **NIPG (the nonsymmetric interior penalty Galerkin method**) or **OBB-DG (the Oden–Babuška–Baumann formulation of DG)**, *s*[*f**o**r**m*] = 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1082.png) for **SIPG**, and *s*[*f**o**r**m*] = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1083.png) for **IIPG (the incomplete interior penalty Galerkin method)**.

 

### 4.6.3 Adaptive mesh

Residual-based explicit error estimators are efficient to compute and may be used to indicate a set of elements that need to be refined, thus guiding adaptivity. However, these residual-based estimators yield only one piece of information for each element. Consequently, they do not provide guidance on anisotropic refinements. In this section, we derive error estimators using hierarchic bases. Unlike residual-based error estimators, hierarchic error estimators give pointwise information on the error, and thus may be used to guide fully anisotropic *hp*\-adaptation. Here, for simplicity, we consider only the anisotropic *h*\-adaptation. Hierarchic error estimators consist of solving the problem of interest by employing two discretization schemes of different accuracy and using the difference between the approximations as an estimate for the error. The advantages of this approach include their applicability to many classes of problems and the simplicity and ease of their implementation.

For a given mesh ℰ[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1084.png), we construct the mesh ℰ[*h*/2]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1085.png) by isotropically refining each element of ℰ[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1086.png). We denote by *C*^(DG) the DG solution in the coarse mesh ℰ[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1087.png) and by *C*^(DG,*F*) the DG solution in the fine mesh ℰ[*h*/2]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1088.png). The following saturation assumption can be made as

∥*C*^(*D**G*, *F*)−*c*∥[*L*²](*t*) ≤ *β*∥*C*^(*D**G*)−*c*∥[*L*²](*t*), 0 ≤ *β* < 1. (4.290)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1089.png)(4.290) 

Recall the error estimate for NIPT, SIPG, and IIPG:

$\\begin{array}{l} \\left. C^{DG} - c\_{L^{\\infty}{({0,T;L^{2}})}} + \\middle| \\middle| \\middle| \\mathbf{D}^{1/2}\\left( \\mathbf{u} \\right)\\nabla\\left( {C^{DG} - c} \\right) \\middle| \\middle| |\_{L^{2}{({0,T;L^{2}})}} \\right. \\\\ \\left. + \\left( {\\int\_{0}^{T}{J\_{0}^{\\sigma}\\left( {C^{DG} - c,C^{DG} - c} \\right)}} \\right)^{1/2} \\leqslant K\\frac{h^{\\mu - 1}}{r^{s - 1 - \\frac{\\delta}{2}}} \\middle| \\middle| \\middle| c \\middle| \\middle| |\_{L^{2}{({0,T;H^{s}})}} \\right. \\\\ {+ K\\frac{h^{\\mu - 1}}{r^{s - 1}}\\left( \\left| \\middle| \\middle| \\frac{\\partial c}{\\partial t} \\middle| \\middle| |\_{L^{2}{({0,T;H^{s - 1}})}} + \\middle| \\middle| \\middle| c\_{0} \\middle| \\middle| |\_{s - 1} \\right. \\right),} \\\\ \\end{array}$ (4.291)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1090.png)(4.291) 

and that for OBB-DG:

$\\begin{matrix} \\left. C^{DG} - c\_{L^{\\infty}{({0,T;L^{2}})}} + \\middle| \\middle| \\middle| \\mathbf{D}^{1/2}\\left( \\mathbf{u} \\right)\\nabla\\left( {C^{DG} - c} \\right) \\middle| \\middle| |\_{L^{2}{({0,T;L^{2}})}} \\right. \\\\ {\\leqslant K\\frac{h^{\\mu - 1}}{r^{s - (5/2)}}\\left( \\left| \\middle| \\middle| c \\middle| \\middle| |\_{L^{2}{({0,T;H^{s}})}} + \\middle| \\middle| \\middle| \\frac{\\partial c}{\\partial t} \\middle| \\middle| |\_{L^{2}{({0,T;H^{s - 1}})}} + \\middle| \\middle| \\middle| c\_{0} \\middle| \\middle| |\_{s} \\right. \\right),} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (4.292)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1091.png)(4.292) 

Then we can observe that for all the four primal DGs, that is, OBB-DG, SIPG, NIPG, and IIPG, that *β*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1092.png) is less than or equal to the following value asymptotically: $\\beta\\leqslant\\frac{1}{2^{\\mu - 1}} = \\frac{1}{2^{\\min(r,s - 1)}}.$![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1093.png) A posteriori error estimator for OBB-DG, SIPG, NIPG, or IIPG can be stated as

$\\frac{1}{1 + \\beta}\\left( {\\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}\\eta\_{E}^{2}} \\right)^{1/2}\\left( t \\right)\\leqslant\\left\\| {C^{DG} - c} \\right\\|\_{L^{2}}\\left( t \\right)\\leqslant\\frac{1}{1 - \\beta}\\left( {\\sum\\limits\_{E \\in \\mathcal{E}\_{h}}\\eta\_{E}^{2}} \\right)^{1/2}\\left( t \\right),$ (4.293)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1094.png)(4.293) 

where *η*[*E*](*t*) = ∥*C*^(*D**G*, *F*) − *C*^(*D**G*)∥[*L*²(*E*)](*t*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1095.png).

In the abovementioned error indicator the fine grid solution is used as a replacement for the true solution to estimate the error of the coarse grid solution. We now use the same principle to select a proper anisotropic refinement. A uniform degree *r*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1096.png) of polynomial space is assumed over the entire domain. The local space for each element *E*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1097.png) is denoted by ℙ[*r*](*E*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1098.png).

For transient problems involving a long period of simulation time, the location of biogeochemical phenomena generally moves with time. Most error indicators for transient problems, including the *L*²(*L*²)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1099.png) and *L*²(*H*¹)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1100.png) error indicators reviewed in the previous section, provide global spatial and temporal estimates. It is desirable that error indicators account for local physics only at the current time, and thus it is preferable to compute error indicators only for a short time interval. The hierarchic error indicator presented in this section is pointwise in time and provides guidance on time-dependent mesh modifications. Because it is expensive to change the mesh at each time step, we divide the entire simulation period into a collection of time slices, each of which may in turn contain a certain number of time steps. The maintenance of a constant number of elements prevents the computational workload from increasing with time steps. The initial mesh is chosen to be a uniform fine grid.

We first present the dynamic and isotropic mesh adaptation strategy. We denote by #(*S*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1101.png)) the number of elements in a set *S*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1102.png). Detailed dynamic and isotropic mesh adaptation algorithm can be described as

Given an initial mesh ℰ[0]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1103.png),

Step 1. Let *m* = 1, *n* = 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1104.png)

Step 2. Compute the initial concentration for the time slice (*T*[*n* − 1], *T*[*n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1105.png)) using either the initial condition (if *n* = 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1106.png)) or the concentration at the end of last time slice (if *n* > 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1107.png)) by local projections.

Step 3. Let ℰ[*m*, *n*] = ℰ[0]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1108.png) if *n* = 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1109.png) and *m* = 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1110.png); or ℰ[*m*, *n*] = ℰ[*M*[*n* − 1] + 1, *n* − 1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1111.png) if *n* > 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1112.png) and *m* = 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1113.png).

Step 4. Compute the DG approximation of the PDE for the time slice (*T*[*n* − 1], *T*[*n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1114.png)) based on the mesh ℰ[*m*, *n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1115.png), and compute the error indicator *η*[*E*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1116.png) for each element *E* ∈ ℰ[*m*, *n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1117.png).

Step 5. Select ℰ[*r*] ∈ ℰ[*m*, *n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1118.png) such that #(ℰ[*r*]) = ⌈*α*#(ℰ[*m*, *n*])⌉![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1119.png) and min {*η*[*E*]:*E* ∈ ℰ[*r*]} ≥ max {*η*[*E*]:*E* ∈ ℰ[*m*, *n*]⧹ℰ[*r*]}![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1120.png).

Step 6. Select ℰ[*c*] ∈ ℰ[*m*, *n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1121.png) to minimize max {*η*[*E*]:*E* ∈ ℰ[*c*]}![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1122.png) subject to that #(ℰ[*c*]) = ⌈*α*#(ℰ[*m*, *n*])⌉![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1123.png) and that ℰ[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1124.png) satisfies the coarsening-compatible condition with regard to ℰ[*m*, *n*]*a**n**d*ℰ[*r*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1125.png).

Step 7. Refine all *E* ∈ ℰ[*r*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1126.png) and coarsen all *E* ∈ ℰ[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1127.png) to form a new mesh ℰ[*m* + 1, *n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1128.png).

Step 8. Let *m* = *m* + 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1129.png), *n* = *n* + 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1130.png) and check whether *m* ≤ *M*[*n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1131.png) and *n* ≤ *N*![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1132.png).

Step 9. Report the solution and stop.

Some error indicators, including the hierarchic error indicators presented previously, do not only provide guidance on the selection of elements to be coarsened or refined, but they also yield information on how to anisotropically refine a given element. If we employ these error indicators, we obtain a more efficient mesh adaptation algorithm, that is, the dynamic and anisotropic mesh adaptation method:

Steps 1–6. These are the same as that in the previous algorithm.

Step 7. Refine all *E* ∈ ℰ[*r*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1133.png) anisotropically, guided by the local error indicator, and coarsen all *E* ∈ ℰ[*c*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1134.png) to form a new mesh ℰ[*m* + 1, *n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1135.png).

Steps 8 and 9. These are the same as that in the previous algorithm.

 

## 4.7 Exercises for reservoir simulator designing

 

1.  1. Consider the flow of water through a horizontal, saturated, homogeneous sand column of length 10 m. The sand is packed in a way such that the first 3 m, the next 4 m, and the last 3 m of the column have permeabilities of 10, 20, and 100 md, respectively. The viscosity of the water is 1 cP. The pressures are given to be 2 atm at one end and 1 atm at the other. Compute the Darcy velocity in this system.
2.  2. A porous medium of 10 m×1 m×1 m is fully saturated and has a permeability of *k*\=2×10^( − 8)*c**m*²![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1136.png). If the Darcy velocity and piezometric head at the outlet are given by 1 ft/day and 10-m water, find the pressure at the other end. (Please assume the density and viscosity of the water to be 1000 kg/m³ and 1 cP, respectively.)
3.  3. A rock sample of dimension 0.1 m×0.1 m×0.1 m is obtained from the subsurface. When the sample is fully saturated with water, the bulk density (i.e., the average density for the entire sample) is 2.275×103 kg/m³. When the sample is completely dry, its bulk density is 2.025×103 kg/m³. Find the porosity of the sample. (Please assume that the density of the water is 1000 kg/m³.)
4.  4. A horizontal porous medium of 1 m×0.1 m×0.1 m is filled with water (viscosity *µ*\=1 cP). The pressures are 106 Pa at one end and 105 Pa at the other, and the Darcy velocity is 9×10^( − 5)![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1137.png) m/s. Calculate the permeability and hydraulic conductivity of the medium.
5.  5. State Darcy’s law for single-phase flow in porous media. Please list the assumptions on the media, fluid, and flow condition for Darcy’s law to hold. Please list as many assumptions as needed. For each assumption, please discuss the consequence of the flow equation if the assumption fails.
6.  6. Consider the steady-state Stokes flow under gravity with periodic boundary conditions for domain boundaries. The computational domain is a 2D rectangle, but the domain may contain both the free space that can be occupied by the fluid, and the space for impermeable stationary solid phase. The no-slip boundary condition is applied to all solid–fluid interfaces. State the problem in mathematical expression, and apply the staggered-grid finite-difference method to this problem using a uniform rectangular grid of size *m*×*n*. We assume that each cell is either occupied by the fluid entirely or occupied by the solid entirely. Present the numerical algorithm in detailed formulas using *i*,*j* indices. Please give full details of the algorithm, including boundary condition treatment.
7.  7. Let us recall we have introduced in [Section 3.3](#chp003_b.xhtml#s0110) a circulant shift matrix (also a cyclic permutation matrix):
    
    $S\_{m} = \\begin{pmatrix} 0\_{1 \\times {({m - 1})}} & 1 \\\\ I\_{{({m - 1})} \\times {({m - 1})}} & 0\_{{({m - 1})} \\times 1} \\\\ \\end{pmatrix}$
    
    ![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1138.png)
    
      
    The circulant shift matrix *S*[*n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1139.png) has a similar definition. The circulant shift matrices are readily available in many high-level languages, for example, in MATLAB or R. We now consider the same problem and the same algorithm as in Problem 1. We adopt the simple (though less accurate) treatment of no-slip boundary using ghost points (suppose, the cell *C*[*i* − 0.5, *j* − 0.5]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1140.png) is occupied by the solid) and we consider the *x*\-momemtum equation within *C*[*i*, *j* + 0.5]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1141.png); instead of imposing the more accurate condition *u*[*i*, *j*]^(*x*, *h*) = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1142.png), we use the more convenient condition (*u*[*i*, *j* − 0.5]^(*x*, *h*) = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1143.png)). Now please present the numerical algorithm using the vector–matrix notation (for the matrix-based implementation) and show the discretized equation reduces to
    
    $R\\begin{bmatrix} {- \\mu\\Delta\_{h}} & 0 & D\_{\\mathit{xc}} \\\\ 0 & {- \\mu\\Delta\_{h}} & D\_{\\mathit{yc}} \\\\ {- D\_{\\mathit{cx}}} & {- D\_{\\mathit{cy}}} & 0 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}R^{T}\\begin{bmatrix} {{vec}\\left( u\_{h} \\right)} \\\\ {{vec}\\left( v\_{h} \\right)} \\\\ {{vec}\\left( p\_{h} \\right)} \\\\ \\end{bmatrix} = R\\begin{bmatrix} {{vec}\\left( \\rho g\_{x}^{h} \\right)} \\\\ {{vec}\\left( \\rho g\_{y}^{h} \\right)} \\\\ 0 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}.$
    
    ![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1144.png)
8.  8. Code the algorithm as presented in your solution to Problem 2 (using the vector–matrix notation). You are given a set of draft code next, where you need to add seven lines. Please add detailed comments for each line! Please plot the pressure and the velocity, as well as compute the average velocity over the entire domain, including the space for the solid phase where the fluid velocity is defined to be zero. Report your complete code with comments, your plots, and your average velocities.  
    *Algorithm 1: Given code for Problem 3*  
    *1 clear all; close all;*  
    *2 Lx=1.0e-3; Ly=1.0e-3; gx=0; gy=-9.807; rho=1000; mu=8.9e-4;*  
    *3 m=100; n*\=*100;*  
    *4 hx*\=*Lx / m; hy=Ly / n;*  
    *5 isSolid=false(m,n); isSolid(50:70, 50:80)=1;*  
    *6 %*  
    *7 % ADD YOUR LINE HERE TO CALCULATE Dcx*  
    *8 Dcy=(speye(m\*n) - kron(circshift(speye(n),\[1,0\]), speye(m)))/hy;*  
    *9 Dsq=mu\*(Dcx\*Dcx’ + Dcy\*Dcy’); O=sparse(m\*n, m\*n);*  
    *10 A=\[ Dsq, O, -Dcx’; O, Dsq, -Dcy’; -Dcx, -Dcy, O\];*  
    *11 A(end,end)=A(end,end) + max(abs(A(end,:)));*  
    *12 %*  
    *13 % ADD YOUR LINE HERE TO CALCULATE THE RIGHT-HAND-SIDE VECTOR b*  
    *14 %*  
    *15 isUx0=isSolid | circshift(isSolid, \[-1 0\]);*  
    *16 isUy0=isSolid | circshift(isSolid, \[0 -1\]);*  
    *17 Rp=speye(m\*n); Rp(isSolid(:),:)=\[\];*  
    *18 Rx=speye(m\*n); Rx(isUx0(:),:)=\[\];*  
    *19 % ADD YOUR LINE HERE TO CALCULATE Ry*  
    *20 R=blkdiag(Rx, Ry, Rp);*  
    *21 %*  
    *22 x=R’\*((R\*A\*R’) \\ (R\*b));*  
    *23 % ADD YOUR LINE HERE TO GET ux*  
    *24 uy=reshape(x(m\*n+1:2\*m\*n), \[m, n\]);*  
    *25 % ADD YOUR LINE HERE TO GET p*  
    *26 %*  
    *27 figure; imagesc(rot90(isSolid));*  
    *28 figure; imagesc(rot90(p));*  
    *29 % ADD YOUR LINE HERE TO PLOT QUIVER PLOT FOR (ux, uy)*  
    *30 % ADD YOUR LINE HERE TO REPORT MEAN VELOCITIES*
9.  9. Consider a 2D sample rock. For numerical computation, we discretize the domain (0, *Lx*)×(0, *Ly*) using the *m*×*n* uniform rectangular mesh. Given *Lx*, *Ly*, and *ϕ*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1145.png), where the void indicator is defined as *ϕ*[*h*]*ϵ*{0,1}^(*m* × *n*).![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1146.png) Please write down the detailed computational procedure to compute the effective permeability *K*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1147.png) of the sample rock, using the staggered-grid finite-difference method for the Stokes equation with no-slip boundary conditions (on the solid–fluid interface) and periodic boundary conditions (on the domain boundary occupied by fluid). Please state the computational procedure using the vector–matrix notation. Finally prove the computed effective permeability *K*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000046si1148.png) is indeed symmetric and positive definite using the vector–matrix notation.

Chapter five 

# Recent progress in multiscale and mesoscopic reservoir simulation

 

## Abstract

Physical phenomena can be modeled at varying degrees of complexity and at different scales. Multiscale modeling and computation provides a framework for constructing mathematical and computational models of such phenomena, by examining the connection between models at different scales, based on fundamental principles, and based on mathematical theory of approximation. Recently, mesoscopic algorithms have been proposed and widely accepted in petroleum industry and lattice Boltzmann method (LBM) is the most famous one. The word mesoscopic is used to describe the flow property with regarding to certain Knudsen number range and the capability of capturing both macroscopic phenomena and microscopic interactions. LBM is proved to have super parallelization property and easy to implement using fully explicit scheme. Complex boundary can be treated well in LBM and numerous mechanisms can be coupled easily. Meanwhile, generalized multiscale finite element method has also been developed with the usage of certain upscaling technique such as proper orthogonal decomposition. In this chapter, we will start from the concepts and properties of upscaling techniques and then step into the application on discretized numerical methods. Multipoint flux approximation methods are also an option to conclude multiscale properties in one scheme. Finally, LBM is presented with rigorous derivations and modified schemes for specific engineering cases are presented to show the coupling with various mechanisms.

 

### Keywords

Upscaling; generalized multiscale finite element method; multipoint flux approximation; LBM
