## 5.1 Upscaling technique

 

### 5.1.1 Upscaling for finite difference system

Recall the mass conservation law for fluid in porous media:

$\\frac{\\partial\\left( \\phi\\rho \\right)}{\\partial t} + \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\rho\\mathbf{u}} \\right) = q\_{m},$ (5.1)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si10.png)(5.1) 

where *q*[*m*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si11.png) is mass injection rate. For incompressible fluid the fluid density is constant. For incompressible fluid flow in incompressible media, mass conservation implies volume conservation:

∇ ⋅ **u** = *q*. (5.2)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si12.png)(5.2) 

Substitution of Darcy’s law into the conservation law yields the pressure equation:

$\\frac{\\partial\\left( \\phi\\rho\\left( p \\right) \\right)}{\\partial t} - \\nabla \\cdot \\left( \\rho\\mathbf{K}\\nabla p \\right) = q\_{m}.$ (5.3)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si13.png)(5.3) 

The previous pressure equation can also be written as

$\\phi\\rho c\_{t}\\frac{\\partial p}{\\partial t} - \\nabla \\cdot \\left( \\rho\\mathbf{K}\\nabla p \\right) = q\_{m}.$ (5.4)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si14.png)(5.4) 

The total compressibility has contribution from the fluid and the media. For incompressible fluid and media, it becomes

 − ∇ ⋅ (**K**∇*p*) = *q*. (5.5)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si15.png)(5.5) 

The fluid compressibility *c*[*f*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si16.png) is defined by *c*[*f*] =  − 1/*V*[*f*]∂*V*[*f*]/∂*p*.![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si17.png) Because *m*[*f*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si18.png) is independent of *p*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si19.png), we can easily have

$c\_{f} = - \\frac{m\_{f}}{V\_{f}}\\frac{\\partial\\left( V\_{f}/m\_{f} \\right)}{\\partial p} = \\frac{1}{\\rho}\\frac{\\partial\\rho}{\\partial p}.$ (5.6)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si20.png)(5.6) 

The rock compressibility *c*[*R*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si21.png) can be defined by *c*[*R*] = 1/*ϕ*∂*ϕ*/∂*p*.![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si22.png) It is easy to derive the total compressibility from the pressure equation:

*c*[*t*] = *c*[*f*] + *c*[*R*] (5.7)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si23.png)(5.7) 

Often the porosity *ϕ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si24.png) is assumed to have the form *ϕ* = *ϕ*⁰(1+*c*[*R*](*p*−*p*⁰))![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si25.png), where *ϕ*⁰![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si26.png) is the porosity at a reference pressure *p*⁰![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si27.png). In this case the total compressibility is

$c\_{t} = c\_{f} + \\frac{\\phi^{0}}{\\phi}c\_{R}$ (5.8)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si28.png)(5.8) 

Finite difference (FD) method is introduced in Chapter 3, Recent progress in pore scale reservoir simulation, and now let us shortly introduce the numerical errors involved. Numerical errors in FD methods can be grouped into two sources: round-off error, representing the loss of precision due to computer rounding of decimal quantities, and truncation error or discretization error, representing the difference between the exact solution of the original differential equation and the exact quantity assuming perfect arithmetic (i.e., assuming no round-off). An expression of general interest is the local truncation error of a method. Typically expressed using Big-O notation, local truncation error refers to the error from a single application of a method. That is, it is the quantity *f*^′(*x*[*i*]) − *f*[*i*]^′![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si29.png) if *f*^′(*x*[*i*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si30.png) refers to the exact value and *f*[*i*]^′![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si31.png) to the numerical approximation. The remainder term of a Taylor polynomial is convenient for analyzing the local truncation error. Using the Lagrange form of the remainder from the Taylor polynomial for *f*(*x*[0]+*h*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si32.png), which is

$R\_{n}\\left( x\_{0} + h \\right) = \\frac{f^{({n + 1})}\\left( \\xi \\right)}{\\left( {n + 1} \\right)!}\\left( h \\right)^{n + 1},{where} x\_{0} < \\xi < x\_{0} + h,$ (5.9)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si33.png)(5.9) 

where the dominant term of the local truncation error can be discovered.

For cell-centered finite difference (CCFD) introduced in Chapter 3, Recent progress in pore scale reservoir simulation, applied on Darcy’s law and discrete pressure equation, we need the conductivity *K*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si34.png) at centers of edges \[in two-dimensional (2D)\] or faces \[in three-dimensional (3D)\]. If conductivity *K*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si35.png) as a smooth function of spatial position, we can simply evaluate it at centers of edges (in 2D) or faces (in 3D):

*K*[*i*, *j* − 0.5]^(*x**x*) = *K*^(*x**x*)(*x*[*i*],*y*[*j* − 0.5]), *K*[*i* − 0.5, *j*]^(*y**y*) = *K*^(*y**y*)(*x*[*i* − 0.5],*y*[*j*]). (5.10)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si36.png)(5.10) 

The discrete Darcy’s law can be written as

$\\frac{\\left( {- K\_{i,j - 0.5}^{\\mathit{xx}}\\left( {{p\_{i + 0.5,j - 0.5} - p\_{i - 0.5,j - 0.5}}/{x\_{i + 0.5} - x\_{i - 0.5}}} \\right)} \\right) - \\left( {- K\_{i - 1,j - 0.5}^{\\mathit{xx}}\\left( {{p\_{i - 0.5,j - 0.5} - p\_{i - 1.5,j - 0.5}}/{x\_{i - 0.5} - x\_{i - 1.5}}} \\right)} \\right)}{x\_{i} - x\_{i - 1}} + \\frac{\\left( {- K\_{i - 0.5,j}^{\\mathit{yy}}\\left( {{p\_{i - 0.5,j + 0.5} - p\_{i - 0.5,j - 0.5}}/{y\_{j + 0.5} - y\_{j - 0.5}}} \\right)} \\right) - \\left( {- K\_{i - 0.5,j - 1}^{\\mathit{yy}}\\left( {{p\_{i - 0.5,j - 0.5} - p\_{i - 0.5,j - 1.5}}/{y\_{j - 0.5} - y\_{j - 1.5}}} \\right)} \\right)}{y\_{j} - y\_{j - 1}} = q\_{i - 0.5,j - 0.5}.$ (5.11)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si37.png)(5.11) 

In mathematics the harmonic mean (or harmonic average, or sometimes called the subcontrary mean) is one of several kinds of average, and it can be expressed as the reciprocal of the arithmetic mean (AM) of the reciprocals of the given set of observations. The harmonic mean *H*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si38.png) of the positive real numbers *x*[1], *x*[2], …, *x*[*n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si39.png) is defined to be

$H = \\frac{n}{\\left( {1/x\_{1}} \\right) + \\left( {1/x\_{2}} \\right) + \\cdots + \\left( {1/x\_{n}} \\right)} = \\frac{n}{\\sum\_{i = 1}^{n}{1/x\_{i}}} = \\left( \\frac{\\sum\_{i = 1}^{n}x\_{i}^{- 1}}{n} \\right)^{- 1}.$ (5.12)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si40.png)(5.12) 

If a set of weights *w*[1], *w*[2], …, *w*[*n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si41.png) is associated to the dataset *x*[1], …, *x*[*n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si42.png), the weighted harmonic mean is defined by

$H = \\frac{\\sum\_{i = 1}^{n}w\_{i}}{\\sum\_{i = 1}^{n}{w\_{i}/x\_{i}}} = \\left( \\frac{\\sum\_{i = 1}^{n}{w\_{i}x\_{i}^{- 1}}}{\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}w\_{i}} \\right)^{- 1}.$ (5.13)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si43.png)(5.13) 

The unweighted harmonic mean can be regarded as the special case where all of the weights are equal. For conductivity *K*, we use harmonic average weighted by cell size:

$K\_{i,j - 0.5}^{\\mathit{xx}} = \\frac{h\_{i - 0.5}^{x} + h\_{i + 0.5}^{x}}{\\left( {h\_{i - 0.5}^{x}/K\_{i - 0.5,j - 0.5}^{\\mathit{xx}}} \\right) + \\left( {h\_{i + 0.5}^{x}/K\_{i + 0.5,j - 0.5}^{\\mathit{xx}}} \\right)},$ (5.14)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si44.png)(5.14) 

$K\_{i - 0.5,j}^{\\mathit{yy}} = \\frac{h\_{j - 0.5}^{y} + h\_{j + 0.5}^{y}}{\\left( {h\_{j - 0.5}^{y}/K\_{i - 0.5,j - 0.5}^{\\mathit{yy}}} \\right) + \\left( {h\_{j + 0.5}^{y}/K\_{i - 0.5,j + 0.5}^{\\mathit{yy}}} \\right)},$ (5.15)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si45.png)(5.15) 

where *h*[*i* − 0.5]^(*x*) = *x*[*i*] − *x*[*i* − 1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si46.png) and *h*[*j* − 0.5]^(*y*) = *y*[*j*] − *y*[*j* − 1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si47.png).

We consider two adjacent cells *C*[*i* − 0.5, *j* − 0.5]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si48.png) and *C*[*i* + 0.5, *j* − 0.5]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si49.png). We assume that the conductivity is constant in each cell. Recall our discrete Darcy’s law:

$u\_{i,j - 0.5}^{x} = - K\_{i,j - 0.5}^{\\mathit{xx}}\\frac{p\_{i + 0.5,j - 0.5} - p\_{i - 0.5,j - 0.5}}{x\_{i + 0.5} - x\_{i - 0.5}}.$ (5.16)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si50.png)(5.16) 

Apply one-sided FD to Darcy’s law within *C*[*i* − 0.5, *j* − 0.5]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si51.png):

$u\_{i,j - 0.5}^{x} = - K\_{i - 0.5,j - 0.5}^{\\mathit{xx}}\\frac{p\_{i,j - 0.5} - p\_{i - 0.5,j - 0.5}}{x\_{i} - x\_{i - 0.5}}.$ (5.17)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si52.png)(5.17) 

Similarly, we have within the cell *C*[*i* + 0.5, *j* − 0.5]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si53.png)

$u\_{i,j - 0.5}^{x} = - K\_{i + 0.5,j - 0.5}^{\\mathit{xx}}\\frac{p\_{i + 0.5,j - 0.5} - p\_{i,j - 0.5}}{x\_{i + 0.5} - x\_{i}}.$ (5.18)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si54.png)(5.18) 

It can be easily inferred from discrete Darcy’s law that

$\\frac{x\_{i + 0.5} - x\_{i - 0.5}}{K\_{i,j - 0.5}^{\\mathit{xx}}} = - \\frac{p\_{i + 0.5,j - 0.5} - p\_{i - 0.5,j - 0.5}}{u\_{i,j - 0.5}^{x}}.$ (5.19)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si55.png)(5.19) 

$\\frac{x\_{i} - x\_{i - 0.5}}{K\_{i - 0.5,j - 0.5}^{\\mathit{xx}}} = - \\frac{p\_{i,j - 0.5} - p\_{i - 0.5,j - 0.5}}{u\_{i,j - 0.5}^{x}}.$ (5.20)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si56.png)(5.20) 

$\\frac{x\_{i + 0.5} - x\_{i}}{K\_{i + 0.5,j - 0.5}^{\\mathit{xx}}} = - \\frac{p\_{i + 0.5,j - 0.5} - p\_{i,j - 0.5}}{u\_{i,j - 0.5}^{x}}.$ (5.21)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si57.png)(5.21) 

Subtracting the second and third equations from the first, we conclude

$\\frac{x\_{i + 0.5} - x\_{i - 0.5}}{K\_{i,j - 0.5}^{\\mathit{xx}}} = \\frac{x\_{i} - x\_{i - 0.5}}{K\_{i - 0.5,j - 0.5}^{\\mathit{xx}}} + \\frac{x\_{i + 0.5} - x\_{i}}{K\_{i + 0.5,j - 0.5}^{\\mathit{xx}}}.$ (5.22)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si58.png)(5.22)  

### 5.1.2 Explicit average schemes

In discrete Darcy’s law and discrete pressure equation from CCFD, we need the conductivity *K*^(*x**x*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si59.png) at centers of the edges (in 2D or 3D) aligning with the *x*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si60.png) direction. If conductivity *K*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si61.png) as a smooth function of spatial position, we can simply evaluate it at centers of edges. In practice, the conductivity *K*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si62.png) can be a discontinuous function that has jumps on edges (or faces). In this case, weighted arithmetic average is recommended for the conductivity *K*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si63.png) on edges. This can be derived from the linear Galerkin finite element with a certain quadrature rule. In mathematics and statistics the AM, or simply the mean or average when the context is clear, is the sum of a collection of numbers divided by the number of numbers in the collection. The term “arithmetic mean” is preferred in some contexts in mathematics and statistics because it helps one to distinguish it from other means, such as the geometric mean (GM) and the harmonic mean.

The AM *A*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si64.png) of the positive real numbers *x*[1], *x*[2], …, *x*[*n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si65.png) is defined to be

$A\_{n}: = \\frac{1}{n}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}x\_{i} = \\frac{x\_{1} + x\_{2} + \\cdots + x\_{n}}{n}$ (5.23)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si66.png)(5.23) 

If a set of weights *w*[1], *w*[2], …, *w*[*n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si67.png) is associated to the dataset *x*[1], …, *x*[*n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si68.png), the weighted AM is defined by

$A\_{n}: = \\frac{\\sum\_{i = 1}^{n}{w\_{i}x\_{i}}}{\\sum\_{i = 1}^{n}w\_{i}} = \\frac{w\_{1}x\_{1} + w\_{2}x\_{2} + \\cdots + w\_{n}x\_{n}}{w\_{1} + w\_{2} + \\cdots + w\_{n}}$ (5.24)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si69.png)(5.24) 

The unweighted AM can be regarded as the special case where all of the weights are equal. For conductivity *K*, we use arithmetic average weighted by cell size:

$K\_{i - 0.5,j}^{\\mathit{xx}} = \\frac{h\_{j - 0.5}^{y}K\_{i - 0.5,j - 0.5}^{\\mathit{xx}} + h\_{j + 0.5}^{y}K\_{i - 0.5,j + 0.5}^{\\mathit{xx}}}{h\_{j - 0.5}^{y} + h\_{j + 0.5}^{y}},$ (5.25)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si70.png)(5.25) 

$K\_{i,j - 0.5}^{\\mathit{yy}} = \\frac{h\_{i - 0.5}^{x}K\_{i - 0.5,j - 0.5}^{\\mathit{yy}} + h\_{i + 0.5}^{x}K\_{i + 0.5,j - 0.5}^{\\mathit{yy}}}{h\_{i - 0.5}^{x} + h\_{i + 0.5}^{x}},$ (5.26)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si71.png)(5.26) 

where *h*[*i* − 0.5]^(*x*) = *x*[*i*] − *x*[*i* − 1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si72.png) and *h*[*j* − 0.5]^(*y*) = *y*[*j*] − *y*[*j* − 1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si73.png).

For *x*[1] = 0.5![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si74.png), *x*[2] = 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si75.png) the arithmetic average *A*[2] = 0.75![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si76.png) and the harmonic average *H*[2] = 2/3 = 0.666…![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si77.png) It is clear that the arithmetic average is larger than the harmonic average at least for this example. For *x*[1] = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si78.png), *x*[2] = 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si79.png) the arithmetic average *A*[2] = 0.5![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si80.png) and the harmonic average *H*[2] = 0.![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si81.png) It is also clear that the arithmetic average is larger than the harmonic average at least for this example. It is interesting to see that in both the cases *A*[*n*] ≥ *H*[*n*].![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si82.png) Why? Recall Cauchy’s inequality ∑*r*[*i*]²∑*s*[*i*]² ≥ (∑*r*[*i*]*s*[*i*])²![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si83.png), and we can easily state the following inequality:

$\\frac{A\_{n}}{H\_{n}} = \\frac{1}{n}\\left( \\sum\\limits\_{k = 1}^{n}x\_{k} \\right)\\frac{1}{n}\\left( {\\sum\\limits\_{k = 1}^{n}\\frac{1}{x\_{k}}} \\right) \\geq \\frac{1}{n^{2}}\\left( {\\sum\\limits\_{k = 1}^{n}\\frac{\\sqrt{x\_{k}}}{\\sqrt{x\_{k}}}} \\right)^{2} = \\frac{n^{2}}{n^{2}} = 1.$ (5.27)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si84.png)(5.27) 

For permeability distribution that is heterogeneous in both *x* and *y* directions (in 2D), numerical simulation shows that harmonic averages usually underestimate, while arithmetic averages usually overestimate the true effective permeability. Geometric average is a natural candidate. For *x*[1]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si85.png) and *x*[2]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si86.png) the geometric average of their harmonic mean and their AM is their GM:

$\\sqrt{A\_{2}H\_{2}} = \\sqrt{\\frac{x\_{1} + x\_{2}}{2}\\frac{2}{1/x\_{1} + 1/x\_{2}}} = \\sqrt{x\_{1}x\_{2}}$ (5.28)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si87.png)(5.28) 

In mathematics the GM is a mean or average that indicates the central tendency or typical value of a set of numbers by using the product of their values (as opposed to the AM that uses their sum). The GM is defined as the *n*th root of the product of *n*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si88.png) numbers, that is, for a set of (nonnegative) numbers, the GM is defined as

$G\_{n}: = \\left( \\prod\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}x\_{i} \\right)^{1/n} = \\sqrt\[n\]{x\_{1}x\_{2}\\cdots x\_{n}}$ (5.29)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si89.png)(5.29) 

The GM can also be expressed as the exponential of the AM of logarithms. By using logarithmic identities to transform the formula, the multiplications can be expressed as the sum and the power as a multiplication: when *a*[1], *a*[2], …, *a*[*n*] > 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si90.png),

$\\left( \\prod\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}a\_{i} \\right)^{1/n} = \\exp\\left\\lbrack {\\frac{1}{n}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}{\\ln a\_{i}}} \\right\\rbrack.$ (5.30)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si91.png)(5.30) 

It is sometimes called the log-average (not to be confused with the logarithmic average). It is simply computing the AM of the logarithm-transformed values of *a*[*i*] (i.e., the AM on the log scale) and then using the exponentiation to return the computation to the original scale, that is, it is the generalized *f*\-mean with *f*(*x*) = log *x*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si92.png).

If a set of weights *w*[1], *w*[2], …, *w*[*n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si93.png) is associated to the dataset *x*[1], …, *x*[*n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si94.png) (assuming the weight is normalized; that is, $\\sum\_{k = 1}^{n}{w\_{k} = 1}$![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si95.png)), the weighted GM is defined by

$G\_{n}: = \\prod\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}x\_{i}^{w\_{i}} = x\_{1}^{w\_{1}}x\_{2}^{w\_{2}}\\cdots x\_{n}^{w\_{n}}$ (5.31)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si96.png)(5.31) 

The unweighted GM can be regarded as the special case where all of the weights are equal. It can be easily stated that *A*[*n*] ≥ *G*[*n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si97.png) holds for *x*[*n*] ∈ ℝ > 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si98.png). This inequality is widely known as *Cauchy’s Mean Theorem* or the *arithmetic mean–geometric mean inequality* or *AM–GM inequality*. Some sources give this as *Cauchy’s formula*.

For permeability distribution that is heterogeneous in both *x* and *y* directions (in 2D), numerical simulation shows that harmonic averages usually underestimate, while arithmetic averages usually overestimate the true effective permeability. Using a Monte Carlo technique, Regardless of the type of permeability distribution, the effective permeability can best be approximated by the GM of the distribution. The GM can also be expressed as the exponential of the AM of logarithms. The logarithm of the permeability (not the permeability itself) usually follows Gaussian distribution. A linear transformation of a (multivariate) normal random vector also has a normal distribution. Gaussian variables usually come with the AM.

Noting that sometimes the arithmetic average works, while sometimes the harmonic average works. More often, the geometric average works. In order to unify the three average systems into one same framework, a *power law average* is proposed. In mathematics, generalized means are a family of functions for aggregating sets of numbers that include as special cases the Pythagorean means (arithmetic, geometric, and harmonic means). The generalized mean is also known as power mean or Hölder mean (named after Otto Hölder). In reservoir simulation papers, it is also known as power law average.

Definition: If *p*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si99.png) is a nonzero real number, and *x*[1], …, *x*[*n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si100.png) are positive real numbers, then the generalized mean or power mean with exponent *p* of these positive real numbers is

$M\_{p}\\left( x\_{1},\\ldots,x\_{n} \\right) = \\left( {\\frac{1}{n}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}x\_{i}^{p}} \\right)^{1/p}.$ (5.32)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si101.png)(5.32) 

In this manner the three averages can be written in similar manners as shown in [Table 5.1](#chp005.xhtml#t0010), as well as some other special cases of mean values:

Table 5.1

Generalized mean formula for special cases.

 
| Special cases || **p** Value || Formula |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Harmonic mean || –1 || $M\_{- 1}\\left( x\_{1},\\ldots,x\_{n} \\right) = \\frac{n}{\\left( {1/x\_{1}} \\right) + \\cdots + \\left( {1/x\_{n}} \\right)}.$![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si1.png) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Geometric mean || 0 || $M\_{0}\\left( x\_{1},\\ldots,x\_{n} \\right) = \\lim\\limits\_{p\\rightarrow 0}M\_{p}\\left( x\_{1},\\ldots,x\_{n} \\right) = \\sqrt\[n\]{x\_{1} \\cdot \\cdots \\cdot x\_{n}}.$![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si2.png) |
| Arithmetic mean || 1 || $M\_{1}\\left( x\_{1},\\ldots,x\_{n} \\right) = \\frac{x\_{1} + \\cdots + x\_{n}}{n}.$![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si3.png) |
| Minimum ||  − ∞![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si4.png) || $M\_{- \\infty}\\left( x\_{1},\\ldots,x\_{n} \\right) = \\lim\\limits\_{p\\rightarrow - \\infty}M\_{p}\\left( x\_{1},\\ldots,x\_{n} \\right) = \\min\\{ x\_{1},\\ldots,x\_{n}\\}.$![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si5.png) |
| Quadratic mean || 2 || $M\_{2}\\left( x\_{1},\\ldots,x\_{n} \\right) = \\sqrt{\\left( x\_{1}^{2} + \\cdots + x\_{n}^{2} \\right)/n}.$![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si6.png) |
| Cubic mean || 3 || $M\_{3}\\left( x\_{1},\\ldots,x\_{n} \\right) = \\sqrt\[3\]{\\left( x\_{1}^{3} + \\cdots + x\_{n}^{3} \\right)/n}.$![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si7.png) |
| Maximum ||  + ∞![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si8.png) || $M\_{+ \\infty}\\left( x\_{1},\\ldots,x\_{n} \\right) = \\lim\\limits\_{p\\rightarrow\\infty}M\_{p}\\left( x\_{1},\\ldots,x\_{n} \\right) = \\max\\{ x\_{1},\\ldots,x\_{n}\\}.$![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si9.png) |

![Image](OEBPS/images/T000058tabt0010.png)

Each generalized mean always lies between the smallest and largest of the *x*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si102.png) values. Each generalized mean is a symmetric function of its arguments; permuting the arguments of a generalized mean does not change its value. Like most means, the generalized mean is a homogeneous function of its arguments, that is, *M*[*p*](*b**x*[1],…,*b**x*[*n*]) = *b**M*[*p*](*x*[1],…,*x*[*n*]).![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si103.png) The computation of the mean can be split into computations of equal-sized subblocks (thus we can apply “divide and conquer (D&C)” algorithm for upscaling):

*M*[*p*](*x*[1],…,*x*[*n* ⋅ *k*]) = *M*[*p*]\[*M*[*p*](*x*[1],…,*x*[*k*]),*M*[*p*](*x*[*k* + 1],…,*x*[2 ⋅ *k*]),…,*M*[*p*](*x*[(*n* − 1) ⋅ *k* + 1],…,*x*[*n* ⋅ *k*])\]. (5.33)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si104.png)(5.33) 

The *D&C algorithm* for upscaling is the essence of the renormalization method, which is to break a large problem down into a sequence of smaller and more manageable stages. At each stage the current grid is divided up into cells, each consisting of a small number of grid blocks, and the effective permeability of each cell is determined. These cells then become the grid blocks of the next coarse grid, and the whole process is repeated. In effect, repeated application of the rescaling operation captures successively the effects of increasingly larger scale heterogeneities on the large-scale flow behavior of interest.

For a sequence of positive weights *w*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si105.png) with ∑*w*[*i*] = 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si106.png), we define the weighted power mean as

$\\begin{matrix} {M\_{p}\\left( x\_{1},\\ldots,x\_{n} \\right)} & {= \\left( {\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}{w\_{i}x\_{i}^{p}}} \\right)^{1/p}} \\\\ {M\_{0}\\left( x\_{1},\\ldots,x\_{n} \\right)} & {= \\prod\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}x\_{i}^{w\_{i}}} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (5.34)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si107.png)(5.34) 

The power mean could be generalized further to the generalized *f*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si108.png)\-mean:

$M\_{f}\\left( x\_{1},\\ldots,x\_{n} \\right) = f^{- 1}\\left( {\\frac{1}{n} \\cdot \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}{f\\left( x\_{i} \\right)}} \\right).$ (5.35)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si109.png)(5.35) 

This covers the GM without using a limit with *f*(*x*) = log (*x*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si110.png).

Consider a block consisting of 3 × 3![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si111.png) equal-sized subblocks *B*[*i**j*], *i* = 1, 2, 3, *j* = 1, 2, 3![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si112.png) with corresponding permeability *K*[*i**j*] = *K*[*i**j*]^(*x**x*) = *K*[*i**j*]^(*y**y*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si113.png). The bottom layer (horizontal slice) consists of *B*[11]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si114.png), *B*[21]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si115.png), and *B*[31]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si116.png), while the first vertical slice consists of *B*[11]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si117.png), *B*[12]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si118.png), and *B*[13]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si119.png). The *harmonic–arithmetic average* is defined as

*K*[*e**f**f*, *h* − *a*]^(*x**x*) :  = *M*[1](*M*[ − 1](*K*[11],*K*[21],*K*[31]),*M*[ − 1](*K*[12],*K*[22],*K*[32]),*M*[ − 1](*K*[13],*K*[23],*K*[33])). (5.36)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si120.png)(5.36) 

The *arithmetic–harmonic average* is defined as

*K*[*e**f**f*, *a* − *h*]^(*x**x*) :  = *M*[ − 1](*M*[1](*K*[11],*K*[12],*K*[13]),*M*[1](*K*[21],*K*[22],*K*[23]),*M*[1](*K*[31],*K*[32],*K*[33])). (5.37)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si121.png)(5.37) 

In physical modeling the harmonic–arithmetic average treats the block as three independent layers, each layer consisting of three subblocks, while the arithmetic–harmonic average treats the block as three vertical slices glued together by two conductors (fractures) and vertical slice consists of three subblocks. A quick comparison of the two averages can be performed as: In deriving *K*[*e**f**f*, *h* − *a*]^(*x**x*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si122.png), we add artificial barriers. In deriving *K*[*e**f**f*, *a* − *h*]^(*x**x*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si123.png), we add artificial conductors. Effective permeability *K*[*e**f**f*, *s**e**a**l**e**d**S**i**d**e**s**B**C*]^(*x**x*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si124.png) is obtained from the flow-based method with sealed-sides boundary conditions. Thus we can state

*K*[*e**f**f*, *h* − *a*]^(*x**x*) ≤ *K*[*e**f**f*, *s**e**a**l**e**d**S**i**d**e**s**B**C*]^(*x**x*) ≤ *K*[*e**f**f*, *a* − *h*]^(*x**x*) (5.38)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si125.png)(5.38)  

### 5.1.3 Simulation-based upscaling schemes

CCFD can apply to the same mesh that the cell-wise constant permeability is defined on; CCFD can also apply to a more refined mesh than the mesh defining the cell-wise constant permeability; but both are expensive. We would like to apply CCFD on a coarse mesh to reduce the flow simulation cost. But whenever we use a coarser mesh than the mesh defining the cell-wise constant permeability, we need a certain upscaling algorithm for converting the permeability from the fine mesh to the coarse mesh.

Note that *coarse-grid finite element methods (FEMs)* (with exact integration or with quadrature defined on a fine grid) can treat the cell-wise constant permeability defined on the fine-grid in a straightforward way. It defines a permeability upscaling scheme itself \[Galerkin FEM (GFEM) with weighted AM while, MsFEM with weighted HM\]. A principal motivation for the development of upscaling techniques has been the development of geostatistical reservoir description algorithms. These algorithms now routinely result in fine-scale descriptions of reservoir porosity and permeability on grids of many billions of cells. The descriptions honor the known and inferred statistics of the reservoir properties. These reservoir-description grids are far too fine to be used as grids in reservoir simulators. Despite advances in computer hardware, most full-field reservoir models still use millions of cells or less, a factor of 1000 down on the geological grid. Upscaling is needed to bridge the gap between these two scales.

Given a fine-scale reservoir description and a simulation grid, an upscaling algorithm assigns suitable values for porosity, permeability, and other flow functions to cells on the coarse simulation grid. The aim is simply to preserve the gross features of flow on the simulation grid. The algorithm calculates an “effective permeability,” which results in the same total flow of single-phase fluid through the coarse, homogeneous block as that obtained from the fine heterogeneous block. Upscaling has become an increasingly important tool for converting highly detailed geological models to simulation grids.

The simulation-based upscaling methods are standard ways to estimate the effective permeability of a grid block. Simulation-based upscaling schemes, also known as flow-based upscaling methods, or pressure-solver methods, are the algorithms based on flow simulation, usually using CCFD or finite volume methods. Effective absolute permeability is needed for two-phase flow, but to get effective absolute permeability by using simulation-based methods, we run only single-phase flow simulation. In the pressure-solver method, we set up a single-phase-flow calculation with specified boundary conditions and then ask what value of effective permeability yields the same flow rate as the fine-grid calculation. The results we obtain depend on the assumptions we make, particularly with regard to boundary conditions.

The effective permeability is determined numerically by solving the single-phase flow equations in accordance with mass balance equations and Darcy’s law. With simulation-based methods or pressure-solver methods, the most common assumptions are single-phase flow, incompressible fluid, constant pressures over the inlet and outlet faces with pressure difference, or constant body force. Local upscaling: the flow-based methods have traditionally been restricted to solving the pressure field locally, that is, for a single flow cell at a time, because it used to be too time-consuming to compute the fine-scale pressure field for the complete geo grid in a single operation.

The effective permeability is computed separately and independently of the other flow cells, which may or may not be correct depending on how representative the imposed pressure conditions along the faces of the flow cell are. Different types of artificial boundary conditions for the flow cell have been suggested over the years, all with the objective of providing as good an approximation of the real boundary conditions as possible. An important design criterion for the artificial boundary conditions is the conservation of flux in and out of the flow cell. Boundary condition needs to satisfy the compatibility condition.

In order to define and calculate our effective permeability, we need a quantity to match between a heterogeneous block with a homogeneous block. Total volumetric flux on the out-flow boundary can be expressed by

$k\_{eff}^{\\mathit{xx}}: = - \\frac{\\mu\\Delta\\mathit{xQ}}{A}.$ (5.39)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si126.png)(5.39) 

The correlation between average Darcy velocity and average pressure gradient can be expressed as

$\\langle\\mathbf{u}\\rangle = - \\frac{\\mathbf{K}\_{eff}}{\\mu}\\left( \\langle\\nabla p\\rangle - \\rho\\mathbf{g} \\right).$ (5.40)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si127.png)(5.40) 

Having solved the microscale problem, we define the effective hydraulic conductivity tensor by

⟨*K*(**x**)∇*p*⟩ = **K**[*e**f**f*]⟨∇*p*⟩ = **K**[*e**f**f*]**G**. (5.41)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si128.png)(5.41) 

This is desired because we would like to have ⟨**u**⟩![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si129.png) and ⟨∇*p*⟩![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si130.png) satisfies macroscale Darcy’s law with the effective hydraulic conductivity. The *Dirichlet boundary condition* can be expressed as

*p*(**x**) = **G** ⋅ **x**, **x** ∈ ∂*Ω* (5.42)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si131.png)(5.42) 

where **G** ∈ ℝ^(*d*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si132.png) is a given fixed constant vector. It is easy to see ⟨∇*p*⟩ = **G**![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si133.png) that |*Ω*|⟨∇*p*⟩ = ∫[*Ω*]∇*p**d***x** = ∫[∂*Ω*]*p***n***d**S* = ∫[∂*Ω*]**G** ⋅ **x****n***d**S* = ∫[*Ω*]∇(**G**⋅**x**)*d***x** = ∫[*Ω*]**G** ⋅ ∇**x***d***x** = ∫[*Ω*]**G****I***d***x** = ∫[*Ω*]**G***d***x** = **G**∫[*Ω*]1*d***x** = |*Ω*|**G**.![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si134.png)

We do not have to solve for all **G** ∈ ℝ^(*d*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si135.png). Since the problem (flow simulation) is linear, we need only solve for each basis of **G**![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si136.png). For 2D problems, we can choose the two unit vectors as the basis: **G** = (1,0)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si137.png) and **G** = (0,1)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si138.png). If the domain *Ω* = (0,1)²![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si139.png), choosing **G** = (1,0)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si140.png) corresponds to the boundary condition of *p*[*w*] = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si141.png), *p*[*e*] = 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si142.png), and *p*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si143.png) linear on the two other boundaries (*n*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si144.png) and *s*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si145.png)); choosing **G** = (0,1)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si146.png) corresponds to the boundary condition of *p*[*s*] = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si147.png), *p*[*n*] = 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si148.png), and *p*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si149.png) linear on the two other boundaries (*w*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si150.png) and *e*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si151.png)). It is clear that Dirichlet formulation is equivalent to the flow-based method with linear (open-sided) boundary condition.

For a *periodic boundary condition*: *p*(**x**) = **G** ⋅ **x**![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si152.png), we can also have ⟨∇*p*⟩ = *G*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si153.png). For a *Neumann boundary condition*:  − *K*(**x**)∇*p* ⋅ **n** = **v** ⋅ **n**, **x** ∈ ∂*Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si154.png). Neumann formulation is similar to the no-flow boundary condition, except we replace the imposed pressure condition on the inlet/outlet boundaries by the imposed Darcy velocity’s normal component. For convenience of analysis, we also consider another Neumann formulation that is equivalent to the previous Neumann formulation, where **v** ∈ ℝ^(*d*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si155.png) is the Lagrange multiplier for the constraint that ⟨∇*p*⟩ = **G**,![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si156.png) where **G** ∈ ℝ^(*d*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si157.png) is a given fixed constant vector.

By using numerical simulation, people have the observation reported that in general the Dirichlet formulation provides an overestimate for the effective conductivity tensor and the Neumann formulation provides an underestimate. For each of the three formulations, we obtain a (possibly different) effective hydraulic conductivity tensor **K**[*e**f**f*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si158.png). Let us denote them as **K**[*e**f**f*]^(*D*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si159.png), **K**[*e**f**f*]^(*P*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si160.png), and **K**[*e**f**f*]^(*N*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si161.png), for the one obtained from the Dirichlet formulation, the periodic formulation, and the Neumann formulation, respectively.

 

Theorem 5.1

For the three effective tensors, the following relation is valid:

**K**[*e**f**f*]^(*N*) ≤ **K**[*e**f**f*]^(*P*) ≤ **K**[*e**f**f*]^(*D*). (5.43)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si162.png)(5.43) 

To prove this theorem, we first note that the effective tensor **K**[*e**f**f*]^(*N*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si163.png) has a variational formulation: For any **G** ∈ ℝ^(*d*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si164.png),

$\\mathbf{G}^{T}\\mathbf{K}\_{eff}^{N}\\mathbf{G} = \\min\\limits\_{p(\\mathbf{x}) \\in \\mathbf{W}\_{N}}\\langle K\\left( \\mathbf{x} \\right)\\nabla p \\cdot \\nabla p\\rangle,$ (5.44)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si165.png)(5.44) 

where the space of admissible function is

*W*[*N*] = {*p*(**x**) ∈ *H*¹(*Ω*) : ⟨∇*p*⟩ = *G*}. (5.45)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si166.png)(5.45) 

To see the previous variational formulation, we let *p*(**x**)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si167.png) be the solution of the Neumann problem with the proper constraint. We know *p*(**x**)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si168.png) satisfies

$\\int\_{\\Omega}{K\\left( \\mathbf{x} \\right)\\nabla p} \\cdot \\nabla\\mathit{pd}\\mathbf{x} = \\min\\limits\_{w \\in W\_{N}}\\int\_{\\Omega}{K\\left( \\mathbf{x} \\right)\\nabla w} \\cdot \\nabla\\mathit{wd}\\mathbf{x}.$ (5.46)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si169.png)(5.46) 

We then have

∫[*Ω*]**G**^(*T*)**K**[*e**f**f*]^(*N*)**G***d***x** = ∫[*Ω*]*K*(**x**)∇*p* ⋅ **G***d***x** = ∫[*Ω*]*K*(**x**)∇*p* ⋅ ∇*p**d***x** + ∫[*Ω*]*K*(**x**)∇*p* ⋅ ∇(**G**⋅**x**−*p*)*d***x**. (5.47)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si170.png)(5.47) 

It remains to show

∫[*Ω*]*K*(**x**)∇*p* ⋅ ∇(**G**⋅**x**−*p*)*d***x** = 0. (5.48)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si171.png)(5.48) 

In fact, by Green’s formula,

$\\begin{matrix} & {\\int\_{\\Omega}{K\\left( \\mathbf{x} \\right)\\nabla p} \\cdot \\nabla\\left( \\mathbf{G} \\cdot \\mathbf{x} - p \\right)d\\mathbf{x}} \\\\ = & {\\int\_{\\partial\\Omega}{K\\left( \\mathbf{x} \\right)\\nabla p} \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\\left( \\mathbf{G} \\cdot \\mathbf{x} - p \\right)\\mathit{dS}} \\\\ & {- \\int\_{\\Omega}\\nabla \\cdot \\left( K\\left( \\mathbf{x} \\right)\\nabla p \\right)\\left( \\mathbf{G} \\cdot \\mathbf{x} - p \\right)d\\mathbf{x}} \\\\ = & {\\int\_{\\partial\\Omega}{K\\left( \\mathbf{x} \\right)\\nabla p} \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\\left( \\mathbf{G} \\cdot \\mathbf{x} - p \\right)\\mathit{dS}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (5.49)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si172.png)(5.49) 

We recall the Lagrange multiplier **v** ∈ ℝ^(*d*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si173.png) and see

$\\begin{matrix} & {\\int\_{\\partial\\Omega}{K\\left( \\mathbf{x} \\right)\\nabla p} \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\\left( {\\mathbf{G} \\cdot \\mathbf{x} - p} \\right)\\mathit{dS}} \\\\ = & {- \\int\_{\\partial\\Omega}\\mathbf{v} \\cdot \\mathbf{n}\\left( {\\mathbf{G} \\cdot \\mathbf{x} - p} \\right)\\mathit{dS}} \\\\ = & {- \\int\_{\\Omega}\\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\mathbf{v}\\left( {\\mathbf{G} \\cdot \\mathbf{x} - p} \\right)} \\right)d\\mathbf{x}} \\\\ = & {- \\mathbf{v} \\cdot \\int\_{\\Omega}{\\left( {\\mathbf{G} - \\nabla p} \\right)d\\mathbf{x}} = 0,} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (5.50)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si174.png)(5.50) 

where we have used ∇**x** = **I**.![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si175.png)

Similarly, we can show that the effective tensors **K**[*e**f**f*]^(*D*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si176.png) and **K**[*e**f**f*]^(*P*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si177.png) have variational formulations: For any **G** ∈ ℝ^(*d*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si178.png),

$\\mathbf{G}^{T}\\mathbf{K}\_{eff}^{P}\\mathbf{G} = \\min\\limits\_{p(\\mathbf{x}) \\in \\mathbf{W}\_{P}}\\langle K\\left( \\mathbf{x} \\right)\\nabla p \\cdot \\nabla p\\rangle,$ (5.51)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si179.png)(5.51) 

$\\mathbf{G}^{T}\\mathbf{K}\_{eff}^{D}\\mathbf{G} = \\min\\limits\_{p(\\mathbf{x}) \\in \\mathbf{W}\_{D}}\\langle K\\left( \\mathbf{x} \\right)\\nabla p \\cdot \\nabla p\\rangle,$ (5.52)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si180.png)(5.52) 

where the spaces of admissible functions are

*W*[*P*] = *{**p*(**x**) ∈ *H*¹(ℝ^(*d*)) *:* *p*(**x**) − **G** ⋅ **x***i**s**p**e**r**i**o**d**i**c**w**i**t**h**p**e**r**i**o**d**Ω*}. (5.53)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si181.png)(5.53) 

*W*[*D*] = *{**p*(**x**) ∈ *H*¹(*Ω*) *:* *p*(**x**) = **G** ⋅ **x***o**n*∂*Ω*}. (5.54)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si182.png)(5.54) 

It is easy to see that

*W*[*D*] ⊂ *W*[*P*] ⊂ *W*[*N*]. (5.55)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si183.png)(5.55) 

Thus the inequality theorem has been proved. For **K**[*e**f**f*]^(*N*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si184.png), **K**[*e**f**f*]^(*P*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si185.png), and **K**[*e**f**f*]^(*D*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si186.png), and their numerical solutions by GFEM, **K**[*e**f**f*, *h*]^(*N*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si187.png), **K**[*e**f**f*, *h*]^(*P*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si188.png), and **K**[*e**f**f*, *h*]^(*D*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si189.png), we have

0 ≤ **K**[*e**f**f*, *h*]^(*N*) − **K**[*e**f**f*]^(*N*) ≤ *C**h***I**, (5.56)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si190.png)(5.56) 

00 ≤ **K**[*e**f**f*, *h*]^(*P*) − **K**[*e**f**f*]^(*P*) ≤ *C**h***I**, (5.57)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si191.png)(5.57) 

0 ≤ **K**[*e**f**f*, *h*]^(*D*) − **K**[*e**f**f*]^(*D*) ≤ *C**h***I**. (5.58)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si192.png)(5.58)  

### 5.1.4 Example of upscaling methods for effective permeability

For a 10 × 6![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si193.png) uniform grid the permeability field is given in [Fig. 5.1](#chp005.xhtml#f0010).

![Figure 5.1 Permeability field.](OEBPS/images/F000058f05-01-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 5.1 Permeability field.

The effective permeability is calculated by using the weighted arithmetic average as

$\\mathbf{K}\_{A}^{eff} = \\begin{bmatrix} 4.37 & 0 \\\\ 0 & 4.37 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si194.png)

If a set of weights *w*[1], *w*[2], …, *w*[*n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si195.png) is associated to the dataset *x*[1], *x*[2], …, *x*[*n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si196.png), the weighted harmonic mean is defined by

$H\_{n}: = \\frac{\\sum\_{i = 1}^{n}w\_{i}}{\\sum\_{i = 1}^{n}{w\_{i}/x\_{i}}} = \\left( \\frac{\\sum\_{i = 1}^{n}{w\_{i}x\_{i}^{- 1}}w\_{i}x\_{i}^{- 1}}{\\sum\_{i = 1}^{n}w\_{i}w\_{i}} \\right)^{- 1}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si197.png)

Then the weighted arithmetic average of this permeability field can be calculated as

$\\mathbf{K}\_{H}^{eff} = \\begin{bmatrix} 3.27 & 0 \\\\ 0 & 3.27 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si198.png)

If a set of weights *w*[1], *w*[2], …, *w*[*n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si199.png) is associated to the dataset *x*[1], *x*[2], …, *x*[*n*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si200.png), (assuming the weight is normalized, i.e., ∑*w*[*i*] = 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si201.png)), the weighted GM is defined by

$G\_{n}: = \\prod\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}x\_{i}^{w\_{i}} = x\_{1}^{w\_{1}}x\_{2}^{w\_{2}}\\cdots x\_{n}^{w\_{n}}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si202.png)

Then the effective permeability is calculated by using the weighted geometric average can be calculated as

$\\mathbf{K}\_{G}^{eff} = \\begin{bmatrix} 3.83 & 0 \\\\ 0 & 3.83 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si203.png)

For the weighted power law average, similarly it can be calculated as

$M\_{p}\\left( x\_{1},\\ldots,x\_{n} \\right) = \\left( {\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}{w\_{i}x\_{i}^{p}}w\_{i}x\_{i}^{p}} \\right)^{1/p}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si204.png)

Then, in the case *p* = 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si205.png),

$\\mathbf{K}\_{p = 1}^{eff} = \\begin{bmatrix} 4.37 & 0 \\\\ 0 & 4.37 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix} = \\mathbf{K}\_{A}^{eff}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si206.png)

Similarly, in the case *p* = 2![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si207.png)

$\\mathbf{K}\_{p = 0}^{eff} = \\begin{bmatrix} 3.83 & 0 \\\\ 0 & 3.83 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix} = \\mathbf{K}\_{G}^{eff},$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si208.png)

and in the case *p* =  − 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si209.png)

$\\mathbf{K}\_{p = - 1}^{eff} = \\begin{bmatrix} 3.26 & 0 \\\\ 0 & 3.26 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix} = \\mathbf{K}\_{G}^{eff}.$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si210.png)

As *p*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si211.png) increases from  − ∞![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si212.png) to  + ∞![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si213.png), the average value increases from min (*x*[1],…,*x*[*n*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si214.png) to max (*x*[1],…,*x*[*n*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si215.png), as shown in [Fig. 5.2](#chp005.xhtml#f0015).

![Figure 5.2 Weighted power law average.](OEBPS/images/F000058f05-02-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 5.2 Weighted power law average.

Treating the block as six independent layers, each layer consisting of 10 subblocks, as shown in [Fig. 5.3](#chp005.xhtml#f0020).

![Figure 5.3 Permeability field with layers.](OEBPS/images/F000058f05-03-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 5.3 Permeability field with layers.

The effective permeability is calculated by using the harmonic–arithmetic average as

$\\mathbf{K}\_{H - A}^{eff} = \\begin{bmatrix} 3.95 & 0 \\\\ 0 & 3.54 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si216.png)

Treating the block as 10 vertical slices glued together by two conductors. Each vertical slice consists of six subblocks, as shown in [Fig. 5.4](#chp005.xhtml#f0025).

![Figure 5.4 Permeability field with layers.](OEBPS/images/F000058f05-04-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 5.4 Permeability field with layers.

The effective permeability is calculated by using the arithmetic–harmonic average as

$\\mathbf{K}\_{A - H}^{eff} = \\begin{bmatrix} 4.24 & 0 \\\\ 0 & 3.80 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si217.png)

 

### 5.1.5 Example of simulation-based upscaling schemes

The mass continuity law tells us that there is no net accumulation or loss of fluid within a grid block:

*q*[*i**n*]^(*x*) + *q*[*i**n*]^(*y*) = *q*[*o**u**t*]^(*x*) + *q*[*o**u**t*]^(*y*)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si218.png)

and the direction is defined in [Fig. 5.5](#chp005.xhtml#f0030).

![Figure 5.5 Fluid direction.](OEBPS/images/F000058f05-05-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 5.5 Fluid direction.

Darcy’s law is used to express the flows in terms of the pressures and permeabilities. The grid blocks in the next figure are of length *Δ**x*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si219.png) and width *Δ**y*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si220.png) (and unit height in the *z*\-direction). Cell (*i*,*j*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si221.png) in the discretization is illustrated in [Fig. 5.6](#chp005.xhtml#f0035).

![Figure 5.6 Discretization grids.](OEBPS/images/F000058f05-06-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 5.6 Discretization grids.

Let us introduce the notation of the transmissibilities

$T^{x} = \\frac{k^{x}\\Delta y}{\\Delta x},T^{y} = \\frac{k^{y}\\Delta x}{\\Delta y}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si222.png)

Assume the height, *Δ**z* = 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si223.png). We can therefore derive the pressure equation:

(*T*[*i* − 0.5, *j*]^(*x*)+*T*[*i* + 0.5, *j*]^(*x*)+*T*[*i*, *j* − 0.5]^(*y*)+*T*[*i*, *j* + 0.5]^(*y*))*P*[*i*, *j*] − *T*[*i* − 0.5, *j*]^(*x*)*P*[*i* − 1, *j*] − *T*[*i* + 0.5, *j*]^(*x*)*P*[*i* + 1, *j*] − *T*[*i*, *j* − 0.5]^(*y*)*P*[*i*, *j* − 1] − *T*[*i*, *j* + 0.5]^(*y*)*P*[*i*, *j* + 1] = 0

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si224.png)

The previous equation applies to internal cells only. For boundary cells (i.e., the cells touching the domain boundary), we need special treatment to be discussed shortly. The transmissibilities are known, and using the appropriate boundary conditions, we can solve this set of linear equations to obtain the pressure in each cell. The effective permeability is then calculated from the total flux and the total pressure drop. A Dirichlet boundary condition is a type of boundary condition that specifies the Pressure value at the boundary, as shown in [Fig. 5.7](#chp005.xhtml#f0040).

![Figure 5.7 Dirichlet boundary condition.](OEBPS/images/F000058f05-07-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 5.7 Dirichlet boundary condition.

For this case,

$q\_{\\mathit{out}}^{x} = - k\_{i + 0.5,j}^{x}\\frac{\\left( P\_{b} - P\_{i,j} \\right)}{{\\Delta x}/2}\\Delta y = - 2T\_{x + 0.5,j}^{x}\\left( P\_{b} - P\_{i,j} \\right)$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si225.png)

We can therefore derive the pressure equation for boundary cell

(*T*[*i* − 0.5, *j*]^(*x*)+2*T*[*i* + 0.5, *j*]^(*x*)+*T*[*i*, *j* − 0.5]^(*y*)+*T*[*i*, *j* + 0.5]^(*y*))*P*[*i*, *j*] − *T*[*i* − 0.5, *j*]^(*x*)*P*[*i* − 1, *j*] − *T*[*i*, *j* − 0.5]^(*y*)*P*[*i*, *j* − 1] − *T*[*i*, *j* + 0.5]^(*y*)*P*[*i*, *j* + 1] = 2*T*[*i* + 0.5, *j*]^(*x*)*P*[*b*]

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si226.png)

If the flux (or normal gradient to the face) is specified at the boundary, then the boundary condition is denoted by a Neumann boundary condition. In this case the specified flux is given in [Fig. 5.8](#chp005_a.xhtml#f0045).

![Figure 5.8 Neumann boundary condition.](OEBPS/images/F000058f05-08-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 5.8 Neumann boundary condition.

For this case, *q*[*o**u**t*]^(*x*) = *q*[*b*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si227.png) and we can therefor derive the pressure equation for boundary cell

(*T*[*i* − 0.5, *j*]^(*x*)+*T*[*i*, *j* − 0.5]^(*y*)+*T*[*i*, *j* + 0.5]^(*y*))*P*[*i*, *j*] − *T*[*i* − 0.5, *j*]^(*x*)*P*[*i* − 1, *j*] − *T*[*i*, *j* − 0.5]^(*y*)*P*[*i*, *j* − 1] − *T*[*i*, *j* + 0.5]^(*y*)*P*[*i*, *j* + 1] =  − *q*[*b*]

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si228.png)

For periodic boundary conditions illustrated in [Fig. 5.9](#chp005_a.xhtml#f0050),

*P*[*i*, 0] = *P*[*i*, *n*[*y*]], *P*[*i*, *n*[*y*] + 1] = *P*[*i*, 1], *P*[0, *j*] = *P*[*n*[*x*], *j*] + *Δ**P*, *P*[*n*[*x*] + 1, *j*] = *P*[1, *j*] − *Δ**P*

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si229.png)![Figure 5.9 Periodic boundary condition.](OEBPS/images/F000058f05-09-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 5.9 Periodic boundary condition.

The pressure equation on the boundary cell can be derived as ([Fig. 5.10](#chp005_a.xhtml#f0055))

(*T*[*i* − 0.5, *j*]^(*x*)+*T*[*i* + 0.5, *j*]^(*x*)+*T*[*i*, *j* − 0.5]^(*y*)+*T*[*i*, *j* + 0.5]^(*y*))*P*[*i*, *j*] − *T*[*i* − 0.5, *j*]^(*x*)*P*[*i* − 1, *j*] − *T*[*i* + 0.5, *j*]^(*x*)*P*[1, *j*] − *T*[*i*, *j* − 0.5]^(*y*)*P*[*i*, *j* − 1] − *T*[*i*, *j* + 0.5]^(*y*)*P*[*i*, *j* + 1] =  − *T*[*i* + 0.5, *j*]^(*x*)*Δ**P*

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si230.png)![Figure 5.10 Boundary cell.](OEBPS/images/F000058f05-10-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 5.10 Boundary cell.

For sealed-sides boundary conditions, by allowing no flow to pass through the sides of the cell, all fluxes are forced to go in the principal direction of flow. Therefore this type of boundary conditions is often referred to as the no-flow or sealed-sides boundary conditions. The boundary is illustrated in [Fig. 5.11](#chp005_a.xhtml#f0060).

![Figure 5.11 Sealed-sides boundary conditions.](OEBPS/images/F000058f05-11-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 5.11 Sealed-sides boundary conditions.

For the permeability field illustrated in [Fig. 5.1](#chp005.xhtml#f0010), the directional effective permeability is calculated by using the simulation-based method with sealed-sides boundary conditions as

$\\mathbf{K}\_{S}^{eff} = \\begin{bmatrix} 4.14 & 0 \\\\ 0 & 3.5 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si231.png)

The directional effective permeability is calculated by using the simulation-based method with Dirichlet boundary conditions as

$\\mathbf{K}\_{D}^{eff} = \\begin{bmatrix} 4.2 & {- 0.16} \\\\ {- 0.16} & 3.75 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si232.png)

The effective permeabilities calculated by using the simulation-based method with periodic boundary conditions and Neumann boundary conditions can be expressed, respectively, as

$\\mathbf{K}\_{P}^{eff} = \\begin{bmatrix} 4.14 & {- 0.07} \\\\ {- 0.07} & 3.59 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix},\\mathbf{K}\_{N}^{eff} = \\begin{bmatrix} 4.04 & 0 \\\\ 0 & 3.37 \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si233.png)

Let us compare the results:

*e**i**g*(**K**[*D*]^(*e**f**f*)−**K**[*P*]^(*e**f**f*)) = (0.007,0.213)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si234.png)

*e**i**g*(**K**[*P*]^(*e**f**f*)−**K**[*N*]^(*e**f**f*)) = (0.068,0.252)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si235.png)

We illustrate the upscaled effective permeability results in fine mesh and coarse mesh as shown in [Figs. 5.12](#chp005_a.xhtml#f0065)–[5.23](#chp005_a.xhtml#f0120).

![Figure 5.12 Permeability (upper) and pressure and velocity (lower) illustration of fine mesh using arithmetic mean.](OEBPS/images/F000058f05-12-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 5.12 Permeability (upper) and pressure and velocity (lower) illustration of fine mesh using arithmetic mean.

![Figure 5.13 Permeability (upper) and pressure and velocity (lower) illustration of coarse mesh using arithmetic mean.](OEBPS/images/F000058f05-13-9780128209578.jpg)

## 5.2 Generalized multiscale finite element methods for porous media

 

### 5.2.1 Multiscale Galerkin finite element method

The multiscale basis function for the node *i*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si236.png) is given by (*a*(*x*)*ϕ*^′)^′ = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si237.png) with the support in \[*x*[*i* − 1],*x*[*i* + 1]\]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si238.png). An illustration of one-dimensional (1D) multiscale basis functions is shown in [Fig. 5.24](#chp005_a.xhtml#f0125). From (*a*(*x*)*ϕ*^′)^′ = 0![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si239.png), it is easy to see that *a*(*x*)*ϕ*[*i*]^′ = *c**o**n**s**t*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si240.png), where the constants are different in \[*x*[*i* − 1],*x*[*i*]\]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si241.png) and \[*x*[*i*],*x*[*i* + 1]\],![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si242.png) which is because in Galerkin approximation, the Darcy velocity may not be continuous. This constant can be easily computed by writing *ϕ*[*i*]^′ = *c**o**n**s**t*/*a*(*x*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si243.png) and integrating it over \[*x*[*i* − 1],*x*[*i*]\]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si244.png), which yields that

$a\\left( x \\right)\\phi\_{i}^{\\prime} = \\frac{1}{\\int\_{x\_{i - 1}}^{x\_{i}}{\\mathit{dx}/{a\\left( x \\right)}}},{on}\\left\\lbrack x\_{i - 1},x\_{i} \\right\\rbrack.$ (5.59)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si245.png)(5.59) ![Figure 5.24 MS basis functions: illustration. MS, Multiscale.](OEBPS/images/F000058f05-24-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 5.24 MS basis functions: illustration. *MS*, Multiscale.

Similarly, we can have

$a\\left( x \\right)\\phi\_{i}^{\\prime} = - \\frac{1}{\\int\_{x\_{i}}^{x\_{i + 1}}{\\mathit{dx}/{a\\left( x \\right)}}},{on}\\left\\lbrack x\_{i},x\_{i + 1} \\right\\rbrack.$ (5.60)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si246.png)(5.60) 

Then the elements of the stiffness matrix *A*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si247.png) are given by

$\\begin{matrix} a\_{\\mathit{ij}} & {= \\int\_{x\_{i - 1}}^{x\_{i}}{a\\left( x \\right)\\phi\_{i}^{\\prime}\\left( \\phi\_{j}^{0} \\right)^{\\prime}\\mathit{dx}} + \\int\_{x\_{i}}^{x\_{i + 1}}{a\\left( x \\right)\\phi\_{i}^{\\prime}\\left( \\phi\_{j}^{0} \\right)^{\\prime}\\mathit{dx}}} \\\\ & {= \\frac{1}{\\int\_{x\_{i - 1}}^{x\_{i}}{\\mathit{dx}/{a\\left( x \\right)}}}\\int\_{x\_{i - 1}}^{x\_{i}}{\\left( \\phi\_{j}^{0} \\right)^{\\prime}\\mathit{dx}} - \\frac{1}{\\int\_{x\_{i}}^{x\_{i + 1}}{\\mathit{dx}/{a\\left( x \\right)}}}\\int\_{x\_{i}}^{x\_{i + 1}}{\\left( \\phi\_{j}^{0} \\right)^{\\prime}\\mathit{dx}}.} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (5.61)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si248.png)(5.61) 

Taking into account that ∫[*x*[*i* − 1]]^(*x*[*i*])(*ϕ*[ − 1]⁰)^′*d**x* =  − 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si249.png) and ∫[*x*[*i* − 1]]^(*x*[*i*])(*ϕ*)[*i*]⁰^′*d**x* = 1![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si250.png), we have

$a\_{i,i} = - a\_{i,i - 1} - a\_{i,i + 1},a\_{i,i - 1} = - \\frac{1}{\\int\_{x\_{i - 1}}^{x\_{i}}{\\mathit{dx}/{a\\left( x \\right)}}},a\_{i,i + 1} = - \\frac{1}{\\int\_{x\_{i}}^{x\_{i + 1}}{\\mathit{dx}/{a\\left( x \\right)}}}.$ (5.62)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si251.png)(5.62) 

An illustration of the multiscale solution and multiscale basis functions in one dimension is shown in [Fig. 5.25](#chp005_a.xhtml#f0130).

![Figure 5.25 MsFEM illustration. MsFEM, Multiscale finite element method.](OEBPS/images/F000058f05-25-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 5.25 MsFEM illustration. *MsFEM*, Multiscale finite element method.

We see that the stiffness matrix has tri-diagonal form and the linear system, where *b*[*i*] = ∫[0]¹*f**ϕ*[*i*]⁰*d**x*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si252.png). If *a*(*x*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si253.png) is a cell-wise constant function in the coarse mesh, we see

$a\_{i,i - 1} = - \\frac{a\_{i - 0.5}}{H},a\_{i,i + 1} = - \\frac{a\_{i + 0.5}}{H}a\_{i,i} = - a\_{i,i - 1} - a\_{i,i + 1} = \\frac{a\_{i - 0.5} + a\_{i + 0.5}}{H}.$ (5.63)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si254.png)(5.63) 

For high-dimensional problems, we can extend the multiscale FEM (MsFEM) schemes. Consider the linear elliptic equations:

$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathit{Lu}: =} & {- \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {a\\left( x \\right)\\nabla u} \\right) = f{in}\\Omega,} \\\\ {u =} & {0{on}\\partial\\Omega,} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$ (5.64)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si255.png)(5.64) 

where *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si256.png) is a domain in (*d*\=2,3)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si257.png). The parameter *a*(*x*) = (*a*[*i**j*](*x*))![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si258.png) is a heterogeneous field varying over multiple scales. It is symmetric positive definite and bounded. MsFEM basically consists of two parts: Part 1: multiscale basis function construction; Part 2: a choice of the global formulation that couples these basis functions.

First, we discuss the multiscale basis function construction. Let 𝒯[*H*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si259.png) be a usual partition of *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si260.png) into simplices. We call this partition the coarse grid and assume that the coarse grid can be resolved via finer resolution called fine scale. Let *x*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si261.png) be the interior nodes of the mesh 𝒯[*H*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si262.png) and *ϕ*[*i*]⁰![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si263.png) be the nodal basis of the standard finite element space *W*[*H*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si264.png). Denote by *S*[*i*] = *s**u**p**p*(*ϕ*[*i*]⁰)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si265.png) and define *ϕ*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si266.png) with support in *S*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si267.png) as follows:

*L**ϕ*[*i*] = 0*i**n**K*, *ϕ*[*i*] = *ϕ*[*i*]⁰*o**n*∂*K*, ∀*K* ∈ 𝒯[*H*], *K* ⊂ *S*[*i*]. (5.65)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si268.png)(5.65) 

Note that even though the choice of *ϕ*[*i*]⁰![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si269.png) can be quite arbitrary, our main assumption is that the basis functions satisfy the leading order homogeneous equations when the right-hand side *f*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si270.png) is a smooth function. In particular, *K*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si271.png) can be chosen to be a volume smaller than the coarse grid. Indeed, in the presence of scale separation, one can use the solution in representative volume element (RVE) to represent the solution in the entire region as it is done in classical homogenization. Once the multiscale basis functions are constructed, we let *V*[*H*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si272.png) be the finite element space spanned by *ϕ*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si273.png).

In general, the global formulation of MsFEM is derived from standard FEMs. In the case of GFEMs and assuming the multiscale basis functions are conforming, *V*[*h*] ⊂ *H*[0]¹(*Ω*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si274.png), the MsFEM is to find *u*[*h*] ∈ *V*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si275.png) such that

∫[*Ω*]*a*(*x*)∇*u*[*h*] ⋅ ∇*v*[*h*]*d**x* = ∫[*Ω*]*f**v*[*h*]*d**x*, *v*[*h*] ∈ *V*[*h*]. (5.66)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si276.png)(5.66) 

One can choose the test functions from *W*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si277.png) and arrive at Petrov–Galerkin version of MsFEM: to find *u*[*h*] ∈ *V*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si278.png) such that

∫[*Ω*]*a*(*x*)∇*u*[*h*] ⋅ ∇*v*[*h*]*d**x* = ∫[*Ω*]*f**v*[*h*]*d**x*, *v*[*h*] ∈ *W*[*H*]. (5.67)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si279.png)(5.67) 

In the previous discussion, we presented simplest multiscale basis function construction and a global formulation. In general, the global formulation can be easily modified and various global formulations based on *finite volume, mixed finite element, discontinuous Galerkin finite element,* and other methods can be derived (

By choosing a linear boundary condition for the multiscale basis function, we will create a mismatch between the exact solution *u*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si281.png) and the finite element approximation across the element boundary. We will discuss this issue further and introduce an oversampling technique to alleviate this difficulty. This technique enables us to remove the artificial numerical boundary layer across the coarse grid boundary element. We would like to note that in 1D case, this issue is not present since the boundaries of the coarse element consist of isolated points.

One can use the representation of multiscale basis functions via fine-scale basis functions to assemble the stiffness matrix. This is particularly useful in code development. Assume that multiscale basis function (in discrete form) *ϕ*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si282.png) can be written as

*ϕ*[*i*] = *d*[*i**j*]*ϕ*[*j*]^(0, *f*), (5.68)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si283.png)(5.68) 

where *D* = (*d*[*i**j*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si284.png) is a matrix and *ϕ*[*j*]^(0, *f*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si285.png) are fine-scale finite element basis functions (e.g., piecewise linear functions). Substituting the expression *ϕ*[*i*] = *d*[*i**j*]*ϕ*[*j*]^(0, *f*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si286.png) into the formula for the stiffness matrix *a*[*i**j*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si287.png), we have

*a*[*i**j*] = ∫[*Ω*]*a*∇*ϕ*[*i*]∇*ϕ*[*j*]*d**x* = *d*[*i**l*](∫[*Ω*]*a*∇*ϕ*[*l*]^(0, *f*)∇*ϕ*[*m*]^(0, *f*)*d**x*)*d*[*j**m*]. (5.69)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si288.png)(5.69) 

Denoting the stiffness matrix for the fine-scale problem by *A*^(*f*) = (*a*[*l**m*]^(*f*))![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si289.png), *a*[*l**m*]^(*f*) = ∫[*Ω*]*a*∇*ϕ*[*l*]^(0, *f*)∇*ϕ*[*m*]^(0, *f*)*d**x*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si290.png), we have

*A* = *D**A*^(*f*)*D*^(*T*). (5.70)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si291.png)(5.70) 

Similarly, for the right-hand side, we have *b* = ∫[*Ω*]*f**ϕ*[*i*]*d**x* = *D**b*^(*f*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si292.png), where *b*^(*f*) = (*b*[*i*]^(*f*))![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si293.png), *b*[*i*]^(*f*) = ∫[*Ω*]*f**ϕ*[*i*]^(0, *f*)*d**x*.![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si294.png) This simplification can be used in the assembly of the stiffness matrix. The similar procedure can be done for the Petrov–Galerkin MsFEM.

If the local computational domain is chosen to be smaller than the coarse grid block, then one can use approximation of *u*[*h*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si295.png) in REV to represent the left-hand side of the MsFEM weak form. In this case, there is often no need to compute the integral over the entire coarse grid block *K* and one can approximate this integral via the integral over REV:

$\\int\_{\\Omega}{a\\left( x \\right)\\nabla u\_{h}} \\cdot \\nabla v\_{h}\\mathit{dx} \\approx \\sum\\limits\_{K}\\frac{\\mathit{|K|}}{|K\_{loc}|}\\int\_{K\_{loc}}{a\\left( x \\right)\\nabla u\_{h}} \\cdot \\nabla v\_{h}\\mathit{dx}.$ (5.71)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si296.png)(5.71) 

The solution of the local leading order partial differential equations (PDEs) is approximated. Similar approximation can be done for the right-hand side. With this formulation, one has

$\\sum\\limits\_{K}\\frac{\\mathit{|K|}}{|K\_{loc}|}\\int\_{K\_{loc}}{a\\left( x \\right)}\\nabla u\_{h} \\cdot \\nabla v\_{h}\\mathit{dx} = \\sum\\limits\_{K}\\frac{\\mathit{|K|}}{|K\_{loc}|}\\int\_{K\_{loc}}{a\\left( x \\right)fv\_{h}\\mathit{dx},\\forall v\_{h}} \\in W\_{H}.$ (5.72)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si297.png)(5.72) 

The heterogeneous multiscale method is a general methodology for an efficient numerical computation of problems with multiple scales. Scale separation is exploited so that coarse-grained variables can be evolved on macroscopic spatial/temporal scales using data that are predicted on the basis of the simulation of the microscopic process on microscale spatial/temporal domains.

 

### 5.2.2 Oversampled techniques

The boundary conditions for the multiscale basis functions play a crucial role in capturing small-scale information. If the local boundary conditions for the multiscale basis functions do not reflect the nature of the underlying heterogeneities, MsFEM can have large errors. These errors result from the resonance between the coarse grid size and the characteristic length scale of the problem (

A deeper analysis based on the homogenization theory shows the main source of the resonance effect. By a judicious choice of boundary conditions for multiscale basis functions, we can reduce the resonance errors significantly. Since the boundary layer in the first-order corrector is thin, we can sample in a domain with the size larger than *h* and use only the interior sampled information to construct the multiscale basis functions. By doing this, we can reduce the influence of the boundary layer in the larger sample domain on the basis functions significantly. Oversampling techniques are often used in porous media simulations to achieve high accuracy. They reduce the effect of artificial boundary conditions that are imposed in computing local quantities, such as upscaled permeabilities or basis functions. In the problems without scale separation and strong nonlocal effects, the oversampling region is taken to be the entire domain. The schematic description of oversampled region is shown in [Fig. 5.26](#chp005_a.xhtml#f0135).

![Figure 5.26 Schematic description of oversampled region.](OEBPS/images/F000058f05-26-9780128209578.jpg)

Figure 5.26 Schematic description of oversampled region.

Specifically, let *ϕ*[*j*]^(*E*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si298.png) be the basis functions satisfying the homogeneous elliptic equation in the larger domain *K*[*E*] ⊃ *K*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si299.png). We then form the actual basis *ϕ*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si300.png) by linear combination of *ϕ*[*j*]^(*E*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si301.png):

$\\phi\_{i} = \\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{d}c\_{\\mathit{ij}}\\phi\_{j}^{E}.$ (5.73)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si302.png)(5.73) 

The coefficients *c*[*i**j*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si303.png) are determined by condition *ϕ*[*i*](*x*[*j*]) = *δ*[*i**j*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si304.png), where *x*[*j*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si305.png) are nodal points.

Extensive numerical experiments have demonstrated that the oversampling technique does improve the numerical error substantially in many applications. On the other hand, the oversampling technique results in a nonconforming MsFEM method, where the basis functions are discontinuous along the edges of coarse grid blocks. Analysis shows that the nonconforming error is small. Analysis also reveals another source of resonance, which is the mismatch between the mesh size and the “perfect” sample size. In the case of a periodic structure, the “perfect” sample size is the length of an integer multiple of the period. We call the new resonance the “cell resonance.” In the error expansion, this resonance effect appears as a higher order correction. In numerical computations, we found that the cell resonance error is generally small and is rarely observed in practice. Nonetheless, it is possible to completely eliminate this cell resonance error by using the oversampling technique to construct the multiscale basis functions, but using piecewise linear functions as test functions.

 

### 5.2.3 Proper orthogonal decomposition

Space reduction is one important model reduction methodology. FEMs have two key ingredients: weak formulation and the choice of trial and test spaces. In FEM, we would like to choose the space that represents the solution well. In MsFEM, we choose the space of lower dimensions but containing fine-grid information. The multiscale space is a subspace of the fine-grid FEM space.

Consider a linear forward problem written in a matrix form:

*A**u* = *b*. (5.74)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si306.png)(5.74) 

This is a discretization of a multiscale problem and our goal is to construct a low-dimensional solution space for *b* ∈ *S*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si307.png), where *S*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si308.png) is a linear space. At this point, we will focus on techniques for finding a low-dimensional subspace for a space spanned by vectors. Assume that the solution is computed for some *b*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si309.png)s and given by *u*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si310.png). Then, we would like to find a reduced dimensional space that represents

*s**p**a**n*{*u*[1], ⋯, *u*[*N*]}. (5.75)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si311.png)(5.75) 

Given *u*[1], ⋯, *u*[*N*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si312.png), we form a matrix *U* = (*u*[1],*u*[2],⋯,*u*[*N*]) ∈ ℝ^(*M* × *N*),![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si313.png) which is *M* × *N*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si314.png) matrix assuming *u*[*i*] ∈ ℝ^(*M*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si315.png). Consider the eigenvalues of *U*^(*T*)*U*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si316.png) as well as *U**U*^(*T*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si317.png):

*U*^(*T*)*U**z*[*i*] = *σ*[*i*]²*z*[*i*], *U**U*^(*T*)*y*[*i*] = *σ*[*i*]²*y*[*i*]. (5.76)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si318.png)(5.76) 

where *U*^(*T*)*U*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si319.png) and *U**U*^(*T*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si320.png) are *N* × *N*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si321.png) and *M* × *M*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si322.png) matrices. Note that some eigenvalues are zero. Then, if we form matrix *Y* = (*y*[1],…,*y*[*M*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si323.png), *Z* = (*z*[1],…,*z*[*N*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si324.png) then

*Y*^(*T*)*U**Z* =  : *Σ*, (5.77)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si325.png)(5.77) 

where *Σ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si326.png) is a diagonal matrix with *σ*[*i*]²![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si327.png) on the main diagonal and zero everywhere. Assume *σ*[1]² ≥ *σ*[2]² ≥ ⋯ ≥ *σ*[*d*]² > 0.![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si328.png)

*Proper orthogonal decomposition (POD) theorem* *U* = (*u*[1],*u*[2],…,*u*[*N*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si329.png) is an *M* × *N*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si330.png) matrix with rank *d*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si331.png). For any *L* ≤ *d*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si332.png), we consider

$\\min\\limits\_{y\_{1},\\ldots,y\_{L}}\\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{N}{\\| u\_{j} - \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{L}{\\langle u\_{j},y\_{i}\\rangle y\_{i}}\\|^{2}}$ (5.78)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si333.png)(5.78) 

where *y*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si334.png) are orthonormal vectors. The minimum is given by eigenvectors *y*[1], …, *y*[*L*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si335.png) of *U**U*^(*T*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si336.png) and it is equal to $\\sum\_{i = L + 1}^{d}\\sigma\_{i}^{2}.$![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si337.png)

In POD, we are interested in find a subspace such that (*u*−*u*[0])^(*T*)*A*(*u*−*u*[0])/(*u*−*u*[0])^(*T*)*S*(*u*−*u*[0])![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si338.png) is minimum, where *u*[0]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si339.png) is the projection onto the low-dimensional space. We claim that the subspace needs to be chosen on the basis of largest eigenvalues of *A**ϕ*[*i*] = *σ*[*i*]²*S**ϕ*[*i*].![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si340.png) Note that the eigenvectors satisfy

*ϕ*[*i*]^(*T*)*S**ϕ*[*j*] = *δ*[*i**j*], *ϕ*[*i*]^(*T*)*A**ϕ*[*j*] = *σ*[*i*]²*δ*[*i**j*]. (5.79)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si341.png)(5.79) 

We note that (*u*−*u*[0])^(*T*)*A*(*u*−*u*[0])/(*u*−*u*[0])^(*T*)*S*(*u*−*u*[0]) ≤ *σ*[*L* + 1]²![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si342.png) if the subspace consists of first *L*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si343.png) eigenvectors and $u\_{0} = \\sum\_{i = 1}^{L}{\\langle u,\\phi\_{i}\\rangle\_{S}\\phi\_{i}}.$![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si344.png)

 

Theorem 5.2

For every *u*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si345.png), there exists *u*[0]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si346.png) such that

$\\frac{\\left( u - u\_{0} \\right)^{T}A\\left( u - u\_{0} \\right)}{\\left( u - u\_{0} \\right)^{T}S\\left( u - u\_{0} \\right)} \\leq \\sigma\_{L + 1}^{2},$ (5.80)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si347.png)(5.80) 

where *u*[0]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si348.png) is in the subspace spanned by first *L*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si349.png) eigenvectors of *A**ϕ*[*i*] = *λ*[*i*]*S**ϕ*[*i*].![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si350.png)

We consider ∂/∂*x*[*i*](*a*[*i**j*](*x*,*μ*)∂/∂*x*[*j*]*u*) = *f*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si351.png) and write its discrete FEM formulation:

*A*(*μ*)*u*(*μ*) = *f*. (5.81)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si352.png)(5.81) 

We assume that

$a\_{\\mathit{ij}}\\left( x,\\mu \\right) = \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{Q}{a\_{i}\\left( x \\right)B\_{i}\\left( \\mu \\right)}.$ (5.82)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si353.png)(5.82) 

In a finite-dimensional setup, we consider some values of *μ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si354.png): *μ*[1], *μ*[2], …, *μ*[*L*].![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si355.png) Then, POD basis is constructed on the basis of snapshots *u*(*μ*[1]),![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si356.png) *u*(*μ*[2]), …, *u*(*μ*[*L*]),![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si357.png) as

$\\underset{\\psi\_{1},\\ldots,\\psi\_{l}{orthonormal}}{\\arg\\min}\\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{L}{\\| u\\left( \\mu\_{j} \\right) - \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{l}{\\langle u\\left( \\mu\_{j} \\right),\\psi\_{i}\\rangle\\psi\_{i}}\\|}^{2}.$ (5.83)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si358.png)(5.83) 

We seek the solution in the discrete case that

$u^{l} = \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{l}u\_{i}\\psi\_{i}.$ (5.84)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si359.png)(5.84) 

Substituting this into the original equation and multiplying by a test function *ψ*[*j*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si360.png), we get

$\\sum\\limits\_{i}u\_{i}\\langle A\\psi\_{i},\\psi\_{j}\\rangle = \\langle f,\\psi\_{j}\\rangle.$ (5.85)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si361.png)(5.85) 

The computation of the stiffness matrix can be done inexpensively if ⟨*A**ψ*[*i*], *ψ*[*j*]⟩![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si362.png) are precomputed in offline:

$\\langle A\\psi\_{i},\\psi\_{j}\\rangle = \\sum\\limits\_{m = 1}^{Q}{\\langle A\_{m}\\psi\_{i},\\psi\_{j}\\rangle}.$ (5.86)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si363.png)(5.86) 

We note that

*v*^(*T*)*A**w* ≤ (*v*^(*T*)*A**v*)^(1/2)(*w*^(*T*)*A**w*)^(1/2). (5.87)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si364.png)(5.87) 

One can show that for any *μ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si365.png)

(*u*(*u*) − *u*^(*l*))^(*T*)*A*(*u*(*μ*)−*u*^(*l*)) ≤ (*u*(*u*) − *v*^(*l*))^(*T*)*A*(*u*(*μ*)−*v*^(*l*)), (5.88)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si366.png)(5.88) 

where *u*^(*l*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si367.png) is any vector from *V*^(*G*) = *s**p**a**n*(*ψ*[1],…,*ψ*[*l*]).![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si368.png) Then, by choosing the interpolant in a proper way, we have

$\\sum\\limits\_{j}{\\left( u{\\left( u\_{j} \\right) - u}^{l} \\right)^{T}A\\left( u\\left( \\mu\_{j} \\right) - u^{l} \\right)} \\leq \\sum\\limits\_{i = l + 1}^{d}\\sigma\_{i}^{2}.$ (5.89)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si369.png)(5.89) 

We consider the eigenvalue problem in the space *U*^(*T*)*A**U**ϕ*[*i*] = *λ*[*i*]*ϕ*[*i*],![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si370.png) where *U*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si371.png) is the matrix with columns consisting of *u*[*i*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si372.png). We can try to get a basis such that it provides best estimate in sup-norm. Reduced basis approach follows greedy algorithm to find such basis. Implementation can be described as two steps: the first step is orthogonalization and the second step is to define a distance function and a tolerance to determine when to stop. For error analysis, we first note that

∥*u*(*μ*) − *n*^(*N*)∥[*A*] ≤ ∥*u*(*μ*) − *v*^(*N*)∥[*A*]. (5.90)

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si373.png)(5.90) 

If we take the distance function to be defined via *A*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si374.png) norm, we arrive an estimate ∥*u*(*μ*) − *u*^(*N*)∥[*A*] ≤ *δ*[*t**o**l*]![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si375.png) by using the previous implementation. This algorithm is implemented for continuous *μ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si376.png) which is not practical. For discrete *μ*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si377.png) the algorithm can be formulated in a similar way.

 

### 5.2.4 Generalized multiscale finite element methods

In generalized multiscale finite element methods (GMsFEMs), we combine local multiscale methods and global model reduction. This method incorporates complex input space information and the input–output relation. It systematically enriches the coarse space through our local construction. The approach of GMsFEM, as in many multiscale and model reduction techniques, divides the computation into two stages: *Offline stage*: we construct a small-dimensional space that can be efficiently used in the online stage to construct multiscale basis functions. *Online stage*: for a given input parameter, we compute the required online coarse space.

In offline computation, we need coarse grid generation, construction of snapshot space that will be used to compute an offline space, and construction of a small-dimensional offline space by performing dimension reduction in the space of global snapshots. In online computation, we need to, for each input parameter, compute multiscale basis functions; solve a coarse-grid problem for any force term and boundary condition; and sometimes iterative solvers are needed as well.

In offline stage, we construct a small-dimensional space that can be efficiently used in the online stage to construct multiscale basis functions. These multiscale basis functions can be reused for any input parameter to solve the problem on a coarse-grid. This provides a substantial computational saving at the online stage. The construction of snapshot space involves solving local problems for various choices of input parameters. This space is used to construct the offline space in the next step via a spectral decomposition of the snapshot space. The snapshot space in a coarse region can be replaced by the fine-grid space associated with this coarse space. However, in many applications, one can judiciously choose the space of snapshots to avoid expensive offline space construction. In dimension reduction using global snapshots, the offline space in this step is constructed by spectrally decomposing the space of snapshots. This spectral decomposition is typically based on the offline eigenvalue problem. More precisely, we seek a subspace of the snapshot space such that it can approximate any element of the snapshot space in the appropriate sense defined via auxiliary bilinear forms. The spectral decomposition enables us to select the high-energy elements from the offline space by choosing those eigenvectors corresponding to the largest eigenvalues.

In online computation, construction of multiscale basis functions is the first step to compute the required online coarse space. This space is computed by performing a spectral decomposition in the offline space via an eigenvalue problem. Furthermore, the eigenvectors corresponding to the largest eigenvalues are identified and used to form the online coarse space. In general, we want this to be a small-dimensional subspace of the offline space. The online coarse space is used within the finite element framework to solve the original global problem. In the solution of a coarse-grid problem, we may use one of several options such as the Galerkin coupling of multiscale basis functions and the Petrov–Galerkin coupling of multiscale basis functions. In some of these coupling approaches, the choice of the initial partition of unity (that can be computed in the offline or online stage) is important.

The GMsFEM techniques differ from many previous approaches that are based on the homogenization theory. In the homogenization-based methods, one usually constructs local approximation based on local solves and these approaches do not provide a systematic procedure to complement the local spaces. It is important to note that one needs to systematically complement the local spaces in order to converge to the fine-grid solution. How to develop an online systematic enrichment procedure and how to construct the initial partition of unity functions play a crucial role in obtaining a low-dimensional offline space. These issues are central points of the GMsFEM method.

 

### 5.2.5 Generalized multiscale finite element method example

We consider the linear elliptic equations:

$\\begin{matrix} {\\mathit{Lu}: =} & {- \\nabla \\cdot \\left( {\\kappa\\left( {x;u} \\right)\\nabla u} \\right) = f{in}\\Omega,} \\\\ {u =} & {0{on}\\partial\\Omega,} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$

![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si378.png)

where *Ω*![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si379.png) is a domain in ℝ^(*d*)![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si380.png). The parameter *a*(*x*) = (*a*[*i**j*](*x*))![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si381.png) is a heterogeneous field varying over multiple scales. It is symmetric positive definite and bounded. The space of snapshots can be constructed by solving local problems. For example, we can define the space of snapshots, *V*[*s**n**a**p**s**h**o**t**s*]^(*ω*[*i*])![image](OEBPS/images/F000058si382.png), by the solution of the following PDE:
